THE OPPOSING SHORE
OceanofPDF.com

1
A Commitment
I belong to one of the oldest families of Orsenna. Years of peace and plenty
are what I recall of a childhood spent between the old palace in the Via San
Domenico and a villa on the Brenta’s banks to which we returned every
summer and where I already accompanied my father, riding round his fields
or verifying his bailiffs’ accounts. A naturally pensive disposition, studies I
completed at the city’s ancient and renowned University, as well as the
fortune I inherited upon my mother’s death, disposed me to be anything but
precipitate in the choice of a career. Orsenna’s Signory survives as if in the
shadow of a glory accrued in ages past from its military victories over the
Infidel and from the fabled profits of its commerce with the East: it
resembles an ancient nobleman living withdrawn from the world and
sheltered by prestige, despite the loss of his credit and the ruin of his
fortune, from his creditors’ depredations; its nugacious activity, still tranquil
and almost majestic, is that of a patriarch whose inveterately robust
appearance generates disbelief as to death’s gradual progress within his
person. Public duties and service to the State, for which the zeal of
Orsenna’s ancient patriciate has remained legendary, therefore offer few
inducements, in this debile condition, to whatever is aspiring and excitable
in its young people’s impulses: declining years mark the time a man most
effectively accedes to duties in the Signory. Consequently something fallow
and fabulous hovered over the libertine and in many respects unedifying life
led by the city’s young nobles. I participated wholeheartedly in their
feverish diversions, their enthusiasms of a day, their passions of a week—a
precocious yawn rewards an elite too long enthroned upon the heights, and I
soon took part in those delights on which the city’s jeunesse dorée prided
itself: delights of the higher boredom. My days were divided between
reading the poets and solitary rides in the countryside; on those storm-laden

summer afternoons which swathe Orsenna in a cloak of lead, I enjoyed
vanishing within the forests surrounding the city; the pleasure of such
untrammeled excursions multiplied hour by hour, even as the speed of a
great-hearted stallion redoubles with each furlong; frequently I made my
way home only at twilight. Such returns through the deepening penumbra
delighted me: even as the crest of its banners is ennobled for us by a luster
all the more precious for rising out of a mist of centuries, Orsenna’s domes
and towers soared more limpid out of the haze; at such moments my steed’s
slackened gait toward the city seemed to me burdened with a secret. My
nocturnal occupations were more frivolous: I measured myself against
young people my age in the Platonic tourneys of the Academies, which
have flourished in Orsenna in proportion as our Senate has emptied; I
granted much to love, and in its exercise proved as ardent and venturesome
as any other man. It so happened that my mistress forsook me: at first such
a defection struck me as no more than a matter of caprice, and I grew
troubled only upon realizing, suddenly, how little inclined I was to take
another. This banal obstacle to preoccupations whose rhythm had, without
my knowing it, gradually but excessively slowed, all at once wreaked havoc
upon what only a few days before I had regarded as an acceptable
existence; my life seemed to me irreparably blighted—the very ground
upon which I had so negligently built was giving way beneath my feet. Out
of the blue I longed to travel, and sought from the Signory an assignment to
some remote province.
Orsenna’s government, like that of all mercantile states, has always been
distinguished by a jealous suspicion of the leaders—and even of the
subaltern officers—of its armies and its fleets. Against the risks of a
conspiracy or a military coup d’état, long dreaded in an epoch when
continual warfare made it necessary to maintain considerable forces in the
field, Orsenna’s aristocracy did not feel adequately protected by subjecting
its military cadres to the civil authority’s closest supervision: from very
remote times, the noblest families saw no disgrace in delegating their sons
to functions within that authority which can scarcely be differentiated from
the practice of espionage, and whose effect had long been to quash any

attempt at armed rebellion. Such are the Signory’s famous “Eyes”: their
powers—unspecified but in fact always unofficially supported by the
weight of a great name and the credit of an old family—generally favor the
greatest possible initiative, even in the course of a campaign: if unanimity
of purpose and energy in the prosecution of Orsenna’s wars have
occasionally suffered from the atmosphere of suspicion and the timidity in
command engendered by such practices, it is assumed, on the other hand,
that the false position in which they find themselves is likely to develop,
early on, a certain political dexterity and sense of diplomacy in those whom
the Signory destines for its great offices. These suspect beginnings as an
accredited spy were long regarded as the obligatory path to the highest
distinctions. Given the decrepitude and nervous irritation to which her
powers were now reduced, Orsenna might have relaxed so intense a
vigilance without much danger, but the force of traditions, as in all
crumbling empires, grows proportionately with the explicit revelation, in
the machinery of government and commerce alike, of the preponderant
action of all principles of inertia: nowadays sons of leading families are
assigned to the Eyes in the same anodyne spirit in which elsewhere they are
sent off to travel abroad and to take part in great hunts, but they are still so
assigned; a ceremony grown almost ludicrous over the years, but
scrupulously maintained, continues to mark this fashion of assuming the
toga. My father, in his semi-retirement, had been distressed by my life of
dissipation; he was pleased to learn of my new attitudes and supported my
application to the Signory with all his credit, which remained great. Only a
few days after he had been informed of a decision favorable in principle, a
decree of the Senate confirmed me in the functions of Observer attached to
the Light Forces the Signory maintained on the Gulf of Syrtes.
In his firm intention of removing me from the capital and of inuring me
to the demands of a sterner existence, my father had perhaps served me
beyond my vague desires for change. The province of Syrtes, on the remote
southern border, is a kind of Ultima Thule among Orsenna’s territories. Few
and ill kept are the roads linking it to the capital, across a region which is
virtually a desert. The coastline bordering it, flat and festooned with

dangerous reefs, has never permitted the establishment of a navigable
harbor. The sea beyond is empty, and the desolation of these shores is
rendered only more apparent by ancient ruins. Indeed, these sterile sands
once sustained a rich civilization, in the days when Arabs invaded the
region and fertilized it by their ingenious irrigation systems; but life has
subsequently withdrawn from these peripheries, as if the too-niggardly
blood of a mummified body politic no longer circulated in them; it is also
said that the climate there is growing increasingly dry and that the scant
patches of vegetation are shrinking from year to year, as though devoured
by the desert winds. State functionaries ordinarily regard Syrtes as a
purgatory in which to expiate some defection of service by years of
interminable ennui; to those who remain there by choice the capital
attributes provincial, even half-savage tastes—the journey “down to
Syrtes,” when commanded, is accompanied by an endless cortege of
witticisms. There was no lack of these in the farewell banquet I offered my
companions in debauchery on the eve of my departure; and yet, in the
intervals of toasts and laughter, there sometimes prevailed round the table a
just-perceptible embarrassment, a silence difficult to vanquish, over which
fell a shadow of gloom: my exile was more serious and more remote than it
had at first appeared. Each man present felt that my life was truly about to
change: already the barbarous name of Syrtes was sequestering me from
that joyous throng. A definitive breach was about to open—and for the first
time—in this circle of young friendships; it had opened, and already I was
disconcerting the others by making it all too visible: I believe some of them
hoped I would vanish on the spot, if only to keep it out of their sight. As we
left one another on the Academy threshold, Orlando suddenly flung his
arms around me with a strained, preoccupied expression quite alien to the
evening’s frivolities, and in a solemn tone of voice wished me “good luck
on the front—at Syrtes.” I left Orsenna early the next morning, in the fast
coach which carried the official post to Syrtes.
There is great charm in leaving a familiar city at dawn for a novel
destination. Nothing was stirring yet in Orsenna’s sleepy streets, and the
great palm fronds spread all the more broadly above blind walls, the

chiming of the cathedral clock wakened a dim yet lingering vibration from
the old facades. With all that seemed chosen for me so decisively, we glided
along well-known streets already strange, chosen for me so decisively, by
their orientation to a distance still indefinite. This farewell weighed lightly
upon me; I gave myself up to savoring the bitter air and my own alertness,
detached already from all this vague somnolence: we were leaving at the
statutory hour. The suburban gardens filed past quite grimly; a chill hovered
over the humid fields, and I huddled deeper into the carriage and began
inventorying with curiosity a large leather portfolio given me at the
Chancellery the night before, when I took my oath of allegiance. Here in
my hands I was holding a palpable sign of my new importance, and I was
still too young not to take, in hefting it, an almost childish pleasure. It
contained various official documents pertaining to my appointment—their
considerable quantity put me in a good humor—as well as specific
instructions concerning my duties and the conduct to be adopted in the post
I would occupy; I decided to read them at my leisure. The last item was a
heavy yellow envelope sealed with the Signory’s arms; the superscription,
in a laborious hand, caught my notice; “To be opened only after receiving
Emergency Instructions.” These were the secret orders; I straightened up a
little and glanced fiercely at the horizon. A recollection, tinged with both
absurdity and mystery, slowly rose to consciousness—it had been obscurely
goading me ever since I had been assigned to this forsaken outpost: on the
Syrtes frontier where I would soon be, Orsenna was at war. What detracted
from the gravity of the matter was the fact that Orsenna had been at war for
three hundred years.
Not much is known in the Signory about Farghestan, which faces
Orsenna’s territories beyond the Gulf of Syrtes. Invasions which have
lashed it almost continually since ancient times—in the latest instance, the
Mongol invasion—have turned its population into shifting sands, where
each nascent wave has been covered over and erased by another, and its
civilization into a barbarous mosaic, where extremes of Oriental refinement
border on nomadic savagery. Political life, on this uncertain basis, has
developed in the form of pulsations as brutal as they are disconcerting:

sometimes the country falls prey to dissension and collapses upon itself,
virtually disintegrating into feudal clans seething with racial hatred—
sometimes a tide of mysticism, born in the depths of its deserts, fuses these
passions together to make Farghestan into a momentary torch in the hands
of an ambitious conqueror. No one in Orsenna knows much more about
Farghestan—and no one seeks to know much more—than the fact that the
two countries, as we are taught at our school desks, are officially in a state
of hostility. It is actually three centuries ago—in a period when navigation
had not yet forsaken Syrtes—that continual Farghestani piracies along these
desert coasts drove Orsenna to launch an expedition of reprisal, which stood
off the enemy coast and bombarded its ports without mercy. Several
skirmishes ensued, and then hostilities, which had engaged no major
interest on either side, languished and quite died down of their own
inanition. Tribal wars paralyzed navigation in Farghestani ports for many
years, and Orsenna’s shipping, for its part, slid into a gradual lethargy: one
by one, merchant vessels abandoned a secondary sea where commerce was
dying out. Thus the Gulf of Syrtes became, by degrees, a veritable dead sea
which no one any longer dreamed of crossing; its harbors, silted up, now
received only coasting vessels of the smallest tonnage: Orsenna was
rumored to maintain, in some ramshackle shipyard, only a few sloops of the
least aggressive nature, their sole function being to police the sponge-
fishing grounds in the appropriate season. But in this general paralysis, the
will to bring the conflict to a legal conclusion was quite as defective as that
to extend it by arms; ruined as they were and stripped of their powers,
Orsenna and Farghestan remained two proud countries, each jealous of a
long and glorious past, and all the less disposed, on either side, to concede
rights which would henceforth cost so little to uphold. Equally reluctant to
make the first overture to a peaceful settlement, both nations immured
themselves in a touchy and arrogant stagnation and henceforth, by tacit
agreement, made sure to avoid contact. Orsenna forbade all native shipping
outside her own coastal waters, and there is every reason to suppose that
analogous measures were taken, at about the same period, by Farghestan.
As the years of so indulgent a war accumulated, Orsenna gradually reached

the undeclared conclusion that even a peaceful diplomatic overture would
be regarded as an impolitic gesture, involving something too specific, too
intense, and likely to disturb in its grave this corpse of a war long since laid
to rest for good. The extreme freedom this uncertain outcome afforded
Orsenna to vaunt without fear of contradiction her great and honorable
victories was moreover a further guarantee of a general tranquility;
belligerence breathed its last during the festivities which still marked the
anniversary of the bombardment; and when the Senate, reversing an earlier
decision, determined to assign funds, initially proposed for an embassy, to
the erection, instead, of a statue of the admiral who had commanded the
expedition against Farghestan, all right-thinking citizens of Orsenna
applauded this essentially prudent decision, and felt that through these
bronze lips the war with Farghestan had truly breathed its last.
Such was the quiescent aspect, leavened indeed with a touch of
buffoonery, under which relations with Farghestan were generally regarded
in Orsenna. There was another one.
Reading the poets of Orsenna, one was astonished to discover that this
abortive war—by and large commonplace in the extreme and quite without
those picturesque episodes deemed likely to inflame the imagination—
occupied a place in their productions disproportionate to that vouchsafed it
by the standard histories. And perhaps even more striking than the
persistence they showed in making it the subject of their lyric flights was
their excessive liberty in adding inordinately to the known facts, in
amassing vast interpolations, engagement upon engagement, in their
accounts of this third-rate war, as if they had found in it a source of
inexhaustible rejuvenation for their genius. Moreover a powerful echo of
these learned poets was to be discerned in Orsenna’s popular traditions:
scholars had managed to assemble a notable catalogue of folk narratives
exclusively concerning Farghestan. Thus adroitly revivified in the poets’
verses, it was worth remarking that even the dead verbiage of ordinary
official documents was concerned, for its part, to preserve intact the ashes
of this historic corpse; thus the Signory had never been permitted—on the

pretext of some specious logical point—to change one word of the residual
wartime vocabulary: the coast of Syrtes remained, for the departments, “the
Syrtes front”; “the Syrtes fleet,” the wretched hulks it was my duty to
supervise; “the Syrtes bases,” the straggling villages strewn along the road
south. Not one leaflet had been removed from the dossier constituted three
centuries ago at the Chancellery; I had had occasion to discover as much
during the course of instruction imposed within all departments by the
Institute of International Law: all the old grievances formulated against
Farghestan slept here, whetted as sharp as on the day they were first written.
“There are seventy-two of them,” the Chief of the Southern Department had
informed me, as one might enumerate the cannons of a ship of the line, and
I had understood, from an inflection of his voice, that these seventy-two
grievances were an eternal part of Orsenna’s patrimony, a precious mandate
to be surrendered only with life itself. One might suppose, dreamily
enough, by the light of these vague signs, that this war’s very
inconclusiveness, actually the token of an irremediable loss of tension, was
the essential singularity which still nourished a few baroque imaginations—
as if a latent conspiracy had been here or there designed by hands still
determined to keep parted, absurdly enough, lips ready to close of their own
accord upon the event—as if someone had inexplicably cherished the
bizarre anomaly of an untimely historical event which had not released all
its energies, which had not exhausted its strength.
Now we were passing through the hilly, wooded region which shut off
the Orsenna countryside to the South. The Roman road occasionally
traversed narrow lanes, sometimes shaded by a vault of dense foliage,
among which vines too would thread their way to form a darker tunnel; at
the end of these prospects, trained like the barrel of a gun, opened out the
matinal blue of valley distances. All the ripe splendor, the opulence of
Orsenna stirred the heart, rising from these autumn-gorged fields; above us,
coolness dripped slowly from the branches, thinning like a fragrance in the
transparent air until the sun’s reticulation at last pierced through it to the
pavement. A calm fulfillment, the salutation of a pure juvenescence,
ascended from this infinite morning. I gulped it down like sparkling wine,

that gentle cavalcade through the open country, but it was less the gaping
future than the environing persistence of an assured and familiar present,
yet one already doomed, which filled my heart to bursting: distancing
myself with all possible speed from my city, I inhaled Orsenna as deeply as
I could fill my lungs. I mused over how tenacious were the cords that bound
me to this country, like a woman whose overripe and over-affectionate
loveliness holds you prisoner; then, from time to time, into this moody
solicitude crept, like a sudden and alarming gust into warm night air, that
disturbing word “war,” and the clear colors of the landscape around me
shifted to a just perceptibly stormy hue. Such insubstantial and fidgety
reveries wearied me—we had reached Mercanza—and I began to pay closer
attention to the countryside.
Once beyond the ramparts of the old Norman fortress, the aura of the
South was already becoming apparent in the gradual wasting away of the
vegetation. The misty dew that poured over the dank forests of Orsenna had
given way to a hard, luminous dryness, against which sparkled garishly, in
the distance, the low white walls of lonely farms. The ground, suddenly
leveling off, stretched to meet us in great bare plains which the road
scarcely bruised, beneath the sun, with a sharper furrow; the wind of our
speed clattered in our ears, forming broader gales over these throbbing
steppes. The clean-swept horizons, where huge herds of storm clouds
frolicked, seemed even more like those of the open sea when the tall
Norman watchtowers would appear, irregularly strewn on the bare plains
and overlooking the flat expanses like so many lighthouses. Herds of half-
wild oxen staggered up out of their mudholes and trotted off, horns high,
the whole massive throng suddenly bristling in the wind. This was a freer,
wilder country where the earth, exposing its true surface, seemed to invite
us, by exasperating our speed, to test its severe curvature and, luring our
galloping equipage ever faster, ever farther, to collapse all horizons into
infinite space. Night was gathering in the East and rose upon us like a wall
of storm clouds; head tilted back on the cushions in the dimness of the
coach, I stared up, exalted, into the calm constellations; those same stars
would shine for us over Syrtes itself.

When I recollect the first days of my stay in Syrtes, it is always with
preternatural vividness that the abnormally powerful impression of
strangeness I had felt upon arrival recurs, and always to that swift journey
that the impression is most fondly attached. We glided along the dusty road
as though within the current of a river of cold air flecked with vague
patches of whiteness; on either side, the darkness loomed up, entirely
opaque; everywhere on these lonely arteries, where any encounter already
seemed so unlikely, nothing equalled the impenetrable ambiguity of the
shapes suddenly silhouetted against the night, only to vanish into it
forthwith. In the absence of any visible landmark, I felt rising within me
that gradual atony of all sense of direction and of distance which paralyzes
us before any real symptom—like the onset of a fever—in the middle of a
road where one has lost one’s way. Above this earth benumbed in a
dreamless sleep, the huge and stupefying glitter of the stars broke upon us
like a belittling tide, sharpening the sense of hearing to a morbid refinement
of its dry blue sparks, as one cocks one’s ears despite oneself to hear the sea
divined somewhere in the distance. Swept on by this enthralling rush into
pure darkness, I reveled for the first time in these southern nights unknown
in Orsenna, as in some initiatory ablution. Something had been promised to
me—something had been revealed; I was entering without enlightenment
into an almost agonizing intimacy, eager for the morning already pressing
upon my unseeing eyes, even as one advances blindfolded toward the site of
revelation.
And it rose, that morning, behind the rainy scrub and the low clouds of
an empty plain. Sharp jolts shook the coach on our bruised and stony path,
strewn with sickly-looking patches of some scant weed. The roadbed took
the form, here, of a shallow trench; on each side, as high as a man, it
seemed carved out of a sea of dense, grayish reeds whose surface the
discouraged eye swept over and whose vanishing lanes the road’s continual
detours seemed to wall up at every moment. As far as the eye could see,
through the liquid mist, there was no sign of a house, of a tree. The soft and
spongy dawn was riddled at intervals by dubious flashes of light which
limped across the low clouds like the groping beam of a lighthouse. The

rain’s suspect and penetrating intimacy, the disorienting touch of the
downpour’s first hesitant drops, stopped up these vague solitudes,
intensifying an irresistible scent of wet leaves and stagnant water; on the
sand’s soft padding, each drop was imprinted with a delicate separateness,
just as one discriminates the more powerful raindrops which drip off the
foliage. To the left, not far from the road, the sea of reeds grew up to the
edge of mudholes and empty lagoons, closed off from the open sea by gray
sandbars where tongues of foam slithered uncertainly under the mist. The
country’s suspect silence was made more apparent by the sudden remissions
and hesitant resumptions of the rain, and the impression of unwonted
suspense communicated by its unequal intervals. Beneath this smoky light,
in this sleepy clamminess and this warm rain, the coach rolled on more
carefully now, affording our dubious journey a fugitive nuance of intrusion.
This stifling languor of a nightmare’s end withdrew into the depths of time,
recovering beneath this warm and moistened breath the summary contours,
the indeterminate fluidity and secrecy of a primal prairie, with its high grass
so suggestive of an ambuscade.
We rode along for hours through this sleepy terrain. From time to time a
gray bird exploded out of the reeds and vanished high in the sky, quivering
like a tiny ball on top of the fountain at the very crest of its monotonous cry.
A foghorn stranded on a reef somewhere pierced the mists on two calm
notes, as though from a huge subsiding bellows. Sometimes a gust of wind
produced a grim rustle among the reeds, and for a moment the lagoons
breathed their mist over a dim mirror, a dead skin without reflections.
Something was smothering behind this wasteland fog, like a mouth under a
pillow. The path suddenly became a road again, a gray tower emerged from
the thickened mist, the lagoons came to meet us from all directions and
sleeked the roadway’s banks at the water’s edge, where several ghosts of
ships took on a certain consistency: this was our journey’s end, we were
arriving at the Admiralty. The wet roadway shimmered faintly; alongside a
silhouette waving a lantern to guide the coach through the wall of mist
appeared a sailor’s oilskins, an old naval cap, and a hard short moustache
beaded with raindrops: Captain Marino, commander of the “Syrtes base.”

People had seldom mentioned him to me in Orsenna, except (the frivolity
of the secret services appeared here in its true colors) in that disagreeably
superficial tone, and with that casual unconcern by which the nuance of
some vague worldly connection is defined, as a man who was simply
“boring.” This summary disqualification was enough to dismiss him, to
push him into a remote background, all this way from the capital. He was
here, now: this massive figure emerging from the rain and suddenly quite
real at the end of this phantasmagoria of fog—we were to live together!—
and I suddenly was aware that I was shaking hands with a stranger, an
unknown quantity. The hand itself was strong, slow, and kindly—the
welcome a courteous one—and whatever good-natured mockery I could
hear in his voice was certain to put me at ease, once I came indoors, about
anything that might be a little scabrous in such an assumption of contact. I
realized at once that between us there would arise no difficulty as to my
singular functions—and this was already a great relief—but at the same
time it seemed to me that it would be a long while before I learned anything
more than that. In his sharp, quick glance there was a veiled penetration
which contrasted with the warm, loud and reassuring voice; in the calm face
and the cool mouth, a visible mastery and reserve. The eyes, shadowed by
the low visor, were a cold sea-gray; the tanned hand which lingered
markedly in the handshake was missing two fingers. Captain Marino
completely emerged from the mist now, and something in me murmured
that henceforth he would not be thrust back into it so readily.
Looming up out of the spectral mists of this desert of reeds at the edge of
an empty sea, this Admiralty was a singular locality. In front of us, beyond
a stretch of moorland overgrown with thistles and flanked by a few long
low houses, the mist enlarged the contours of what looked like a ruined
fortress. Behind the moats half silted up by time, it looked like a huge and
heavy gray mass, its smooth walls pierced by only a few arrow slits and an
infrequent embrasure for a cannon. The rain sheathed these gleaming
stones. The silence was that of an abandoned wreck; on the muddy paths,
you could not even hear a sentry’s steps; occasional tufts of dripping grass
burst out of parapets covered by gray lichen; the piles of broken stone that

slid into the moats were mingled with twisted bits of iron and broken
kitchenware. The postern gate revealed the formidable thickness of the
walls: Orsenna’s great days had left their mark on these enormous vaults,
through which circulated a whiff of ancient power and of mold. Out of
embrasures level with the paving, cannons stamped with the arms of former
Podestas gaped over a motionless gulf of white vapor, from which rose the
sea’s icy breath. An atmosphere of almost overwhelming desolation
suffused these empty corridors streaked with black saltpeter. We remained
silent, as though swathed in the disappointed dream of this anchylosed
colossus, this inhabited ruin on which the now so derisive name of
Admiralty set the irony, as it were, of a fantastic legacy. This numbing
silence finally brought us to a standstill at one of the embrasures, and here
there occurs for me the memory of a gesture which was ultimately to
become deeply significant: our eyes, fixed on the open sea, avoided each
other more readily; leaning familiarly, as though in mockery, on the carriage
of a huge cannon, Marino drew a pipe out of his pocket and knocked it
repeatedly against the breechknob. A yellow glint slid toward us through
the mist, and from the interior courtyards somewhere came the calm crow
of a rooster, absurdly domesticating this cyclopean ruin, and there also fell
upon my ear, quite strangely, the quick, dry “So!” with which Marino
seemed to close the visit and break the spell, hammering his boot-heel more
loudly.
Already the mist was turning to ink: night was falling. Captain Marino
introduced me to the three officers who served under his command: they
were all from the cadres of the “Syrtes fleet.” The welcome banquet was
served, exceptionally, in one of the fortress redoubts—on most occasions
everyday routine instinctively withdrew from it and no longer disturbed its
dreams: one would have said that these legendary bastions alarmed ordinary
life. Beneath these vaults with their troubling echoes, conversation was
difficult to sustain; I was questioned about the Orsenna I had left the day
before. Orsenna was far away; I looked at the smoke of the festive torches
rising vertically toward the low, bare stone, I breathed that cold smell of
cellars and moldering pavement, I listened to the heavy, nail-sheathed doors

waking the echoes of other corridors. Under this faint and theatrical
lighting, a halo of mist still lingered round faces I could barely make out,
the hesitant constraint of a first meeting added to the impression of
unreality which overwhelmed me; in the intervals of silence, which Marino
made no effort to break, the guests’ faces turned to stone, momentarily
assuming the bleak profile and austere mask of the old portraits from heroic
days hanging in Orsenna’s palaces. The moment for toasts arrived: the
youngest officer welcomed me to the “Syrtes front,” and Marino, hearing
the official formula, raised his glass to the height of a visibly ironic smile.
My apartment was made ready in the command pavillion: one of the low
buildings; the same cold and moldering smell dwelt in these long damp
rooms, crudely paved and virtually empty. I opened my bedroom window
onto the darkness—it overlooked the sea; a faint palpitation came off the
lagoons through the opaque air. The huge shadows fluttering after the light
trembling across the walls intrigued me; I blew out my candle and slid
between the rough sheets with their stale shroudlike smell. The faint sound
of waves glided toward me in the returned darkness; the evening’s
astonishment persisted—I pinched my arm; I was really in Syrtes! A dog
barking, the flutter and squawking of a barnyard were distinctly audible in
the dark. I fell asleep almost immediately.
OceanofPDF.com

2
The Map Room
More than anywhere else, it was easy at the Admiralty to be convinced of
all that was defunct in the petty policy of espionage favored by the Signory.
The image of an irremediable decadence attended any glance that swept
from the top of the signal tower over the “Syrtes base.” Opposite the
fortress, a crumbling weed-grown jetty enclosed a sort of harbor which at
low tide became no more than a stretch of mud flats. At the breakwater’s
widened tip rose the pyramid of a huge pile of coal; this was so rarely
broached that weeds and even little bushes had ultimately colonized it,
reducing it to landscape, like those strangely shaped hills that are the dumps
of abandoned mines. Two ramshackle-looking sloops were anchored
alongside the jetty, three or four motor launches rocked in the mud flats at
low tide. Behind the little harbor, a ramp up which launches could be
dragged led to a caulking shed. Toward the sea, a canal banked with grey
mud wound between the reed beds, reaching the open water through a
channel dug through the shifting labyrinth of the lagoons. The harbor’s
usual aspect was one of deep sleep; in mid-afternoon on those still warm
days before the boats were laid up for the winter, a mist of heat made the
yellow grasses on the jetty tremble; within the harbor not even the lapping
of waves was audible, and it was extremely rare that a thread of smoke,
heralding some patrol, twisted in the Redoubtable’s smokestack; spiteful
tongues, at the Admiralty, claimed that such an “event” was a sign of storms
—exceptional in Syrtes—and Captain Marino’s propitiatory philosophy saw
no harm in that. Some of the crews were housed on land in one of the
buildings that flanked the fortress; service needs were increasingly reduced,
along with the labor force on these forsaken outposts, the surplus usually
scattered among the few fortified farms remaining in the backlands of
Syrtes, where huge flocks of half-wild sheep are raised—the departments of

Orsenna, seduced by the considerable savings thereby accruing to the
management of this absurd base, had long since closed its eyes to such
unwarlike practices. Hence Captain Marino might be observed, more
frequently than on the bridge of the Redoubtable, booted and spurred to set
out early in the morning for long rides across the plains to engage in thorny
arguments with the uncouth farmers about lodgings and wages, disputes in
which the sailor more and more frequently gave way to the steward of a
peaceful enterprise of land reclamation. Everything concerning the budget
and quartermaster’s expenditures had thereby come to occupy, in the
concerns of the Admiralty’s staff as a whole, an essential place: the Syrtes
base had turned into a curiously profitable enterprise which prided itself, in
the capital’s records, on revenues rather than on military prowess;
meticulous bookkeeping and judicious placing of the labor force had
gradually become the touchstone by which the Signory judged its officers’
capacity. Orsenna’s mercantile genius thus succeeded, in the long run, in
turning to advantage the discipline which by nature should have offered it
the most energetic resistance; at the same time, even in this tiny observation
post, one could note the progress of its alarming somnolence, in the
backwash of military life and in the mute summons rising from the
reassuring and limited earth. Sitting on a fortress crenellation, during one of
those cool mornings which constitute the beauty of an autumn in Syrtes, I
could observe in one direction the empty sea and the empty harbor,
devoured under the sun by the leprosy of its mud flats, and in the other
Captain Marino riding into the countryside at the head of some detachment
of hireling shepherds; my hand brushed the huge warm stones which had
known the breath of bullets, and I felt a wave of sadness rising in me: it
seemed as if the blind colossus, betrayed, was suffering a second death.
My functions as Observer would, in this state of stagnation, afford me no
trouble whatever. It very soon appeared that there was nothing to observe at
the Admiralty; to spare myself a certain absurdity and to moderate the
tedium of isolation, my sole recourse was to attempt to befriend my
suspects, themselves apparently harmless. Roberto, Fabrizio and Giovanni,
Marino’s three lieutenants, were young men my own age, quite restless in

their exile and eagerly anticipating leaves when the Admiralty car would
take them to Maremma, the nearest town; these mysterious excursions were
the subject of endless arguments and witticisms during our meals together:
no women were to be seen at the Admiralty. I soon made friends with all
three and took a special pleasure in the company of Fabrizio, quite recently
posted here from Orsenna and as disoriented as I by the somnolent abandon
of this pastoral garrison. Roberto and Giovanni spent most of their days
belly-deep in the reeds, shooting at the migratory birds that flocked in these
marshlands; sunning with Fabrizio in some recess of the ramparts where we
had carried our books, I could follow their hidden progress by a wake of
mild detonations; a faint blue smoke rose straight up from the motionless
reeds; in the golden air of that late autumn weather, the raucous cries of the
sea birds fluttering up at each shot sounded like perforations. Evening was
falling; the hoofbeats of Marino’s horse rang out on the lagoon causeway,
returning from some distant farm; the bustle which enlivens any barracks at
dinnertime afforded the Admiralty a last fugitive trace of animation. Night
gathered all five of us round heaping platters of game, and we enjoyed these
evening meals when a noisy cordiality prevailed at table; for leagues and
leagues roundabout, empty shadows seemed to draw us together at the heart
of this oasis of intimacy. Marino’s somewhat monkish silence and reserve
dissolved in this bath of youthful spirits; he enjoyed our gaiety, and on days
when the mists, ringing our little haven, left us idle and dreary, he was the
first to call for one of those pitchers of redolent Syrtes wine that is still put
up in the old style, under a layer of oil. The dinner was coming to an end;
Giovanni the hunter coughed in the air thickened by cigar smoke, and
suggested a stroll on the jetty. A salty savor hovered over the lifeless
waters; at the jetty’s end, a beacon winked rather feebly; the shadow of the
fortress darkened on the lagoon behind us, an obsessive presence. We sat
down, legs dangling, along the quay where the tide offered a barely
perceptible pulsation; Marino lit his pipe, staring at the clouds through half-
closed eyes, and in a businesslike tone of voice told us what the next day’s
weather would be. A moment’s intense silence followed this invariably
correct forecast, like the brief interval of meditation at flag-lowering; this

was the end of the evening’s ritual. The voices echoed longer; one by one,
our little group disintegrated; one by one, doors slammed on the wall of
silence. I opened my window to the night’s salt air: all was at peace over
fifty leagues of shore, the beacon on the jetty shone over the sleeping water
as impotently as a candle forgotten in a crypt.
There was a certain charm for me in this diminished life. My occasional
reports to Orsenna were succinct, but the letters I sent from Syrtes to my
friends were very long. There were moments when, on a calm and luminous
afternoon, it seemed possible to contain within my own heart, quite
effortlessly, the faint pulsations of this little cell of dozing life that trembled
on the desert’s edge. Leaning out over some corner of the fortress ramparts
where a clump of dry weeds bristled above the void, I would embrace its
threatened extent in a single glance; the antlike progress of the rare
expeditions, the clatter of a team of horses, the isolated pounding of a
hammer in the shed, sounded as distinct as the vibrations of a bell at that
height—such familiarity was comfortable, was even sweet to me, yet all
this naive barnyard activity generated a certain anxiety, a certain alarm. A
dream seemed to weigh upon the somnolence of these ordinary actions
which I watched from on high as though within a cloud; if I lingered to
follow them still further, I felt rising within me that fascination with the
alien which keeps us paralyzed as we observe the stir of pure
unconsciousness in an anthill under our raised heel. My thoughts would
often return to Marino and to my first visit to the fortress; again I saw him
knocking his pipe against the cannon breech, a reassuring exorcism, and
suddenly I had the sense of his massive and defending presence at the heart
of this tiny colony. He was its very pulse, I saw his honest hands delicately
disperse the shadows impeding a desperately simple life; I felt how
different I was from him, and I felt, too, how much I cared for him.
The life I was leading had no rule. Our routine at the Admiralty was not
particularly severe—amid this diminished and quite ambiguous activity,
subject to the hazards of the weather and the whims of the sea, it bore the
mark of an almost peasant variety and discontinuity, and I more than

anyone else escaped its minimal requirements. I had suffered, those early
days, from a kind of vertigo—the freedom, the very blank before me—and I
had at first eagerly flung myself into the violent exercises which my
comrades enjoyed and which shortened for us these overwhelming hours of
solitude; we would go harpoon fishing for the huge sharks that ventured
into the lagoons, or would gallop after hares on the bare stretches of the
moor. Sometimes we would be summoned, at a nearby farm, to one of those
battues which periodically slaughtered the rabbits devastating the wretched
pasturage of the sheep; these were occasions of high festivity, where we
gathered to gossip and drink by torchlight until far into the night. The day’s
booty, piled high in the barnyard, emitted a strong stench in the evening air;
we would ride back, exhausted and half asleep; as the daylight crept over
the moors, the gleam of campfires paling on the horizon betrayed the end of
other such hecatombs. I was anything but robust, and these amusements left
my body helpless, my heart drained; I was not escaping Orsenna so much as
I had supposed into the health of this brutal life. Gradually, though, it had
begun to be tinged with a curious reflection; the idleness of the first days
tended to be organized, in spite of myself, round what I could not long
hesitate to acknowledge as a mysterious center of gravity. A secret was
attaching me to the fortress, like a child to some hiding place discovered
among ruins. Early in the afternoon, under the scorching sun, an emptiness
declared itself within the Admirality at the hour when most of us were
taking naps; through the thistles, I followed the moat without being seen,
down to the postern gate. A long vaulted corridor, then wet and disjointed
stairs led me to the inner redoubt of the fortress; the chill of a tomb
immediately enveloped me as I entered the map room.
The very first time, during my explorations in this labyrinth of courtyards
and casemates, I had pushed open the door out of mere curiosity, I had been
gradually invaded by a feeling I could define only by saying that it was of
the kind which disorient (as it is said a compass needle wavers in crossing
certain hopelessly ordinary steppes of central Russia) that invisible magnet
which keeps us true to life’s comfortable directions—which point us,
without any kind of justification, to an alluring place, a place where we

must, without further argument, proceed and remain. What first struck me
in this long, low, vaulted chamber, surrounded by the decrepitude of the
crumbling fortress, was a singular aspect of cleanliness and order—a
meticulous and even fanatical order—an arrogant refusal to submit to
decay, a semblance, at once festive and ruinous, of standing alone at
attention, a surprising aspect which the room offered to a first glance, amid
all this desolation, of remaining stubbornly ready to serve. Making the
hinges creak on this vigilant solitude, as on the theatrical and intimidating
array of a banquet before the guests enter, I could not help feeling, each
time, the faint shock one experiences in opening unawares the door of an
apparently empty room upon a face suddenly more sinister than that of a
blind man—absent, dissolved, petrified in the absorbing tension of
vigilance.
The room did not seem in actuality dark, but the daylight, falling through
windows virtually frosted by the many bulls’-eyes that embossed their
panes, preserved here an uncertain and somehow perpetually fading quality;
its penumbra, at all hours of the day, seemed to liquefy a stagnant
crepuscular melancholy. The place was severely furnished with polished
oak work-tables; against the bare walls, dark wood cupboards contained
books—almost all heavy folios in faded bindings—and old-fashioned
navigation instruments. Against the rear wall, halfway up to the vault, ran a
narrow, rickety gallery along yet another row of grille-fronted cupboards.
The bare walls, the maps, the smell of dust, the scrubbed look of tables
worn uneven as a palm, suggested a schoolroom, but one which the
thickness of the walls, the cloistral silence, and the sinister light had
confined to the study of some singular and forgotten discipline. This
comfortingly material impression was spoiled almost at once by another,
more disconcerting one: it seemed as if there lingered in the room
something of that heavy atmosphere—of stale and faded thoughts—which
hangs about places where ex-votos are tacked up. And—as if guided by the
thread of this vague analogy—when one took a few steps toward the center
of the room, one’s eye was suddenly caught, in the midst of these dim
shades of ink and dust, by a great, apparently fresh bloodstain smeared on

the right wall: this was a huge red silk flag, its whole length falling in stiff
folds against the wall: the Banner of Saint Jude—the emblem of Orsenna—
which had floated over the poop of the Admiral’s galley during battles with
Farghestan. In front of it, a low dais furnished with a table and one chair,
which the trophy seemed to designate as the focal point, the radiant center
of this chamber set like a trap. The same magical impulse which leads us,
before taking thought, to try the throne of an abandoned palace we are
visiting, or a judge’s armchair in an empty courtroom, had drawn me to this
chair; on the table were spread maps of the Sea of Syrtes.
I sat down, still a little troubled by this dais which seemed to call for an
audience, but soon fastened there as though by a spell. In front of me
stretched in a white expanse the sterile lands of Syrtes, dotted with the
flyspecks of isolated farms, bordered by the delicate tatting of the lagoons.
Parallel to the coast a black dotted line ran for some distance across the sea:
the limit of the patrolled zone. Beyond this, a bright red line: here was what
had long been tacitly accepted as the frontier, which nautical instructions
forbade crossing under any circumstances. Orsenna and the habitable world
ended at this critical confine, even more enthralling for my imagination
because of all that was so strangely abstract in its delineation: by having let
my eyes glide in a kind of total conviction along that red thread, like a bird
stupefied by a line drawn in front of it on the ground, the frontier had
ultimately become impregnated for me with a peculiar reality: without my
being willing to acknowledge the fact, I was ready to endow that perilous
passage with quite concrete wonders, to imagine a crevasse there in the sea,
an admonitory sign, yes, a Passage of the Red Sea. Far beyond,
prodigiously far beyond this magical prohibition, stretched the unknown
spaces of Farghestan, serried like holy lands in the shadow of the volcano
Tängri, its ports of Rhages and Trangées, and its belt of cities whose
obsessive syllables coiled around my memory: Gerrha, Myrphea, Thargala,
Urgasonte, Amicto, Salmanoé, Dyrceta.
Standing over the table, both hands flat on the map, I sometimes lingered
there for hours, frozen in a hypnotic immobility from which not even the

tingling of my palms awakened me. A faint rustling seemed to rise from
that map, to people the closed room and its lurking silence. A creak of the
woodwork sometimes made me raise my eyes, uneasily staring into the
gloom like a miser visiting his wealth by night and feeling beneath his hand
the faint, stirring luster of gems in the darkness, as if I had kept watch in
spite of myself, in the cloistral silence, over something mysteriously
awakened. My mind a blank, I felt the darkness around me seeping into the
room, weighting it with that yielding heaviness of a head collapsing into
sleep, of a ship engulfed by the waves; I was foundering with it as I stood
there, like a wreck crammed with the silence of the deep.
One evening, when I was about to leave the map room after a longer visit
than usual, a heavy footstep on the tiles wakened me with a start and flung
me, before I had time to think, into a posture of studied curiosity whose
haste could no longer deceive me as to the guilt I felt weighing upon my
presence in the room. Captain Marino came in without seeing me, keeping
his broad back complacently toward me while he lingered over the door,
closing it with that offhandedness born of a long intimacy with the void so
characteristic of all watchers of the night. And indeed I had, suddenly faced
with the intimate violence everything in this room expressed, the very sense
of absorbing strangeness one feels in the presence of a night watchman
limping on his rounds through a museum. He took several more steps, with
that slow sailor’s gait of his, raised his lantern, and caught sight of me. We
looked at each other for a second without a word. What appeared on that
heavy, closed countenance, rather than surprise, was a sudden expression of
sadness which extinguished it altogether, a singular expression of knowing
sadness, the sort one sees in old men on the verge of their last illness, as
though touched by a ray of mysterious knowledge. He set his lantern down
on a table, turning his eyes away, and said in a voice even more muffled
than the dimness of the room required:
“You work too much, Aldo. Let’s go have dinner.”
And, swaying between the huge shadows his lantern cast upon the vaults,
we made our way, awkwardly enough, to the postern gate.

This trivial incident was to come back to me with such insistence that it
finally made me realize its importance. Lying in my bed at the heart of the
tomblike silence, what I was trying to recall was above all that expression
of immediate sadness suddenly darkening his face like a shutter, and also
the singularly meaningful intonation of that voice which even then made me
turn my head and listen, as though to a sentence heavy with secret
suggestions. For hours on end, I was to let that low murmur slide over my
memory before finding myself one morning, with the suddenness of an
illumination, face to face with its too obvious meaning: Marino knew of my
frequent visits to the map room, and secretly disapproved of them.
This matter ultimately preoccupied me more than it had any reason to do,
creating, at least in my imagination, a kind of complicity between Marino
and myself whose slightest signs I set myself to watch for. I was soon able
to convince myself—though to be frank there was never again any question
of that evening’s encounter—that Marino had not forgotten. At the end of
dinner, amid that laughter he liked to provoke and to maintain, and when his
tanned face turned slightly purplish, I saw in his eyes, when they met mine,
a sudden shadow of embarrassment which obliterated me, passed me over,
excepted me from the unison of the joking group, as if we would henceforth
be dealing with each other only in a realm where it was harder to move and
to abide.
My life was gradually changing. I had longed for this exile in a sudden
need for ascesis: it was affording me a certain equilibrium. The vanished
pleasures of Orsenna caused me no regrets. I scarcely ever left the
Admiralty; I amazed Fabrizio by refusing even the easy pleasures and
immediate amours he went looking for almost every week in Maremma. I
no longer needed them. The scarcely justified impoverishment which
characterized this forsaken life in Syrtes, the ruinous sacrifice which it
implied and which I had consented to, bore within it, for me, the pledge of
an obscure compensation. In its very vacuity, its destitution and its severe
routine, it seemed to claim and to deserve the reward of an emotion more
overpowering than everything the festive life of Orsenna—so refined and so

mediocre—had afforded me. This denuded life was clearly offering itself, in
all the evidence of its very futility, to something which would ultimately be
worthy of taking it; disdaining all vulgar support, and as though venturing
out over a gaping void, it called for a sustenance proportionate to its craving
for the abyss. Its desolate charm was the kind which deceives the lookout’s
expectations; its outstretched antennae, insensible to the calm effluvia of
earth, were the entreaty of a breath from the open sea; its watchman’s cry,
an echo already implicit in the extreme suspension of the vigil it provoked.
This sleeping vessel Marino had managed so well to anchor at land was
setting sail, under my fresh gaze, as though of its own accord for other
horizons—its motionless navigation seemed to me dimly promised—I felt it
trembling beneath me as the deck of a good ship suddenly acknowledges
the tread of a venturesome captain. All was comatose at the Admiralty, but
with that troubled sleep of a night pregnant with divination and with
wonders; I extolled this life derived from my own patience; I felt I belonged
to that race of watchmen whose endlessly disappointed expectation feeds at
its powerful sources the inevitability of the event.
Impatiently I waited for the leave-days when the car to Maremma would
drain the Admiralty for hours at a time, leaving me sole master of a secret
territory which seemed, to my eyes alone, to release the faint shimmer of a
buried treasure. In the silence of its empty casemates, of its corridors buried
like mine galleries in the dread density of stone, the fortress purified of
indifferent eyes recovered the dimensions of a dream. My muffled footsteps
whispered through the corridors like a ghost, hesitant yet compelled,
relearning its rounds; I moved within the fortress, a dim life indeed, yet one
suddenly radiant as those lights caught between mirrors whose power
suddenly coincides with a mysterious source. My steps took me toward the
recess where I had lingered with Marino during my first visit. The dreary
mists that had walled it in then often gave way to great sunsets which
silhouetted on the horizon, like the mouth of a furnace, a flamboyant gob of
hard light. From the shadowy depths of this redoubt suspended in midair,
within its naked frame of cyclopean stone, I stared, half dizzy, at the dark
and dazzling sheet of sparkling blue as it coiled and uncoiled its sparkling

chains, as in a sea cave, along the gray stones. I sat on the cannon breech;
my gaze, sliding down the huge bronze column, espoused its thrust and its
nakedness, extended the frozen impulse of the metal, trained with it in hard
fixity on the sea’s horizon. I fastened my eyes upon that empty sea where
each wave, sliding noiseless as a tongue, seemed to insist on producing the
absence of any trace in the forever incompleted gesture of pure erasure. I
was waiting, without admitting it to myself, for a signal which would draw
from this excessive vigil the confirmation of a marvel. I was dreaming of a
sail suddenly born out of the sea’s void—and seeking a name for that
coveted sail. Perhaps I had already found it.
Those hours of silent contemplation flowed by like minutes. The sea
darkened, the horizon drew in upon a light mist. I was returning along the
parapet walk as though from a secret rendezvous. Behind the fortress, the
scorched fields of Syrtes unfurled, already gray. I was watching, from the
bastions, for the thread of dust which would be raised in the distance by the
car returning from Maremma. It zigzagged a long time between the scrubby
bushes, tiny and familiar and quite unthreatening, and I felt that Marino
would not appreciate my gesture of welcome which, like some watchman
on his tower, I let fall from too great a height upon this peaceful return.
When I think back to those days apparently so empty, it is to no avail that
I seek a trace, a visible prick of that needle which kept me so singularly
alerted. Nothing was happening. There was a faint feverish tension, the
injunction of an imperceptible and yet continuous forewarning, as if I were
caught in some telescope’s field—an imperceptible itching between the
shoulders you sometimes feel when working at a desk with your back to the
hallway of an empty house. I summoned up those Sundays vacant as some
supplementary reaches of the sense of hearing, the way one tries to read the
future in crystal balls. For me they unmasked the silence of an armed vigil
and an observation post, a hard stone ear fastened like a leech upon the
vague and disappointing murmur of the sea.
These clandestine preoccupations gradually alienated me from my
companions. Conversations murmured just out of my hearing at evening

meals, allusions veiled by smothered laughter would accompany the returns
from Maremma; some of the families of Orsenna, by a whim which
betrayed the extravagant moods of its nobility, had settled in the town to
while away summer’s end in that forsaken place; Fabrizio and Giovanni
frequented them assiduously. Certain names familiar to me were dropped
into the conversation; around them, for Fabrizio who pronounced the
syllables with a touch of ironical deference, I realized that there was
gradually gathering, as around a consecrated jewel held a moment in one’s
hand, a romantic luster of ancient nobility and a more exalted life; even
Marino’s eyes grew brighter for a moment—but to my ears their eroded
vocables now offered the very resonance of ennui and a singular
disenchantment; I felt, hearing them slip into the conversation, something
of the exasperation and embarrassment of an explorer who suddenly
discovers he has country neighbors. I would rudely interrupt Fabrizio’s
account of a picnic or an expedition out on the lagoons, and topple one of
his aristocratic idols from its pedestal with a sarcastic remark. I withered
Orsenna with my scorn; I soared a hundred leagues above it; and I despised
Fabrizio and Marino himself, who shared the outward aspect of my secret
life, for lowering me to their own level before these absurd caricatures of a
higher existence. One evening, at the reverential description of the
Aldobrandi country house, I gave way to a more elaborate outburst than
usual and suddenly left the room, virtually in tears. Fabrizio ran after me
and caught up with me out on the moor.
“What’s the matter, Aldo? What is it that makes you so angry?”
“Let me alone. You wouldn’t understand.”
“I understand you better than you think.”
“Is that so?” I turned completely around. The misty moon was blurring
his face, but his eyes, in the darkness, were singularly wide, his voice
incisive and under control.
“You’re a proud man, Aldo. You weren’t like that when you came here.
Something has made you change.”

“Nothing, Fabrizio, believe me, nothing between us, at least. It’s just the
loneliness of this place that makes me nervous.”
“But you like it that way—you make a point of it. You’re looking for
something you don’t want to share with the rest of us. You’re always
climbing around the fortress. It’s as if you’ve found a treasure in these old
stones.”
I let out a laugh that was a little too careful. “You didn’t think I was so ...
miserly?”
“You’ve changed, let me tell you. I know you’re my friend. But you
don’t have much use for me. You pity us for leading this life here, burying
ourselves ... even Marino ...”
“I have nothing against Marino, I give you my word. There’s no one here
I care for and respect more than Marino ...”
“You’re growing away from us, Aldo; I can tell. It makes me feel bad.
You’re getting so ... detached from everything ...”
I shrugged, embarrassed to speak. But his next sentence freed me from
my show of unconcern.
“Is it that you’re expecting something to change?”
I burst out laughing—a rather offensive laugh. “A big promotion,
Fabrizio. The salons of the capital are asking for me. They want to make me
an aide-de-camp of the fleet commander. Obligatory service at every dance
—officer in charge of compliments for all armed forces. What do you say to
that, Fabrizio? A giant step in any man’s career!”
“I say you’re lucky. Don’t laugh. Anything’s better than rotting in this
godforsaken hole.”
“Well, I’m having nothing to do with it, Fabrizio—think of that: nothing
whatever.”
He shrugged rather grimly and smiled—a sad smile. “You’re funny,
Aldo, you know that? In a year, you’ll think it over and see ...”
“I’ve done quite a lot of thinking already.” It was my turn to shrug.

Fabrizio’s voice suddenly became strained—a voice which seized me in
the darkness: “What are you looking for? There’s something strange about
your coming down here in the first place. Everyone knows who you are,
you could have chosen...”
“Is this a cross-examination?”
Rage seized me again. Infuriated by that inquisitorial tone, so childish
and yet so embarrassing, I cast about for the most wounding thing I could
say—no doubt about it, my conscience was far from clear:
“Did Marino put you up to asking that?”
“Marino never asks questions. But Marino doesn’t have much use for
poets, at least not at the Admiralty. I’ve heard him say as much. And you’re
a kind of poet, Aldo.”
The voice lingered over Marino’s name with that nuance of caressing
respect which was part of the Admiralty’s ritual, and which tonight was
unendurable to me:
“And the worst kind too, isn’t that what he told you?”
“No, Aldo. Marino really cares for you. But he’s afraid of you.”
I became completely unhinged. “I’m so likely to denounce him, is that it?
And I’m here to spy on him! That’s why all of you think I’m here, that’s
what accounts for my little excursions in the fortress. How simple it all is.
Everything can be explained. All those Sundays spent sniffing around the
corridors. And things are made so easy for me, aren’t they? All so kind—so
polite. Investigate, why don’t you, my friend? I’ll make room for you. After
all, I’m the enemy, aren’t I? Aren’t I the kind of informer you put in
quarantine?”
The expression on Fabrizio’s affectionate and woebegone face made me
stop. “You’re crazy, Aldo, that’s what I think. Look at me! Marino cares
more for you than for any of us. But he’s afraid of you, and he knows why. I
don’t ...” Fabrizio frowned with that naive and theatrical show of boyish
thought which made me relax at once, as if I had been talking to a child. “...
and sometimes I think he’s right.”

I clapped him on the shoulder, already beginning to smile. “It’s all right,
Fabrizio. Don’t hold it against me. And don’t let such interesting fears keep
you awake. Besides, I hear the sandman coming—it’s already late for the
children to be up.”
Joking between us had become inveterate. Fabrizio made a gesture as if
to chase me across the moor; our fists exchanged a few mock blows. We
were still quite close to childhood; I was just two years his elder. The
reconciliation touched our hearts with an easy warmth. But Marino ... that
was something else; Fabrizio didn’t know how to lie, and Marino never
spoke lightly.
The evening was a calm one, and like a wounded animal escaping into a
thicket, I plunged into that lukewarm darkness. My steps led me toward the
sea. I was fleeing the Admiralty like a creature that has just been driven out
of the herd and rushes into the night, in a frenzy of solitude. I had been
sounded in every organ and recognized as a different species, forever alien,
separate. I tried to imagine Marino, gripping his pipe between his teeth,
staring with gray, preoccupied eyes into the darkness and pronouncing the
sentence that cut me off. At that moment, I suddenly grew disgusted with
myself, every limb tense with an agonizing rage. I had supposed I would be
leading the most innocent life at the Admiralty, and everything had spoken
against me. Marino’s gray and inattentive gaze, that gaze whose heavy
intensity seemed to focus not on the face, but somewhere imperceptibly
beyond, again passed before my eyes at that moment like an inflexible
datum to which I could not help referring everything in my behavior, since
our first meeting, that was faltering and indecisive. There was not a word,
not a gesture of this life without mystery which I would not have tried in
spite of myself to conceal from him, not a moment when I had not felt,
before him, at fault.
Without my having even become aware of the path taken, I had reached
the thin tongue of sand which blocked the lagoon and ran parallel to the
coast. Amid these moon-varnished waters bristling with reeds, the sea
spread before me like a wide margin of dark fur, and extended to the

vanishing point in a long horizon the night brought closer. Behind me, the
Admiralty rose stark white out of the mists over the lagoon. I lay down
facing the sea in a hollow of the beach, and exhausted by my thoughts, my
mind a blank, for a long time I watched the moonlight flickering over the
sea in a silence that seemed to grow deeper every moment. I must have
stayed in that dazed contemplation a long time, for the chill at the night’s
heart fell on me and I stood up for a second to pull my coat closer round my
shoulders. It was then that I saw sliding in front of me, only a short way out
to sea, across the puddles of moonlight, the just-discernible shadow of a
boat. It followed the coast a moment and then, veering to the right of the
harbor channel and crossing the limit of the patrolled zone, headed out to
sea and soon vanished over the horizon.
OceanofPDF.com

3
A Conversation
Early the next morning, I paid a visit to Marino’s quarters. Sleep had been
out of the question, and when I tried to sort out my thoughts in preparation
for this delicate interview, I found the excitement generated by my
discovery of the night before to be anything but normal. I was eager to test
on Marino the very reality of this suspect apparition. Without admitting as
much to myself, I attributed to his accumulation of calm the singular power
of annihilating what I had seen, restoring it to the threatened realm of order.
At the same time, I realized that my discovery could scarcely please him,
and I had the feeling I was defying him, challenging a secret prohibition,
forcing him to show his hand. The mere fact of doing so in his own quarters
was already to assign so simple an action a certain weight, a certain
ambiguity. As I walked through the pale corridors that chilly morning,
Marino suddenly seemed to overwhelm my thoughts and my actions, not
because he influenced them but because he could charge them, against my
will, with some irrevocable weight under which I seemed to stagger.
I found him in his basement office of the fortress—he always spent the
mornings there drawing up the reports he would send to Orsenna.
Something secret and monkish hovered about this room which had
ultimately seemed to coil itself around him like a shell around its conch,
and where his imposing seated figure merely added a finishing touch,
consummating a remarkable masterpiece of placement. At the end of the
narrow corridor which constituted a kind of frame—as in certain paintings
where the magic seems generated by some miracle of balance—one almost
expected to see him miraculously moving. But this was the real Marino
whom I had before me and whom I was about to engage in combat: in
collusion with familiar things, sustained by them and sustaining them with
his protecting mass—a barricade of mild tenacity against the unexpected,

against the sudden, against the alien. His pipe, lying on a pile of dossiers,
was a challenge to the powder keg. His slow, deliberate peasant’s hand was
festooning its daily thread of ink across page after page. A long series of
identical days, days without either date or secret, had wrought this rustless
armor whose mere touch dispelled ghosts, had caulked this diving bell in
which—always as if nothing special had occurred—an absorbing mystery
of the commonplace was being consummated.
Alerted by the sound of my footsteps on the tiles, Marino had glimpsed
me while I was still some distance away, his swift gaze immediately
extinguished as though he had turned down a lamp, and had buried himself
again in his papers. He saw me coming. This too was a part of his defense.
He did not like being surprised. He waited until I was quite close to him;
even before his gray eyes looked up, his hand almost unconsciously set
down the pen, signifying to me as though against its will that there was no
hope of doing any more work this morning. He had been expecting me.
Such singular divination disconcerted me.
“Bright and early this morning, Aldo. A nasty fog today, isn’t it? It
always wakes you up early here—your throat starts pricking. That’s what I
always tell Roberto: a morning fog means the first sign of winter at the
Admiralty.” He stared complacently out the window; I felt he liked those
clouded panes. That was how he always looked at things, a sort of veil
floating over his gray eyes concealed what must not be seen. “Remember
what the weather was like the day you came here? ... I do—an old habit of
mine. Even a familiar face I always remember against the same sky where I
saw it first, and the shadows too, the clouds, the wind, the heat of the sun.
All the clouds ... I could actually draw them. Now in your case, I always see
you against a wall of fog, with a kind of halo. No, it’s no joke, a real halo—
the circle the lantern made in the mist.” His slightly forced laugh ended
awkwardly. We had never found it easy to talk to each other. Even Marino’s
intimacy had a certain willed quality about it, more obligatory than friendly,
that kept us at a certain distance, provoking an uneasiness no good will

could dissipate. His voice turned cool, becoming slightly constrained as the
interrogation grew apparent. “Nice of you to come and talk to me.”
“I’m afraid it’s more serious than that.”
“Is it? Something about the Service?”
“That’s for you to judge.” And quite coolly I described, making every
effort to be exact, what I had seen the night before. As the story proceeded,
I could hear my voice assuming an almost metallic and offensive hardness,
as if, moment by moment, I had felt credibility escaping before me. Marino
stared at me, his face motionless; I felt it was myself he was hearing and not
the passage of that ghostly ship by which I had hoped to waken his hunter’s
instinct—the way a doctor listens when his pretended sympathy disguises,
in the breaks of his voice, in the twitches of his face, the fleeting signs of
disease.
“All right,” he said at last, after a decent interval of silence. “I’ll give
orders for a channel patrol tonight. Though it’s not likely the boat will put
in a nightly appearance.”
His voice was dismissive. This was what I had feared most: the even,
professional tone was reducing the apparition to the status of a service
detail, degrading it to a breach of regulations. And yet his excessive
detachment alerted me—there was something about it a little too well acted.
I insisted: “It doesn’t matter if it comes back—what’s serious is if it left for
good.”
“Left? I’m not sure what you mean by that, Aldo.”
“It’s clear enough, isn’t it?” I was getting excited.
“Where would it go? Except for Maremma, there’s not a harbor within
three hundred miles. It’s someone in Maremma, having a little nighttime
fun.”
“Out beyond the patrol zone?”
“They were probably drinking.”
“Or maybe they knew what they were doing and had decided to go
farther.”

For the first time, Marino’s gaze betrayed annoyance, even hostility, like
that of a man who has unsuccessfully tried, down to the last minute, to
avoid making a blunder: “I don’t follow. Out to sea? That would be
ridiculous.”
“There are harbors opposite ... There’s the Farghestan coast.”
The word fell into a catastrophic silence. I was the one who had uttered
it. Yet there had never been a question of anything else. Marino said
nothing. I could hear the nastiness in my voice: “It’s a name that doesn’t
seem to have much currency around here.”
The answer offered me a wall of blank hostility. “Right, a name without
much currency.”
“But one you’ll permit me to use, all the same. When I came here, I had
reason to believe I was assigned to a military post. One that was more or
less inactive, I grant you. And I’ve certainly had even more reason to
believe that, since I’ve been here. But there’s no use closing your eyes: after
all, we are at war.”
I had spoken the last sentence with the traditional note of mockery, but
Marino’s voice suddenly had a ring of harsh pride I had not heard in it
before: “If there’s anything you find to criticize at the Admiralty, you can
put it in your service reports. That’s your duty. But your jokes are quite out
of place, Aldo, let me tell you. I lost these fingers for the Signory. I’m here
to guard these coasts, and I don’t think I neglect my duties. As for the way I
go about it—well, I’ll be the judge of that, and I think you’re a little too
green to offer your advice ...” His gaze gradually rose until he was looking
over my head, and the assertiveness in his voice gave his face a sudden
beauty.
“I’ll put that in my report too.” I felt strangely uncomfortable, confused
by the challenge of his tone of voice, serious as it had become. But already
Marino was realizing his mistake from the expression in my eyes, and he
recovered that resonance of dismissive mockery so habitual to him.
“We’ve let ourselves get carried away, I think, by a wretched sloop trying
to do a little smuggling. We’re not going to quarrel over such nonsense, are

we, Aldo?”
The gray eyes, behind the screen of slow speech, sought a sanction which
would exorcize doubts, trying to divine how much I had read in his sudden
confusion.
“I didn’t mean to offend you, you must realize that.”
“You’re a young man, Aldo. I understand. I used to be like you, eager to
do my duty. Eager and ... egotistic, in fact. Like you, I used to think
something extraordinary had to happen to me. I believed it was my fate.
You’ll grow old, just as I have, Aldo, and you’ll understand. Extraordinary
things don’t happen. Nothing happens. Maybe it’s not good that things
should happen. You’re bored at the Admiralty. You want to see something
appear on that empty horizon. I’ve known other men, before you—but
young men, like you—who would get up in the middle of the night to see
ghost ships going by. They ended up by seeing them. We know all about
that here: it’s the mirage of the South, and it goes away. There’s no need for
imagination in Syrtes, let me tell you. But we defeat it here—we end up by
wearing it out. You’ve seen those birds that run across the plains down here
—the ones with atrophied wings. They’re a good example: where there are
no trees to perch on and no hawks to escape, there’s no need to fly. They
adapt. At the Admiralty we’ve adapted too, that’s the way life works, and a
good thing too. That’s how we live here ‘more or less inactive.’ If you’re
too bored, Aldo, if you don’t want to listen to that monotony which gives
such good advice around here—you understand me, don’t you?—let me
give you some advice of my own, the advice of a father and a friend. You
know how fond of you I am, Aldo. You have a famous name and your
family carries a lot of weight at the Signory. My advice to you is: leave.”
“Leave?”
Marino’s eyes drifted away, remote as if they were searching the open sea
for some ineffable point of reference. “There’s a kind of balance that I’m
keeping here. It’s not easy, and it requires me to take away anything that
weighs too heavily on one side or the other.”
“And what is it that weighs so heavily?”

“You.”
I caught my breath a second before answering. There was no mistaking
Marino’s tone: at that very moment he cared for me, loved me even; I felt it
deeply. But I was determined to press on. “You’re sending me away. You
wouldn’t be doing that without good reason. May I know more precisely
what it is about my behavior that has displeased you?”
“Don’t turn things around. It’s too easy, refusing to understand. I’ve put
myself in your hands. A word from you to Orsenna is all it takes, and you
know it, to get me out of here for good. This is no service matter, Aldo—
this is a man-to-man conversation we’re having; I thought you understood
that already. I resent your being what you are, in spite of yourself. I resent
your being a troublemaker around here—until you can be more dangerous
than that.”
“I don’t think I have any such magical powers. Would you kindly show
me, once and for all, the evil spell I cast?”
Marino remained silent a moment, as though groping about for the key to
his thoughts. “I spoke just now of a certain balance. The reassuring thing
about balance is that nothing moves. But the truth is, all it takes is a breath
to make everything move. Nothing moves here, nothing has moved for
three hundred years. Nothing has changed in any particular, except for a
certain way of not looking at things. And yet between Rodrigo” (this was
the admiral who had bombarded Farghestan) “and me, there is certainly a
difference. Things are heavy around here, and stay in place: you wouldn’t
have much luck collecting the stones that roll into the ditches every day.
But maybe you can do something else. There’s a certain pitch of inertia
which has supported this motionless wreck these three hundred years—the
same one that sends avalanches rolling in other places. That’s why I live so
minimally here, holding my breath and making this empty shell into the bed
of that heavy sleep that scandalizes you so. I’m not criticizing you for
running around the way Fabrizio does, like a puppy let off the leash.
There’s plenty of room here to have your fun—the desert has worn out
stronger men than you. I’m criticizing you for not being humble enough to

refuse the dreams of these sleepy stones. They’re violent dreams ... I’m an
old man now, and I’ve learned what it is to die. It’s a long and difficult
thing, dying; it takes all the help you can get, and all the good will. Let me
tell you something, Aldo: things are killed twice over. Once in their
functions and once in their signs—once in what they’re used for and once in
what they go on wanting from us. I’m only criticizing you for your self-
indulgence.”
“Very kind. And if you’ll forgive me, somewhat over-imaginative. I
didn’t think life at the Admiralty had so many fantasies to hide. I’m afraid
you’re adding a few.” I had felt the sudden and stupid craving to recover the
advantage. I immediately realized that our conversation had passed the
critical point: all Marino wanted was to be reassured.
“All sailors are ‘over-imaginative.’” He laughed quite warmly. “... They
have to be if they can tell a storm is coming just by sniffing the air. But you
can set your mind at rest, Aldo, there won’t be any storm. There’s nothing
coming. Nothing will happen. Nothing happens to sensible men ...” The
voice was teasing now, but faintly troubled too. “And in spite of everything,
maybe you’ll get used to things here. The winter season has a charm all its
own. By the way—I was forgetting—a whole world of parties is in the
offing, so I hear. We have friends in Maremma, friends who are very eager
to see you. As a matter of fact, I’m supposed to pass on an invitation—
according to the rules.”
