“You know that I never leave this place.”
“And that you’re making a big mistake not to—but that’s your business.
Princess Aldobrandi’s inviting you to a party she’s giving tomorrow. She
wants to see you very much and has asked me to make sure you’ll come.
Do as you like. You know her already, I believe. In any case, I’m not giving
you advice, the way I would to some young recruit in the interests of his
promotion. You’re a big boy now ... About tonight’s patrol—I’ll give the
orders now ...” He shot me a half-amused glance. “Why don’t you come
with us? It’ll give you something to do.”

Leaving Marino, I realized I was in a singular state of mind. As a
consequence of this tense conversation, which in certain respects was to
become so portentous for me, a sudden impulse had managed to scatter the
storm clouds at the very last moment. Marino had wanted to get me away
from Syrtes ... Once out of the building, I was no longer even surprised by
my own lack of concern. A host of memories surged up, dissipating those
clouds with the exuberance of a morning breeze. I began thinking about
Vanessa Aldobrandi.
The Selvaggi Gardens, in May, as you come out of the marble labyrinth
that overhangs the hill, are an unbroken pool of pale sulfur which glows at
the bottom of the slope, breaking into festoons on the opposing cliff of dark
forests which bound Orsenna on that side like a wall. Once past the crest of
the hill which isolates the place from the town’s habitual noises, at noon the
scent of narcissus and hyacinth flows back over the valley in a swirling
vertigo, like the attack on your ears of a too-shrill note which intensifies,
before satisfying, your thirst for a note even shriller and more lacerating.
On the last marble steps, invaded by the unbroken sheet of blossoms as
though the staircase were plunging into the sea, the leaves of an aspen cast
that veil of shadows so similar to the reflections of shifting water on a wall,
and the suddenness of the silence, after the racket of the streets, is that of a
magic site, of those abandoned cemeteries where the faint and faded
suspension of all things gives the mere buzzing of bees the plenitude of a
pedal point, and somehow the heavy weight of a visitation. These half-
deserted gardens were virtually unknown in Orsenna; I often slipped into
them toward noon, when I was certain to find no one there, and with that
ever new enchantment one feels at opening a secret and vaguely
conspiratorial door. The garden was there, each time as though for me
alone, revived in all its incandescence—in an immediate and mocking
transcendence of its promise, too, inexhaustibly bestowing... .
I had left the University, that morning, quite early and taken leave of
Orlando a few streets away from here: these clandestine promenades of
mine could somehow make me ashamed of his company. I was already

walking down the last steps from my chosen belvedere when an unexpected
apparition stopped me dead in my tracks, irritated and embarrassed: at the
precise place where I usually leaned on the balustrade was standing a
woman.
It was difficult to retreat without being clumsy about it, and I had felt in a
particularly solitary mood that morning. My indecision, in this rather false
position, immobilized me, one foot raised, holding my breath, a few steps
behind the figure, which was that of a girl or a very young woman. From
my slightly higher position, the three-quarter profile was silhouetted against
the sheet of flowers, affording the tender and aerial contour usually
produced by the reverberations of a snow-field. But the beauty of this
largely invisible face struck me less than the feeling of exalted
dispossession I could sense mounting within me, minute by minute. In the
singular correspondence of this ruling outline to a privileged site, in the
impression of an exclusive presence dawning upon me, my conviction grew
that the garden queen had just taken possession of her solitary realm. Her
back turned to the town’s noises, she dispensed to this garden, with a
statue’s immobility, the sudden solemnity a landscape assumes under an
exile’s gaze; here was the solitary spirit of the valley who tinged the field of
flowers for me with a suddenly deeper shade—like the tonality of the
orchestra when the anticipated entrance of a major theme casts upon it a
certain high, cloudy shadow. Suddenly the girl turned on her heel with a
single movement and smiled at me cunningly. That was how I had met
Vanessa.
I was not to realize until much later her gift of becoming immediately
inseparable from a landscape or an object which her mere presence seemed
to open to the realization of a secret hope, so that her being was reduced
and at the same time exalted to the significant role of attribute. “Bather on
the beach,” “chatelaine at her spinning wheel,” “princess in her tower”—
such were the quasi-emblematic terms which came to mind when I later
tried to account for the formidably residual power of that enchanted hand.
Things, to Vanessa, were permeable. By a gesture or by some wonderfully

natural inflection of her voice, however unforeseeable, just as a poet’s
infallible word seizes upon reality, she fastened herself to the world with the
same loving and inwardly granted violence which a leader perpetrates when
his hand magnetizes the mob.
Old Aldobrandi was the head of a family famous in Orsenna for its bold
and restless wit. His name was proverbially linked to every street
insurrection, to all the nobiliary conspiracies which sometimes shook the
Signory to its foundations. Scandalous abjurations, conspiracies, romantic
elopements, murders, deeds of military prowess crowded the chronicles of
this princely stock whose private conduct revealed the same unbridled and
arrogant violence which carried it in public life to avowed treasons as well
as to the highest offices of State. By measures of remarkable discretion, an
Aldobrandi had put down the agrarian revolt and the Mercanza secession;
another was said to have silenced the Farghian fortresses during the great
bombardment. Proudly immured in its Borgo palace surrounded by the
poorest neighborhood, this race given over to its powerful and withdrawn
moods brooded in its eyrie above Orsenna, fecundating or fustigating the
city like a splendid storm. Citizens would repeat the insolent challenge of
the Aldobrandi motto: fines transcendam, never failing to impart a certain
irony to the phrase, recalling for how many of the tribe’s exiled members
the words had assumed a bitterly concrete meaning. Vanessa’s father, a
conspiring demagogue convicted of subverting the townships he had bribed
with his huge fortune, had been the latest victim of these repeated
banishments; this circumstance, magnified by the imagination of a very
young man, had qualified my relations with Vanessa by an exalted and
romantic respect; she was one of the greatest matches to be made in the city
and quite heedless of a father she had scarcely ever seen: I protected, I
venerated in her an orphan threatened by mysteries on all sides. We met
each other almost nowhere but in this garden which lavished its secret
efflorescence for our sake, and rare were the occasions, I believe, when we
even spoke—we remained without words for moments on end, confronting
that scorched ocean Vanessa revealed to me whenever I considered the
world through her eyes, for in them tossed the murky reflections of the

distant seas the exile had crossed—her misery, which I exaggerated to
myself, cast over my joys a shadow of secret reserve, and over the less
chaste thoughts which occurred to me something like the ban of a sacrilege;
I loved her in absentia, without hoping she would become closer to me and
as if her dreamy, immaterial hand were created only to give order, in an
infinitely deepened distance, to the perspective of my dreams. When she
spoke to me of those countries from which her father occasionally wrote,
her voice, deepening until it was almost guttural, betrayed a calm
exaltation. Facing Orsenna and the affairs of everyday life, her detachment
was extreme and disdainful. At the heart of this city so complacently coiled
upon its wealth, and in which she was the Alien par excellence, these
conversations led me into a mysterious and dissolving labyrinth. Next to
this frail figure which cast its anathema upon a mediocre and indulgent life,
I mentally renounced Orsenna and its reassuring—and detested—health; I
would return from the Selvaggi Gardens through dim and disenchanted
streets, my afternoons dragging through an interminable languor.
Sometimes I would reopen the garden gate at twilight, with all the
precautions of a smuggler; I crept to our now empty place. The sun would
be setting behind the ink-black forest wall; a mist already covered the
garden’s lower slopes and was rising like a tide toward our belvedere; tense
and motionless, I stared through the waning light at the outlines of the dark
trees silhouetted against the horizon’s luminous strip. Here Vanessa’s last
gaze had fallen; I waited for the appearance of what it had mysteriously
indicated to me. The garden was vanishing in its hostile, sequestered silence
and drove me away; I kissed the cold stone where Vanessa had been leaning
and returned through the suburb’s black streets, among the low houses
squeezed between their yews and pointing cypresses where they resembled
nothing so much as lighted graves.
Gradually I detached myself from a life without accidents, without
excitement. Vanessa withered all my pleasures and wakened me to a subtle
disenchantment; to me she opened deserts, and these deserts spread by
patches and scraps, like an insidious leprosy. Little by little I gave over my
work; my door was no longer open to friends most of the time, and nothing

pleased me so much, now, as the prospect of an empty day focused at noon
upon that one meeting with Vanessa. Regular tasks and occupations had
become wearisome to me. With all the provocative extravagance of youth, I
reduced the manifestations of my disgust to absurdity: astounding my
family, I claimed that Orsenna stank of swamps and refused, except for
these brief encounters, to venture out into sunlight. I prowled the city by
night, brushing past those figures—just too fast or too slow—whom the day
momentarily mechanizes and whom the depths of night, like the pitiless last
lap of the race, scatters about the streets, as tragically naked as lame cattle
or beasts of prey. Something louche and wounded loitered in Orsenna’s
streets at this moment of bleak exhaustion. It was as if the stagnant water
lapping at the pilings of the lower town had withdrawn to their lowest level
and revealed the mud-stained dilapidated forest of fevers which served to
prop it up; with delectation I plunged into those fermenting depths; an
instinct suddenly revealed to me as if to a visionary the threatened city,
great pieces of its ruined crust collapsing into these swamps of which it had
been the ultimate flowering. Like the face of a woman still beautiful yet
irremediably aging, suddenly revealed by the tragic light of dawn in all its
crackled horror, Orsenna’s countenance confessed its exhaustion to me; a
breath of remote annunciation passed through me, warning that the city had
lived too long, that its hour had come, and as I struggled against it during
those murky nights when all betrayals are declared, I felt that the forces
which had hitherto sustained it were changing camp.
It had occasionally seemed to me that I might find an unavowed
justification for these childish dreams, some excuse which could become
active. To one contemplating it with an unprejudiced eye, the city
sometimes seemed to doze in broad daylight, as though caught in a net
mended with greater difficulty each day—a net of exhausted habits. The
perspective now afforded by my journey to Syrtes warranted still keener
perspicuity; memory decanted, at this distance, impressions hitherto
jumbled and dissolved in that tumult of the quotidian which inundates us in
its trivial agitation. For me, Vanessa had been that tiny crack which reveals
the depth of an invisible crystal; but appearances at Orsenna—as a stranger

is the first to discern the alterations of disease on a face familiar to us—
once I had taken my distance, were no longer those of health. The
expression of the people in the streets on feast-days—still sumptuous and
still faithfully observed—was that of boredom beneath an over-scrupulous
imitation of pleasure: this festive garment kept something faded and
hopeless about it, like those soldiers’ uniforms which retain the dusty folds
and creases of the closet. Loyalty to tradition, having become almost a
fetish, expressed the impoverishment of a blood incapable of fresh
creations. One was sometimes reminded of those desiccated, well-preserved
old men who manage to deceive those around them for some time—as life
recedes, they seem to reveal instead, each year more imperiously, more
emphatically, the strong and convincing reality of their skeletons: thus it
had come about that everywhere abroad the Signory’s constitution was cited
as an example of a model political machine, which in actuality functioned
for the satisfaction of connoisseurs with the mocking perfection of a
museum piece, as though at the heart of a disturbing void which no longer
resolved doubts as to the vigor of the mechanism which still kept it in
working order. The skepticism of the upper classes had become profound
indeed, as the meticulous service regulations had grown more demanding
and a pitiless point of honor was attached to their strict execution. One
symptom which upon reflection might appear especially disquieting was
represented, for me, by the cravings for travel, by the cosmopolitan spirit,
the bizarre whims now beginning to depopulate, in one place after the next,
this anthill all too ingeniously arranged—as if the blood had coursed to the
skin to be refreshed—by the wandering humor which had seized upon the
most cultivated minds. Was I myself not an example of this? I began
thinking more attentively about that singular colony of Maremma by which
Fabrizio set so much store. Yes, Vanessa would find her place there marked
out in advance.
While the Redoubtable was gradually making way through a calm sea, I
began smiling at what this imminent encounter promised for me, and for me
alone, in the way of striking news. I had long since lost sight of Vanessa:
old Aldobrandi, mortally bored with exile, had ended by noisily demanding

his family’s presence abroad. I was very young—I forgot; or thought I was
forgetting. I had inattentively learned, subsequently, of his return and his
sudden recovery of favor, and I concluded with an ironic smile that matters
were proceeding according to plan: henceforth Orsenna would make terms
with forces which were scarcely likely to work to her advantage.
The night had become very dark. Standing beside me on the bridge,
Marino stared off the bow. His body vanished beneath the shimmering
reflections of black oilskin. His face was oddly framed, the features
sharpened by the tension of his vigil. He was expecting no results, I knew,
from this banal night patrol, but Marino did nothing by halves. On this
reassuring sea the Redoubtable was steaming ahead, ready for any
encounter, its crew alerted, its cannons loaded. Absurd as it might seem,
confronting the tense reality of this little military world advancing into the
dark—a world which I had so frivolously unleashed—I felt troubled and
uncertain. I suffered from something like a belated remorse and the panic of
the sorceror’s apprentice when, at first quite gently and to his incredulous
astonishment, objects impudently mistreated began to move; as the ship
moved ahead with the grudging dislocation of a wakened animal, I too
moved, at grips with a faint embarrassment and yet with the exalting
sentiment of a magical release. I had made the Redoubtable take to the
waves; dozens of wide eyes were relaying my own uncertain gaze out over
the dark water. Through the opaque night, a muffled network of voices was
echoing to harsh commands—those brief and alluring sounds, those fatal
resonances which rise from human throats within any machine launched
toward an adventurous horizon. A careful efficacy, tense and vigilant, was
accumulating around us through the darkness; I felt it seething and
crackling with the exact intervals of a mechanism in working order. Even
Marino’s gaze, that steady and coldly lucid gaze, grew slightly feverish, as
at the first whiff of the subtly magnetized atmosphere of action. This
military stir, like the ambiguity of a game that might become serious from
one moment to the next, granted in its turn consistency and reality to the
suspect apparition of the night before, released a subtle series of operations:
I almost expected to see looming up before me the enigmatic silhouette—I

searched the darkness with a gaze more intent by the moment; once or
twice, when a brighter reflection played over the waves, I consciously kept
my hand from clutching at Marino’s arm. Was I seeing things? Such a
gesture would have been, at that moment, the sign one makes to an
accomplice. The blood of the corsairs spoke aloud in Marino; I felt him,
beside me, suddenly almost as nervous as I was. We were, at that moment,
two hunters prowling through the night, the whole craft beneath us
shuddering as in the squall of a sudden fever of risk.
“A fine night, Aldo, wouldn’t you say?”
There was a repressed tremulousness in his voice which suddenly
betrayed him, within his element, as inexplicably committed. I realized that
he would resent me, tomorrow, for an effusion so uncharacteristic. But
tonight brought together, in the two of us, two very intimate foes; by this
ship which trembled beneath our feet, we were communicating in the
depths.
“A fine night. The best I’ve spent in Syrtes so far.”
On the bridge in the half-darkness, there then occurred something very
solemn, without shifting his gaze, Marino’s hand groped for my arm and
rested on it a second. I felt my heart swell as though granted some
extraordinary permission—like a man in front of whom a door opens where
he had not even dared knock.
“But you don’t really like taking out the Redoubtable ...”
“Not too often, Aldo. In fact, as little as possible. It seems as if I’m not
earning my pay. It seems as if I’m taking a vacation.”
The moon rose over an absolutely calm sea, through a night so
transparent we could hear, from the thickets along the coast, coming closer
and closer, the faint clucking and cackling of the marsh birds frightened in
the reeds by our wake. The coast we were skirting bristled like a black wall
against the moon with the motionless spears of its reeds. Silent as a night
prowler, the Redoubtable’s flat hull slid through these shallow channels
with a sureness that revealed its captain’s infallible eye. Behind the dark
rim, the desert stretches of Syrtes reflected the majesty of a field of stars all

the way to the horizon. It was good, tonight, to be out to sea with Marino; it
was encouraging to venture with him into the mystery of this warm
darkness.
OceanofPDF.com

4
The Ruins of Sagra
Fabrizio woke me early the next morning, bursting into my room with an
ironic, knowing expression. “Here I am starving for the details of last
night’s expedition. Apparently you drew a blank ...”
Such swaggering, at such an hour, was unpalatable. Last night’s
confusions had certainly left me nothing to say to Fabrizio. There were too
many barriers between us. “Go to the devil, Fabrizio! People with real work
to do need their sleep. Run along and play somewhere else!”
But Fabrizio was not persuaded to leave the field. Crossly huddled under
my blankets, I watched with open hostility as he fidgeted around the room,
throwing the windows open, glancing at the maps of Syrtes that littered my
desk.
“Chilly in here, isn’t it ... What’s this? My, that’s nice, careful work you
do ... God, it must have been exciting for you ... ‘the patrol line,’” he
murmured significantly, staring at the map with an inquiring look. “I’ll bet
you didn’t run any risk of crossing it with Marino. How far from the coast
did you get?” he asked with a sly turn of his head.
“Marino knows what he has to do, and he can do it without any help from
you. Shut that window, Fabrizio—shut it now, or you’ll be sorry. You want
me to catch my death? I’m sleeping, do you realize that, sleeping! I think
I’d better throw you out of here,” I added, though without much conviction.
“‘Throw you out of here’ ... Kindness itself, from my comrade-in-arms ...
All right, all right, whatever you say. There’s still time. You can tell me all
about it later, as we march along.”
“I feel as much like marching as I do like being hanged. Damn it,
Fabrizio, close that door!”

Fabrizio looked at his watch. “We’ve got about fifteen minutes to get out
of here, the two of us, or else we’ll be late. You’d better start getting
dressed.”
“Once and for all, Fabrizo, go to hell!”
Fabrizio shrugged and rolled his eyes with an exaggeratedly pained
expression. “All right, Aldo, at least try to pull yourself together. The
ceremony’s for this morning!”
Getting up, a dreary prospect, was in fact becoming an imperative one:
Marino, in a very official tone of voice, had mentioned this ceremony in
honor of the dead, quite a ritual at the Admiralty, and the Observer assigned
to the Light Forces could not fail to be present without causing a sort of
scandal. I pulled on my clothes, grumbling. Moreover, I couldn’t quite
convince myself that this was just a routine task—Marino would be taking
part in it, and this ceremony awakened in me a vague curiosity.
The road, under our boots, on this crisp morning, rang out hard and clear.
Fabrizio led the way. The Admiralty cemetery, lost in the wilderness of
reeds, was no more than a few hundred yards off. A bright sun was
glistening over Syrtes. As I walked beside Fabrizio, who was comically stiff
in his finest uniform, my spirits were gradually restored: on this dry and
frosty morning, clattering along the smooth roadway, you could take a
lively pleasure in not being dead.
We said nothing. From time to time Fabrizio shot me a sidelong glance.
Evidently his curiosity was seeking some means of drawing me out. Just
then we passed a little mound which afforded a view of the sea.
“You must have heard the loons, off Sands Point. There are supposed to
be thousands of them out there—it means the winter will be a cold one.
Giovanni says he’s never seen so many as this year.”
“Yes, we went past Sands Point, if that’s what you want to know. And
kept close in to shore—close enough to touch, if you enjoy hearing that ...”
I felt, in my turn, the craving to attack Fabrizio. “... What made you say,
before, that we didn’t run a risk of crossing the patrol line?”

Fabrizio looked solemn. “Marino would never agree to that. You don’t
know him, if you think he would.”
“You didn’t just cross it yourself, as it happens?”
“As it happens, I did, just once.” It was apparent that this recollection
was not a particularly pleasant one.
“And he chewed you out for it?”
“My God, what a fuss! Marino went really crazy. You know the voice he
puts on when he thinks there’s something wrong: it’s enough to freeze your
blood. My heart was really in my boots. ‘You’re a brainless fool, incapable
of understanding the meaning of your actions.’ ... That kind of thing.
Thunder and lightning. Let me tell you, I’m not about to try it again ... Of
course this is just between us—between friends, Aldo. No use letting it get
back to the city.”
“I’m honored by your confidence. But tell me one thing—what could
make you do something that stupid in the first place?”
Fabrizio’s expression became quite contrite: “Well, you know how it is—
I was taking the Redoubtable out for the first time. And I was quite full of
myself, up there on the bridge all alone. I wanted to do something big.
Something that would make a noise. Besides, doesn’t it seem funny—I
mean, if you’re a sailor it’s not exactly normal for a warship to spend all its
time poking around the mud flats, when it could just as well be—going
right across ...”
“And that’s what you did—go across?”
“Yes, I wanted to forget about the mud flats for a while. So I headed
straight east. The old crew gave me some funny looks.”
“Funny looks?”
“It’s hard to explain—it wasn’t anything you could put your finger on.
They didn’t know what to say. It made a difference for them, you know ...
losing sight of their reed beds ...” Fabrizio paused a moment, as if he were
dreaming. “... But I think they’d have taken to it soon enough. Habits don’t
mean a thing. I think they get bored sometimes at Syrtes, you know ...”

I looked Fabrizio right in the eyes, suddenly, and I let the harsh accents
of provocation fill my voice. “Have you ever thought much about ... the
Other Side?”
Fabrizio stopped walking, astonished, but less sensitive, I think, to my
question than to the bitterness in the tone that scorched my lips. “No, I
never think about that. I didn’t have anything particular in mind, you know.
It was a schoolboy prank. The war with Farghestan doesn’t make my blood
boil, to tell you the truth. You’ve got to admit it’s pretty stale by now.
They’re savages, all right, but at least they leave us in peace. Of course I’m
as ready as the next man to give them what they deserve, if they come back.
What a day, Aldo! ... Can you imagine the old Redoubtable spitting fire
across the lagoons? That’s a sight not to be missed—like St. Jude fireworks!
Too bad it’s only for bedtime stories in Orsenna. At least it would be a
distraction around here.”
“You make it easy for yourself.”
“I don’t think about it so much as you do, Aldo—that’s all. What’s past is
past. Do you want to know something? Farghestan is like the bogeyman: all
it’s good for now is to scare little children.”
“There’s more to it than that.”
Fabrizio pretended to put his hands over his ears. “There you go with
your conspiracy theories. I know you spend all your time thinking about it,
no need to tell me.”
“I think about it now and then.”
“But you don’t think about it the way I do, when it’s happening.”
“What do you mean?”
“I mean, I think about it the way I think about the Other Side ... it’s like
any coast, like any coast anywhere. You make a vice out of it, Aldo. You
think about it for your own sake: you need to think about it. You’d invent it,
if you had to. You invent the bogeyman to scare yourself.” Fabrizio put his
hand like a megaphone around his mouth and with a grotesque gesture
turned toward the side of the road, whispering.

“What’s got into you?”
“I’m telling the great secret to the reeds, like Midas: Aldo’s invented
Farghestan! Aldo’s invented Far-ghes-tan!”
“Enough of your foolishness.”
“I didn’t mean to make you mad. After all, everyone’s odd about
something. And you’re not the only one around here who’s odd about that.”
“I’m not?”
Fabrizio turned serious again. “Marino thinks about it too. It must be
catching. He thinks about it more than you do, maybe.”
“What makes you say that?”
“I’m going to tell you. I’ve noticed something funny. One afternoon I had
nothing much to do, and I went to the library for something to read about
Farghestan. I couldn’t find a single book. Not one. And there are any
number in the catalogue. They’re not on the shelves. Giovanni told me
where they are. All of them are in Marino’s room.”
“So what?” Without even realizing it, my voice had assumed a very
unpleasant tone. Against Fabrizio, I was suddenly defending an ally.
“So what? So nothing, if that’s how you’re going to take it ... I thought
you’d be interested. No one wants to take your Farghestan away from you,
you know,” he added, exasperated.
But I didn’t care about Fabrizio’s mood. I kept hearing Marino’s harsh
voice, that tone of voice so unfamiliar to me, saying: “You’re a brainless
fool, incapable of understanding the meaning of your actions ...”
The cemetery came in sight, on a hill overlooking the sea, a crude square
of low walls swept by the sea winds and filled with the chatter of the reeds.
The harsh oblongs of the stones, the treeless paths, the meticulous
maintenance of this official necropolis added a final touch of melancholy
that even the desert’s isolated graves do not possess. There was something
sickening about the sight of this well-tended vacuum, where the very idea
of death inspired something too vivid; you felt that three centuries of

anonymous chores had relayed each other here, absorbed in their turn by the
anonymity of the sands, in order to level a site of utter effacement.
They were rotting away at attention, the defenders of Orsenna. Just as I
might have seen, emerging from its swamp, the patiently buttressed forest
of pilings which propped up the city, here lay before my eyes, in isometric
perspective, three centuries of the fatherland’s foundations. The bodies
devoured by sand, one on top of the next in strict perpendicularity, drove
into the earth, with repeated blows, their grove of vertical pillars. The city’s
sinister genius, even in these extreme outposts, showed in the patient
mutilation which, out of so many new and naively surrendered lives, by a
merciless pruning, had formed this careful frame, this funereal housing.
Generation after generation had consumed their lives mortising an exact
pigeonhole, fitting themselves to the measurements of the anticipatory pit
being deepened for them in the sand. The voracious city maintained itself
on the earth’s surface at the dizzying summit of a garden of monsters, a
foundation of skeletons devoured alive. It existed, it endured, a thin
membrane alive but utterly pathological—victim of a giant necrosis, using
every last drop of its humors to secrete more bone, thrusting underground in
a nightmare verticality the collective carcass which the geological eras
spread flat.
And while Fabrizio and I were absentmindedly strolling along these
dreary paths, a little troop of armed men had silently gathered at the
cemetery gate: a landing crew from the Redoubtable, and a squad of farm
workers, recognizable by their clumsy gestures and the wisps of straw still
clinging to their tunics. A sharp command rang out, the troop presented
arms, and Marino dismounted in front of the grille.
We saluted him as we entered. Clumsy and slow in his great boots, his
expression that of a farmer in his Sunday best, the captain had pinned to his
gray tunic the Medal of Valor, a distinction rarely awarded by the Signory.
He led us stiffly toward the far end of the cemetery. Between freshly
plastered patches, an ancient tablet still presented the arms and date of
office of the Podesta who had erected it; the city’s sole and arrogant sign

glittered on that bare wall, drawing the eye like a target—the arms of
Orsenna and the strange device in which its ageless genius was petrified: In
sanguine vivo et mortuorum consilio supersum. The troops lined up in front
of the wall, two gray rows which the banner of Saint Jude spattered with
red; the oldest boatswain presented Marino with a wreath of myrtles and
laurels from Syrtes; the captain, bending down with a great effort, placed
the wreath at the foot of the monument, and straightening up again,
removed his visored cap. There was a moment of grim silence. In that
stillness, only the tuft of his gray hairs rumpled by the sea wind stirred,
vaguely animate. At the base of the monument, I suddenly noticed, during
that hypnotic silence, a long dirty streak which stained the stone and ran
down to meet a spongy layer of blackened leaves. Year after year the faded
wreaths slid down to this absorbent cushion, evoking a gentle, rotting
continuity—a recognizable emblem still. This well-supplied humus
delighted the august nostrils: even as a symbol, Orsenna continued to
fabricate cemetery ground.
It was at this moment, with all the brutal violence of a squall, that the
trumpets blared: an old anthem of Orsenna, a tune from heroic days when
the stiff brocades filed past, the barbaric tiaras, hieratic robes over marble
steps, the wingbeats of triumphal flames, red evenings filled with galleys
trailing their canvas on the sea—a splendid and noble fanfare, like the
gradual unfolding of long swags, one after the other, of some interminable
drapery through which rippled all the impalpable irridescence of the east.
Bolts of the voluntary rained down on the cemetery. Long, short, long, the
notes pursued one another in a superhuman summons, one hot red flow of
joy, stifling as a clot of blood. Then at last they ceased, and the torches were
lit once again. Nothing had moved. Syrtes stretched away to the limits of
the horizon, gray and dim. The wreath was still hanging on its hook.
Marino’s cap circled between his clumsy fingers. Around the tubes of their
instruments, before returning them to their cases, the trumpeters rolled
coverings stamped with Orsenna’s arms, as if they were folding up an
illegible parchment.

There was to be a formal banquet at the Admiralty, to which Marino had
diplomatically invited several of the rich farmers who constituted the chief
clientele for his available manpower. Their rustic, actually crude manners,
and the excessive cordiality Marino showed them, disgusted me: the
bargaining during dessert left my nerves raw, though it proved that Marino
administered his little troop with the most scrupulous honesty. The
reception’s pomp reached its height when he proposed to conduct his guests
on a visit through the fortress: the penniless Sun King showing his tax
collectors over Versailles would not have scandalized me more; I ordered
my horse saddled and excused myself, claiming to be taken ill. I virtually
was; the notion of those still-muddy boots tramping across the noble stones
disgusted me: I saw it as a sort of sacrilege. Fabrizio was not deceived and
managed to intercept me as I crossed the room.
“You’re not forgetting about the Aldobrandis? We’re counting on you for
tonight. The carriage leaves at six ...”
“To hell with the lot of you!” I whispered, carried away by an impulse of
rage I could no longer control. At that moment Vanessa herself seemed to
belong to the sordid crew, one of those civilians to whom Marino made
those overtures I so resented.
“I think you’re out of your mind.” Fabrizio shrugged just as he had this
morning. I rather abruptly avoided his whispered sermon and ran to the
stables—I needed to be alone.
I had a long afternoon of fine weather ahead of me, and I decided to
devote it to an often-postponed visit to the remote ruins of Sagra. Giovanni
had described this forsaken town where his hunter’s forays had occasionally
taken him, as having altogether reverted to the wild, so that you could shoot
game in weedy streets. This prospect of solitude allured me; the sun was
still high when I thrust a hunting-rifle into my saddle holster and set out.
The nearly washed-out trail which wound between the reeds and led to
the ruins crossed one of the dreariest parts of Syrtes. Hard-stemmed rushes
which were green in spring for a few weeks but dry and yellow all the rest
of the year, and which the slightest breeze rattled like knucklebones, grew

hereabouts in dense clumps, and no scythe had ever thinned out these
disinherited fields. I made my way along the narrow slit through the dry
stems, their rustling vivifying these solitudes in a sinister fashion, distracted
only now and then by a glimpse, to my left, over dull lagoons that looked
like a pewter blade edged with yellow where the even duller yellow of those
obsessive stems died out. Yet even the melancholy of that flaming sun over
a dead land failed to extinguish in me a throb of happiness; I felt in
complicity with the tendency of this country to absolute desolation. It was
both end and beginning. Beyond these realms of lugubrious reeds extended
the desert sands, ever more sterile, and—like the decor of a navigable death
—behind a sparkling mirage of mist, the peaks to which I could no longer
deny a name. Like savage peoples who recognize an active virtue in certain
orientations, I kept walking southward ever more intently: a secret
magnetism was leading me in the right direction.
Yet the sun was already beginning to move down the sky. I had ridden for
what seemed hours, and nothing on these naked plains hinted at the
proximity of the ruins whose broken silhouette I was trying to discern on
the far, flat horizon. I had been heading these last few moments toward an
isolated and tree-covered hillock overlooking the lagoon and toward which,
to my amazement, were also leading quite recent traces of a carriage which
seemed to have followed this narrow trail and to have knocked down in
passing the rushes whose broken stems I noticed everywhere. As I pondered
what it could be that had lured Marino or his lieutenants toward these
forsaken woods, I made out quite distinctly, at no distance at all, the
surprising murmur of a stream; the reeds gave way to various bushes, then
to a thick growth of trees, and all at once I found myself in the very streets
of Sagra.
Giovanni had not lied. Sagra was a baroque marvel, an improbable and
disturbing collision of nature and art. Very old underground canals, between
their disjointed stones, had ultimately managed to spew through the streets
the waters from an underground spring which they had brought from
leagues away; and slowly, with the centuries, the dead city had become a

paved jungle, a hanging garden of wild trunks, a frenzied gigantomachia of
tree and stone. Orsenna’s taste for massive and noble materials, granite and
marble, produced the singular character of lavish violence, even of
exhibitionism which this struggle everywhere assumed—the same
complacent muscle-effects displayed by a county-fair wrestler constantly
appeared here in the ostentatious resistance, in the overhangs which here
opposed a balcony to an enlacing branch, there a half-collapsed wall
suspended above the void, to the turgescent thrust of a tree trunk—until
weight was flouted altogether, and the disturbing obsession of a slowed
combustion, an earthquake snapshot, was imposed.
I rode on, astounded, through this green penumbra where the motionless
branches cast a mesh of sunlight on the sopping pavements. A heavy
moisture crept across the ground, covering the rubble with a layer of moss
that muffled every sound, letting through only the shrill tinkle of the water
trickling everywhere in swift rivulets across the stones—the same
unconcerned dripping which seeps out of masonry after a bombing or a fire.
I tied my horse to a doorjamb that had half worked loose and began
wandering through the avenues, occasionally stumbling over a thick layer
of rotten leaves. Sagra, it was obvious, had not been a city at all, little more
than a sort of trading post, a tiny checkerboard of intersecting streets beside
the lagoon. The ground floors revealing fragments of vaulted rooms in the
rear, the spacious cellars over which the paving was falling in along the
gutters, betokened warehouses and shops; a few ghosts of handsome villas
appeared, crouching in their teeming gardens as though behind a fortress of
underbrush. But the motionless gloom caught up these ordinary fragments
into a magic crystal, and a reverie was spun by the murmur of fountains that
seemed still to recall the vanished occupants to their humble tasks, to braid
into a mute garland round well and pond those profound gestures which
make the heart leap like a panic sentiment of life’s permanence. I was
suddenly filled with an anxious desire to awaken, if only for a moment, the
echoes in these streets, to shout for some living soul forgotten in this
labyrinth of silence.

But of course there was no one. The daylight was beginning to wane in
these somber alleys, and I was just deciding to leave when I thought I made
out a faint sound of waves, and almost immediately emerged, to my
surprise, on the ridge above an irregular basin of lapping water which had
once—how long ago?—been the harbor of Sagra. Huge trees encircled it,
trailing their lowest branches in the water, leaving a brighter patch only in
the middle. But half concealed under the drooping branches, an unexpected
outline united all that was left of the light in one metallic gleam: along the
ruined quay was moored what looked like a boat.
An instinctive gesture made me shrink back under cover of the trees, as if
I had felt, at that very instant, that I must not be seen, at any cost. I suddenly
recalled the fresh tracks on the path, but another memory spoke to me still
more distinctly: in that vague, still barely divined silhouette, something
reminded me of the apparition seen from the beach.
The thickets, luckily, grew thick at the basin’s edge, and I could easily
reach a more convenient observation post. The boat—a small one—which I
had some difficulty making out under its vault of foliage, suggested a
pleasure craft of one kind or another, yet sturdy and certainly capable of
taking to the open sea. Only the stern was clearly visible, and I could
rejoice in my discretion: there was no name on the sternboard nor any trace
of the nautical registration obligatory in Orsenna. My heart was suddenly
gripped, along with a hunter’s fever, by a kind of justifying pang: I had
scored against Marino. Something here was no longer within the rules.
Standing on tiptoe, I trained my eyes on the boat half revealed through
the leaves. It fascinated me—like any feverishly desired apparition—like an
elusive quarry brought within close range yet mysteriously outlined by the
gunsight. I had it at my mercy. In this jungle silence, the boat would have
seemed abandoned there, had not the shining brass and fresh paint testified
to recent maintenance. For a second, I felt on the point of yielding to a
terrible craving to leap onto the deck and enlighten myself as to what could
no longer fail to be a splendid prize, when I suddenly realized that the boat
might be guarded from shore, and I tried to stare, in the now very dim light,

through the thick underbrush along the rotting quay. Then I made out, not
far away under the trees, the shape of a tiny, half-collapsed cottage, and I
realized with an irritation depriving me of any desire to leave my hiding-
place, that a thread of smoke was rising from the ruins.
While I was pondering how to avoid this unexpected obstacle, I suddenly
heard behind me the ill-timed neighing of my horse echoing through the
woods, and almost immediately the figure of a man, rifle in hand, came out
of the house. Walking uncertainly, anxious and indecisive, he advanced as
though by reflex toward the boat which it was obviously his job to guard,
stopping occasionally to cock his head, and for a moment I could see him
more distinctly in a clearing of the underbrush. He was dressed like the
shepherds of Syrtes, but I was immediately struck by something singularly
loose and supple in his walk, and especially by the very dark, almost exotic
tinge of his face and hands. The shadows were already robbing me of the
barely glimpsed figure, blurring an almost indefinable impression, and yet
—no, this was not the product of an imagination fevered by surprise—I
could have sworn that this was someone you would not have expected to
meet in Syrtes. After a moment of motionless vigil, and with the same
supple swiftness, the man, no doubt reassured, slipped back into the ruins.
The boat was obviously well guarded, and there was nothing for me to do
but leave. As quietly as I could I slipped through the darkness until I
reached one of the phantom streets, and leading my horse by the bridle
through the tunnel of diffuse light which marked the entrance to the ruins, I
left seductive Sagra without incident.
I ran no risk of losing my way in this rather pallid night, and gave my
horse its head through the trench between the reeds which would lead it
straight as a rail to the Admiralty. There was an evident link between the
presence of this boat and the carriage tracks, and the more I thought of it,
the more inclined I was to regard it as the key to those clandestine visits to
and from Maremma. The likelihood of smuggling occurred to me, but the
looks of that pleasure-yacht could scarcely confirm such suspicions. Its
presence in Sagra suggested a hundred banal interpretations that were just

as plausible, but I felt an instinctive reluctance to admit any of them; my
suppositions were already falling into place of their own accord, projected
in that obsessive direction where everything outside the context of daily life
was already gravitating for me.
I could no longer conceal from myself the excessive importance which
anything related to Farghestan, however remotely, was beginning to
assume. In my idle life at the Admiralty, that country had initially been the
object of a vague reverie—against the lure of the void, I had sought a prop
within reach of my hand. Orsenna’s uneasy sleep, too vulnerable to the
assault of obsessive memories, like that of an old man ill-defended against a
long memory, had warranted my venturesome dreams, and it was
significant that I had instinctively treated these dreams as dreams and
Farghestan as an accommodating figure which I exhumed from the silence
of the map room in order to immerse myself within it whenever I chose.
Amid this harmless somnambulism, my morning conversation with Fabrizio
had suddenly opened my eyes: certain all-too-reassuring mists had been
dissipated. In front of me was a coast where ships could land, a country
where other men could imagine and remember.
It was in this new perspective that I was led to consider a boat which
seemed to take such liberties with nautical rulings, and this is what caused
me instinctively to avoid sharing my discovery with Marino. As for my
conduct in other respects, I felt in no hurry to reach any specific decision
now. Since the previous day, I had had the sense of being in contact with a
chain of events which had taken me in tow. My discovery of Sagra was a
link in this chain, and while urging my horse toward the Admiralty,
magnetized by the presentiment of an immediate future rich in surprises, I
pondered once again the appeal Vanessa had sent me. I began to regret my
impulsive mood; suddenly I spurred my horse to a gallop, in hopes that
perhaps the carriage had been delayed, and it was with great impatience that
I came out of the roadway through the lagoons and found myself alone on
the sand barrens, the Admiralty looming up before me already plunged into
darkness.

OceanofPDF.com

5
A Visit
I was exasperated, and for the first time the Admiralty’s solitude seemed
oppressive to me. A heavy mist had accompanied the darkness across the
lagoons: dampness streamed over the bare walls, and the glow of my
lantern, as I crossed the barrens, produced the same unreal halo which, I
remembered, had aureoled Marino. I felt tense, anxious, suddenly rejected
like a punished child, locked in his dark room, who longs for the party’s
light and warmth. Suddenly I was struck by all the disorienting possibilities
of the notion that Vanessa and Marino should know each other. As on every
occasion that two beings linked to disparate episodes of our life make
contact with each other independently of ourselves, a suspicion of
complicity began to dim the lights of that distant festivity. In the theatrical
setting which I had conceived for it, and which Vanessa’s anticipated
appearance had just filled with sudden tension, a scene fraught with
significance was being acted out by my reverie, a scene which left me with
the disturbing impression that in some way my fate was being decided.
These anxious presentiments intensified the tedium of a very glum
evening. For a long time I paced my room. Benumbed as I became by this
mechanical shuffle, the place ultimately grew strange, producing, without
my being able to discover why, that faint uneasiness of a familiar space in
which some piece of furniture—but which one?—has been shifted.
Suddenly I realized that the maps of Sagra, brought here this afternoon and
covering the desk in disorder, had attracted my gaze each time I passed—or
rather I realized that for an hour now I had been craving to visit the map
room.
The body of the fortress rose up before me across the barrens, even more
daunting in the almost complete darkness of the illusion it gave of casting a
shadow, of communicating to this somnolent camp the faint and just-

perceptible pulsation of a heart of darkness, ponderously throbbing behind
the night. Protected by this enormous screen against the sea winds whistling
in the crenellations, I walked into the center of a heavy, sealed immobility.
That warm, wet night, all too gentle now, added to the confinement of these
walls the melancholy of a yawning jail: the dampness chilled the corridors
like the walls of a cave. Guided by the swaying glow my lantern sent down
these tunnels, I was struck as never before by the extraordinarily
inhospitable character of the place. Its silence was the sign of an arrogant
hostility. Something threatening seemed to take ambush behind this
contrived shadow, within this clump of arteries knotted round a black heart.
The faint light of my lantern on the walls of the map room produced,
almost materially, the faint shudder of waking whose tense vibration I had
felt in every nerve during my first visit. Like the frozen cry of cave
sculptures suddenly released from their centuries of clay by the thaw of a
lantern, the panoplies of gleaming maps came alive in the darkness, bright
patches restoring the network of a magical fresco by all the devices of
patience and of sleep. Because of the late hour and the fatigue of my
afternoon’s expedition, it suddenly seemed that the very energy draining
from my dissociated mind was reinforcing these vague outlines and—
obscuring their ordinary meaning—gently revealing at the same time their
hieroglyphic sorcery, evading one by one the resistances conjured up
against an enigmatic command. Gradually I slipped into a lethargy menaced
by nightmares and, still half conscious, I heard a clock in the sleeping
fortress suddenly chime ten.
The discomfort I felt was difficult to throw off. As I emerged from this
stupefaction, I seemed to find the room inexplicably altered. In a spasm of
fear, under my now wide-awake gaze, the walls continued to shift a little, as
if the dream were reluctant to abandon this ill-defended space. From the
coolness round my shoulders, I could tell that there was still a draft in the
room, and I suddenly realized that the dancing shadows were swaying on
the walls in time to the flame of my lantern, and that the door behind me
had been silently opened several seconds before.

I whirled around and shuddered as my cheek brushed against a woman’s
dress. A light, musical laugh rang out in the darkness, dragging me back
into the sea of sleep, rolling me in a final comber of dreams. I clutched at
the gown and raised my eyes toward the face drowned in shadow. Vanessa
was before me.
“Hardly overdoing security measures around the Admiralty—I’ll report
as much to the captain. And this is why you’ve abandoned your best
friend,” she added, leaning inquisitively over the desk.
Already she was sitting on the arm of my chair, swinging one leg and
unfolding the maps quite at leisure, as one might idly push open a country
neighbor’s door. Instantly I recognized that inimitable way she had of
plunging into a subject, the ease with which she could suddenly pitch her
tent in a gale. “What about your guests? ... How did you get here?” I
stammered uncertainly. Deprived in this fashion of any sort of preamble, I
felt as if I had been caught, somehow, with my hand in the till.
“My guests are very well, thank you. They’re doubtless drinking to my
health in Maremma at this very moment.”
“But ... Vanessa?”
“At least he remembers how to say it ... He hasn’t forgotten everything.”
The light laugh rang out again, suddenly preposterous in this echoing room
—like a stage laugh behind extinguished footlights. Vanessa lay her hand
on my forehead and looked at me steadily, solemnly. “What a child you are
still,” she added, with an almost tender inflection. “Enjoying yourself
here?” She peered round the dim room. “... Marino says they can’t tear you
away from the Admiralty. Is that so?” She was gradually securing herself
now, to my eyes blurred by amazement, with the perfect fixity, the calm of a
candle flame rising straight up in the still air. Against the map room’s dusty
litter, the pale, even tint of her arms and throat suggested some
extraordinarily precious, radiant substance, like a white gown in a garden
by night.
“It’s true, I don’t get away much. And I do enjoy myself here ...”

“Not so amusing as the Selvaggi gardens ... But I suppose it has its
charm.” Her gaze, accustomed now to the darkness, suddenly grew fixed.
She raised the lantern: the seething chaos of maps emerged from the
shadows. Her face froze with an intense, almost childish curiosity. “Is that
why you come here—to look at these maps?”
“Is this a cross-examination?”
“Only a compliment to you. I can’t imagine more decorative
surroundings.”
The lantern’s beam rested on an old map embellished with strange,
looping letters. Vanessa’s voice suddenly took on an accent of direct
provocation. “I have the same one in my room at Maremma. You’ll see it
there?”
“What are you doing in Maremma?”
“Syrtes is becoming very fashionable in Orsenna. We’ve always had a
palace there—it’s falling to pieces. I was bored, so I took it into my head to
put it back together. You seem to be the only man here not to realize what
I’ve been doing. And besides, one meets some very pleasant sorts here—
your friend Fabrizio, for example ...” Vanessa’s face grew tense, as if she
were listening for a stone that had been dropped down a well. “... Captain
Marino.” The name stirred something in me, and reawakened my regrets at
having missed the party.
“Captain Marino is not exactly what I’d call a pleasant sort.”
“You’re mistaken, Aldo. And I can tell you one thing: he thinks the world
of you.”
“Delighted he’s chosen you to transmit his approval.”
Vanessa ignored my interruption. “He can’t praise your zeal enough—
only he thinks you’re a little over-imaginative, a little ... quixotic.” She
stared into my eyes with a certain insistence. “I reminded him how young
you are, and I pointed out that one must never complain of high spirits in
the young—I told him you would settle down ...” The staring eyes in the

mocking face questioned me even more intensely than this banter
suggested. “And you see what serious conversations we have.”
“What else do you talk about?”
“We have many interesting subjects in common.”
“All Marino cares about is the service.”
“Which is no reason for us to limit our conversations.”
Vanessa had made her point, and I felt myself turning crimson with rage.
“Fine. If you’re so well informed about the service, you’ll realize that there
are certain duties, as well. I’m sure you realize how sorry I am not to have
been able to give you this evening.”
“What an elegant dismissal! Naive of me to presume you’d enjoy a visit
from me. It never occurred to me you’d have so much work on your hands.
I’ll complain to Marino—he ought to be ashamed of wasting your nights in
this jail! I’ll tell him he’s turning you into a real Cinderella.”
Her laughter was a direct provocation. “I work when I like, and where I
like ...”
Vanessa’s taunt became an uncontrollable giggle. That generous laughter
of hers, a rain of tender gaiety, pricked the bubble of my mood and led me
back to the Selvaggi gardens. She shone the lantern, still shaken by her
giggles, into my eyes, and I felt on my forehead the rousing contact, the
melting warmth of her familiar fingers mussing my hair. “There. That’s
better. You know, you’re quite delectable when you’re sulking. Really
adorable, Aldo.” Her breathless voice broke on an inflection that made the
blood tingle in my hands and lips. The tiny dispute had restored our
intimacy. I seized the hand that was lingering in my hair and the lantern fell,
leaving us in darkness. I thrust my face into that warm palm and kissed it
over and over. Vanessa pressed it gently against my lips in the darkness.
Suddenly she pulled back, as though waking from a dream, and turned
away. “How did you find the ruins of Sagra?”
“The ruins of Sagra? ... Really, Vanessa, you must have second sight. I
was just thinking how much they would interest you. Really ...”

Vanessa stood up, drew her cloak around her, and made a point of
meeting my eyes. “If you love me at all, Aldo, you’ll keep your impressions
to yourself.” The sharp tone which contradicted her muffled voice cut short
any comment, and I too stood up, hesitating. Vanessa fastened her furs and
the white patch of her gown vanished, relegating her once again to the
room’s darkness. “I’m taking you back with me, of course.”
“All the way to Maremma—this late?” But I was already giving in. I
didn’t want to leave her.
“Don’t be a child. I promised I’d bring you back. You don’t want to make
me break my promise ... I’d be compromised,” she added with a sly smile.
“Besides, I want you to see my party. It’s all arranged. I was giving it for
you.”
I heard rising in her voice that childlike exaltation I knew so well. I was
rediscovering Vanessa. On the delicate flesh of that countenance, one saw
emotions and ideas not so much forming as being born. Vanessa’s desire
sparkled in her eyes with a sudden brilliance, like stars rising out of the sea.
“Let’s go, then—never let it be said that I stood in the way of a spoiled
child.”
Much more than the prospect of the party, it was the thought of this
solitary expedition with Vanessa that had persuaded me. Vanessa drove. I
had put my arm around her, and in the warmth of her furs, I felt the consent
of her gentle, yielding weight. Now and then we skirted one of those huge
fortified farms somnolent in the warmth of the Syrtes night; at the edge of
the sandy road, gray walls shimmered an instant in front of the carriage;
deceived by the unaccustomed light of our headlamps, cocks occasionally
crowed. The glare paralyzed any number of clay-colored creatures on the
lumpy road, setting the coruscation of jewels in their eyes. Vanessa was
carrying me off, through the balmy night. In her, I was coming to myself: I
felt her beside me like the deeper bed which flood waters seek, like the
wind on one’s forehead as one courses down steep hills, eyes closed, in a
total submission to our course at breakneck speed. I “took” Vanessa amid
this wilderness, as one takes a road one guesses leads to the sea.

A night milky with fog and moonlight overhung the lagoons when we
reached Maremma, shimmering with diffuse light from the silvery plane of
the calm waters. The sense of unreality penetrating this white night
persisted: Maremma mingled with its night seemed to me a nebula of a city,
nothing but vague clots of mist which the jolting of the carriage might have
produced as we passed, dissolving immediately afterwards. Suddenly the
carriage stopped; I felt beneath my feet the slippery cobbles and on my face
the raw air which scours the sleep-moist skin of a traveller tumbled out of
his warm nest. A quay in front of us dropped straight down to the black
water. Vanessa, without turning back, walked straight to the edge. I watched
her, silent with amazement on the empty quay, as one might watch some
passerby in the mist climbing up the parapet of a bridge. Then she turned,
surprised to discover she was alone, saw me, and again burst out laughing.
A boat was waiting for us at the quay.
As I roused myself out of my stupor, I suddenly remembered Maremma’s
ironic and indulgent nickname: the Venice of Syrtes. The image I had often
recognized in the documents in the map room recurred to me: a hand with
tapering fingers that thrust out into the lagoon, representing the muddy,
uncertain delta of one of the few riverbeds that actually reach the sea in
Syrtes. At a period when Farghian invasions made land an uncertain haven,
colonists along the coast had taken refuge among these low mudbanks—
once the stream’s course had been deflected to keep the lagoon from silting
up, a canal had cut off the delta on the land side—Maremma, like Venice,
had launched itself, had slipped its moorings; perched on its trembling flats,
the city had become a floating island, an enchanted hand submissive to
effluvia from beyond the sea. A brief splendor had ennobled the town
during the period of the Peace of Syrtes: its sailors and colonists had
swarmed all over the coast, draining seaward its wool and its fruit from
remote oases and bearing in their galleys the gold and uncut gems of
Farghestan. Then war had come, and life had withdrawn from these
channels; today Maremma was a dead city, a closed hand clutching its
memories, a wrinkled and leprous hand hummocky with the crusts and

pustules of its ruined warehouses and its squares invaded by thistles and
crabgrass.
In a sort of reverie, I watched this flotsam pass before us, like the
afterbirth of a great city flung upon the coast by some sort of deluge. From
the abandoned canals rose a stench of fever; the viscous water clung to the
blades of the oars. Over a crumbling patch of wall, one skimpy tree leaned
toward the dead waters mirroring these ruins. High walls which seemed to
be the outworks of monasteries raised occasional islets of hostile
fortifications, like the last bastions to be brought down by some disaster.
The faint liquid sound of the oars and the lunar mist deepened the pestilent
silence, and then I noticed that the faintly phosphorescent surface of the
canal was continually streaked with tiny triangles: with the minor gurgles,
the all-too-intimate noises that rise out of a flooded ditch, water rats were
colonizing this necropolis.
I rested my hand on top of Vanessa’s where it lay on the gunwale. From
her silence I had guessed that she too was absorbed in the contemplation of
this cemetery of stagnation, this poignant surrender of a city to its supreme
dissolution. This silence, like love’s, betrayed her: Vanessa was welcoming
me to her domain. I remembered the Selvaggi gardens, and I knew what
lure drew her toward this den of rotting mud. Maremma was Orsenna’s
senescence, the final vision which coagulated the city’s heart, the
abominable manifestation of its rotten blood and the obscene froth of a last
death-rattle. As a warrior imagines an enemy already lying on his bier, a
murderous enchantment inveigled Vanessa to hover over this corpse. Its
stench was a pledge. And with this figurehead beside me to show me the
way, I realized that here on these forsaken shores Vanessa had joined, at
last, her chosen vision.
Cut off from the city by waste stretches where one divined the traces of
its former gardens, the Aldobrandi palace loomed up at the tip of one of the
fingers of the open hand, and its isolation on the right of the passage
through the lagoons and at the end of the widened canal seemed to me a
singular figuration of the temper of the easily offended race which had built

the place in its own image. This pleasure dome, cast like a sneer upon the
fever-ridden waters, still recalled something of the stronghold, the fortress.
Separated from the tongue of sand by a narrow inlet crossed by a wooden
bridge, it hovered over the canal like a long, low jetty crouching over the
water, from the far end rising one of those slender, very tall square
watchtowers which characterize in Orsenna the noble palaces of the great
period. Under the faint moonlight which blurred every detail, the harsh and
military contours hinted at the powerful foundations, the strength and
massiveness of a rocky bastion rooted like a tusk in these shifting mud-flats.
While the lower arcades shed across the water, like the mouth of an oven,
streaks of harsh light, the structure’s upper gallery, sound asleep beneath its
lunar terrace, stretched like a blind fillet from end to end, leaving the
dominant impression of a hostile reserve, a secret respiration in the
darkness.
Clearly the festivities had long since passed their peak: the fever was
falling. In the voices which rose from scattered groups I could hear that
dying fall, that detached tone of a return to calm which strikes the ear in the
still-animated street conversations after an accident has just occurred. I
greeted Marino with a hint of embarrassment and avoided answering his
mischievous glance. The captain was in a very good mood, and I assumed
that the morning’s transactions had been profitable. I was surprised, though,
as by an excessive familiarity, to feel him seize my arm, and as we strolled
among the groups, I thought I noticed that he was trying to overhear the
remarks being exchanged around us. I saw on his face once again that same
expression of intense concern I had already noticed on the bridge of the
Redoubtable when he was seeking the passage through the lagoons, and I
suddenly realized he was more preoccupied than he had appeared.
“Do you know who these people are?” he suddenly asked in a very
serious tone of voice, bringing us both to a halt and pointing with a vague
gesture to the rooms that opened in all directions around us.
Struck by this uneasy tone so anomalous in Marino, I began examining
more carefully the guests around us. I had noticed in passing a gleam of

sudden attention in some eyes, and occasionally a friendly gesture I had
responded to only vaguely, troubled as I was by an impression of a still
indefinable déjà vu. Certain memories were now coming back to me more
clearly. There were people here whom I had almost certainly met at my
father’s house, before my mother’s death had put an end to his worldly life,
and I was able to whisper in Marino’s ear several of Orsenna’s great names
—names whose mere mention in our city’s salons would have sufficed to
rank a party. Vanessa had not lied when she assured me that Syrtes was
becoming fashionable in Orsenna. Yet perhaps the distinction of the party
tonight was less of fashion than a bizarre whim, and the impression given
by this considerable crowd in no way confirmed the guarantee a few
indisputable names seemed to bestow. Rather than the glitter of a
fashionable gathering, these over-eager glances, apparently absorbed in a
common obsession, evoked the spontaneous relationships, the intimate
freemasonry of resorts people visit to cure some serious illness. I was no
longer surprised that Marino’s health seemed excessive in this crowd.
Moreover I had now realized that mingling among the guests—and with
what familiarity!—were people one never acknowledged in Orsenna.
“The Aldobrandis have always entertained peculiar guests. It looks as if
they’ve come to Maremma to take the fevers.”
“Yes, the air is unhealthy here. I was wrong to leave the Admiralty
tonight. Let’s go to the buffet.” I followed Marino, and we raised our
champagne glasses in silence. His preoccupied expression persisted. “I
think I’ll be leaving, Aldo. The princess has promised to see that you get
back later—I’m not worried about that. You’re quite at home here ...” he
added, half closing his eyes.
“And Fabrizio?”
“The wretch is already waiting for me in the carriage ...” Marino made a
deploring gesture toward the buffet. “He’s made himself sick ... I’ll leave
you here to defend the honor of the fleet,” he added, with a pathetic
grimace. I laughed aloud. Marino’s clumsy kindness returned repentantly to

our great argument, offering me a timid token. All of the man was in the
gesture, and I felt once again how fond of him I was.
“Vanessa will be sorry. You know, she talked a lot about you—she told
me you’ve been having long conversations.”
Marino coughed and blushed with a naïveté which went straight to my
heart. “A remarkable woman, Aldo. Very, very remarkable.”
I felt slightly stung. “Lucky you were able to appreciate her so ... readily.
Vanessa’s not an easy character to get to know.”
“That’s not quite what I mean.” Marino’s voice sounded calm and even.
“... She hates me. Well, it’s time I was leaving,” he continued, evidently
eager to bring our exchange to an end. “See you tomorrow, Aldo. Enjoy the
rest of the party.” He hesitated an instant. “And watch out for the morning
mists—that’s how you catch a fever.”
I had done nothing to deter this abrupt departure. Marino was releasing
me from an exasperating scrutiny. I felt renewed, my shoulders lightened as
if by a sudden frivolity, like a girl at her first ball when her mother, having
gallantly resisted sleep, finally decides to leave the party. I knew Vanessa
would not fail to come and find me soon enough, but there was no hurry,
and I wanted to make closer contact with some of these equivocal summer
people of Maremma. I headed toward a room from which gusts of music
emerged, and which lay along the sea front. I was counting on the brief
distraction of music to yield up these faces to me: here, more than anywhere
else, I would have the opportunity of seeing without being seen.
Vanessa’s party did not bely her reputation for sumptuous prodigality.
The bay windows of the arcades overlooking the lagoon had all been
thrown open: the intoxicating smell of the stagnant waters mingled like a
tide with the scent of huge bunches of flowers, giving them that same
funereal and moist opacity which chills our flesh beside a deathbed.
Through the dark bays, one glimpsed a swarm of boats carrying flowers and
lights on the lagoon below. The lighting, filtered by close-packed clusters of
drooping leaves, made the room float in the greenish, vitreous penumbra of
a mossy grotto, a kind of habitable pond which made every gesture sticky,

trailing from each sparkling wrist the silvery drops of a perceptible wake,
and where the music being played sustained a vibration of the almost liquid
air, creating a zone of deeper, more intimate disturbance. I avoided making
an instinctive gesture of recoil—as if I had lifted the curtain on a spectacle
much too private. There were not many people in this room, but I was
struck by something singular in the attitude and the disposition of the
groups which, rather than a concert hall, suggested an opium den or some
other chamber of clandestine ceremonies, and which advised me to return
to the ranks as soon as possible. I made for a sofa in the gloom, and
hurriedly sat down, holding my breath in spite of myself.
The heavy, somber music, the vague lighting, and the absorbing perfumes
were disconcerting me. It seemed that I was very slowly coming to my
senses, as if I had fallen through a trap door into this room and was
recovering them only one by one, at first led only by the thread of this
intoxicating sound, then dilated into the explosion of these feverish scents. I
was beginning to see a little now, and once again I was struck by the
liberties of attitude and gesture taken by the couples lured here, as might
have been expected, by the promise of a relative isolation. A subtle
atmosphere of provocation, an insidious sensual magnetism suddenly
seemed to become incandescent, here exposing the curve of a neck too
readily offered, there a glance too heavy, the swollen gleam of a mouth
opening to half-darkness. Certain faint movements were being made here,
scarcely perceptible, but suddenly suggestive to the eye—at the same depth,
one might have said, as the gestures of a sleeper. Yet in all this sea-cave
wakening, I suddenly felt, like a cold breath on the back of my neck, a more
intense awareness, a closer presence. I glanced behind me. Almost close
enough to touch there appeared, so abruptly had I come up against it, as
against a door, the face of a young woman turned toward mine. And I
realized from the naked possession they took of mine, in a sovereign
heedlessness of scandal, that there could be no question of avoiding her
eyes.

Everything that can rise from the life of the depths which is most hidden,
most nocturnal, was turned toward me in those pupils. These eyes did not
blink, or shine, or even attempt to focus—rather, upon meeting them, their
soft, even moisture suggested a scallop shell gaping wide upon darkness—
they simply opened there, floating upon a strange, lunar rock among the
coils of algae. In the confusion of hair like a sheaf of ripe wheat, the
intensity of this calm gaze opened like a starry sky. The mouth, too, lived as
though under one’s fingers, producing a retractile shudder, naked as the tiny
crater of some marine jelly. Suddenly it grew very cold. As the coils of a
writhing serpent are discovered in a stupor to belong to one and the same
creature, around this medusa-head there appeared, in successive glances, a
strange conformation. The head was fastened to the hollow of a shoulder,
covered by some dark material. Two arms formed a stole, a collar of
panting comfort which thrust as if into a full trough in the depths of her
bosom. This being disengaged itself—herself?—from the depths as if under
enormous pressure, rising fixedly into its sky of serenity like a full moon
through the branches.
Though I made every effort to take one drink after another, letting myself
be shifted by the crowd toward the gayest points of the party, it was only
gradually that I came to myself. As though I had been blinded by too
powerful a sun, dark specks floated before my eyes against the scintillation
of the lights. Unlikely as might appear, on such an evening, the open
celebration of this very intimate amorous liturgy, I did not feel at all
scandalized. The eyes which had stared into mine were not those of a judge.
They bore witness. When I tried to identify the strange weight which had
suddenly fastened me to them, an obsessive image recurred: that of those
natural wells lying open on the surface of the earth, down which the ear
vainly seeks to catch the echo of a pebble’s fall. Meeting this sickening
void, one stumbled a moment, the mind elsewhere, but as if nothing had
occurred, one’s path was now and forever altered. It was hopeless to resume
one’s way. Those eyes moved beside me, and the faint exhalation of their
abyss extinguished the lights; they made the party sway a little against the
background of a nightmare.

Suddenly it seemed to me that while I was circulating among the various
groups, thinking about the curious guests Vanessa had gathered, one of
those faces to which I had occasionally tried to attach a name was appearing
more frequently than the rest. A dry, glabrous face whose eyes, as though
veiled by a leucoma, nonetheless kept a sharper watch for that very reason
—a face that was not unknown to me and whose insistent reappearance
seemed to tug my sleeve. Vaguely intrigued, I leaned a moment against a
pilaster, waiting as if between two soundings, as the whirling crowd passed
by, for its reappearance. A voice murmured right in my ear, clear but low
and deliberately modulated, already set in the register of an intimate
dialogue. The face was before me. “A splendid party, don’t you think, my
dear Observer ... May I take advantage of my friendship with your father to
remind you of my name?” the voice went on, reading the surprise on my
face without being at all disconcerted, smiling slightly. “Giulio Belsenza ...
I used to know you when you were no more than a boy ...” The voice
assumed an inflection of complicity. “... And without trying to confuse the
Service with pleasure, it occurred to me that, this evening at least, our
duties might bring us closer together.”
I suddenly remembered that name. Instructions I had received upon
departure had designated him as the secret agent the Signory maintained at
Maremma. My reply was brief, and I tried to make it as little professional as
possible. Something in that countenance reminded me of a police spy, and I
didn’t like hearing such things in Vanessa’s house.
“... Yes indeed,” the voice continued, without seeming to take offence,
“you’ll forgive me for having decided to eschew appearances, since I have
the opportunity of meeting someone who concerns the Signory so closely ...
I’m quite isolated at Maremma—there are no orders for me here—and
without information ...” His voice had emphasized a bitter parenthesis.
Suddenly he raised his eyes to meet mine with a greedy expression. “There
are these rumors ...”
In the sudden fixity of his gaze, an anxious intensity belied the smile. I
immediately stopped being vague. “No doubt I’m less well informed than

you suppose ...”
“No one knows anything at the Admiralty? Well, at least that’s
reassuring.” The smile grew more insistently ironic. I suddenly felt
annoyed.
“No, I must say I don’t see ... I have virtually nothing to do here myself,”
I added disdainfully, “and I try not to concern myself with gossip.”
“People always say so many things in Maremma—perhaps they say too
many.”
“About the Admiralty?”
“About Farghestan.” The voice had hefted, as if in a hand, a word heavier
than the others. I felt a thrill, a wave running through my whole body, like a
fisherman who sees his cork yanked into a calm pool and then becoming the
very image of detachment.
“Really? There seems to be a real craving for disinterested speculation
hereabouts. Is there much talk of the moon, as well?”
Belsenza looked at me with a sly expression. “There might be, as a
matter of fact. There’s no lack of astrologists with the best intentions. That’s
what’s so strange about the thing: it’s as impossible to discover the origin of
these rumors as it is to put a stop to them. Maremma, my dear Observer, is
not a very healthy town, as you may realize ... I am paid a salary to know as
much” (paid badly, the voice said quite distinctly, and now I noticed the
yellowish tinge of his face, the features not so much ascetic as drawn, the
abject expression: a colonial who isn’t taking care of himself, I suddenly
thought—in a few years, Belsenza would be a pathetic wretch) “and I’m
beginning to think these little bouts of fever don’t all come from the
marshes.”
“Very troublesome, I should say. But you’d be doing me a favor by
bringing me up to date.”
Belsenza’s eyes went vague and he clasped his hands like a man
attempting to muster his fleeting impressions—like the dreamer who, in
order to tell his dream, lets himself drift into a mimicry of the sleeper. “It

was wrong of me to speak of rumors, and right to speak of fevers. In a
sense, it’s less than nothing. The fever by itself is nothing, it’s merely a sign
... Don’t think me delirious—along with all these others ... I live here, you
see, and it’s difficult to make oneself understood. I myself understand, I
understand all the better for seeing you. Because I wanted to speak to you
this evening. You’re not from Maremma, and talking to you—whether you
believe me or not—is like opening the window of a sickroom. No one
breathes properly in Maremma—we need air—that’s the word for it: we’re
looking for a breath of air.”
“People seem to come here a good deal, for a sickroom.”
Belsenza’s eloquent features called me to witness: “Pure extravagance,
my dear Observer. Everyone is mad in Orsenna, raving mad ... Hear me out,
please hear me out.” he interrupted himself, aware of my impatience.
“About a year ago, the thing began—that is,” he corrected himself, “I began
noticing something. Hereabouts no one had a word to say about Farghestan,
I assure you. It was as if the place had never existed. Erased from the map
... There were other problems. Life’s not easy here, and despite
appearances, no one’s very comfortable ... I’ll show you the town,” he
added, gesturing contemptuously toward the other salons, “it’s not all so
sumptuous as the Aldobrandi palace.”
“I know. There was a moon tonight.”
“Ah! Then you saw. Although by night, you know ... there’s always the
picturesque aspect that beguiles everyone. These people prefer to wander
