through the town by night. But it occurs to me ...” he broke off, reassuring
me with a wave of his hand. “Nowadays people talk about the Other Side;
they’re beginning to know things.”
“The Other Side?”
“I was forgetting, you’re not from around here. Habits are contagious,
after a while. No one takes precautions any more. It’s very seldom you’ll
hear the word “Farghestan” in Maremma. Never, in fact. People say: ‘the
Other Side.’”

“Odd. From a certain point of view, you wouldn’t suspect so much
familiarity.”
“From a certain point of view, you wouldn’t suspect anything, but here
people suspect a great deal. At least, that’s what I’d like to believe. It would
be more reassuring. People might say ...”
“Just what is it they do say?” This time I was really annoyed. Belsenza
grew perfectly still, and his eyebrows drew together, as if at a difficult
question.
“My dear Observer, it was you who started it ... I like things to be black
and white myself. But as soon as I begin a report, the pen falls from my
fingers. Once you try to take matters in hand, they immediately assume a
new form. As if they were afraid of being caught, of being checked ... as if
people were afraid they couldn’t get away. Or that the rumors might stop
...” Belsenza made a face, irritated and mocking. “It all comes down to ... if
you want to bring it down ... to very little, to nothing at all. To less than
nothing. To this, more or less: there seem to have been some major changes
in Farghestan. Someone, or rather something, has taken over. And—on this
point there’s universal and energetic agreement—this someone, this
something ... this change ... bodes nothing good for Orsenna.”
“Rumors! ... Come now. That’s pure fantasy. An invention!”
Belsenza stared at me challengingly. “I’d tend to think it was, as you do
... But I can also assure you that proving it was—which would be even
better than saying so—will be of no help in making it stop.”
“You could issue an official denial.”
“I’ve thought of that ... No, believe me, it’s too late. There’s a smoldering
fire here. Anything can become fuel for it. An official denial will just feed
the rumors. It’s a question of temperature.”
“Who’s doing the talking?”
“Today, everyone. At first, it seems to me,” Belsenza lowered his voice,
“it was mostly outsiders. Of course I’m forgetting,” he went on rapidly,
“here, ‘outsiders’ are people from Orsenna. And when you ask ‘who’s doing

the talking,’ believe me, you must realize, no one actually talks. No one
talks at all. It’s more a matter of allusions, people talk by omission. It’s
what they don’t say—nothing positive. Everything remains indirect,
wrapped up in something else. Everything refers to the rumors, but nothing
gives them away. As if words, all the words spoken on a single day,
managed to create a mold—the mold of something—but the mold itself
remains empty. I’m not making myself clear. Let me use another figure.
You know the game of Hunt the Slipper? Everyone makes a circle, you all
hold the string, you don’t see anything, but hands are accomplices and the
slipper runs along the string, from hand to hand. It’s never there. Each hand
is empty, but each hand is merely a warm hollow to receive it—to have
received it. That’s the game Maremma plays all day long. And I’m not sure
it’s a game ... No, a denial would be no use at all,” Belsenza concluded
pensively. “You’d have to cut the string, but first of all you’d have to find
it.”
“Find the string?”
“Find the string the slipper hangs on ...” Belsenza smiled with a
preoccupied expression. I said nothing for a moment, and I didn’t feel like
smiling at all. This kind of talk was less incoherent to me than I should have
liked it to be.
“I understand. But even without that, sometimes you do manage to find
the slipper. Haven’t you arrested anyone?”
“No. Any more than I’ve issued a formal denial. For the same reasons.
Besides ...” Belsenza glanced cautiously around the rooms. “... I have
neither support nor credit in Orsenna, and it’s easy to end up with a nasty
business on your hands.”
I felt that my voice was trembling: “I’m in the Signory’s service,
Belsenza, just like you, and my friendships are not my first consideration.
Suppose you be a little more specific. Are you afraid that the string would
lead you here?”
“Perhaps.”
“Is that an impression or a fact?”

Belsenza’s voice rang out with an accent of frankness. “An impression.
Everything’s an impression, I’ve just told you that. Maybe I was wrong to
speak. It’s possible I’m getting a little overwrought ...”
“I don’t see too much harm in that. Maremma isn’t much of a town for ...
distractions. It might be a remedy for boredom.”
“I hope so, my dear Observer.” The voice was resuming its official and
neutral tone, and I felt that the keyhole to which I was pressing my eye was
about to be closed.
“One thing you said just now really astonished me—unless you’ve made
a miscalculation. When you were discussing the so-called coup d’état on
the Other Side, you said: someone ... or something.”
“Exactly. It’s no miscalculation. That’s one more odd thing ...” Belsenza
seemed to hesitate in front of an obstacle. “... You’re very close to seeing,
my dear Observer, how useless any denial would be. It’s as if the rumors, as
soon as they appear, were made immune to any material proof whatever.
That expression coup d’état is anything but accurate. According to the
rumors, nothing on the Other Side has happened that’s at all visible.
Nothing has changed that you could notice. It’s even been noted that
nothing has changed in the appearances—that’s why there’s something
really disturbing about these rumors. The notion—insofar as one can see
clearly enough to arrive at any such thing as a notion—the notion would be
that a kind of occult power, let’s say a secret society, whose purposes are
anything but specific but certainly exorbitant, inadmissible, has managed to
take over the country, to keep it in thrall, to rule without anything giving it
away in all the government cogs and gears ...”
“Excessively imaginative. You’re not trying to tell me that anyone here
believes such stories ... ?”
“It sounds incredible enough, I grant you, but please let me point out one
thing. I mentioned fever just now, and disease. The effects of the disease are
strange. I’ve had occasion, in Orsenna, to conduct certain investigations
concerning charlatans and quacks. And of the crudest kind. Believe me, it

was rare that they failed to number among their clientele some of the most
remarkable, the most enlightened people of the city. I could cite names ...”
I was not interested in hearing them. Again, there was an insinuation in
Belsenza’s voice which I found disagreeable. “I don’t see any major victims
around us here.”
Belsenza glanced speculatively at the groups in the room, and I suddenly
thought of Marino. “Neither do I. All the same ...” He drew closer, with a
nervous gesture. “... It so happens, my dear Observer, that I know this town
like the back of my hand. In a sense, it’s always the same ... I rub my eyes
and there’s nothing to be seen. Everything is where it always was. And yet
something’s changed. Something ...” Again his eyes went vague and
seemed to drift away. “There’s something that’s not right.”
Belsenza’s helpless manner and the anxiety that filled his voice disturbed
me. The visit to Sagra suddenly came to mind. “In any case, that’s your
business. But I can’t believe that all these rumors are born ... spontaneously.
And it’s their source that interests me. You must have wondered, surely, if
someone in Maremma wasn’t, one way or another, in relation with
Farghestan?”
Belsenza’s face became the picture of consternation, and I suddenly felt
certain I had said something stupid.
“In relation ... ? That’s hardly possible.”
My mood carried me away. “You mean it’s forbidden. That’s not the
same thing.”
A singular expression appeared on Belsenza’s face: that of a man
shocked and deeply scandalized, whom concern for appearances
nonetheless keeps from retorting. I suddenly felt he was dealing with me
like a foreigner whom, by the gestures of mute embarrassment, one tries to
spare the consciousness of a breach of decorum too subtle to explain.
“Absolutely impossible, I should say.” Belsenza coughed and met my
eyes with a frozen smile. “... You know that better than I do, my dear
Observer. Your very presence at the Admiralty would make any
interrogation in that direction a matter of insult.”

“Sorry. In that case, I no longer see the point of these confidences.”
Once again Belsenza’s tone became jocular and worldly, and I felt he was
breaking off, this time for good. Throughout the whole of this conversation
in suspicious feints, he had been the infuriating silhouette which appears in
the arena and then exasperatingly vanishes behind a shred of red cloth.
“Oh, it was merely a conversation, nothing official, I assure you. Of
course there was nothing about the Service involved. I was quite certain the
Admiralty disdained such frivolities. Now I’m convinced, and there’s no
more to be said.”
The party’s tension was waning now; the fever had fallen altogether.
Belsenza’s remarks had slid over my mind, leaving me distracted rather
than shaken, like the meaningless gesticulation of a distant hunter firing a
shot before the sound reaches you. The indistinct voices mingled, making
what sounded to me like the same endlessly resumed and interrupted noises
as the waters of the lagoon. I felt like their hostile shore. I stood in the heart
of this crowd like an intruder who has not been given the password, and
who feels each face turning toward his own an intolerable interrogation.
Belsenza’s cautious and muted voice, as if someone were lowering the
lights, had dimmed the party’s luster for me; it was time to find Vanessa.
As I left the noise of the crowd and the bright lights, the upper gallery
seemed to be sound asleep. Ahead of me, a tiled corridor vanished into the
silent gloom; through the high midnight-blue windows open onto the
lagoon, the links of moonlight reflected from the water stirred over the
vaults like a faint murmur of brightness. I leaned a moment on one of these
open sills. The night was as calm as if someone had raised a lamp. In front
of me, in a just-visible distance, the thin white line of the waves breaking
on the sandbar marked the entrance to the channel through the lagoons. The
faint oscillation of the reflections on the walls, the luminous streaks which
intersected on the surface of the water, the tense silence in the darkness of
this somnolent bridge over a deep and confused agitation, reminded me of
the night on the Redoubtable, suggesting the notion of getting under way, of
a cruise, lights out, into the darkness. The palace was keeping vigil for

Maremma in this sleepy gloom. Already far away on the road, Marino’s
carriage was moving like a tiny star through this empty night which dilated
one’s gaze. Sounds were muffled in the town, and Belsenza was returning to
his feverish rooms. I recalled, smiling, how uneasily he had indicated the
palace as the source of those obscure rumors; I thought of Vanessa’s ironic
promise at the Admiralty, and I pushed open her door with a nervous finger.
Her apartment filled the entire width of the palace wing facing the sea. It
was an enormous room which open windows on three sides filled with the
faint rustle of the lagoon below. A single corner emerged from the dim
light, but I was struck as I came in, despite the Oriental glow of the carpets
and the richness of the marble facings, by a particular sense of dilapidation.
In this vast room made to the measure of a forgotten life, the existence that
had occupied it seemed to shrink, to float, as if in too large a garment. A
pool of emptiness spread in the middle of a room; like a cargo shifted by
successive pitching and tossing within a giant hull, the scattered pieces of
furniture sought a timid refuge against the walls.
“Marino left quite early this evening. Did he have some work to do at the
Admiralty? Come and sit down over here. Don’t be afraid,” Vanessa added,
laughing at my hesitation to cross the empty room.
I sat down facing her, intimidated. The veiled lamp left her almost
completely in shadow, stretched out on a low couch. The unexpected echoes
of these walls disconcerted me, belying the precarious intimacy of this
alcove lighting, of these deep warm cushions. The bare spaces of the room
behind me made me feel uncomfortable, as if an empty theater were
weighing on my shoulders.
“No, certainly not. The captain’s comings and goings seem to concern
you to excess.”
How nervous Vanessa seemed, as if she had been thwarted! “You didn’t
say anything, did you? I mean, about your visit to Sagra.”
“No, of course not. What a notion! As a matter of fact, I hope to be
rewarded for my nobility of spirit. All the same, back at the Admiralty just
now, you were a little too discreet. I see no reason for such carryings-on.”

Vanessa remained serious. “I should be very upset if Marino discovers
that boat.”
“Is it an important secret?”
Vanessa shrugged sullenly, her expression preoccupied. “Childishness.
But that’s not how Marino would see it.”
“He might have good reasons. I seem to have seen that boat myself,
maneuvering quite a ways from Sagra. Under conditions which were
anything but legitimate, to say the least.”
Vanessa glanced at me, her expression less alarmed than inquisitive.
“And what did you conclude?”
“I informed Marino what I had seen. We went on patrol the next night.
And luckily for you, as I must tell you now, we found nothing.”
Vanessa lowered her eyes. “There’s no ban on nautical excursions. Those
statutes are ridiculous. Maremma’s becoming a popular coast, and the
Admiralty might just as well close its eyes now and then. It’s time Marino
understood.”
“Suppose you try to convince him.”
Vanessa hesitated a second, choosing her words. “The captain is a man I
esteem. But he’s not very ... intelligent.”
“Certainly he’s intelligent enough to understand such pleasures. He’s a
sailor. Perhaps a direct approach—a frank appeal ...”
Vanessa frowned slightly and shot me a serious glance. “I’m not enjoying
your melodramatic insinuations, Aldo. Do you have some notion that
Maremma is a smuggler’s hideout?”
“No.” It was my turn to meet her eyes. “But if you want to know, I find it
an odd sort of playground, to say the least. And I suppose that Marino is
wondering just as much as I am what you’ve come here to do.”
There was a moment of silence, and I suddenly realized, from my own
sense of anxious deliverance, how much weight I had put into my question.
Vanessa abandoned her studied persiflage and turned her face toward the
open window. “What I’ve come here to do? Nothing at all, Aldo, I promise

you. I was tired of Orsenna. We had this old ruin here, so I came. I’ve
stayed longer than I expected. That’s all there is to it.”
There was an accent of incredulous sincerity in her voice: every word
told the truth, but as a dreamer tells his dream.
“You find these sands so ... diverting?”
“I never see the sands. I never leave this place.” Vanessa turned toward
me. Her toneless, half-whispering voice was like the passionate murmur of
the darkness. “I’m waiting.”
“You’re talking in riddles, Vanessa. Don’t you want to tell me ...?” I felt
oddly stirred. In spite of myself, I could hear in my voice the nuance of
gentle consideration one uses at a sickbed. Vanessa’s voice, in turn, fell on
my ears with a tender confidence.
“It’s hard to make you understand, Aldo. Something’s going to happen,
I’m sure of that. Things can’t go on this way. I came back to Orsenna. You
know I was away a long time. I saw people, streets, houses. And I had a
shock. It was like seeing someone again after years and years and suddenly
realizing, clear as day, that he has death in his face. People around you
laugh and go through the motions—coming and going as if nothing were
happening ... But you see, and you know. All by yourself. And you’re
afraid.”
“Sometimes you resign yourself.”
Vanessa’s eyes challenged me. “I haven’t changed. I hate Orsenna, as you
know. I hate its complacency, its discretion, its comfort, its sleep. But I live
on it, too. And I’ve been frightened.” Vanessa became pensive. “It’s a long
time now that my people have been in this city. It’s got their claws and their
fangs in its flesh. For the first time, Aldo, I’ve seen the end, and I’m dizzy
from what I’ve seen. I’ve been thinking about the worm that finally devours
its apple—I realize that the apple won’t last forever.”
“And you’ve come to Maremma to ... reflect. Nothing more than that?”
“I don’t see what you mean.”

“Reflection isn’t the only thing being done in Maremma, it appears.
People talk. In fact, it appears people talk a good deal, Vanessa.”
“Belsenza was eager to meet you, I noticed. And I see you haven’t lost
sight of your professional obligations,” Vanessa snapped, with a certain
mockery in her glance.
“Belsenza seems quite surprised such gossip is welcomed here. And if
that’s true, he’s quite right to be surprised. You should put a stop to it.”
Vanessa’s face went blank. “I have no means of putting a stop to such
rumors. And I have no desire to.”
“Be careful, Vanessa. Belsenza has his suspicions. One of these days the
Signory will take an interest in this ... childishness. It is suspicious, as you
have good reason to know. The Aldobrandi palace is not an inn, and what
would be gossip elsewhere might suggest something more serious here.”
“You don’t understand, Aldo. In this business, everyone’s an accomplice.
Belsenza first of all—who condemns what he hears but is so eager to tell
you about it.”
“Vanessa, be honest with me ... What’s behind this business?”
“I don’t know a thing about it—I don’t even try to know. What interests
me is not what’s behind it ... but what’s ahead.”
“Ahead? Either I don’t know the Signory, or there’s going to be a
roundup of certain babblers.”
Eyes half closed, Vanessa turned away. “No. I want you to understand ...
In summer, it’s very hot along the beaches of Maremma. There are days
when the air grows so still, so heavy, that it becomes impossible to breathe.
It’s stifling. Then, in the middle of the afternoon, during this sun-drenched
calm, you see suddenly forming over the dunes these little, tiny whirlwinds.
The dust rises up in cones, suddenly a clump of weeds flies into the air, no
one knows why. Ten yards away you can’t feel a thing. Not one breath. It’s
as incongruous, as unexpected as ... as a sneeze. Behind, there comes a
storm. You can laugh at that grotesque cyclone. But it’s the cyclone which
understands best—it understands, because it’s whirling, that the air has

grown thinner and that there’s a sort of vacuum which has to be filled
somehow, anyhow ...”
“Light heads and empty minds, to begin with.”
Vanessa smiled ironically. “Wait. I’m not through with my allegory. If
stones think, they must find it ridiculous for the dust to dance in the air that
way, when there’s no wind.”
I began to smile, a little piqued—a forced smile. “Very seductive, that
image of yours. Have you given the captain the benefit of it?”
“Marino takes this business more seriously than you do.”
“Do you talk to him about it?”
“You’ve got it wrong, Aldo: Marino talks to me. I tell him what people
say around here, that’s all. He never gets tired of it—he could listen to me
for hours.”
“That doesn’t sound like him at all.”
“He’s come around. And you will too, Aldo. You’ll come around.”
Vanessa began toying with her belt buckle.
“Of course, Vanessa. I suppose I’ll have to make some sense out of this
business.” I smiled and gently took between mine a hand which remained
quite lifeless.
“I don’t think Marino comes here for information. I think he comes here
for his ... drug. He has to come. People come all the way from Orsenna, as
you’ve seen.”
“What drug are you talking about?”
“The same one, Aldo, that you keep looking for in that fortress where
there are so many maps. The captain doesn’t know why he keeps coming
back to Maremma. I could tell him. He comes back here because it’s
sickening to sleep too long, because when you sleep too heavily, you turn
over in bed looking for a cooler place, and because if you’re going to live
you need to realize, however vaguely, that the troops of Orsenna are not
doomed to harvest potatoes forever.” Vanessa’s voice fell silent, and there
was a gap. I felt an odd twinge in the region of my heart. In my mind’s eye I

saw the Admiralty ramparts, and I heard the summons their heights
addressed to the void. Now I wanted Vanessa’s voice to remain silent—too
many shadows were being dispelled. I was suddenly afraid of myself.
A low murmur came in through the windows, peopling the silence that
had fallen upon us and making the great empty room gradually come alive
around us. I felt the space behind me weighing on my shoulders; with a
nervous gesture I stood up and walked toward one of the open bays. The
moon had risen, and a dome of mist hovered over the lagoon. On the sea
front, the nearest facades of Maremma, whitish and close-set, emerged
faintly from the gloom. In the salons downstairs the music had stopped, and
a remote murmur immobilized these stone fronts. The sandbar closed off
the horizon with a black line; through the open channel, the scrolls of the
waves broke in phosphorescent landings of foam, huge stages which
seemed to collapse with theatrical shocks at the very heart of the night. A
solemn hissing rose from the sands, and like the fringe of the carpet
overhanging a dream staircase, blinding sheets of surf smoothed themselves
out at my feet over the dead waters.
I felt a faint touch on my shoulder, and even before turning my head I
knew that Vanessa’s hand was resting there. I remained motionless. The arm
brushing against me was feverish, and I realized that Vanessa was
frightened.
“Come back,” she said suddenly, her voice tense, “the night is cold.”
I turned back to the dark room. The wall opposite me seemed to float in
the diffuse pallor of the lagoon, and as though by a silhouette outlined
against a vague distance, my attention was immediately attracted by a
portrait which had been behind me when I came in and which now gave me
the sudden impression, by its almost indiscreet presence and an unexpected
and awkward sensation of proximity, to have suddenly emerged while I had
been distracted by this lunar surface. Though the painting remained very
dark and at first would have seemed no more than a vague shadow glimpsed
out of the corner of my eye, a violent sensation of the hitherto unseen made
me turn up the lights with the same brutal gesture with which one might

unmask a spy behind a hanging. And I suddenly realized what had been the
uneasiness weighing on me when I entered this room, a discomfort which
had persisted throughout my discussion with Vanessa. There had been a
third party between us. Like the gaze inevitably distracted by a sea vista or
a view of snowy peaks, two wide-open eyes appearing on the room’s bare
wall set the room afloat, reversing its perspective, taking it over as a captain
commands his ship.
I knew this famous work, one of the portraits Longhone was reputed to
have painted with that anguished jubilation characteristic of his final
manner and often materialized in the works of his old age by a faint squint
and a touch of distraction in the smile, so that some declare his masterpiece
to be this portrait—painted at the age of eighty—of the Podesta Orseolo. I
had often been mesmerized by an old copy of this work in Orsenna’s
Council Gallery, before which an age-old rite decreed that one must put on
one’s hat, in execration of the still-rankling memory of a traitor. It was the
portrait of Piero Aldobrandi, a defector from Orsenna who defended against
her besieging forces the Farghian fortresses of Rhages, and whose most
furious assault, in fact, the painting managed to evoke. But this time it was
the original which was before my eyes, as fresh, as scandalously intense as
the sheen of muscles under a fresh-peeled skin: the copy resembled the
work about as much as an agreeable nude resembles a body flayed alive.
The last wooded slopes of the Tängri, descending to the sea in gentle
lines, formed the painting’s background. The bird’s-eye view, naively steep,
truncated the mountain’s peak, whose converging lines from the lower
foothills nonetheless suggested the imminent and vital enormity of the
mass, as if the grasp of a giant paw had gripped the landscape from the edge
of the frame down to the sea. At the water’s edge, the sparkling afternoon
sunlight played over the amphitheater of houses and the town ramparts like
a sea-borne mirage. Rhages appeared, caught in the loving torpor of its
siesta, with all the indolent activity of its terraces, the mild somnambulism
of the tiny figures which ventured into its streets. A rich pelt of flames,
forming architectural scrolls, constituted a border for the besieged town.

The disturbing impression produced by this vision of a massacre derived
from the extraordinarily natural and even relaxed character which
Longhone’s serene cruelty had succeeded in imparting to his painting.
Rhages was burning the way a flower opens, without laceration and without
drama: rather than a conflagration, it seemed the peaceful unfurling, the
tranquil voracity of a greedier vegetation, a burning bush outlining and
crowning the town, the opening scroll of a rose around the swarm of insects
at its heart. Orsenna’s fleet was ranged in a semicircle in the town’s harbor,
but the wall of calm smoke rising in heavy puffs from the sea suggested not
so much the deafening fracas of artillery as some picturesque and visitable
cataclysm, the volcano of the Tängri once again dousing its lava flows in
the sea.
All that mere dissociation can communicate of what is cynically natural
to the spectacle of war somehow exalted the unforgettable smile on the face
which thrust like a clenched fist from within the canvas and seemed to burst
through the painting’s foreground. Piero Aldobrandi, unhelmeted, was
wearing the black cuirass and the red commander’s scarf and carrying the
baton which linked him forever to this scene of carnage. But the figure,
turning its back to the spectacle, relegated it to the mere status of landscape,
and the face, strained by a secret vision, was the emblem of a supernatural
detachment. The half-closed eyes, with their strange inward gaze, floated in
an immeasurable ecstasy; a wind from farther away than the sea stirred
those locks and reanimated the entire countenance with a fierce chastity.
The arm in its polished steel sheath, bright with reflected luster, raised the
hand to the face in a gesture of great concentration. Between the fingertips
of his gauntlet with its hard chitinous carapace, its cruel and elegant
insectlike articulations, in a gesture of perverse and almost amorous grace,
as though his nostrils were breathing in a drop of ultimate attar, ears deaf to
the cannons’ thunder, Piero Aldobrandi was crushing a blood-red, fleshy
flower, the red rose of Orsenna.
The room floated away. My eyes were fixed on that face rising out of the
armor’s sharp collar with the phosphorescence of the hydra’s newly severed

head, like the blinding ostension of a black sun. Its light ascended above a
nameless distance of faraway life, devising in me something like a dark and
promised dawn.
“That’s Piero Aldobrandi,” Vanessa murmured, as if to herself. “Didn’t
you know he was in Maremma?” And in a different tone of voice she
added: “Don’t you love him? It’s really a wonder, that thing. You feel as if
you were living under his very eyes.”
OceanofPDF.com

6
A Bout of Fever
There are certain privileged mornings in life when the omen appears, when
upon waking we hear, during a long aimless stroll, the resonance of a
deeper note as we linger, uncertain of our feelings, toying one by one with
the familiar objects in our room just before setting out on a significant
journey. Something like a faraway warning reaches us in the morning
brightness quicker with admonitions than any dream; perhaps it is the noise
of a single footstep on the paving stones, or the first cry of a bird faintly
heard in our dissolving sleep; but that echo of footsteps wakens a cathedral
reverberation in the soul, that cry resonates as if across the broadest
beaches, and our ear strains through the silence toward a void in ourselves
which suddenly has no more echo than the sea. Our soul has been purged of
its murmurs, of the uproar that dwells there; a fundamental note sounds and
rejoices in the newly wakened capacity, which is no more than its own. In
the intimate measure of life which is granted us, we are reborn to our
strength and to our joy, yet sometimes this note is a deep and solemn one, a
resonance that surprises us like the footsteps of someone walking through a
grotto, rousing echoes; it is because a breach has been opened during our
sleep, a barrier has collapsed beneath the pressure of our dreams, and now
we must live for days on end as though in a familiar room whose door
swings ajar, suddenly, upon a cavern.
It was in this state of unprovoked awareness that I wakened the next day
at Maremma. The lagoon was still somnolent—as if the whole town had
respectfully arranged its hours according to the prolonged sleep of the
palace. The sun was scorching the empty canals and the lifeless beaches as
if they were no more than an arid stretch of salt flats, making the laundry
strung from the windows of the poor quarters glisten with untoward
brilliance. A fisherman’s boat, on the deserted waters, drifted silently

toward the channel. The sound of voices came from Vanessa’s salon,
muffled by the distance; their distinct yet incomprehensible murmur
mingled with my dreams of the night, rejoined that faraway thunder I had
felt impending in the earlier conversation with Belsenza. People were
already talking in Maremma; throughout the sleeping city awoke, with these
muted voices, the faint throb of fever I felt beating now in my own wrists.
I got up to take my leave. There were already many people in Vanessa’s
apartment, but as soon as I pushed open the door a wave of silence seemed
to break before me. I felt uncomfortable. A sleepless night and the raw light
stripped the faces bare: despite the elegance of their clothes and their
smiles, the crowded salon at this unaccustomed hour suggested the
vigilance and uncertainty of an improvised camp, the arrival of a group of
refugees at dawn. Just as I was about to leave, Vanessa, with a swift gesture,
drew me aside. “I’m leaving for Orsenna tomorrow ... I’ll be back at the end
of the month. As soon as I get here, I’ll send for you, Aldo. Only this time
you’ll come here first thing in the morning. At dawn ...” And in a lower
voice she added: “We’ll have a long way to go.”
“It’s an expedition?”
“Yes and no. In any case, a surprise, I hope. I’ll let you know the minute
I’m back.”
Her rather feverish voice seemed to insist on confidence, and I suddenly
thought of Marino with a certain embarrassment: “Should I inform the
captain?”
Vanessa seemed annoyed. “Come alone. You have business in Maremma
—that’s all.”
An accident on the road delayed my return. The Admiralty, when I
reached it after lunchtime, seemed abandoned, every door and window shut
in the late summer glare. A desultory hammering from the sheds made the
shuddering heat even harsher on the stones. My room overlooking the torrid
plain seemed uninhabitable, and I took refuge in the cool office I sometimes
worked in, next to Marino’s: some mail had been left for me, and I began
leafing indifferently through several official notifications. In the deathly

silence, the scratching of my pen was all that opposed the faint hum of the
flies. I suddenly felt overcome by sleep; I flung myself on a cot and
immediately lost consciousness.
I wakened with a heavy head. The sun’s rays had barely shifted on the
red tiles. Someone was talking in the next room. The monotonous voice was
taking up the thread, linking me to the morning’s awakening as to a dream
interspersed with insomnias, and I reluctantly forced myself back to work,
still longing to sleep some more. Yet the voices continued murmuring
through the door, gently tireless, with the singsong, insipid calm of a
peasant argument. I now recognized Marino’s voice, whose delivery he
could retard as if on stage, and I tried with some amusement to follow, by
its mere inflections, the vicissitudes of a dispute whose subject I could more
or less make out. There was no doubt about it: that was his “farm contracts”
voice, the one Fabrizio could imitate so well. The captain’s heavy tread
rang out on the tiles, and the door opened.
“Ah! You’re awake now, Aldo. You had a short night, I see...” There was
no real malice in Marino’s wink—he seemed preoccupied. “... come and
help me out. We’ve got problems.”
In the captain’s office, Beppo, one of the foremen of our farm crews, was
tugging at the ribbons on his cap with an embarrassed look. “Do you know
what Beppo’s come to tell us?” Marino rasped incredulously. “Ortello’s
refusing to take our men back.”
Still not wide awake, I glanced up at Beppo. The news was strange, and
the setback a considerable one. Ortello was one of the largest farms in
Syrtes and supplied the Admiralty with its oldest and most loyal clientele.
Famous in Syrtes for its great hunts and its luxurious hospitality, this farm
was in a sense Marino’s firstborn and the light of his eye—he regarded
himself as something of a patriarch, the founding father of these forsaken
fields; the place had flourished thanks to him, and he set great store by it: it
seemed, when he mentioned its name, as if he had cultivated it with his own
hands.
“What’s the trouble?”

“He’ll tell you himself,” Marino snapped, obviously irritated. “Damned if
I can make anything out of it.”
Beppo coughed to clear his throat, and I could tell that Marino’s
reception had been a cold one. “The captain doesn’t want to believe me, but
they told me to say they had no complaints about the men, about the work
... It’s circumstances, that’s what they kept saying.”
“You have your own axe to grind. Circumstances!” Marino exclaimed.
“What the hell does that mean? What’s changed, I’m asking you?”
Beppo’s discouraged voice obviously had no hope of being believed. “It’s
not that, Captain ... They say they can’t advance two years’ salary—they
say they can’t commit themselves so far ahead.”
“Why not—do they want to sell the land?”
Beppo clutched at this straw, and it was clear he knew he would be
pleasing Marino: “No, it’s not that, Captain. Not land like that. They’ve just
mended the roads, and last year they put in olive trees all along the dunes.”
“Then where the hell do they expect to find men to work the farm?”
“Well, as far as that goes, Captain ...” Beppo’s tone became pathetic once
more. “They say they can manage ...”
“There’s something behind this,” Marino grumbled, looking Beppo in the
eyes. “You’ve done something stupid, that’s what it is.”
“I swear, Captain,” Beppo gasped, on the verge of tears.
Suddenly Beppo interested me: something had come into his voice, for
all its confusion, that reminded me of Belsenza. Marino’s cold rage was
obviously paralyzing him, and I realized he had not said all he could. I
made my voice sound as sympathetic as possible: “You don’t have any idea
what they mean when they use a word like ‘circumstances’?”
Beppo clung to me as if I were a life-buoy. “Not exactly, sir. They’re old
men, you know—they chew up their words between their teeth as if they
know things they don’t want to tell.” Beppo frowned, in an effort to think
out what he meant. “They say the times are uncertain, that’s what they keep
saying.”

“Uncertain?”
“They say there’s something new coming, and that they can’t make
contracts in advance.”
“Which means?” Marino’s voice was trembling.
“Something new, Captain—something bad: war, in other words. That’s
what they’re saying now.”
Beppo’s voice dropped as though acknowledging some shameful disease.
There was a moment’s heavy silence. I was trying to put a good face on
matters. Marino’s gaze, when I turned to look, was alarming. Yet his voice
sounded very much in control, and at that moment I admired him: “All
right. Carlo’s getting old, that’s what it is. You can go now, Beppo. I’ll visit
Ortello myself and settle matters there.”
Beppo’s departure exposed me. Marino, head down, hands behind his
back, walked up and down, his expression preoccupied. The silence became
so suffocating that quite mechanically I went over and opened the window.
The blank boredom of the late afternoon filled the room like an odor. The
sound of footsteps stopped, and Marino’s voice rose behind me, strangely
gentle, like the voice of a critically wounded man. “This is a bad business,
Aldo. I don’t like it.”
I shrugged as casually as possible. “I don’t think it’s anything serious.
Carlo will change his mind. I don’t see how Ortello can do without our
men.”
“You don’t?” So abruptly dependent, he seemed older; his almost
breathless voice seized me like a hand. “... That’s what worries me,” he
went on wearily, as if talking to himself. “They can’t do without us, and
they know it.”
“You should have a look. They all swear by you—you’re the only one
they trust.”
Suddenly I wanted him far away from me, the way you want to get out of
a sickroom. He seemed to be waiting for my dismissal: “Yes, you’re right.
I’ll go there right away ...” Suddenly he stopped, with a hesitant look. “I

meant to tell you, Aldo ...” He seemed almost embarrassed. “Well, it’s your
business, you’ll do as you please. You heard what Beppo said just now.
There may be something in it that concerns you.”
“I think there is.”
Marino seemed relieved. Out the window, I watched his horse a moment
as the captain rode along the causeway through the lagoons, a slender black
silhouette against the flat horizon; and it seemed to me that a gust of air
reached me over the lagoons. I almost ran to my room, temples throbbing
with a sickly exaltation. Between Marino’s weakness and his return, I was
eager to put something irreparable.
An hour later, when I had finished rereading, before sealing it, my report
to Orsenna, I set the signed page on my table and opened my window
overlooking the plain already darkened by the lengthening shadow of the
fortress; as if the chill rising from the ground had disintoxicated me, I stood
a moment with my forehead pressed against the cold pane, and for the first
time I felt creeping into my exaltation a touch of fear.
The report itself was irreproachable, and as I reread it with a head that
could not keep quite cool, I had to concede that it was a model of clarity
and moderation. Belsenza’s remarks had effortlessly come to mind, down to
their last details, and his very evasions had been transcribed as though of
their own accord and with considerable skill. And yet I remained disturbed,
less perhaps by the harmless content of these banal pages than by the
impression I retained of the abnormal ease with which they had been
written. This impression resembled that of a virtuoso who recovers from a
long sickness and feels his fingers escaping him, flying of their own free
will over the familiar keyboard. The Admiralty carriage stopped under my
window—it was mail time; I hurriedly sealed the envelope, and for a long
while I watched the carriage jolting over the causeway toward Maremma,
under a dull sky. The air was no longer so hot, and the late afternoon was
quite gray. I felt as light and empty as if I had been delivered of a child.
That evening, Marino returned very late, and we waited for him a long
time over our already empty glasses, around the dinner table. Conversation

was awkward in the darkening room, and there were long intervals of
silence it was difficult to break. One glass, still full, in front of Marino’s
chair drew our glances in spite of ourselves, like a rejected offering to the
genius of the place: here where he was not present, the wilderness came in
through the open windows. The sound of his horse’s hooves over the plain
roused us, sending round the room the undulation of a gentle flame. Marino
came in without a word and sat down, his forefinger mechanically checking
the buttons of his service jacket; this was a recognizable sign: I knew that
the transactions had gone badly. It seemed to me that the lights suddenly
lowered, and I felt a kind of tightening at my temples: something was about
to begin.
The dinner, that evening, was over very quickly. I couldn’t take my eyes
off the captain. There was a sudden and enormous fatigue in his gestures. I
noticed that he was breathing with difficulty, and seemed to be trying to
catch my attention more often than usual. Those eyes of his addressed me in
a kind of solitude, and when they met mine they momentarily shook off
their heavy mist of weariness. At that instant, I felt that Marino was
hesitating and I felt, too, that it was already too late: Fabrizio, Roberto, and
Giovanni had stopped talking, one after the other, so that gradually a
complete silence fell round the table, and in the vacuum of this greedy
silence, the news was already exploding.
When the five of us were alone, Marino lit his cigar with a sudden
gesture, mumbling behind the flame of his match: “Did you tell them,
Aldo?”
“Not a word ... You were still hoping to settle things,” I added with a
touch of cruelty.
Marino’s hand made a gesture of resigned impotence, but he suddenly
looked up, and his blue-gray eyes confronted me resolutely. His voice rose
clear and calm, addressing us all, and I noticed once again that remarkable
half-concealed authority which he exerted so rarely. “I’d like all of you to
stay here a minute. A difficulty has arisen at the Admiralty, and now is the

time to share it with you ... Fabrizio, will you close that window? We need
to be quite alone.”
Fabrizio stood up with clownish solemnity, the sense of occasion like a
sun on his face. The captain used to enjoy teasing him after our dinners. “To
hear is to obey, Captain. We don’t have a council of war every day.” The
expression fell in a sudden silence. The corner of Marino’s mouth began
trembling.
“Your commentary is unnecessary, and stupid besides ...” Fabrizio
immediately turned bright red and slid back into his chair without a sound.
One or two embarrassed coughs were audible. Marino explained the Ortello
business in a few words. There was no longer any question of “rumors,”
and the reasons for breaking the contract remained unspecified. Marino let
it be understood that the Admiralty crew had not given satisfaction. As he
spoke, I noticed that he was staring at me in a hostile, almost provocative
manner: his voice was formally warning me, accentuating syllable after
syllable of the definitively adopted official version. He ended by a swift
allusion to the conviction he had acquired this afternoon that any further
negotiations were futile. The narrative had been summary and abrupt;
evidently Marino, refusing to acknowledge the astonishment apparent on
every face, was eager to get it over with. “And now, it’s a matter of deciding
what we’re going to do ...”
He raised his head in a sudden gesture which disposed of any
commentary on the incident. Roberto sucked on his cigar with a
preoccupied expression, his eyes fixed on the window. It was growing very
dark. The vague mass of the fortress facing us swelled in the drapery of
mists it was gathering round itself. “How many men did we have at Ortello,
Captain?”
“Eighty ... Eighty-two with Beppo and Mario.”
“And it’s impossible to assign them to another farm?”
Fabrizio made a timid gesture indicating that he wanted to say
something. With an irritated thrust of his chin, Marino acknowledged him.
“It won’t be easy. I happened to be in Gronzo yesterday for the payrolls,

Captain. At first I thought it didn’t mean anything, but it was the same there
—they’re talking about sending our men back next year.”
Giovanni squinted a little. “All the same, it’s odd.”
Marino’s eyes shifted, seeking from one man after another a response
which did not come. The room gradually grew quite dark, and for the first
time everyone felt that this motionless twilight had been accompanied by a
certain anxiety. Marino once again broke the silence, his voice quite dry:
“That’s not the question. Whatever happens, we have to find some way to
use the men we have. They’ll be on our hands from tomorrow. And Orsenna
won’t let us feed them for doing nothing ... It seemed to me you had an
idea, Roberto ...”
Marino’s voice softened, seeking support. Roberto was an Admiralty
veteran, and the Captain enjoyed his slow, heavy mind, benumbed by nights
of patient waiting which reassured him, buttressing his massive calm.
“Maybe. I was thinking that if we wanted, there could be plenty of work
right here.”
“Here at the Admiralty?”
Roberto’s voice grew stronger, taking audible satisfaction in the
obviousness of common sense. “At the Admiralty.” He gestured toward the
window. “You wouldn’t say that this place does us much credit, would you?
It’s falling to rack and ruin ... Our men have been farmers for years ... they
might just as well become masons.”
“You want to repair the fortress?” A note of shrillness had sounded in
Marino’s voice, an uncontrollable vibration which a contraction of his
throat cut short, but so perceptible, so indicative of an inner panic that even
Roberto was alerted to it and remained speechless for a second.
“Repair ... may be saying too much. It’s a huge job, and we don’t really
have the means to do it. But we could clean the place up. It was once a
famous building,” Roberto added, staring out the window again, “now it
doesn’t even seem to have been built by human hands. A jungle, that’s what
it looks like—something in the bush.”

A tide of warm approval seemed to rise in the room—eyes gleamed.
Roberto’s clumsy words ignited the air. “Yes, it’s demoralizing for the men.
We can hardly take ourselves seriously living in this rubble ... You might as
well pitch your tents in the streets of Sagra and begin excavating,” Giovanni
added jokingly.
“Trust me with the Ortello crew, Captain.” Fabrizio stood up, very
excited. “In two months I can promise you a brand-new fortress. And the
cannons positively gleaming.”
There was no mistaking it: a little storm had broken over the Admiralty, a
kind of intimate sedition. Marino’s eyes shifted from face to face, stupidly
docile to the alternating impact of voices, to this abrupt wakening of energy
—already in tow, reduced to a hopeless defensiveness. He sighed deeply
and chose his words carefully, eyes fixed on the table. “All well and good,
but you’re behaving like children! The fortress is abandoned, to all intents
and purposes, and the Signory won’t put up money for useless work.”
Faces darkened, suddenly hostile. Marino’s retort came too late: a ray of
light had slipped through the half-open door, and now shoulders were
pressing against it. “If we worked out the figures—accounted for
everything, it seems to me Orsenna would be likely to subsidize you.”
“It’s a question of upkeep—they’ll extend you credit for maintenance
projects.”
“You’ve saved enough for them, Captain. The fortress still has its
weapons, and the Signory should have that much respect for itself, after
all.” There was a low murmur of approval and wounded dignity, which
suddenly sounded almost comical. Marino glanced at me out of the corner
of his eye. With a gambler’s cold excitement, I watched all these marked
cards being trumped. Marino was holding his own hand close to his chest:
“Gentlemen ... gentlemen!” He rapped sharply on the table and silence fell
again. “It seems to me you’re letting yourselves be carried away. Orsenna is
watching us, hearing us too ...” he added, shooting me an indecipherable
glance. “Don’t forget that. Aldo may be our friend, but there are limits. And
it seems to me that his silence means something ...”

I felt with a pang that Marino was playing his last card. I stood up,
feeling the blood drain from my face. I was going to betray him again.
“Roberto’s proposal sounds quite reasonable to me. In any case, the Ortello
men are our responsibility. Orsenna can’t object to our putting them to good
use.”
I saw something flash in Marino’s eyes. He stood up with a sudden
gesture. “So be it. Do whatever you like, Fabrizio. I’ll look over the fortress
with you tomorrow.” With his heavy night watchman’s tread, he walked
slowly out of the room, hesitated on the threshold, one hand sketching a
gesture he left unfinished. His head sank lower than ever between his
shoulders; his eyes had grown quite lusterless. All of a sudden I
remembered how he had looked that evening in the map room, when he had
raised his lantern. In a gesture of virtually senile capitulation he nodded his
head, wretchedly. “These are big changes ...”
Everyone looked up, astonished, but the sentence was never finished. The
nodding continued quite mechanically. The gaze wandered without any
particular focus, strangely inward, like the eyes of a sick man lost among
the dim messages from his flesh. The captain pulled on his cap and strode
stiffly away.
From that day on, a remarkable change came over the captain. Something
was vacillating within him, something which touched the very roots of his
life. When he came in for evening meals and took off his heavy service
overcoat, his figure seemed to grow slighter every day, pared away ... Each
morning his immutable silhouette in his silent office was still framed in the
remoteness of that corridor protecting it from time, exorcizing it, the way its
stone casing protects a mummy within the stiff swaddling of eternity. But
the face quite terribly lived now, with a kind of lugubrious and mechanical
vivacity in which the mind took no part, all the features having become
strangely, involuntarily contractile in their strained immobility, as if they no
longer served any purpose but to amplify, to reinforce the exacerbated
vibrations of his sense of hearing. Heavier, denser than ever beneath the
hunched shoulders, his body’s mass loomed inert. Work seemed to continue

as usual, and the pile of papers stacked on his left in the morning and
reappearing on his right each evening, like an hourglass overturned,
remained the emblem of the unbroached time of these Admiralty days; but
his face, as though severed from the body and the active hands, was riddled
with twitches and autonomous tics. Marino was listening. An impulse, a
probing sting reached him from the depths of the fortress, wakened, shaken
now from morning to night by the shock of heavy boots. Marino’s eyes, by
daylight, maintained the blind stare of a dislodged mole. Sometimes, when I
was working beside him at his desk, I raised my eyes in spite of myself and
clandestinely stared at him, startled to receive the revelation of that sudden
and disquieting animality. No doubt about it: Marino had aged, and yet this
animality was not senility—it represented a degeneration of intelligence
only insofar as it now lived at a greater depth from the surface. Or rather, it
evoked the sagacious stupidity of total attention, and sometimes reminded
me of the fascinating expression granted to the features by a life amassed
round a profound organic questioning: that of a physician auscultating, of a
woman measuring her pregnancy, of some terrified creature brooding deep
in its den over the obscure harbinger of a typhoon or a tidal wave. We
realize, in this almost forbidden tension, that there enters, into the act of
merely looking on, something sacrilegious; an instinct warns us that the
spirit which here goes to ground in front of us keeps coming dangerously
close to certain forbidden centers where something stirs, just as a faint
wrinkle traced on Marino’s brow suddenly seemed to me to balance an
enormous pressure elsewhere: I hurriedly turned away and felt that my heart
was beating faster.
Meanwhile the Admiralty was emerging from its somnolence. On the
jetty of the little harbor, on the ramparts, on the plain, an unwonted
agitation now drove away the silence which returned only in the siesta
hours, a ritual in the climate of Syrtes, though we were already approaching
the winter season. Since there was no room to lodge the Ortello contingent
in the long-abandoned sheds which threatened to collapse at any moment,
Fabrizio had had some of the plain cleared behind the fortress; tents pitched
in straight lines and the regular rows of campfires smoking for the evening

meal evoked something stricter and more military than anything hitherto
visible at the Admiralty. Marino never ventured in the direction of what he
disdainfully called the gypsy camp, and the ironical tone with which he
taunted Fabrizio about his “refugees” made it quite clear that this undesired
reinforcement, which was the source of bitter associations, remained his
own responsibility, but the continual racket of clashing rifles and sentries’
cries, growing louder as they became accustomed to the open air, was a
delight to the rest of us; it had become the liveliest part of the Admiralty.
This encampment suddenly growing out of the ruin’s side like a weed was a
kind of unexpected rising of sap in these fields; the very fact that it was
provisional seemed to suggest a future, and when, after dinner, we would
stroll in spite of ourselves toward the plain where the drifting smoke from
the campfires glowing in the dark mixed with the early mists rising from the
lagoons, the happy voices calling to one another round the invisible tents
sounded an unexpected note, a note of freedom and savagery, like the noise
that floats over a mustered troop or an embarking ship, and suddenly we felt
an incipient intoxication of adventure mounting within us. Marino had not
been mistaken: there were great changes. Like a young tree whose roots
cling wherever they can find a purchase, this anarchic but vital cell drew on
every surface of the lapsed and worm-eaten machinery of the Admiralty,
and we could hear the creaks and cracks of the place, which troubled the
captain’s torpor. Almost every day there were new problems to be solved on
the spot: lack of supplies, camp materials to be bought at Maremma, needed
equipment which made Fabrizio fume, suddenly convinced as he was of his
own importance—actually minor problems, but around which every man
busied himself beyond the bounds of his authority with an excessive zeal
which included a share of playfulness and the intoxication of pure activity,
but which only showed all the more the need for fever that had infected the
Admiralty. Lunches and dinners at the fortress were now quite noisy with
projects and decisions, with figures of estimates and discussions of service,
which now and then made Marino shake his head with that mechanical
gesture of driving off a cloud of gnats; occasionally, after an overly
animated dinner, he would gently doze off at one end of the table, or

perhaps he was faking—at least I suspected him of it: that protective
somnolence helped him regain a patch of darkness beset by all-too-legible
figures. The same profound respect surrounded him which he had been able
to command previously, yet his calculated deliberation in deciding matters,
which invariably served him as a means of slowing things down, sometimes
provoked signs of impatience which were now no longer more than half
repressed: the accelerated rhythm of life at the Admiralty seemed to be
pushing him toward immobility, and he did not resist, perhaps saving his
strength for a remoter circumstance. I noticed that he tended to set up for
himself, in the regular course of events, certain kinds of short circuits which
left him out of the running: thus it happened that Fabrizio, in charge of
fortress repairs, would apply for supplies directly to Giovanni, who was
responsible for questions of materiel. Almost every evening, councils of
two or three were thus established at the end of the table, in half-whispers,
by which Marino was not deceived; his half-closed eyes, sliding toward me
with a strange little squint, sometimes registered his ironic amazement
confronted with such zeal and with the miracle that such complicated and
unaccustomed matters could be dealt with so rapidly. At these moments his
expression was that of a profound cunning whose implications escaped me
and might have disconcerted Marino himself, in that they suddenly assumed
a curiously impersonal quality: it seemed, sometimes, that the captain was
smiling for someone else, and it was a fact that this slightly cruel smile did
not seem his customary expression; it was as if something infinitely harder
and older had been substituted for his usual wink of complicity, and in the
slits of those suddenly ageless eyelids, the sharp and icy reflection was like
a burst of blood-chilling laughter.
It was as if boredom had vanished from the Admiralty. Fabrizio’s activity
worked wonders. He had taken to the task with passion, and he found in the
men, who had expected to fall fast asleep on the rotting straw of their
stables, that shock of exuberance and wild agitation evident in those who
have escaped ... Teams for dangerous jobs had a choice of volunteers who
suddenly recalled their masting skills, and on certain days it was as if a
troop of monkeys had mounted the assault of these scowling ruins, for

Fabrizio, fearing the now-imminent advent of the rains which accompany
the winter season in Syrtes, was eager to complete the restoration of the
upper terraces and parapets, where water leaked through gaping chinks,
postponing indoor repairs for the confinement of the endless days of bad
weather. In a few days, the fortress was cleared of its overgrowth, and
suddenly there was nothing but the great pile itself. Day after day it
emerged from its cast-off garments with all the evidence of a perfect
musculature, the simplicity of a motionless impulse, of a signal, tragic and
naked beside these flat waters. Its sharp ridges nipped at the blank horizon.
Seeing it gradually rise out of its sheath, like a statue being pulled out of the
ground, it suddenly seemed to us that the air of the Admiralty circulated
more freely, and that these high virgin walls sought the likes of a sea wind
to cleanse their armor; from morning to night, our feverish eyes ran over
their piercing silhouette like a tongue over the sharp edge of a newly broken
tooth. It was difficult to understand how such insignificant changes could be
responsible for such disturbance, could reach the point of changing the very
taste and flavor of the air we breathed and make our hearts beat faster, yet I
felt it; that was how it was: the fortress was growing among us now,
piercing our flesh like a new tooth, and there was no more repose for
anyone; the fortress was there, the very image of uneasiness—established,
prevailing, disturbing, incomprehensible—the faint and continuous pain of
a sharp point, extending the disquietude of a subtle needle to the remotest
extremities of the nervous system.
Although the command of the Admiralty itself and its internal services
were not my direct concern, I too was caught up in this whirlwind of new
activity, and I visited the map room less often now. It was no longer that
receptacle of silence whose cold and mildewed breath had clutched at my
throat like that of some hypogeum. The vine-stripped windows now
permitted a brighter light to shimmer over the blackened tables, and
occasionally a ray of sunlight, which slowly shifted its column of dust with
the hours, trailed over the pile of maps, sleepily extracting from the
shadows a foreign name or the contour of an unknown coastline. The dim
echo of the inner courtyards woke to the summons of the masons who

leaped from battlement to battlement and were suddenly outlined against
the panes. These shouts and cries, this feverish racket penetrating to the
very heart of this profoundly somnolent retreat beneath its shroud of dust,
shook off their unexpectedness and their picturesqueness: just as a
landscape painted against the background of a black room loses its vital
iridescence but thereby acquires a mineral stability and seems to filter out
of things what best translates their dim reverie of inertia, it was as if the
sounds here were decanted, filtered through a cloak of snow, whereby they
lost their ordinary meaning in order to swell to a deep and indistinct
murmur which became the very sound of returning life. Thus that familiar
noise of tools and voices echoed deep in this meditative gloom, rather like
the voluble and ominous capture of these ruins by a colony of migratory
birds—as if the time had come, as if its secret season, which belied the grim
approach of winter, long hatched beneath the dust of ages, had returned to
blossom over the fortress which it was bringing back to life like a thaw.
Fabrizio now talked about “his” fortress as if he had created it. In truth he
was reluctant to speak of anything else. The place was like a giant toy in the
hands of a huge child, and the fantasies which passed through his mind on
this subject were sometimes disturbing indeed, for with him it was “no
sooner said than done”; he had the gift of immediately communicating his
intoxications, the most preposterous enthusiasms, to his crews, who were
delighted by the baroque improvisation of his plans and longed for nothing
so much as to be perfectly unaware the day before of what they would be
undertaking on the next. It was also apparent that these frantic efforts
reminded them of the unpredictability of life at sea; a kind of esprit de
corps was generated in his men, who increasingly affected to despise the
stay-at-home life of the regular crews, and not a day passed without
requests to be assigned to the work crew coming to the captain’s attention.
Such letters, which particularly annoyed Marino, went into the wastepaper
basket one after the next, and the reception given to more audacious
petitioners was not a warm one.

“Devil take the place!” the infuriated captain would mutter. “We’re
getting what we asked for. Fabrizio will debauch every man here. The
Admiralty’s being demoralized ...” And the expression in his eyes was so
somber and despairing that I refrained from sarcasm. Moreover matters
went no further, and Marino was so scrupulous about keeping his word that
I found him, ultimately, very odd indeed; Fabrizio had a free hand, and the
captain permitted himself no remarks about the work.
One evening, as we were returning from our ritual after-dinner walk
along the parapets, from which Fabrizio, full of his subject, showed us like
a commander on the battlefield the projects to be undertaken the next day,
he drew me aside, his eyes sparkling more than usual. “Marino’s given me
carte blanche. He doesn’t know how suitable the expression is. He’s going
to Orsenna for a few days. When he returns, I’ll have a surprise for him.”
“We’re all having one surprise after another, Fabrizio. You’re outdoing
yourself.”
“Don’t make fun of me, Aldo. But this time Marino won’t get over
what’s happening to my fortress.”
“Intriguing. You’re planting hanging gardens? Or putting the whole thing
out to sea?”
Fabrizio rested one hand on my shoulder and stared at the fortress,
gauging it with narrowed eyes and a possessive expression. “Not bad the
way she looks now, I’ll grant you,” he began in an unassuming tone. “But
she lacks the artist’s finishing touch. You’ll see what I mean. I’ll admit she’s
more or less clean now, but there’s still not much more to see than an old
black rock. Now look here ...” He picked up at the base of the wall a stone
that had worked loose: under its black patina a fresh break revealed a
brilliant, crystalline white. “The stone is really magnificent—you can’t
imagine the brilliance! You see, it’s like the slice of a sugar loaf. There’s
three centuries of grime on it, the filth of ages, actually. I’m going to scratch
it all off—scrape it clean. I can get rid of that patina. In fifteen days I’m
going to give Marino the present of a brand-new fortress. It will be my

triumph!” And he added, in a voice already savoring it: “Do you think he’ll
be surprised?”
Marino’s prolonged absence made things easy. It was as if a dike had
broken. Whipped on for the first time by a youthful command too long
restrained, the Admiralty took the bit in its teeth. For this contraband labor,
Fabrizio had nothing but accomplices and delved with a free hand into the
reserve labor force. The whole Admiralty service clambered up the walls,
like termites on their nest; the fortress hummed all day long, and until dark
had fallen, with a frantic feverishness, as if in preparation for a party.
At the late hour when the Orsenna post brought Marino back, it was
already dark. The captain seemed preoccupied, and it was evident to me
that the cloud of indifference and abstraction which had been protecting
him for several weeks against too close an approach had grown even more
intense. The almost ritual questions which familiarly assailed him on the
subject of Orsenna were now meeting with increasingly brief and absent
replies, and I was beginning to fear that Fabrizio would not provoke all the
enthusiasm he had hoped. The moon had risen before dinner was over, and
Fabrizio, who was surreptitiously watching the window, as soon as Marino
had lit his pipe, with an air of assumed indifference led the little group
outside for the evening promenade.
Although the fires were already doused for the night, a confused noise of
voices still rose from the encampment through the calm dark, dissolving
gradually as we crossed the sleeping plain in the gentler and ampler
respiration of the lagoon; we turned the corner of the command pavilion,
and a sudden shock nailed us where we stood. Something never seen before
and yet long awaited, like a monstrous and motionless beast risen up out of
its very expectation at the indicated spot after interminable hours of vain
stalking, something at the edge of the lagoon, long nursed in darkness, had
at last emerged without a sound from its ruined shell as from an enormous
nocturnal egg: the fortress was before us.
The moonlight falling straight down on the terraces and the upper
surfaces left the moats and the base of the walls plunged in transparent

shadow, severing the structure from the ground, seeming to make it
weightless, to suck it gently upward; and thus anchored at the edge of the
lagoon hemmed with barbs of light, the fortress suddenly appeared afloat,
carried on a fluid element which, against the inert background of the plain,
gave it the faint, slow rhythm of a vessel at its moorings. Thus surprised in
its oneiric immobility, one would have said, however, that it rode in a
measureless ease, like the silent frolics one comes upon by night in certain
forest glades. Like the first snow which touches the highest peaks with a
grave finger, its unreal whiteness mysteriously consecrated it, wrapped it in
a shimmering vapor which rose in a column toward the moonlit night,
marking it with the incandescence of a glowing coal.
“It’s an apparition!” Roberto breathed at last, breaking the silence which
seemed to extend around us: “A ghost in its shroud.”
“You’re not very polite to Fabrizio. More likely in its wedding dress,”
Giovanni broke in, but the silence suddenly closed over us all, and we
seemed to feel, falling upon us, the whole weight of that bright night’s chill.
OceanofPDF.com

7
The Island of Vezzano
“There’s mail for you,” Marino announced as I came into his office the next
morning. “Apparently the Signory knows we’re alive.”
For all his show of detachment, there was an anxious interrogation in his
voice. He held out two sealed envelopes. I recognized the emblem of the
Council of Vigilance, which controls all special police matters in Orsenna—
that boded something serious. I gave Marino a receipt without a word, and
put off opening them until I could be alone.
I was anything but accustomed to the administrative style employed in
the Signory’s offices, and once I had finished reading the first document,
which consisted of intricate and particularly prolix instructions, my first
impression was of holding in my hands one of those documents from
abandoned archives whose enigmatic and consistently allusive phraseology
seems so because it occurs in a context of familiar references to which we
do not possess the key. Taken separately, all the words of this text were
readily comprehensible, yet the meaning of the whole continued to bewilder
me. From certain disconcerting formulations, from the superfluous
accumulation of linguistic precautions where they were least expected, I
deduced that for the writer, the exact quality of meaning implied in some
term of commonplace appearance could not have been quite the same as the
one I attached to it. I now recalled the remarks Orlando had once made to
me when we were both attending the Academy of Diplomatic Law—
remarks which I regarded at the time as exaggeratedly romantic and which
concerned the “secret” peculiar to Orsenna. According to Orlando, centuries
of unquestioned political stability had permitted Orsenna to benefit from an
almost unique experience: a subtle and extended decantation. The
continuous and hereditary control of all major affairs by a few great
families had ultimately forced to the summit of the torpid social structure,

as though by the effect of a patient chemical action, those volatile principles
generated in the depths of this ageless swamp the city had become. Yet
what had startled me in Orlando’s rather obscure language was that, far
from considering this slow incarnation of vital principles as the appanage of
strength and consciousness which seems to legitimize the rights of an
aristocracy, he alluded to it rather as a suspect and altogether dangerous
process, as if Orsenna’s intenser consciousness of its reasons of state
thereby created, with this quintessence of political wisdom, a latent threat of
dislocation. According to Orlando, this notion of Orsenna—a notion of the
ulterior elements of its life and its future—held by a number of minds
belonging to the oldest families of the Signory and usually to be found not
in the highest honorific functions but in certain apparently subaltern
positions from which, in actual fact, the heavy political machinery was
controlled—this notion had at length become as radically incomprehensible
to the common people as the world of the ocean depths must be for the
denizens of translucent waters. He claimed further that the organs of life in
Orsenna, for a mind sufficiently enlightened, had ultimately become as
profoundly differentiated as, in a tree, the root from the leaf.
“The leaf is the tree’s beauty,” he would tell me, “and the copious and
brilliant expenditure of its life—it breathes by day and knows every
suspicion of the breeze, it orients the trunk’s growth according to the subtle
impressions it constantly receives from the light, the air ... And yet the
tree’s truth is perhaps more deeply based on the blind suction of its roots
and its nourishing darkness. Orsenna is a very great tree, a very old tree,
and it has sent down very long roots. Do you know why trees cannot grow
tall, down there in our Syrtes? Spring explodes there like a squall once
March comes in, and the thaw is of a matchless brutality. The foliage
spreads like flags over a parade and draws up the sap as a nursling takes the
breast—but the thaw hasn’t touched the earth’s depths, the root still sleeps
in the ice, the heart’s fibers snap, and the tree dies amidst the flowering
meadows. I have no love for Orsenna’s too-green old age, which sets in
when it would be wiser not to live at too high a pitch, nor for whatever
conspires to keep the place from a sufficiency of sleep.”

In one of our last conversations he had insistently alluded to the
stranglehold of a clan of bold and dangerously lucid minds, and had given
me to understand that such dominance, following the latest nominations to
the Senate, had intensified, unbeknownst to the public, in a fashion he
found distinctly alarming—as if, he had added, “a shadow were falling over
the city.” Moreover, old Aldobrandi’s return to favor, which indicated, to
the eyes of close observers, a profound modification in Orsenna’s
equilibrium, had made me more sympathetic to Orlando’s dark views—
especially because of the absence of such political eddies as it might have
been expected to provoke, an absence which revealed a careful preparation
and a remarkable skill in the manipulation of the complicities which this
return implied in every echelon of government. Thus Orlando’s suggestions
had silently worked upon me, and now I questioned this document with all
the feverishness of a hunter who comes upon a commingling of scents: I
sought less to determine the practical steps I was being told to take than to
observe the murky gleam of the accommodations perhaps being suggested
to me.
According to a custom which dated back, in Orsenna, to the remotest
times—apparently to a period when the archival precaution of preserving
duplicates of each piece of correspondence was not yet observed—the
document began by a painstaking review of the points established by my
own report. The instructions which had been communicated to me were
then articulated around three points, which I was enjoined to regard as
entirely distinct.
With regard to the very origin of the rumors, the instructions—here
particularly fertile in hollow formulas—remained nebulous to a point
which, upon reflection, astonished me. It was declared to be “highly
desirable,” of course, but perhaps scarcely compatible with my functions at
the Admiralty—and here the writing vanished into the sands of
extraordinary circumlocution and intricate politesse—that I should venture
into the details of an exhaustive police inquiry whose results promised, in
advance, to be disappointing and whose object would ultimately turn out to

be of a secondary nature. The impression which I received from this almost
deliberately turbid prose, and which derived less from its general and
indiscernible meaning than from the urbane and compact tedium it so
eloquently expressed, was that it was not so much a matter of alerting me to
this specific subject as of the Signory’s being protected, by a purely formal
allusion, against the incurred risk of an omission. For one reason or another,
I was courteously being led to understand that in this regard it was prudent
to function according to the routine of “ordinary procedure” and advisable
to make haste slowly.
The precise degree of these rumors’ reality seemed to concern the author
of the text much more closely, and here, for the first time, I began to discern
a subtle difference of perspective between the report I had sent in and this
ambiguous document. The notion that such extravagant stories might
possess any degree of reality whatever had not seriously occurred to me—
or perhaps I had instinctively repressed it from the start as a breach of
decorum which might have disqualified me at the Signory. Yet now it
appeared, to the point of quite disconcerting me, that it was precisely this
skepticism in my report, which I had assumed to be obligatory, that had
been found unacceptable. In the eagerness shown to deal with such rumors
as a reliable datum, to grant them, without further verification, a basis and a
future, there was evident an impulse to arrive through them at a long-
forbidden perspective, to afford them a likelihood and a range whose
possibility was being subtly and carefully suggested to me, as if it was
particularly feared that I might shut too soon a door suddenly ajar, a door
which it had been secretly hoped would open. “The Signory,” my
instructions ended on this point, “attaches the liveliest interest in the efforts
you cannot fail to make in order to shed some light on this essential point.
The extremely strict regulations now in effect with regard to navigation off
Syrtes—regulations inspired, in other circumstances, more perhaps by the
immediate concern to avoid certain perilous encounters on the high seas
rather than by the demands of an exact intelligence whose exorbitant cost
has become evident with time and has on occasion made it appear desirable
that it be somewhat relaxed—may doubtless render such verifications

delicate and incomplete, but it is incumbent upon your intelligence and your
zeal to give them all the precision and scope compatible with the powers
entrusted to you.”
“With regard to the restoration of the fortress to a condition of active
defence” (here my eyes opened wide: beyond the fact that never, even for a
second, had there been any question of military operations at the Admiralty,
my report, written before Roberto’s proposition, could only have been mute
on the subject of the fortress; but I realized that here I was being referred
for clarification to the attached papers) “the Signory is astonished to have
been informed only indirectly of an initiative about which it was regrettably
not consulted and to which it would be even more serious to allude further,
for reasons which will be explained by the enclosed document. The
Signory, while acknowledging that such a decision may well have been
taken spontaneously by a legitimate concern for security, one which
ultimately corresponds to the requirements of the situation, trusts that in the
future decisions of such a nature, likely to involve its general policies, will
not be made without its being thoroughly informed within the briefest
possible interval.”
The official instructions stopped there. They afforded me substance for
much reflection and even more surprise. But now someone, in his own
name, began speaking, someone whose indecipherable signature zigzagged
across the bottom of the page; a voice drew me aside, one that was
singularly recognizable, it seemed to me, without my ever having heard it,
by its full sonority and something like an ancient patina of power. This
voice now pierced through the official sputter and rose in isolation, as if,
more than to the flood of disappointing words, it had been of great
importance to orient my hearing, now, to the profound, almost hypnotic
suggestions of a certain timbre.
“I am pleased,” the note said, “to acknowledge the clarity of your report
and the discernment of which you have shown yourself capable by not
hesitating to bring to the Council’s attention a matter whose precise
importance was difficult for you to estimate; yet it is now necessary for me

to warn you against a certain frivolity, whose signs I have been regretfully
compelled to observe, and which your youth does not entirely excuse. The
time has come to remind you in confidence that an Observer’s functions,
implying an entire communion of thought with the Signory’s government,
require you to concur at all times with its views and to maintain a special
vigilance against the suggestions of public opinion. To all it is permitted—
within certain limits—to speak; to some it is reserved to know. The official
state of hostility—that of the Signory with regard to a certain foreign power
—may with the years have faded in the consciousness of our people to the
point of becoming a subject of mockery and jest; it is incumbent upon you
to recall, if need be against the tendencies of that consciousness, a
dangerous truth which has never ceased to exist, and to be prepared, in all
circumstances, for any contingency it may bring about. This truth has its life
in you and in several others who remain its sole depositaries; it is
incumbent upon you, and upon them, that the State which they alone may
claim to incarnate under certain disappointing circumstances, continue to be
served lucidly. I urge you to ponder the motto of Orsenna. It was the
