professed opinion of the men who created the Signory’s greatness that a
State survives according to its inveterate contact with certain hidden truths,
of which only the continuity of its generations is the depositary—truths
difficult to recall and dangerous to exemplify, and thereby all the more
subject to the people’s neglect. They called these truths the Pact of Alliance,
and rejoiced, even in danger and the city’s temporary reverses, over any
circumstance which made them shine forth like a visible manifestation of its
election and its eternity. Circumstances may one day cause you to be
engaged in the defense of this Pact which the city cannot without peril fail
to observe. Orsenna expects you to be, in Syrtes, the consciousness of the
city’s danger; failing this, you must resign your post.”
The page attached was a report by Belsenza, who in his turn had
evidently determined to break his silence. The “restoration of the fortress to
a state of active defense” (I reflected in passing that it was at the very least
curious that the Signory should show so much eagerness to believe, on no
more than his word, one witness so remote from the scene) on which, it

appeared, all Maremma was daily focusing its field glasses (the huge mass
of the structure was visible, even so far away, above those flat shores)
seemed, by the confirmation it afforded such alarmist rumors, to have
noticeably heightened the fever which made the city buzz—to the point
where Belsenza had taken alarm. He had even brought himself—at least, a
modest periphrasis tended to suggest as much—to have quite discreetly
arrested, in various public places, certain chatterers who exceeded all
bounds. This entire report, written with an extreme prudence and reticence,
expressed, moreover, Belsenza’s hesitations at the moment of determining
which way the wind was blowing: careful, in his determination to stifle this
public panic, to shield himself against any reproach of negligence, yet
uncomfortable at appearing to play the cynic if the “restoration of the
fortress” happened to portend, in fact, serious developments.
Once I had read Belsenza’s note, I reread every word of the Council’s
instructions with the scrupulous attention one pays to a text which must be
translated, and I lay the papers down on the table, profoundly disoriented.
Like the first imperceptible tremor of a hull sliding into the sea, it seemed to
me that something, before my incredulous eyes, had changed. A glance was
raised behind me, aimed at the horizon, though I had thought it stubbornly
fixed on the ground, and my whole perspective was thereby altered. As
though at the lookout’s solemn voice from high on the mast, an inner
presentiment with that glance, shouted “land ho!” within me, giving a shape
and consistency to the phantom that had already fascinated me.
The noise of a motor roused the drowsy afternoon, and a reflection in the
pane of the open window showed me the Maremma car which had come to
a gentle stop in front of my door. There was a letter for me: Vanessa wanted
me to come early the next morning. Many things were known, it appeared,
at the Aldobrandi palace. Marino had left the Admiralty for two days on the
Redoubtable, which at this season was visiting the sponge beds for the relief
of the surveillance teams; this was so ostensibly a way of making things
easy for me that I was annoyed. Vanessa’s way of taking matters in hand
was infuriating; I couldn’t help thinking that she was treating Marino like a

cuckolded husband, and I was humiliated for him. The asides into which
she was drawing me made me instinctively react in the captain’s favor: I
never felt my friendship for him so intensely as when Vanessa showed me
that casualness in her preferences and in the requirements which were her
specialty.
Driving through the cool dawn toward Maremma, I recovered something
of the charm of pure expectation I had relished on my initial journey to
Syrtes. I wasn’t even trying to guess where this summons, which Vanessa
was making into such a mystery, would lead. The birds began their
melancholy chirping with the daylight, muffled and monotonous like each
of their days, strewn like so much sand over these limitless reaches; the
calm of the gray plains, still damp with morning mist, resembled those
languid summer dawns which linger as though stupefied under the traces of
a storm. Twice I turned around to glimpse the fortress behind me, a livid
bone color beneath its cloak of fog; ahead, in the distance, quicksilver
reflections from the lagoon nibbled the horizon into a thin black line of
lace, and in this already oppressive morning I seemed to feel these two
poles—around which my life now oscillated—being charged beneath their
veil of mist with a subtle electricity. Belsenza’s report came to mind all the
more forcefully: I fastened my eyes on that dark hem which lay along the
sea—already its powerful exhalations reached me in the gusts of a sleepy
wind; I listened in spite of myself for the low, stubborn murmur which the
hidden town produced in my memory, like a swamp on a stormy afternoon;
it nourished that heavy atmosphere, made its cocoon of mist throb softly,
pulsing like a secret heartbeat.
Its doors wide to the dancing reflections of the lagoon, the palace seemed
sound asleep. My calls brought no one. I walked in, hesitating in this
unaccustomed series of bare rooms I hardly recognized. A chilly
indifference hovered about the vaults, and I suddenly felt my vexation
returning. Irresolute, I could do nothing but stroll from one great salon to
the next, raising a weary eye toward the frozen saraband of the ceiling
frescoes, like a visitor to a museum. In this manner I entered a remote

gallery which overlooked the bridge and the neglected garden adjoining the
palace beyond a channel of stagnant water, and there, on the other side of
the water, standing in a covered pathway, I suddenly caught sight of
Vanessa.
There was every indication that she believed she was unobserved. She
had stepped out of the water and pulled on some sailor’s pants and a loose
shirt that left her arms bare. Now she was wringing out her sopping hair.
Brown tufts stirred under her arms, and in the hollow of her breasts lay a
dark shadow. She was holding hairpins in her tightly clenched mouth, and
her whole face was rinsed in a sudden wash of childhood; in her ambiguous
innocence, her stubborn schoolgirl deliberation, it was as if that
concentrated mouth, so fixed upon its task, was alive with all the intensity
of a carnivorous blossom, in the one blind gesture of seizing and holding.
I remained out of sight, my heart pounding, before this strange girl
suddenly given over to the disturbing grace of her pure animality. Her
fingers rippled in the soft hanks, the tilt of her head transformed the throat
into a pale shower and gently twisted the breasts as though around the
handle of a dagger. She was like the tremor one sees in the air above a
flame. For the first time, Vanessa had become flesh. She rose up out of the
tide of my feverish reveries, firm and elastic as a wet beach, made for the
sole and the palm, a gentle earth loosened beneath the lashing rain of her
hair.
I tapped on the windowpane. Vanessa looked up, turned and came toward
me across the little bridge. “I gave everyone the day off. The palace is
empty. Today’s for the two of us. I’m carrying you off.”
“Out to sea, apparently.”
“Right, very far out. We’ll need a whole day. We’re going to Vezzano.”
The name immediately awakened certain associations, and I felt an
impulse of curiosity. In my mind a black dot appeared on the blue sheet,
where I had so often imagined sailing, during those hours in the map room.
Vezzano was no more than a tiny islet, and the navigation treaties I had
leafed through at the Admiralty accorded it no more than the briefest notice:

it was mentioned chiefly for the singularity of its steep coasts, inlets
confronting the half-submerged sandbars of the lagoons which could offer
shelter to ships surprised by sudden gusts of the southern winter. In the days
when piracy was rife in these seas, Vezzano had played the part of a home
port and a fortified supply station for the sea rovers; chosen no doubt for its
sheltered creeks and its spacious caves which in places riddled the island, it
had served still better because the mainland’s proximity permitted night
transport of contraband on skiffs to the nearby coastal harbors. But all these
bygones of bloodshed and savage wealth were not what held me now. For
me, no more of a memory or a landscape was attached to that black dot on
the map than to a star’s fleck of light. Vezzano was one of my personal
stars, the gleaming point of one of my fixed constellations. If you thrust one
leg of a compass into the Rhages station, Vezzano was, from all points of
Orsenna’s territory, the one which appeared inside the circle with the
shortest radius.
The sun was glistening on the lagoon as we left the palace: the day
promised splendid weather. The wind entered my loose clothes like a hand,
voluptuously; before leaving, Vanessa had insisted that I put on the same
sort of sailor’s trousers and shirt that she was wearing. “It’s best you not be
recognized on the boat. You’ll see why. Besides, it’s more comfortable,” she
added, looking away from my bare feet, her throaty voice a little tense.
The sense that in these clothes my limbs were as free as Vanessa’s
betrothed me to her, united us like some night garment. I felt the wind
sliding over her skin and over mine, wedding us like her very breath upon
my mouth. Sitting docilely beside one another, we smiled into each other’s
eyes without saying a word, delighted with this schoolboy escapade and the
gusts of this wind that tousled her hair. My new accoutrements were a
pretext for little familiarities which left me oppressed and froze my words
on my lips, so apprehensive was I, suddenly, of betraying myself by my
choked voice; I felt the light caress of her fingers on my neck as though
they had burned me, and, when the boat suddenly pitched, her foot rested
on mine and she held me in her warm arms, laughing a little too quickly; I

was in no state to speak, but I pressed that bare foot, so chilly on the wet
planks; her arm lingered round me a second too long, and her hair smelled
of childhood and wilderness. At that moment I no longer even desired her, I
no longer felt anything but the fortifying wind which kept slapping our
faces, and a tenderness opening its thousand arms in a trusting darkness,
sure of closing them over their exact measure of sweet warmth.
The boat was now sliding into the channel of the lagoons and took us
toward the open sea. At that moment, nothing could have surprised me,
even to have undertaken the Vezzano crossing in this tiny skiff. I turned
toward Vanessa and gestured in a fashion so resolute and at the same time
so comically interrogative that she burst out laughing, with that same laugh
I had heard the first night, on the edge of the quay.
“Vezzano’s a bit too far, you know, Aldo. Our boat’s out here.” She added
in an anxious tone which uncertainty made a little harsh: “... You recognize
her?”
I recognized her. Anchored off the sandbars, a very slender outline, since
it was facing us prow on, narrowed further by the brilliance of the sea and
the sun, there lay the mysterious boat from Sagra.
“I should warn you, Aldo, I’ve neglected to have her registered. It’s very
bad of me, isn’t it? You won’t have too many scruples about embarking in a
contraband vessel?”
In her carefully pitched voice I detected an accent of involuntary
arrogance, and the compassionate eyes turned away, but I understood their
summons and her harsh attack. I would be embarking on the ship only as a
prisoner. At that moment I felt that something was being decided for good,
and I tried to meet Vanessa’s eyes. They shone upon me for an instant,
starry and fixed, then passed over me toward a distance of which I knew
nothing—at that moment Vanessa was not even looking at me. She was
leaning against me, silent, tense, a heavy and nocturnal weight, her naked
breasts hard under the shirt that was like a taut sail in the breeze. My eyes
slid down between them where a mist blurred their lawless rhythm, and I
bowed my head without a word, my mouth dry and my palms sweating.

“Come,” she said abruptly, and I stood up to follow her.
I remember that crossing as one of those days of plenitude when the
warm flame of joy flickering within us devours and calmly assimilates all
things to itself, seemingly kindled by the mere transparency of sky and sea,
as if they had been some enormous lens. The sun had scattered the mist: the
warm and meditative amber of late autumn, like a delicious exudation of the
earth, was to summer’s heat like the warm flesh of the fruit you bite into
compared to its burning skin. The fierce little waves of the Syrtes sea sent
up countless spirals of foam; around us, flocks of sea birds hovered, then
flew off over the shifting surfaces of the water, as in the calm evening over
ploughed fields. Everything around us ascended, gently aspiring toward a
paradise of feathery efflorescence: long muffled wingbeats of the gulls
interspersed with raucous cries, soft feathers snatched from the foam, the
wind’s throbbing plumage in our faces, the vacillation of the swell lifting
the boat as though on a swan’s back.
The foredeck, sheltered by a low partition which masked the hatchways
and by rolls of canvas and ropes, formed a narrow platform open to the sea
on all sides. We had brought pillows here; lying beside Vanessa, my fingers
lingered in the crook of her elbow where a gentle pulse throbbed, and I
watched the great galleons of the clouds swaying overhead in a rhythm that
matched the sea’s silent roll. The brief but intense anxiety that had seized
me as I embarked had vanished; it seemed to me that things were coming to
pass and that all was arranged and proceeding without haste according to
the rhythm of our fraternal blood. Vanessa now seemed relaxed and happy,
and when I pressed my lips to her cool palm, her hand lay on my mouth
with all its weight, and the curving, lifeless fingers of that isolated hand
came to close my eyelids and reveal a daylight of their own. The disturbing
name of Vezzano rustled in my ears like the sound of a bell carried by the
wind over a desert or a glacier; this was our rendezvous and our betrothal; it
seemed to me that at its summons the light planks we were lying on were
flying over the waves and that the horizon ahead of our prow was
mysteriously altering and emptying ....

When its white cliffs emerged from the shimmer of the sea’s distances,
Vezzano suddenly seemed oddly close. It was a kind of rocky iceberg,
notched on all sides and broken into great parapets polished by the waves.
The cliffs rose straight out of the sea, almost unreal in the glitter of their
white armor, weightless on the horizon like a windjammer under its banks
of sail, except for the slender grassy rim along the top which here and there
ran down into the steep zigzagging notches of the ravines. The reflections
of these white cliffs silvered the island in places, in others dissolved it into
the faint haze of a fine summer day, and it was a long time before we saw,
rising out of the calm water, a kind of rough and rocky donjon of dirty gray
which bore its frowning cornices at an enormous height above the waves.
Dense flocks of sea birds, flying in formations and then falling back in
looser spirals around the rock, produced something like the plumed
respiration of a geyser; their tormented cries, sharpening the wind and
echoing it in the harsh reverberation of the cliffs, turned the island into a
sullen and malevolent solitude, immuring it even more fearfully than its
inaccessible cliffs.
The boat dropped anchor in the lee of these, which cast a calm upon the
sea and a cavernous chill; a skiff was lowered and Vanessa signalled me to
get down into it—only the two of us. “This was how you wanted to get
there, isn’t it?” she whispered into my ear as though in excuse, with an
ambiguous smile. “Besides, my captain doesn’t care: no one comes here,
and all the landing places have been forgotten. But try not to drown us ...”
Rowing the skiff to land, I couldn’t help, as we entered its shadow which
sent a chill down my spine, being overcome by the solitude and hostility of
this gloomy Cythera toward which we were heading. These wild and
desolate cries of the sea birds that covered the island and chilled this
spectral shadow, these naked rocks with the pallor of bones and the memory
of that funereal past, cast an unexpected pall upon this festive sea. Along
these smooth walls, alarming the colonies of birds nesting high up in the
hollows of the rock, we rowed in silence for some time, as though under a
cathedral vault: no gap seemed to open in this tremendous enclosure, when

a faint lapping of waves against the cliff suddenly mingled with the sound
of the open sea, and almost at the same moment we slid into a creek only a
few yards wide and so deep that it seemed sawed into the mass of the
plateau. A ravine widened at the far end of this creek, and a little stream ran
into it, clattering in its bed of pebbles.
We jumped ashore on a stony beach. It was very dark in this crevice in
the very entrails of the rock—a transparent and liquid twilight seemed to be
emitted by the sound of the stream. Here the murmur of the waves no
longer reached us except as a dim rustle. Across the open slash above us,
the pale sky was turning a darker blue; in the chasm of the ravine where the
day was being engulfed, a single tree, very high over our heads, was
silhouetted, steeped still in sunlight, and seemed to be urging us to climb
up, toward the rocky heights. The silent intimacy and the penumbra of this
gorge were so unexpected that we stood for a moment without saying a
word, abashed and smiling at each other like children who creep into a
forbidden cellar. And so suddenly complicitous was the secret embrace of
this crypt that Vanessa, overcome by an involuntary anguish at the snapping
shut of this trap, took a few stumbling steps over the stones as if to escape; I
could tell that her breath was uneven and too fast, but coming up behind
her, and suddenly pulsing with a brutal energy at this confession of
weakness which affected me with a delicious thrill, I took her arm in mine
and pulled her head down on my shoulder, and all at once she seemed to
collapse heavily, no more than a warm, soft weight, loosened and
abandoned to my mouth.
We must have spent hours in that well of oblivion and sleep. The crevice
in the rock over our joined heads was so narrow, and the sky between so
distant and so calm, that the day’s variations, in the absence of more
extended play of shadows, no longer reached us; we lay resting in the same
security as tomb figures beneath that false crypt light where the shadow was
diluted as though in deep water; the faint sounds around us: a noise of fresh
water over the pebbles, the almost imperceptible lapping and the tiny
gurgle, in the rocky hollows, of the rising tide, gave the passing time, by

their long, suspended intervals and their sudden recurrences, a drifting
uncertainty interrupted by rapid spells of sleep, as if the faint consciousness
which surfaced in us now and then had taken from this very emergence the
tiny addition of weight which immediately plunged it back into a brief
swoon. I had carried Vanessa to the edge of the stream, which left between
itself and the rock the width of a narrow couch on which grew deep black
grass; my hand resting on one of her breasts, I felt her lying quite calm
beside me and utterly caught up in a dim expansion of powers; that breast
gently riding upon this deep odor of the earth brought me something like
the fortifying news of that good sleep which is the presage of profound
cures; then the excess of my tenderness for her reawakened: my frenzied
kisses rained down all over this vanquished body; I nibbled her hair that
mingled so easily with the grass. Vanessa almost awakened and, her eyes
still closed in the excess of her lassitude, let her half-open mouth relax into
a smile; her hand groped toward me, and no sooner had she touched me
than, benumbed with a confident certainty, she heaved a sigh of comfort
and sank once more into a deep sleep.
Yet the sun must have gone down a good ways, for the walls of the gorge
had turned gray, and only one of the lips of the overhanging rock was still
radiant with a narrow band of light; the sound of the waves seemed to fade,
and a few inconceivable stars, like that fleeting gleam which torchlight
wakens in certain jewels, twinkled faintly against the pale blue of the sky. A
chill rose from the wet grass; I helped Vanessa up and pressed against me
that warm, weakened weight which surrendered to my hands in a long, an
interminable embrace.
“Are we going back to the ship?” I asked sleepily. “It must be getting
late, by now.”
“No. Come.”
Suddenly lively, almost feverish now, turning toward me those eyes of
earlier days which I knew so well, she pointed toward the top of the ravine.
“The boat doesn’t expect us before dark. Why do you think I’ve brought
you here?” she asked with that cutting arrogance which at once wounded

and exalted me, because I had the impression of being snubbed by a queen,
but almost immediately afterwards she lowered her eyes and gently laid her
hand on my shoulder. “The least we can do is explore our kingdom. Just
think, Aldo, we’re all alone on an island. And you’re already wanting to
leave.”
We climbed, with some difficulty, up the shifting path of stones that was
the bed of the little stream. Vanessa clung to me over these uncertain rocks,
and soon her bare feet began to bleed. I suddenly felt the intoxication come
to an end; the already dimming light seemed to me a bad omen, and this
island of ill repute vaguely suspect; I again suggested that we return, but
Vanessa answered sharply: “We can rest up there.”
Gradually the ravine widened and flattened out; we emerged from the
gorge and were walking now, noiselessly, on smooth grass in the hollow of
a little valley which gradually led up to the island’s high plateau. In the
open air, it was still daylight; emerging into the light of these still warm
heights, every breath was delicious. The summit of the island was a blank
plain, notched round the edges by the radiating incisions of the ravines.
Sudden waves ran over the dry grass; the faint detonations of those other
invisible waves breaking in the hollows of the cliffs gave the wind the
sound of a faraway storm. Here and there, with the late afternoon’s
coolness, wisps of white mist were beginning to run and mingle over the
ground, like a panic-stricken herd—the island was already being invaded by
night—as if ahead of time the evening’s ghosts were trying to regain
possession of the plain. Vanessa led me rapidly now toward a rather steep
hill—the only eminence of this plateau—which rose before us where the
island narrowed and pointed eastward like a rising prow; to our right and
left, now very close, the ravines no longer left between them anything more
than a narrow, winding crest. Vanessa was walking in front of me without
saying a word, her breath short, her gait hurried, and it occurred to me for a
second that the island was perhaps still inhabited, and that a figure would
appear out of these rocks to incarnate her fever and my uneasiness.

Having reached the top of the hill, she stopped. The island broke off in
front of us in steep precipices; the wind here lashed it furiously, and we
could hear rising from the foot of the cliffs the waves’ continuous roar. But
Vanessa paid no attention, and probably did not even remember that I was
there. She had sat down on a split rock and fixed her eyes on the horizon: it
was as if she had suddenly taken up a vigil on this forsaken lookout, like
those mourning silhouettes that from the top of some promontory endlessly
await the return of a sail.
My eyes followed, in spite of myself, the direction of her gaze. A
brightness lingered on the hillside which still split the cloak of mist. Facing
us, the sea’s horizon was edged with a paler and astonishingly transparent
strip in the deep twilight, like one of those sunny streaks which move across
the water under the dome of mists announcing the end of a storm. My eyes
scanned this empty horizon and lingered a second over the contours of a
tiny white cone-shaped cloud which seemed to float on the water in the
fading light, and whose unexpected isolation in this clear evening air and
whose heavy shape were immediately associated in my mind with the
notion of a distant menace and the apprehension of a storm rising over the
sea. A sudden chill now broke over the island, the wind freshened, and at
the approach of darkness the sea birds had stopped crying; I was suddenly
eager to leave this sullen and savage place, as abandoned as a sinking ship.
I touched Vanessa’s shoulder. “It’s getting late. Let’s go back.”
“No, not yet. Did you see?” she said, turning her wide eyes toward me in
the darkness.
Suddenly, like a slowly saturated pool, the daylight sky had turned to a
lunar one; the horizon was an opaque and milky wall which became violet
above the still faintly shimmering sea. Filled with a sudden presentiment, I
glanced in the direction of the singular cloud. And all at once, I saw.
A mountain was rising out of the sea, now distinctly visible against the
darkened background of the sky. A snowy white cone, floating like a
moonrise above a faint mauve veil which separated it from the horizon, in
all the isolation of its snowy purity and the surge of its perfect symmetry,

like those glittering lighthouses that rise on the brink of glacial seas. Its
starry ascent on the horizon did not bespeak the earth, but rather a midnight
sun, the revolution of a calm orbit which had brought it round to the hour
said to be of the depths, washed in the fateful blossoming of the sea. It was
there. Its cold light shone like a source of silence, like a desert and starry
virginity.
“It’s the Tängri,” Vanessa said without turning her head. She was
speaking as if to herself, and I doubted once again whether she was aware
of my presence.
We stayed there a long time, wordless in the gathering darkness, our eyes
fixed on the sea. The sense of time left me altogether. Moonlight
imprecisely drew the enigmatic peak out of the obscurity, only to thrust it
back again, causing it to throb fantastically over the invisible sea; our
fascinated eyes wore themselves out following these waning phases, as in
the final gleams, more sinister and more mysterious, of an aurora borealis.
Finally it was night, deep night, and the cold was piercing. I took Vanessa in
my arms without a word, and she leaned against me with all her weight. We
walked away, heads heavy, eyes smarting from staring too hard, legs
throbbing. I was holding Vanessa close on the dangerous and slippery path
we had difficulty following in the dark, but the support I was giving her was
no more, now, than a mechanical reflex, without tenderness. It seemed to
me that over this day of sweet and caressing warmth something like a wind
from snowy fields had passed, so lustral and so fierce that my lungs which
it had stung could never exhaust its deadly purity, and, as though to keep its
sparkle a little longer in my eyes and its cold savor in my mouth, on the
stony path, in spite of myself, I walked with my head tilted back toward the
star-clogged sky.
OceanofPDF.com

8
Christmas
I visited Maremma frequently now, availing myself of the car which the
demands of work on the fortress kept sending to the city. I would leave the
Admiralty after luncheon, and even the short ride made me impatient. When
we reached the first houses of the town, I noticed that the mere—and
familiar—sight of the Admiralty flag fluttering on the fender immediately
drew a group of inquisitive onlookers, and that glances as we drove past
betrayed a certain intensity; I felt that the car’s appearance was in itself a
piece of news which shed a certain light on their day, and our presence a
sign, a confirmation that something was being prepared; occasionally I even
glimpsed out the rear window the ritual gesture of an arm raised in the
salute usually reserved, in Orsenna, for solemn occasions. It was as if
people were seeking contact, quite instinctively, with someone who seemed
closest to the secret, and I knew that a rumor would be running from street
to street: “The Admiralty car is back.” Just to get out, one had to brush
away these idlers like flies, and their glances followed my back a long time,
greedy as mouths gasping for air.
This was not the only change to be noticed in Maremma. When I dropped
in on Belsenza for the latest news, I found him more anxious with each visit
to the shabby office with its dusty smell of overheated wallpaper. He held
out the reports without a word, frowning still from his reading, a cigarette
stuck in a corner of his mouth, tilting his head back and giving me a quick
glance through half-closed lids. The rising curve of fever infecting even this
wretched neighborhood of Maremma showed up inexorably on these finger-
smudged accounts, and judging by the suspect clues which accumulated
before my eyes like discarded wrappings on the sweeper’s spike, it was as if
this fever was now suppurating. Police statistics afforded daily evidence of
a strange collapse of morality, especially in cases of exhibitionism and of

provocation to debauchery, often difficult for the police to uncover, so
greatly did they seem to benefit from the witnesses’ tacit complicity ...
Belsenza sometimes burst into the cynical cackle of a disillusioned man as
he pointed out some piquant detail, but to me such eposides seemed more
like a clinical sign of some disease, and what I had glimpsed at the
Aldobrandi palace left me wondering. The police were giving a good deal
of publicity to the business.
“It keeps people from thinking about other things,” Belsenza confided,
with an amused wink. “The police have known all along, and I shouldn’t be
surprised if my men had an occasional hand in it.”
But it was clear that Maremma did not stop thinking about “other things.”
Belsenza’s gaiety collapsed when his men brought him, as they frequently
did, some fortune teller with apocalyptic claims or one of those hairy
“prophets” (this was what the people called them) with shifty eyes and
obsequious manners, who raved nowadays on the docks at nightfall,
collecting crews from the ships.
“Birds of ill omen, those fellows. There’s something behind it—or
someone. I’d like to get my hands on whoever’s paying them!” Belsenza
murmured between clenched teeth, with a hiss of impotent rage.
Their attitude was always the same, characterized by an exaggerated and
apparently genuine respect for the official emblems and representatives.
When they were pulled into the police offices, they saluted everyone in
sight with a kind of ceremonial and exalted bombast, granting the precise
degree of consideration merited by each officer according to his function or
his rank, and then leaned against the wall and fell dumb, eyes fixed on the
floor, after which there was no hope of getting a word out of them. No
matter how Belsenza bullied them, threatened them with beatings, they
broke their silence only to utter a few ready-made phrases which constituted
the inept leitmotif of their predictions, formulas that invariably assigned to
Farghestan a vaguely apocalyptic role, a bizarre mission of Providence in
reverse.

“The hour has come ... We’re all numbered Over There ... The words are
spoken ... We’ve been counted, from first to last ...”
Their shrill, singsong voices, pitched to fill the empty squares, echoed so
forlornly in the skeptical indifference of these bare walls that they fell silent
almost at once of their own accord, and ducked their heads between their
shoulders like night birds, frightened and morose, intimidated by the sound
of their own voices, shivering like creatures caught in a snare. Belsenza
shrugged his shoulders and, depending on his mood of the moment, kicked
them out of his sight or sent them to the city jail for a few days; he had
them searched before they vanished, but oddly enough—and apparently
belying what Belsenza had said—they almost never had any money in their
pockets.
Such interrogations made me uncomfortable. Those black mouths
suddenly and reluctantly gaping over some childish nightmare haunted me
with a sinister foreboding. The desolation of those trembling and almost
obscene lips struck me particularly—as if the last defence of life had given
way, as if something had insidiously managed to incarnate a profound
human defeat. That ruined voice which seemed to come from the depths,
no, from the dregs, and which sent chills down my spine and a sudden wave
of silence through the policemen sitting at their desks, was in the true sense
of the word panic. In that filthy, somnolent office surrounded by a
mummified city stewing in its ruinous immobility, it was like a crevice of
darkness gaping in broad daylight, like the rotten nightmare of an age-old
sleep that was escaping, materializing before us, descending the stairs.
There were prouder silhouettes in that swarm of ghosts. One day they
brought in, during one of my visits, a girl—wretchedly dressed but with
delicate, almost noble features—who read the future in ashes at a stall in the
vegetable market. The interrogation started badly: her stubborn silence
became so insolent, her remote and disdainful gaze so provocative, that I
saw the cold rage gradually rising on Belsenza’s face; he seemed more
nervous than usual, or perhaps touched by a darker suspicion: “You don’t

feel like talking? We’ll see! You asked for it!” he snapped in a low, harsh
voice. “Give her a good whipping.”
In the dimness of the room, I seemed to see the girl’s eyes darken. Hands
tied behind her back, her neck was fastened inside a ring fixed in the wall,
and then they pulled up her skirts behind and threw them over her head.
There was a throb of greedy tension and excitement in the room. Belsenza
did not often afford such entertainments, banal though they were back in
Orsenna, where the authorities had a heavy hand and where a long intimacy
with violence caused it to be treated with facetious familiarity. But
something unaccustomed in that tomblike silence stopped the usual jokes.
“Have you made up your mind?” Belsenza muttered between his teeth.
A series of tiny sobs was audible through the folds of material, and I
knew that now she would never talk. For her, the worst was over: it was that
cattle collar on the auction block, that rump glistening with health and
obscenely full, which flouted our faces now, like an irresistible giggle.
Trembling monotonously under the lashes, that rump was hatched with
red streaks. A tense embarrassment fell on the entire room; there had been a
mistake here: it was as if they were whipping a dead woman.
“That’s enough!” Belsenza growled uncomfortably, vaguely realizing I
was not enjoying the scene. “Get out—don’t let me see you around here
again.”
Her face still inflamed, she pulled her skirt down, patting it smooth,
quickly arranging her hair with a provocation of childish indifference belied
by her dry, burning eyes which leaped from one object to the next as though
under an unendurable lash, as if the whole room had been grilled red-hot.
“All right, no more about it, no need for such a fuss!” Belsenza muttered,
touching her shoulder, suddenly jocular in his coarse way. “Try to see a
little brighter future next time, or you know what’ll happen ...”
But the eyes came to rest on him, black and searing, with a sudden
victorious glow: “You’re scared ... scared! ... scared! ...You’re whipping me
because you’re scared.”

Belsenza pushed her out and she scurried away, but we could still hear
the bare feet on the tiles between hysterical bursts of laughter, and her shrill,
stubborn, childish voice hanging in the air, like a wasp: “Scared! Scared!
Scared!” As she ran past outside, windows opened without a sound, like
clam shells in the sun, sucking up her cries one after the next in the silence
of that miserable neighborhood, and we all felt like fools. Grim fools.
There were more disturbing indications. Though the rainy season was
already on its way, the city’s little foreign colony made no haste to leave
Maremma, and it was already clear that many, following Vanessa’s
example, were making arrangements to spend the winter here, however
uncomfortable the various tumbledown palaces might seem, where the wind
circulated all too familiarly. Already this unexpected surplus population
was making considerable inroads on the region’s poor resources and
suggested the procurement difficulties that were facing Belsenza, leading
him to inquire even more harshly what could be keeping these idlers here in
the dead of winter. His spies were unable to learn much about their
occupations and their projects; it was a delicate matter for the police to
show too close an interest in the comings and goings of people whose
names were the very breath of Orsenna and whose influence at the Signory
was all too certain. Moreover they had all too many opportunities to meet
without suspicion in those midnight festivities that glistened ever more
provocatively at the Aldobrandi palace, and when he faced this enigma
which seemed to delight in showing itself so openly, Belsenza felt irresolute
and flouted.
“Try to understand me,” he said one day, alluding to one of these parties
with that half-squint he affected in his moments of perplexity, so that his
stare slid out between his eyelids like a coin. “Last night, who was there?
Count Ferzone, Senator Monti’s wife, and the Secretary of the Presidio
Council! If that’s a conspiracy, then Orsenna’s conspiring against itself. I’m
beginning to wonder just who the police are working for. Can you tell me
these people aren’t the first ones who read my reports?” His evasive eyes
sought mine insistently now. I knew that my intimacy with Vanessa had

raised a barrier between us: it was as if those cunning eyes were seeking
some sort of password, making peace overtures through me ... There was a
lassitude in his heavy shoulders, a kind of collapse. “What bothers me,” he
went on, “is that Orsenna has nothing to say. In any case, what we’re doing
here isn’t much use. I don’t enjoy having girls whipped. And besides ...” He
made a disabused gesture and glanced toward the window. “... Maybe what
they say is true. Maybe it will end badly ...” There was a moment’s silence
in the room; the sound of footsteps limped along the canal, lost in the sleepy
afternoon. It seemed as if something—sand, say—was yielding gently
under my weight, and quite unconsciously I took a step toward the door.
Belsenza started, like a man waking out of his sleep: “You’re going to the
palace—the Princess is back from her trip. Lucky man! I don’t go as often
as I’d like.” He looked at me knowingly, and continued in a serious tone of
voice: “... Sometimes I’m afraid I get invited only to make up for what I
have to do on active duty. Please assure the Princess that she’ll never have
any trouble from my department.”
Thus the uneasiness was gaining ground, and day after day you could see
some new defense collapsing in an unexpected fashion. As if we were
troops advancing into fog, a subtle disorientation of the adversary was
preparing and precipitating our movements. When I thought of the
instructions I had received from Orsenna, and of the complacent echoes I
kept hearing from the city, the rumors filling the place with fever, it
sometimes seemed to me that Orsenna was growing weary of its slumbrous
health, and without daring to admit such a thing to itself, greedily hoped to
feel alive, to wake up altogether in the numb anguish that now was reaching
its very depths. As if the happy city, which for so long had swarmed over
the seas in all directions, and whose inexhaustible heart had electrified so
many vital bodies and venturesome minds, now sucked the bad news deep
within its sullen dotage like a richer vibration of all its fibers.
I left Belsenza and made my way through the labyrinth of mean streets to
reach the quay where a boat was waiting for me. Impatient as I was to
rejoin Vanessa, I sometimes found a certain charm in lingering in these

alleyways that zigzagged between blind facades and the melancholy little
gardens won from the sands, where great patches of shade fell quite early in
the afternoon. Here was a whole sullen suburb pitched on the scalloped
stretch of dunes which marked the edge of terra firma, its crumbling age
and leprous abandonment made still more desolate by the return of the sand
the scorched gardens no longer resisted; sometimes, under the gusts of the
sea breeze, I could see the delicate shining plumes tirelessly pouring over
the wall of a ruined enclosure and carpeting the narrow pavement, like so
many cascades of silence; but if I peered over the wall, the persistent
murmur of the open sea and the wind’s clatter suddenly overwhelmed me,
and my eyes stung. I relished that threatened silence and its coigns of
darkness that seemed to be suspended over a vast, deep clamor; I would
slide my fingers through this sand that had been winnowed by so many
storms and that now was muzzling the city in somnolence; I watched
Maremma silting up, and at the same time, my eyes stinging, battered by
the furious wind pelting the sand, I seemed to feel life itself throbbing more
fiercely at my temples, as if something was rising behind that entombment.
Sometimes, at the corner of a street, balancing a pitcher or a basket of fish
on her head, a fisherman’s wife would appear beneath the eternal black
veils which make groups in Maremma into so many funeral processions,
and which the women draw over their mouths to protect themselves from
the hail of the sand: soundlessly she passed close to me, like a ghost
wandering through the dead city, bringing an odor of the sea and of the
desert too, and what she made me think of, appearing that way from this
uninhabitable necropolis, was one of those wandering and funereal flames
which rise and flicker over a landscape gorged with death. On these
extreme verges, life risked itself, more vulnerable, more naked, appearing
against the horizon of salt and sand like an exhausted banner, fluttering
through the vanishing streets like a shred of forgotten shadow in broad
daylight. Already the light was waning over the sea, and I seemed to feel a
desire rising in me, a desire of terrible fixity, to make these hurrying hours
shorter still, a desire that the final days should dawn and the last dubious
combat approach: eyes wide open on the thickened wall of the open sea, the

city was breathing in the darkness, breathing with my own lungs, like a
watchman in the shadows, eyes fixed on the night where it was darkest.
I would find Vanessa sometimes inert, sometimes feverish: as if these
afternoons she saved for me, in all the agitation she chose to generate
around herself, disoriented her, and tender and playful as she could
sometimes appear, it seemed to me that this silence and this empty
tranquillity of ours left her disconcerted and uncertain, as if she had been
afraid of finding herself alone too long—not so much with me as with an
image of herself to which my mere presence had wakened her. When the
weather was fine, she often signalled to me from over the canal, in the
abandoned garden where I had found her the morning of the Vezzano
expedition; on gray days, which the season made more frequent now, she
would be waiting for me in the empty salon which still intimidated me. A
chill rose from the calm water and enveloped the silent palace; through the
door open wide onto the canal came at intervals a peaceful noise of oars
dipping into the dead waters. I was sure at this hour to find no one but
Vanessa, and I would linger a moment beneath these cold vaults where my
footsteps echoed on the tiles: it seemed to me as if I were rousing a castle
from its sleep; through the bays giving onto the inner courtyard, the
motionless foliage of the winter garden seemed caught in transparent
crystal. Here the gathered centuries had worn down the corners one after the
next, filtered the light, muffled all things with an impalpable dust until they
had achieved this masterpiece of quietude and somnolence, and nowhere
perhaps better than in this ancient dwelling could one perceive the
profoundly neutralizing genius of the city, which relieved things of any
power of suggestion that might be too lively and managed ultimately to
give the very decor of everyday life the gently balsamic virtue and the
profound insignificance of a landscape. Then I would think back to my visit
to Sagra and the observations Orlando had made to me in the past—and as I
lingered in the halls of this palace which disclosed itself to me in the
silence, as I plunged into the crystal of these dead mirrors and these
benumbed canals, as I breathed this liquid transparency of the autumn,
listened to the panelling creak in the exhausted silence, it seemed to me that

something had been revealed of its spell and of its irremediable doom; as if
all of Orsenna’s age-old effort, all the images of its life the city had
indulged, had sought an almost terrifying loss of tension, a final
equalization in which everything and everyone would be released from their
assertion of offensive presence and of their dangerous electricity: the
excessively humanized forms, too long eroded by a too-continuous friction
among which life itself had been perpetuated, created for it a kind of
garment of ever deeper unconsciousness, through which no contact would
ever rouse it again. Waking each morning, Orsenna put on the world like a
jerkin long worn and shaped to its body, and in that excess of comfortable
familiarity the very notion of its frontiers was dissolved; the city’s faint
consciousness of itself had slowly taken root in an earth now so profoundly
kneaded by humanity that it seemed, ultimately, to have entirely absorbed
it, and its soul, having entered the imprint it had made at the heart of things,
left Orsenna shivering over a void, leaning so far over that it virtually
touched the excessively exact likeness rising from these motionless canals,
like a man who felt himself slowly sliding through to the other side of the
mirror.
When I think back now on those unbroken and monotonous days, full as
they were of expectation and vigilance, like the nausea-crammed languor of
a pregnant woman, I recall with astonishment how little Vanessa and I
seemed to have to say to each other. The ardor which flung me upon her
flagged and waned quickly enough, like the fevers of those sad afternoons
on the lagoons. This palace so recalcitrant to life, with its doors swinging
wide, its cathedral dimness and ecclesiastical echoes, where the shifting
reflections from the canals moved eternally over the walls, was a kind of
temporary encampment for us, an open and inhabitable grove beneath its
motionless shade; yet a perpetual wakefulness prowled there. I never felt I
was entirely alone with Vanessa; on the contrary, lying beside her with my
fingers hanging over the edge of the bed, I sometimes felt, in my irresistible
exhaustion, the uninterrupted pressure of a swift current sliding along with
us: it was sweeping me on as it had at Vezzano, gently launching the
massive palace on the dead waters—such afternoons of swift and feverish

tenderness passed as though swept away on the tide of a river, more silent
and smoother for already showing, far away, the plumed and final collapse
of a cataract. Sometimes I watched Vanessa sleeping at my side,
imperceptibly divided from me as from a shore, and suddenly setting out to
sea with a deeper breathing, as though swept away by a flood of euphoric
fatigue; at these moments she was never naked, but had always, having
fallen away from me, tugged the sheet up to her chin with a swift and
shivering gesture—the reef of her shoulder streaming under her submerged
hair seemed to ward off the imminence of some huge mass: the bed’s long
solemn reach buried her, with all its silent surface ferrying her away;
propped on one elbow beside her, it seemed to me that I was watching the
leeward drift of that heavy head from wave to wave, ever more remote and
inaccessible. I glanced around me, suddenly chilled and isolated by the
ashen daylight that hovered in the room with the canal’s reverberation: it
seemed as if the tide bearing me on had just withdrawn to its lowest mark
and that the room was slowly draining through the black hole of sleep,
haunted by bad dreams. With her arrogant immodesty and aristocratic
unconcern, Vanessa always left the high doors of her room ajar: in the half-
light that fell like a fine ash from the glow of those short days, limbs
outspread, heart heavy, I could feel on my skin something like a cold
exhalation from that series of high, dilapidated rooms; it was as if the fallen
tumult of pillage had forgotten us there, huddled in a corner—as if my ears,
listening in spite of myself, were trying to catch in the distance, in the
darkness, somewhere in the waiting silence of a beleaguered city, the gusts
of a wild hunt. Anxiety would bring me to my feet, and I would find myself
standing in the middle of the room—it seemed to me that the distance
between objects and myself had imperceptibly increased, as if there had
been a movement of faint withdrawal, a sullen and persistent hostility in
things. I groped toward a familiar support that suddenly failed, and my
equilibrium gave way, like the void that opens before us among friends who
already know a piece of bad news. My hand would clutch at Vanessa’s
shoulder in spite of myself; she awakened slowly; on her tilted face I saw
her pale gray eyes floating beneath me, set like jewels in a dark and sleepy

curiosity—those eyes riveted me, drew me like a diver toward their viscous
gleams; her arms stirred, reached for me, groping in the darkness; I
collapsed with her into the fathomless water of a dark pool, a stone tied
round my neck.
I took a lugubrious pleasure in these Maremma nights, every hour of
which I sometimes spent beside her, sinking together—like the lagoon
pilings in each morning’s swell of black water—in the hollow of lassitude’s
breaking wave, as if the loss of my substance which left me empty and
exhausted had matched me with the feverish defeat of the landscape, its
submission and its torpor. Through the saturated atmosphere of this watery
country, the stars no longer twinkled at the window; henceforth it seemed
that not even the faint breath escaping from a collapsed lung could rise from
the prostrate earth; the night pressed down on what seemed no more than
some beast’s den, heavy and hot. Occasionally, behind the sandbar of the
lagoon, an oar groped through the sticky water, or even closer rose the faint
obscene shriek of a rat or some other creature prowling round the larders. I
tossed and turned in that oppressive darkness as if in a hank of greasy wool,
gagged, isolated, gasping for air, wrapped in a suffocating damp; Vanessa
lay beside me, under my hand, like the intensification of a heavier and even
closer darkness; sealed, leaden, blind beneath my palms, she was that night
I could not enter, a tenacious entombment, an ardent and remoter gloom in
the coil of her hair, a great black rose loosened and proffered yet knotted
hard over her heavy heart. It was as if these nights, for all their sopping
sweetness, endlessly nursed a storm that would not break—I would get up,
walk naked through the row of rooms as empty as a forest glade, almost
moaning with loneliness, as if something burdened yet fluttering had
summoned me and at the same time fled from door to door through the
stagnant air of these high moldering galleries; sleep barely closed over my
listening ears, as when we are wakened at night by the remote glow and
murmur of a conflagration. Sometimes, coming back to bed, I saw a shadow
stirring over the floor and, in the gleam of a lamp, Vanessa’s hands, holding
up her tangled hair, making huge moths flutter across the walls—how faint
her features looked in that light!—so weary and pale, still swathed in a

dream too ominous to share, and the lamp’s motionless glow failed to
reassure me. Once her voice rang out, oddly impersonal—a medium’s
voice, a sleepwalker’s that seemed a victim of the visions of some calm
delirium: “You left me alone, Aldo. Why did you leave me alone, all alone
in the dark? I felt you had left me, I was having such a sad dream ...” She
fastened her slumbrous eyes on me: “... You know there are no ghosts in the
palace. Come back, don’t leave me alone.”
I stroked her forehead and kissed the smooth place where the hair began,
overcome with sudden tenderness by that childish voice. “Were you
frightened, Vanessa? Frightened at night, in the heart of your fortress ... And
what a fortress! Good heavens ... Weapons everywhere, even in our
bedroom. And the fourteen Aldobrandis standing guard in effigy.”
Her eyes tight shut, she held out her warm arms and her little girl’s
pouting lips, and I kissed her impetuously, the way you bite into the warm
cheeks of a proffered apple, but the momentary passing of a breeze threw
her back on the bed, teeth chattering, shivering from head to toe. “Oh, I’m
so cold!” She seized my hand, suddenly nervous, serious, her stare drifting
through the open gallery as through some remote undergrowth. “How grim
it is here, Aldo! Why did I ever come back? I hate these bare walls—never
anything to look at but the waves and the fogbanks ...” Her voice was in my
ear. “It’s as if we were in a plundered port, and all the tide gates were
broken. We seem to be cut loose in these huge rooms. As if the ship were
slipping anchor ...”
“But you’re the one who wants to keep all these doors open, Vanessa. It
always seems to me as if we were sleeping in the street.”
“Poor Aldo!” She caressed my hair distractedly. “... How well-behaved
you are—what an obedient little boy!...” A flicker of darkness passed over
her, and she turned her face away. “And suppose we were in the street, even
if everyone were to come through here, through this room, this street—so
what, Aldo ... What difference would it make? What do you care who sees
us?” Her voice rose now like a muffled and mournful confession. “... Who
do you think we’re dealing with here, really? When I came back to

Maremma, I was beside myself with boredom, it was more than I could
bear. I was all knotted up, I wanted to smooth myself out, to become
smooth and hard in my own hands like a stone, the stone you throw in
people’s faces. I wanted to meet with something finally, to break something
—the way you break a pane of glass, in all this suffocation. As for scandals
and provocations, I promise you, things have happened here which went
beyond all bounds. No laughing matter, Aldo: seriously, yes, seriously ...”
She shrugged her shoulders, suddenly exhausted. “... It was like a stone you
throw into the lagoon. There was a little ripple of vague curiosity, and then
the water closed over it. And not because I aimed badly. But some animals
digest even the stones you throw at them, they’re nothing but one huge
digestive tract—a belly, a crop ... And I felt I’d been swallowed up too,
digested. Harmless—you understand what I’m saying? Assimilated—it’s
terrible, that sense of equality as feed, as if you had turned to swill, like
seeds in a stomach—and if there happen to be grains of sand in it, the
creature will digest all the better for that! You contribute ...” She shook her
head in despair. “... Suppose we were out in the street, what if we made love
in the street, what difference would that make? What do you think? People
are watching you here, Aldo, but you must realize it goes no further than
that, no further than eyes. There’s no consideration. And I need that—I
need consideration. Oh yes, that’s what I need—to be considered. But not
by all those eyes. For real. I need to be in the presence ...”
I leaned over her, listening to her, incredulous as this panic cry escaped
from her lips, this vehement flood of words like a spreading pool of blood.
Suddenly she seemed extraordinarily beautiful—it was the beauty of
perdition—beneath her heavy hair and in her armored, chaste severity, like
those cruel and funereal angels who brandish their fiery swords above a
blasted city. She propped herself up on one elbow, and fixing her eyes on
mine, spoke in a calm voice now: “What I’m thinking is what you’re
thinking too, Aldo, isn’t it? I’m sure you understand me.”
I kept my eyes fixed on hers. “I think I do, Vanessa, but by now you must
know there’s a name in Maremma for that consideration of yours. It’s not

exactly a pretty one, and you’ve known all along—you and your people—
what it means.”
She gripped my arm with a calm, nocturnal hand. “Yes. You know it too,
and since you’ve come here you’ve lived for nothing else. That’s why I
visited you in the map room, and why I took you to Vezzano; and what you
have to do now—you know that too, don’t you?”
I couldn’t get back to sleep that night, and I spent the hours in the terrible
nervous exaltation of a first night of love. Vanessa lay beside me as though
drained of her blood, her head severed by a dreamless sleep; spread out
there like a woman in labor, she burdened the bed beneath us. She was the
pollinated efflorescence at the end of this corruption, of this stagnant
fermentation—the bubble that formed, that freed itself, that sought the air in
a mortal yawn and gave up its exasperated soul in one of those sticky
explosions which produce, on the surface of swamps, a kind of poisonous
sputter of kisses.
Daylight was filtering into the room. Vanessa was already up. Dressing
hurriedly, she came and went in the room, and through half-closed eyelids I
noticed that she was watching for the moment when I awoke. In her long
fluttering gray peignoir, she made the uncertain gestures of a bird sheltered
in a cave, looking for its way out, its direction. She came to me, knelt at the
edge of the bed with a tender gesture, put her cool arms around me, and I
seemed to taste salt on her lips. “I’m going to leave you for a few days,
Aldo. You know I have to be back in Orsenna.”
“Already, Vanessa?”
She did not answer, but lay her head on my chest, and I pressed her to me
with a passion I had not yet known.
“It won’t be long. You’ll remember this night? ...” And she added,
lowering her embarrassed eyes: “It was splendid, Aldo ...” And suddenly,
with a clumsy, impulsive gesture, she kissed my hands. “... How strong
your hands are, Aldo. So powerful, so strong...” She rubbed her cheeks
against them, gently. “... Hands to hold joy, or else ruin; the kind of hands

you want to trust yourself to, even if it means death and destruction—even
if it means ending it all.”
“But there’s no question of ending anything, Vanessa. You make me so
happy. Aren’t you happy too?”
She kept her huge eyes fixed on mine. “Oh yes, my darling, yes, I’m
happy. But what I meant was that I’m brave—I’m not afraid of what those
hands will bring me. Even if it means ending everything ...”
She shuddered, scattering her hair around her like a sinister cloud; I
plunged my hands into that warmth, eager to take refuge in that false
security, and my heavy heart felt the minutes drain away, like a schoolboy
nibbling at the seconds separating him from the alarm clock’s icy invasion.
“By the way, I’m taking Marino with me to Orsenna—he asked for a seat
in the car. They’re very interested in him at the Signory these days, no
doubt about that,” she added, her voice heavy with meaning. “In any case,
you’ll be alone at the Admiralty for a few days ...” And she added, in a
strange tone that seemed to me only half ironic: “Supreme commander after
God, Aldo ... Isn’t that the formula you use?”
Once Vanessa had left, I felt depressed and at loose ends, and I decided to
spend another day in Maremma. It was Christmas Eve, and on such a night,
confinement between the Admiralty’s damp walls suddenly seemed more
than I could endure. There would be crowds in the streets, and some instinct
impelled me to mingle with them one last time. In these equivocal days
when I felt the genius of the city wavering, it was the instinct which brings
us up onto the deck, cheek against other fresh, full cheeks, when the ship
trembles on its keel and the huge shock rises toward us from the vibration
of the depths.
Strolling along Maremma’s few shopping streets, it seemed to me that on
the eve of this anticipated holiday, the pulse of the town was throbbing
more feverishly. The tradition in Orsenna’s territories, on Christmas Eve,
was to dress up in motley woolen capes which recalled the desert and
restored the Nativity’s commemoration to its remote Orient, but this year it
seemed to me that the pious disguise had assumed, in many people’s minds,

a double meaning and an aspect of special significance. Among the
processions which filled the streets and glimmered here and there under the
dim lights, I noticed certain figures passing which, more than the age-old
Orient, reminded the eye of the red and gray garments and the full striped
cloaks of the desert tribes whose wear had remained popular in Farghestan.
Their passage provoked shouts from the children in the streets, in whose
eyes this finery had long since characterized the ogre of their bedtime
stories, but it was doubtful that it was only children these costumes were
out to frighten. Much more vigilant glances were suddenly fixed on these
figures—were evidently watching for them before they even appeared; it
was obvious that this equivocal disguise sharpened the already tense
atmosphere, and that the crowd took a kind of morbid delight in the fact, as
one finds a certain charm and perhaps the feeling of a murky self-
realization in the first shudders of a light fever. It seemed as if the crowd
were preening itself at the evocation of this spectre, as at the only mirror
whose reflection still lent it warmth and stability.
“What does our distinguished Observer make of this horde of Bedouins?”
Belsenza suddenly inquired as he bumped into me, apparently accidentally,
as I came around a corner. He was in a grim mood, and evidently in a
coarse vein: “I don’t know what keeps me from pulling off one of those
filthy head-cloths. I suspect they’re covering up more than one runny nose
I’ve been wiping for them lately.”
“I don’t advise it,” I answered a little sharply. “People are nervous—and
this is hardly a day for a police raid.”
“Don’t worry about that—I’ve got other reasons, and better ones, for not
doing anything like that today.” With a mysterious look, Belsenza suddenly
drew me by the sleeve into a doorway. “... You know what they’re saying ...
They’re saying that our holy masquerade’s a convenient excuse for certain
people to come out of hiding, and that behind those veils are some who are
anything but local types taking the air on Christmas Eve ...”
“So?” I could smell, now, that Belsenza had been drinking.

“My orders are to proceed with care. Very well. To hear is to obey—rules
of the game. But I promise you, my dear Observer, that these carnival faces
won’t be coming here to jeer at me for long. They’re getting a little above
themselves, Over There ...” He seized my arm and with a theatrical gesture
drew back even further, more excited minute by minute. “We’ve eaten
enough crow, my dear Observer, as you have ... observed, no? But enough’s
enough. I may lose my job—so be it. But as I was saying just tonight to the
Syndic of the Consulta—there’s a time for patience. Orsenna’s not a
doormat; they’ll find us ready”—the gesture was decisive, and decidedly
noble... . “Come to Saint John Damascene tonight,” he added with a wink,
his voice suddenly furtive, the words mumbled.
I watched him scuttle away, wondering how much he was faking, taking
advantage of the wine to accomplish a certain transition. But the meaning of
this crude language was no longer in doubt: ultimately Belsenza had found
his isolation intolerable. The mechanical drift of this vulgar soul, suddenly
pulling away from its moorings, showed that the waters had now reached a
certain critical level.
I returned to the palace for a dreary dinner: the electrifying contact of the
crowd had made my solitude all the more depressing. When the first bells
tolled for the evening’s ceremonies, I found myself almost involuntarily
making for the rendezvous Belsenza had suggested, in front of the high
Persian domes of Saint John Damascene. My idleness was not the only
reason; the place itself had wakened my curiosity.
In all our southern territories, there was no church so famous, even less
for its striking adaptation of Eastern architecture revealed in the gilded
vermiculation of its domes, than for the stubborn suspicion attached to the
liturgy and the rites it sheltered. Much more profoundly than in the North,
the official church must have compromised here, in the past, with the
heresies and internecine disputes of Eastern Christianity, and the domes of
Saint John Damascene had for centuries represented the chosen rallying
place of all that was venturesome and turbulent in the religious thought of
Orsenna. As the age-old center of a tiny community of Syrtes merchants

reattached by the accident of their voyages to the Nestorian Churches of the
East, then of an initiatory sect whose links with the secret groups of the
“Integral Brothers” in Moslem lands seemed to have been unquestioned, the
church’s legends had made much of the councils sheltered under these
Moorish cupolas and these high black vaults trickling like grottos, under
which had prayed, at the feet of an inscrutable God, Joachim de Flora and
Cola di Rienzi. Ultimately deconsecrated, the incorrigible rebel church had
long remained closed, gradually surrounded, moreover, by a bizarre popular
respect which no doubt derived from its shape and from its exotic and
misunderstood ornamentation, and perhaps too from the sentiment of an
obscure guarantee entered into with regard to the reigning and official
divinity; so that Marino, a splendid connoisseur of Syrtes when he chose,
wittily observed that Maremma had married San Vitale (the cathedral) in
God’s sight, and Saint John Damascene morganatically. Doubtless the
clergy had ultimately decided that the danger of heterodoxy was, all things
considered, less serious than the accumulation of dreams which weighed
upon this forbidden shrine, too alluring and too ostensibly dedicated to the
obscure, for in the last few years, after an expiatory ceremony, the church
had been reopened for worship, and its malign attractions officially
exorcized by what the monastic clergy’s intransigence did not hesitate to
call a disguised capitulation. The course of events did not contradict these
intimations; it was evident—the documentation gathered by Belsenza left
no doubts on the matter—that Saint John Damascene had forthwith turned
out to be, in the very special atmosphere now prevalent in Maremma, the
chosen and not-to-be-monitored meeting place of alarmists and
rumormongers, as well as the fashionable rendezvous of the skeptical rich
winter visitors, whose number in the town was increasing. Vanessa herself,
unbeliever as she was, frequented the place assiduously, and gave no more
than evasive reasons for doing so; she was supposed to be a protectress of
the clergy, whose illuminist tendencies had taken root again as though by
magic—and by her intervention, perhaps. I had the impression that there
hung over this murky brazier, initiated in the highest circles of Orsenna, that
effect of permission from on high so intimately evoked for me by my

instructions from the Signory. Saint John Damascene was one of the
fissures through which the suspect vapors had invaded the streets. A glance
at this crypt that reeked of sulfur would not be amiss.
The church stood near the place where the sandbar thrust out from the
coast, in the heart of a wretched district of fishermen which even on this
solemn day the naive and intentionally pauperized elements of its entire
decoration recalled. Mended nets were hung along the walls, and according
to the ancient custom of the Syrtes mariners, a fishing boat with all its gear,
drawn on rollers to the altar itself, replaced the creche: under its thicket of
lights, the floating cradle strangely transposed this peasant scene to a more
threatened nativity, a berth in peril of the sea. Around the cluster of lights
directly under the dome, the rest of the nave was very dark, but from it
proceeded that magnetic and virtually tactile communication, like the
shimmering air over a scorching highway, which rises from a crowd taking
communion in a state of extreme fervor. This fervor owed nothing to the
bovine rumination of Orsenna’s over-familiar Sundays, which expressed
only the well-being of the carefully counted flock, pinioned to the nostrils
in the maceration of its own odor; what could be sniffed out here and there
in the narrow streets, like one of those exotic smells which suddenly dilate
the nostrils, here suddenly landed in your face like a blow of the fist. A
powerful leaven worked in this crowd and rose high above it into the very
domes; this dense horde among which it drifted shouldered the mystic boat,
which swayed monotonously to the rhythm of a deep and rediscovered
song; on this Christmas Eve borne to the winter’s heart like a nocturnal egg,
it seemed to me that at the breath of these warm and wakened voices I felt
the ice cracking and melting beneath my feet and a kind of sinister fever
welling up from underground—a thaw too sudden, a doomed spring. Like
the squall that stirs the dead leaves, the old Manichean chant rose up from
the crowd, like a wind blowing in from the sea:

Out of the darkness comes
One whom my eyes long for
His Death delivers me
His Cross sustains
O dread Redemption
O sign of my distress
The womb is as the tomb
For the Birth of travail
How poignant it was, that voice which bore the strange and funereal
canticle out of the mists of time, like the rustling of a black veil over this
joyous festival: that intimate voice which so naively alighted in the
lugubrious tonality of its remote past. And I could not hear it without a
shudder for all the secret panic it betrayed. Like a man in danger of death
who finds his mother’s name on his lips, Orsenna at the moment of its as
yet unrecognized dangers was seeking refuge in its deepest Mothers. Like a
ship in the storm, which instinctively presents itself broadside to the waves,
the city was reinvesting all its long history in a single cry, incorporating
itself into it; confronting the Void, Orsenna was thereby assuming its lofty
stature and its deepest difference; and for the first time, perhaps, coiled in a
terrible vehemence, I was hearing the naked timbre of my own voice rising
from its depths.
Meanwhile the chanting stopped: a more vigilant silence showed that the
crowd’s emotion was now waiting for an intelligible sign to reach its
climax, and that the officiating priest would now mount the pulpit. I
watched him with the closest attention: he was wearing the white cassock of
the southern monasteries, and something about him—his vague and myopic
gaze, of a remote gentleness and yet of a maniacal concentration—
suggested those redoubtable visionaries, like coals half devoured by the
flame of mirages and the fire of the sands, whom Orsenna had seen appear
so often on the desert’s fringe. Walking toward the pulpit, he swayed like a
white flame between the rows of the congregation without touching anyone;
then, when he had mounted the steps, the cluster of candles illuminated him
from below and projected a harsh, predatory shadow over his jaws; the

entire face seemed to rise to the unsettled surface of the night; there was an
almost imperceptible stir among the congregation, as intimate as that of
hands just touching one another, and I realized that the time of the prophets
had returned.
He reminded us first in a neutral tone, expressing a kind of hesitation or
exhaustion perhaps, of the particular importance which the liturgy accorded
this festival, and he exulted, as at a particular sign of providential favor, that
it could be celebrated this year with all its habitual luster in Saint John
Damascene, “a voice among all the voices united on this night in the choir
of the Church Militant to which was ever granted a special resonance and,
in the heart of our people, a signal effectiveness.” After this colorless
exordium, the voice paused and then rose, gradually sharper, clearer, like a
blade slowly being drawn from its sheath.
“There is something deeply troubling, and for some among you there is a
kind of bitter mockery, in the thought that this year it should be given us to
celebrate this festival of expectation rewarded and of the divine exaltation
of Hope upon a sleepless earth beneath a sky devoured by bad dreams, and
in hearts seized and anguished as though by the approach of those very
Signs whose redoubtable decree is written in the Book. And yet, in this
scandal of our minds wherein our hearts have no part, I invite you to read,
brothers and sisters, a hidden meaning, and to discern in fear and trembling
what we may anticipate in the profound mystery of the Birth. It is in the
dead of winter, and at the very heart of darkness, that this pledge of our
Hope was bestowed upon us, and in the desert that the Rose of our salvation
has bloomed. On this day which it is now given us to live through once
again, all creation was mute and prostrate, the Word was no longer heard,
and the very sound of the Voice no longer found an echo; in that night when
the stars leaned down from their courses, it seemed that the spirit of Sleep
penetrated all things and that the earth, in the very heart of man, rejoiced in
its own Heaviness. It seemed that the very creation weighed like a crushing
stone upon the sealed breath of its Creator, and that man had laid himself
down at full length upon that stone, like one who gropes in darkness for the

place of his sleep. For a man finds it sweet to pull the covers over his head;
and who among us has not pursued his dreams and supposed he might sleep
the better if he made of his very body a bed, and of his head a pillow? There
are also curtained beds for the mind. Here, on this night, I curse within you
that engulfment. I curse the man thus woven into the things he has done, I
curse his complacency and I curse his consent. I curse an earth too heavy, a
hand which has thrust itself into its own works, an arm benumbed in the
mud it has kneaded. On this night of hope and trembling, on this night of
the world that gapes widest and most uncertainly, I denounce your Sleep,
and I denounce your Security.”
Among the people there was a shudder of attention, and an occasional
cough was choked off in the shadows.
“... Let us turn in our hearts, with trembling and with hope—and this is
easier for us than for many another—to that profoundly disappointing night
which is day, to that dawn which coils yet like a veil around the uncreated
Light. Already the earth is gravid with this anticipated birth, and yet the
earth has chosen to hide itself in a night of troubled counsel and evil omens,
and what walks before it and heralds it as the dust before an army, is a
sinister rumor, bloodshed, and the very omens of destruction and death. On
this very night, centuries ago, men lay waking, and their anguish pressed
round their temples; from door to door they went, smothering each newborn
child snatched from its mother’s womb. They were watchful that hope be
not fulfilled, leaving nothing to chance in order that sleep be not troubled
and the stone be not rolled away and the tomb unsealed. For there are men
to whom it is ever an evil thing that there be a birth; a ruinous and
disturbing thing, blood and cries, pain and impoverishment, a terrible
upheaval—the hour that has not been set, the plans it interrupts, the end of
repose, the sleepless nights, a whole tumult of accidents around a tiny box,
as if the very wineskin of the fable in which the winds have been caged had
just broken open. (And it is true that the birth brings death as well, and the
omen of death. Yet it is the Meaning.) I am speaking to you of a race of men
which is not dead among us, of the race of the closed gate, and of those who

hold that the earth is even now fulfilled and replete, that it has its
sufficiency; I denounce among you the sentinels of an eternal repose.
“O brothers and sisters, in that dreadful uncertainty of the night, how rare
are they who welcome the Birth from the bottom of their hearts. They come
from far in the East, and they know nothing of what is asked of them; they
have for guide only the fiery sign that gleams unknowing in the sky when
there shall be shed the blood of the vines or the blood of transgression and
disaster; theirs is a realm of fabulous wealth, and it appears that upon their
garments in the depths of that night there is a glow and a light still, as when
we see the inestimable accumulation of treasure gently crumbling in the
depths of a cellar. They have set out nonetheless, leaving all behind them,
bearing the rarest jewel in their coffers, and they know not to whom it will
be given them to offer it. Now let us consider, as a great and terrible
symbol, at the heart of the desert, this blind pilgrimage and this offering to
the pure Advent. It is the royal part of ourselves who with them begins its
journey upon this dark way, behind that mute and moving star, in pure
expectation and in the profound mystery of their course. In the depths of
that night, already, they are on the move. I call upon you to enter into their
Meaning and to seek with them blindly what will be. In this indeterminate
moment when it seems that all things are held in suspense and that the
moment itself hesitates, I call upon you to take part in their supreme
Desertion. Blessed be he who can rejoice in the heart of the night, who can
rejoice only because he knows that such darkness is great with
consequence, for the shadows will bear fruit, for the light will be lavished
upon him. Blessed be he who leaves all behind him and lends himself
without pledge or warranty, and who hears deep in his heart and in his belly
the summons of the obscure deliverance, for the world will wither beneath
his gaze in order to be reborn. Blessed be he who abandons and deserts
himself, and in the heart of darkness itself no longer worships anything but
the deepest fulfillment ...”
Once again the preacher paused; his voice now rose more slowly, muffled
by the gravity within it.

“... I speak to you of the One who was not expected, of the One who has
come even as a thief in the night. I speak to you of Him here, in a moment
of darkness and on an earth that may be doomed. I speak to you of a night
when there must be no sleeping. I bring you tidings of a dark birth, and I
announce unto you that the hour has now come when the earth will once
again be weighed in His hand, and the imminent moment when to you as
well it will be given to choose. O may we not deny our eyes to the star that
gleams in the deep night and realize that from the very depths of dread,
stronger than dread, the inextinguishable voice of desire rises in the dark
passage. My thought turns with you, as to a deep mystery, to those who
came from the depths of the desert in order to worship in his manger the
King who bore not peace but a sword, and to lull the Burden so heavy that
