the earth shuddered beneath its weight. I prostrate myself with them, I
worship with them the Son at His mother’s breast, I worship the hour of the
agonizing passage, I worship the Way opened and the Gateway of the
morning.”
The crowd suddenly wavered as it knelt, with that deliberate and almost
indolent subsidence of ripe wheat beneath a stroke of the scythe, and all the
spaces in the church surged together, overwhelming me beneath a powerful
and fierce murmur of prayers. The congregation prayed shoulder to
shoulder, in a formidable immobility, paralyzing the air in those high vaults
into a block so compact that it tightened round my temples, and the very
breath suddenly seemed to drain from my lungs. The smoke of the candles,
suddenly, stung my eyes. Between my shoulders I felt a heavy burden, and
that sort of bewildering nausea one feels watching a man who is bleeding to
death.
I did not look for Belsenza in that crowd. In the emotion that had seized
my throat, I imagined with disgust—an inexpressible disgust—the way his
slow and myopic gaze would scrape over me, like a blade seeking some
chink in the armor. I leaped into a boat for hire—the heavy and humid night
lured me on; instead of returning to the palace, I ordered the boatman to
take me across the lagoon.

This cold and salty darkness did me good. Before me, the Aldobrandi
palace, entirely in darkness, drifted on the water like an ice floe; to my left
the few lights of Maremma plunged a shrunken constellation into the very
sea, as if, once the land was devoured, the horizon itself had withdrawn
before the gulping swarm of stars. Maremma seemed to be melting into this
nocturnal mass, dissolving there, a city vanished into its hour and its figure,
engulfed beneath the beacon of these tiny nails of fire.
I gave myself up for a long while to this promised night, fleeing myself
in the heart of its mystery and its remoteness. The dampness dripped from
my coat in cold beads; in the circle of faint light projected by the boat’s
lantern, the lagoon lapped tirelessly against the planks. I gradually sank into
somnolence. The image of Marino sitting in his office at the Admiralty
flashed before me—I could see his strange smile of cunning and
connivance; it wavered in front of me with the rhythm of the boat like a
man walking on the waves, like a ridiculous clown; then the oscillation
diminished; for a second the face remained before me, motionless, and I felt
his taciturn and fixed eyes meeting my own, but at that very moment I had
fallen asleep.
I found the Admiralty less somnolent than might be supposed after the
festivities of the night before. The Redoubtable was at its mooring, its decks
cleared of their usual disorder; men were busy with the heap of coal.
Fabrizio, who came out of the great hall, hurriedly turned back upon
catching sight of me, and suddenly I heard, exploding from inside, a
deafening concert of official whistles, as when the admiral joins the fleet at
anchor under all flags. “Everyone on deck. Captain’s here!” shouted
Fabrizio.
I realized that the joke had been carefully worked out. The three acolytes
were waiting for me, swords drawn, stiff in a perfidious alignment; I could
even make out a few notes of the official anthem. Saluted by hurrahs, I
immediately ordered a distribution of liquid provisions. There were great
slaps on the back, and strangely moved, I sank back into that unthinking
camaraderie—we were young, the four of us, and so spirited, so strong, so

united in the sun of that bright dry morning, that I felt like embracing them
all.
“... And he shall com-mand the seas ...” Fabrizio sang out, with a whistle
of respectful admiration. “Just between us, it’s high time you got here. First
of all, let me give you the briefings from the Holy See,” he added, suddenly
serious as he handed me the envelope. On account of his patriarchal
deliberation and his decidedly sedentary tastes, this was the nickname we
sometimes gave Marino, among ourselves.
Marino’s message was short, and seemed to have been written in a hurry.
In his sensitive friendship, he had not bothered to use the official forms, and
somehow, as he made this simple observation, his kindness and his trust
suddenly filled my heart like a warm wave, so sudden and so immediate
that I felt I must have been blushing. Once again I recognized that
astonishing virtue he had of impregnating things merely by touching them,
and in a simple phrase so like the sound of his voice, to revive the very
music—yes, the touching and clumsy melody which was evoked by his
entire frank conduct, as if his fingers, on everything he touched, could
produce only the simplest and the most affecting chords. He informed me,
in putting me in charge of the Admiralty, that he had given orders for a
night patrol, and that he had no doubt that I would keep a good watch.
“Take care of the Redoubtable,” he added, “I always worry about those
damned shoals, and our fleet is no longer brand new. Make sure the men
take soundings before turning into the channel—it tends to silt up; Fabrizio
stranded me out there last time. All those fellows are only scatterbrains who
think they know something about navigating, but you’ll be there, and I can
rest easy. Don’t forget—without taking any special trouble—that brandy is
to be issued only after night watches, and don’t let Fabrizio tell you
otherwise. Whereupon may San Vitale (this was Marino’s patron and, I
think, the providence of our coastal waters) watch over you at sea as on
land.”
“You’re taking me, Aldo, promise me that,” Fabrizio shouted behind me,
his hands cupped around his mouth, in the doorway. “Be a friend. We’ve

drawn lots, the three of us. I’ll take the Redoubtable anywhere you want,
up, down, sideways ...”
The day was spent in feverish comings and goings; soon I was
surrounded by open cabinets in my disorderly room, as if I were preparing
to take a long trip. This agitation kept me afloat, like the swimmer
supported by his movements; careful not to interrupt it, I was half losing
sight of what was collapsing beneath me. Suddenly I recalled with a sense
of timidity and embarrassment that I was going to occupy Marino’s cabin;
this sleepwalker’s agitation and these yanked-open drawers had merely
deceived my need to get on board right away. I was like the tardy passenger
who hears the whistle blowing, and I was afraid of seeing the Redoubtable
leave without me; I wanted to be out to sea already. I hurried to the dock,
suddenly filled with a miraculous certainty of seeing the ship there, a
wakened creature gently shuddering beneath the undulations of its bright
smoke, yet disappointed, like a child wakened from his dream, to find it so
paltry and so small.
The Redoubtable was deserted—a huge insect of ill omen, inhabited, in
this marshy drowsiness, only by the imperceptible and gnawing trepidation
that came from its lower depths. I scarcely knew the vessel—during my
first night on patrol, I had not budged from the bridge—and I wandered
aimlessly over the sun-baked deck where the iron of the railings was
already warm under my hand, intimidated by this demanding machinery,
like gears into which one would be afraid to stick a finger. I tried Marino’s
key on several doors; the creaking noise of the iron plates underfoot was
disturbing in that silence; the atmosphere of the dim corridors suffocating; I
was about to give up, exasperated, when a little iron door finally gave way
and opened onto a room so tiny that I almost bumped into the opposite wall,
where an old service cap that I knew very well was hanging.
A bright light penetrated the cabin through the rear porthole, but before I
had had time to notice the slightest detail, Marino’s presence surged up until
I had to close my eyes in that odor of cold tobacco and dried flowers that
burst upon me as in his Admiralty office, as extraordinarily intimate as the

effluvium of a mummy from which the swathings are unwound. Dazed, I
gazed around me, once again seized by that feeling of a presence larger than
life which always seemed to nail me to the floor when I confronted Marino.
It was hardly enough to say that the room was in his image, or that it was so
in the very fashion of those Egyptian hypogea with their walls painted with
doubles and with a withered garland of exploits hung round the empty
sarcophagus. Yet there was very little inside this little room. From the usual
weapon rack hung Marino’s familiar pipes; on a little table, a narrow-
mouthed vase of the green Syrtes faience still held a few withered flowers;
the fat book of Nautical Instructions served to wedge it fast and now stuck
to it, covered with a film of mildew. Beside a pair of horn-rimmed
spectacles lay an open ledger, which I glanced into: Marino had taken the
farm accounts to sea with him, to check them over. I suddenly had such an
intense sensation of that confined tranquillity, like that of an open
herbarium from which the age-old pollen still gently teases the nostrils, and
which moored the ship to land more solidly than its hawsers, that I opened
the porthole with a desperate gesture, as if I were struggling to get some
fresh air, and then lingered a moment as I considered the contents of a tiny
glassed-in frame hanging beside it. There was an old, yellowed diploma of
the Navigation Academy with its date and, all around it, Marino’s
decorations: the Syrtes Medal (fifteen years of faithful service in the
desert), blue with red stripes; the Rescue at Sea Ribbon; and the noble red
circle of the Medal of Saint Jude, which is conferred, as everyone in
Orsenna knows, only on those wounded in action. I examined these for a
moment—all of them extraordinarily faded, of the consistency of dry leaves
in their glass reliquary. I tried to imagine Marino poring over his medals
under the glass, with that naive pucker of frowning attention so
characteristic of him: such remoteness, such dizzying distance kept from
oneself bewildered me; I decided to stretch out on the narrow cot for a
minute, uncomfortable as it was in this dreamlike chamber; a faint
movement on the ceiling made me start: it was the compass needle which
Marino consulted from his bed, stirring over my head like an awakened
animal. The room was pursuing me; I got up and for an idle moment leafed

through the Nautical Instructions: filaments of mildew stuck to the damp,
smelly pages: judging from appearances, Marino had been navigating by
guesswork for a very long time; once again he rose up before me, an
hallucination out of this book with its sealed pages—heavy, burdensome
even, sowing the earth with calm, his eyes fixed on what is close at hand,
yet with the almost alien glow of their mysterious sick man’s anxiety ... A
footstep suddenly rang out on the plates over my head: the notion of being
surprised here vexed me, and I went to the mirror to adjust my jacket; for a
moment my gaze stuck, I plunged my eyes into its gray depths and it
seemed to me that out of such exact images an infinity of exactly
superimposed ones slid away, endlessly replacing each other at tremendous
speed before my eyes. I dropped the porthole cover on the glaring light, and
after a moment’s hesitation pushed closed the door on the scent of faded
flowers, with the wary gesture with which you seal off a dead man’s room.
I went to the Admiralty office to give some orders. I was taking Fabrizio,
a plan long since decided on; I checked that the usual provisions and
ammunitions were on board. Beppo, now quartermaster following the farm
shutdowns, raised an almost invisible eyebrow: these were not customary
orders; they were superfluous, I realized with embarrassment: nothing was
ever touched on the Redoubtable; I imagined the unbroached cases, lined up
under the faint greenish mildew: the loaded revolver, forgotten underneath
the papers at the bottom of the drawer in the night table.
“You expect to get into something?” Fabrizio smiled, bustling around me,
always excited by preparations, even those for a game of cards.
“Fool! ...” and I punched him on the shoulder. “You’d be only too glad to
go across only one way, wouldn’t you?” I added slyly.
“Bah, crossing ... You know, with these seamarks ...” Fabrizio shrugged,
annoyed, pointing to the stark white fortress. “Childsplay now, even at
night, that’s what Marino doesn’t realize. And they won’t award me the
Danger at Sea Medal: how unfair can you get! ... All the same, it’ll be fine
tonight, out there on the Calmed Sea” (the Calmed Sea—the Sea of Syrtes
in Admiralty slang).

Fabrizio rubbed his hands as he scrutinized the sky, with Marino’s
familiar nod. There was more than the usual jubilation in his manner,
however controlled—the kind you find in very young children on the eve of
a long-awaited holiday.
By noon, everything was ready, the last preparations settled, down to the
merest detail: what little work there was to do had melted away in my
hands, irresistibly twitched away from me by some alien force: the coil of
rope between the harpooneer’s fingers. Our sailing was set for very late—
the sea would be high only after dark. I was facing an intolerable vacuum. I
had my horse saddled; my nerves were devouring me, and this was a good
excuse for solitude.
The air was dry and very clear now; the sun-flooded sands as crisp as any
frost, and the dry kelp glistened. I remembered just in time that we still had
some money to collect at Ortello: the remains of the pay for our work teams
—this would take me long enough to be away with good reason. The gray
path threaded its way through the heath, strangely distinct under the sun in
this vacant landscape between the slopes of kelp where the sea wind
rustled; a deafening hum of insects rose from the warming soil. When I had
climbed the first dune, I turned back toward the sea: an ink-blue half circle
ringed the pale beaches more closely at each step my horse took. Beneath
me I saw the Admiralty, tiny already, nestled in the heat like a new-laid egg,
dissolving in the cruel shimmer of the salt flats; a vast white reverberation,
effervescent now, devoured the fortress that stuck up like a bank of living
chalk above its square of black shadow. The Redoubtable lay against the
finger of the jetty, stuck to it like the jewel in a ring. Everything rested in a
petrified immobility, and already the landscape had drunk down man as
thirsty sand consumes water; only the heavy smoke of the little ship, rising
out of its stack like a feathery staff, produced a note of imperceptible
alertness in this desert, and the smell of a cheap kitchen. The landscape
plunged behind the curtain of sand; the faint smoke rose a few seconds over
the horizon, entirely alone in the calm air. I put my horse at a trot on the

hard ground. In this transparent air I felt myself burning like dry wood; my
whole body was on the move, intensely, dangerously alive.
The Ortello farm appeared in the distance on the side of a steep hill,
surrounded by olive groves and bushes, its long stone buildings scaling the
slope like huge gray steps. The dusty ground in front of the entrance was
quite empty, and the shed was vacant where the heavy, greasy wool hung,
and where I had participated in more than one hunt breakfast. It seemed to
me that the sight of my uniform, which must have been familiar enough,
was causing a certain disturbance—at once respectful and alarmed—among
the farmhands dozing in the barnyard’s narrow shade, as if this worn
emblem had regained its full meaning, as if all at once it too had been
scrubbed clean of an invisible patina.
“Master Carlo will be glad to see you,” the foreman said, taking my
bridle. “We haven’t had much news here, since ...”
He stopped, embarrassed, and hurried away to announce my arrival.
I found old Carlo on the veranda overlooking the sea. A trellised canopy
sheltered him where a vine grew up the side of the building; over the low
wall, a rectangle of dazzling reddish earth sizzled in the sun; in the distance,
above a bank of dunes, the sea’s mineral blue lay out in a line scarcely
thicker, yet of an unexpected acuity and polish: the watchman’s gaze
through his arrow slit. Old Carlo was stretched out on a wicker chair in a
shady corner, the very image of extreme old age; a faint, patient respiration
seemed to glow faintly over his huge inert body, like an ember forgotten
among the cinders of a forge. Near him, on a low wicker table, were set a
glass and one of those shiny, dew-beaded pitchers of the South which keep
their coolness all afternoon. Now and then the sea birds’ cries could be
heard, harsh and abrupt, more desolate here on these ashen plains than
anywhere else.
“Come all alone, Aldo?”
The old man squinted, a sign of welcome. He was like a planet gone cold,
no longer reacting except by a few cracks, a few folds running over the
surface of his skin.

Without waiting for my answer, he pointed behind me. The foreman
reappeared almost immediately and set down a sack of coins on the table. I
turned toward the old man, startled by this behavior, and took his hand with
an attempt to smile, but the smile froze as if it had met with a pane of glass:
that face was already staring back with the insolent indifference of death.
“I didn’t come as a bill-collector, Carlo,” I murmured.
“Sure, Aldo, I know you didn’t ...” The old man patted the back of my
hand. “But you see, everything was ready. It’s time to put the accounts in
order,” he added in a strange tone of voice, turning his eyes away as if the
reverberation of those scorched plains were hurting them.
Suddenly he met my eyes again in an extraordinarily inquisitive manner,
still patting my hand in silence, as if he had dislodged a piece of news
which had vanished, watching now for its appearance on my face. “My
time’s coming,” he said after a moment’s silence, “nothing you can do
about it. I tell you, Aldo, the desert wears a man out!” With this last
sentence came a gleam of malice in his eyes: he didn’t expect to be
believed. “... My time’s come,” he repeated, his voice pensive and bitter,
“and now it’s come too soon.”
“No, give yourself another ten years, Carlo—we’ll talk about it ten years
from now. ‘Not before the olive trees have grown,’ you know the old Syrtes
saying ... And Beppo told us you’ve been planting them.”
The voice froze my artificial laugh. “No, Aldo, it’s now, and it’s too
soon.”
The old man drank a mouthful of water and said nothing more. You could
hear the birds now, flying over the dunes: the tide was rising.
“All right, Carlo, when it comes, it comes ...” I felt my voice was
different now, and I touched his shoulder in an impulse of genuine
friendship. “... Isn’t everything in order here?”
His face turned toward the horizon where the sand met the sea.
“Everything’s in order. Only I’m tired of order, Aldo. That’s what it is.” He
squeezed my hand with an almost unconscious movement. “Things are over
for me, Aldo, try to understand. My work’s been blessed, as they say, and

everything you see out there—that’s good land I’ve got now. I’m bowing
out with more than my share of rightful property ...” His eyes drilled into
mine. “... If you knew how it ties a man down! I stuck my thread
everywhere I could, and now I’m rolled up in my cocoon, that’s what it is:
rolled up, tied up—packed up. Here I am, and I can’t move my arms or my
legs; you think that’s sickness, Aldo? Not two weeks ago I could still outrun
a rabbit. But I’ve done too much for what’s left, that’s what it is. Once
you’ve understood that, it’s over—the spring breaks. That’s what it means
to grow old, Aldo; what I’ve done has fallen on top of me, I can’t lift it any
more ...” And he repeated with a meaningful look: “When you can’t lift
what you’ve done, that’s the lid of your coffin.”
A servant girl brought out food, then lingered with one excuse or another.
This mute stratagem, after the foreman’s, ended by taking a suspicous turn:
it was as if they didn’t want to let the old man out of their sight for too long
at a time; I noticed the murderous gaze the old solitary fixed on the back of
the girl’s neck.
When she finally left, Carlo kept silence, absolutely motionless, and it
seemed to me that the respiration in that huge body had become more
difficult. I half stood up, disturbed, my mouth very close to his ear: “Don’t
you feel well?”
“Not well, not sick, Aldo, enough for what I still have to do. But it’s hard
to breathe out here, you know; not enough air.”
“You couldn’t be much closer to the sea.”
The old man shrugged, bitter, stubborn, and gave up trying to make me
understand. “No, there’s no air. There’s never been any air. It’s Marino who
says different.”
“Why did you send away his men, Carlo?” My question was out before I
could think of checking it. The old man stared at me intently, there was life
in his glance again; evidently I had brought up something he enjoyed
remembering.
“He didn’t like that much, did he, Aldo? He came to see me right away. I
can tell you, he was one surprised fellow.”

“Why did you do that to him?”
“Why ... ?” Suddenly Carlo’s face darkened and he seemed to fall back
into a kind of lethargy. “Hard to make you understand ...” He tried to
reflect. “... Don’t go thinking I don’t like Marino; he’s my oldest friend. I’ll
tell you. When I was a boy, our old servant used to sleep in the loft, without
any light. He was so used to it he could walk in the dark without even
feeling his way, as fast as in broad daylight. Well, you know, the temptation
was too much for me: there was a trapdoor up there, so I left it open ...” The
old man seemed to think for a moment, with difficulty. “... I don’t like
people being so sure things will always be the way they are.” He half closed
his eyes and began nodding, as if he were falling asleep. “...And maybe it’s
not such a good idea, things always staying the way they are.”
During the last few minutes, the foreman had resumed his mute watch at
the end of the veranda. I understood I was outstaying the time permitted. I
said goodbye to the old man, strangely moved. “Goodbye, Aldo, we won’t
be seeing each other again,” he said, resting one hand on my shoulder.
“Don’t worry too much about Marino,” he added with a shrug and a look of
amusement. “He’s the kind of man who’s never known how to say yes.” He
stared at me a moment, still nodding: “Marino’s never known how to say
yes.”
The foreman led my horse to me. He thanked me for my visit and told me
with some insistence how pleased the old man had been to see me—the
kind of polite excuses made for a child or an invalid. I felt surprised and
shocked: it had been clear Carlo wasn’t so bad as that. “I see you’re quite
concerned about him,” I said a little sharply as I got into the saddle.
“We have to keep an eye on him: he’s failed a good deal lately. He’s
losing his wits ...” He brought his mouth close to my ear, speaking in a
guilty, muffled voice: “The night before last he almost set the place on fire.”
The sun was already sinking as I took the path back to the Admiralty. The
oncoming evening brought silence to the plain. On this omnipotent
horizontality, movements were rapidly absorbed, so that they had the short
and incoherent agitation, the insignificance of a sleeper’s gestures. From

time to time a sand jerboa crossed the path, zigzagging before plunging into
the brush, sending up fine plumes of dust; under the birdless sky, this lump
of noiseless life hugged the ground, rendering the evening’s immobility
almost stormy, seeming to flatten itself out under an invisible dome of fear.
I returned from Ortello in a sombre mood; I understood that in spite of
myself I had studied this landscape for a sign, the way you instinctively
raise your eyes upon hearing something too serious, hoping to catch the
reassuring denial of a cloud across the sun or a flower swayed by the wind;
suddenly open to all the burdensome confirmation this evening afforded, it
seemed to me that I knew now that old Carlo was going to die.
Dinner that night was very silent. Giovanni and Roberto were as useless
as a boat run aground; Fabrizio kept rushing in and out, busy with the last
preparations for getting under way. It was a farewell dinner, and I should
have liked to preserve those moments of calm which Fabrizio was tearing
into tiny pieces; my heart laden with friendship and with habit, I felt cut off
from this banal and gentle community: I knew already that this meal was
our last. As soon as dinner was over I lit my lantern and went to the map
room for the ship’s papers and sailing documents. This formality filled me
with uneasiness: I had known from the start I would perform it only at the
last moment.
It was already very dark in the almost underground chamber; once the
door was closed, all the chill of winter and solitude flowed over me out of
this icy heart, yet for all the grim welcome of this shivering confinement,
once again everything dissolved into the overwhelming, ever-new sensation
that this place—more than anywhere else in the world—was crammed with
that imminent existence which distinguishes a baited trap from a stone. The
sneer of this cave-like atmosphere alarmed me; I kept my lantern beam on
the ground—I was moving fast—temples throbbing, hands nervous, turning
around, sometimes, in spite of myself toward that yawning void which
swallowed me up as if something had suddenly grimaced on the wall. I
gathered up the maps hurriedly; at the heart of that unapproachable silence
which my furtive gestures sullied like a faint pattering of mice, I was

ashamed—ashamed as I had never been in front of human beings. I now
belonged to this irrecusable violation; I backed out of the doomed room,
and I could feel how pale I was, holding the roll of maps pressed against my
chest like a tomb robber impelled by naked hunger who feels the dreamed-
of jewels in his hands and the force of the curse already slowly curdling his
blood. The sea wind had risen with the dark, and its great cold layer
surrounded me as soon as I came out onto the heath; I wrapped myself in
my sea cloak; at the end of the dock a cluster of little lights was flickering
around the Redoubtable, whose lanterns were being set: a forgelike red
glow flared now and again under its puffs of smoke and sent icy black
reflections along the coal heap, like a damned dawn. I quickly shook hands
with Roberto and Giovanni in the darkness—the indistinct faces made their
voices sound shorter and lower—someone shouted “good trip!” in a voice
which the night’s freshening wind blew out like a torch. Everything was
black on the bridge; under my feet I felt the ship’s faint tremor, its blind
force which was already boring through the dark. The Redoubtable gently
backed away from the dock; a gleam of calm water widened in front of the
quay, a chain clanked on the stones of the jetty—already, the idle voices
there were being severed from us. A darker mass rose up in front of me
toward the bow—I tried to see what it was and did not recognize Fabrizio,
frozen at attention, his huge oilskin cloak bunched up around him, seeming
to rivet him to the deck; the cold black smell of coal suddenly reached me
on a puff of wind, then a brutal shower swept away the few remaining lights
as if a curtain had been drawn, and the opaque night enveloped us.
OceanofPDF.com

9
An Expedition
On a night so charged with the likelihood of a storm, the sea off Syrtes was
unrecognizable. Though we were still in the lee of the sandbars, the waves
were already swelling with a long, black rhythm, evidently of remote
origin, its very slackness menacing among the disheveled reeds. A cold
wind, pure as if it had passed over snow-fields, grew chillier by the
moment, slapping the Redoubtable amidships, one harsh blow after the
next. And through this chaos of whistlings, pitchings and scrapings, our
black shadow slid alongside like a clearing of silence. A dim light bathed
the bridge; the movements of the men of the watch were numbed by the
densities of water. Next to me, Fabrizio maintained a statue’s silence,
occasionally touching his delicate instrument with a pianist’s fingers. His
incomprehensible and precise gesticulation fixed my gaze in this jumbled
darkness, like the arabesques of a surgeon’s hand wandering above his field
of white linen. Suddenly Fabrizio turned his head and spoke to me in a
voice whose warm-hearted simplicity restored my own strength—the way it
feels when blood flows back into your face—and I realized that this sweat-
drenched countenance before me was smiling.
“It’s the narrows ... Scared, Aldo? Well, if Marino hadn’t brought me out
here once, I’d say we were in over our heads.”
I stared back, stunned. “You’d never seen the new channel before?”
He lay one hand on my arm. “Now that we’re through ... I didn’t want to
tell you,” he added, in a lower voice. “I wanted to come.”
I looked at him again, with curiosity this time, my eyes squinting against
the wind, while he turned his head away. It seemed to me that the Admiralty
was suddenly falling far behind, lost on the horizon somewhere on the other
side of that wall of fog.

“You can go down for a rest now,” he added, his voice tense. He
squeezed my arm, and I guessed he was smiling in the dark. “I’m taking
over now. Everything will be all right.”
It was cold and damp in Marino’s cabin. I finally managed to light the
lantern that began swaying in the ceiling, shifting sleepy shadows across the
tiny room. I stretched out on the berth without taking off my clothes. I could
hear a faint sound of rushing water that seemed to come from a great
distance into this confined space, yet its persistence kept me from sleeping,
like a finger scratching at the porthole. Marino’s sea cloak beat
monotonously against the wall. On the ceiling compass my eye
mechanically followed the Redoubtable’s sinuous trajectory through the
straits: the distant engines throbbed faintly, with the endless halts and
gradual resumptions of a night train: as if the emptiness and ennui of the
motionless plains around us had annexed this vacant sea to themselves, this
close and dusty cabin with its sweetish smell of petroleum, like some
abandoned lamp-works. For a moment the memory of the Aldobrandi
palace, its doors open to the damp night, recurred to me like a scent of
flowers in the darkness, and I pressed my lips against Vanessa’s loose hair
which the night seized and swelled in our bed like the tide playing with a
clump of kelp. Then I rolled my cloak around me and began a gloomy vigil.
I pushed back the bunch of dried flowers and the volumes of Nautical
Instructions on the little table and unrolled the bundle of maps. Seeing once
again, under the cabin’s dirty yellow light, those contours which had
become so familiar to me, I experienced a feeling of unreality, so strange
did it seem that these armed symbols, which I had interrogated so long in
the depths of their underground reliquary, were now here, unfolded, for use.
Fabrizio was following the coastal channels; I consulted my watch,
estimating the ship’s speed, and touched the place on the map where we
must be by now: almost exactly abeam of Maremma. I pushed open the
porthole, taking the incredulous pleasure of a child testing the mechanism
of a new toy: a gust of wind dashed at my face and shoulders, like a pack of
dogs struggling behind the door; at the farthest reach of the horizon, on the

edge of the vast inky tillage which flung its glistening clods past my eyes, a
rough semicircle of lights ringed the guarded water like the floats of a seine:
the pacifying lights of Maremma, gentle as the open eyes of a corpse,
mounting their sleepy guard over the tamed sea. The screw throbbed more
faintly, the Redoubtable’s whistle exploded above me, terrifying and
ludicrous in the midst of this black void, like a single elephant, trunk raised,
trumpeting in a clearing; the ship turned gently, the lights of Maremma
shifted faster and faster to starboard; now there was nothing left but sea and
sky, imperceptibly clearer above the black water.
I gazed at that sky gradually diluted by the dawn, as though brushed
beneath the horizon’s farthest edge by the palpitation of a faint fan of light.
The night which had risen for the first time over Syrtes recurred to my
memory: like a mist leveling off mountains and valleys, its indistinct folds
concealed the adventitious land. Orsenna was transmigrating, evaporating
in this dust of stars by which Fabrizio was reading our route. They gleamed
inexhaustible and undeterred. For a new night after so many nights,
Orsenna was sprawling in the bed of its stars, dissolving at ease in the
figure of its constellations, entrusted wholly, like a dead planet, to sidereal
intimacy and inertia. I remembered an odd remark Orlando had made, on
one of those stifling evenings of summer’s end when we were trying to get
a breath of air on the rampart walks: that on the calmest nights in a foreign
place one could hear the warm breathing of an animal, the singular beating
of a heart, but that on Orsenna’s clear nights our consciousness became that
of a child at the breast, so that we could catch the humming of the spheres.
A rougher pitching of the ship sent Marino’s cloak sliding to the floor
beside me, and I realized I was smiling: I sensed how soundly on this night
our captain must be sleeping.
The Redoubtable had resumed its regular and sleepy course; beneath my
porthole, which was quite close to the stern, the water was furrowing a deep
trough the length of the hull, standing out from the ship’s side as from a
ploughshare. Darkness concealed the flat shore, though it was still so close I
could hear a dog barking: the shepherds sometimes abandoned for weeks at

a time these creatures the solitude reduced to half-savagery as they
wandered up and down the beaches. The desolate howling rose high in the
calm night air, interrupted by uneven silences, as if seeking an answer from
the depths of this wilderness, an echo that never came. I recognized that cry.
The walls of the Aldobrandi palace had sent it back to me: it was not a cry
of fear, it was not a call for help, but it passed high above our heads, and the
plains of the sea would not muffle it. It was the lofty moan of a being who
falters on the brink of the void, the naked provocation rising at the heart of
every desert, even the habitable one of Orsenna. Vanessa’s smile, that smile
of a fallen angel which seemed to float upon vertigo, suddenly appeared to
me in the harsh accents of this wandering lament: what remained for me to
do, I would now do.
Once again I sat down at the table and carefully began measuring certain
distances on the sea maps. However routine, however automatic I strove to
make this task, I was nonetheless astonished to find the distances I was
measuring so slight—as if the shores of that closed sea had shrunk in a
semicircle before our prow, suddenly almost within arm’s reach, and all at
once I seemed to understand, to recall my reveries in the map room, how
Orsenna’s somnolence and the loosened grip of its hand had finally
drowned even the closest frontiers in distant mists: there is a scale of
actions which, to the resolute eye, brutally contracts the spaces distended by
dreaming. Farghestan had raised before me the shoals of dream, the
fabulous beyond of a forbidden sea; now it was a steep fringe of rocky
coast, two days’ sail from Orsenna. The final temptation, the irremediable
temptation, was taking shape in this tangible phantom, in this sleeping prey
beneath fingers already open.
When memory takes me back—raising for a moment the nightmare veil
which hangs over the embers of my destroyed country—to that vigil when
so many things were held in suspense, I can still feel the fascination of the
intoxicating mental speed which seemed to consume the seconds and
minutes, and I momentarily recover the still singular conviction that consent
had been granted—or rather its grimacing parody—to penetrate the secrets

of those moments which reveal the inspired to themselves. Even today,
when I examine my hateful history, in default of a justification which
everything denies me, seeking at least a pretext to ennoble an exemplary
disaster, it sometimes occurs to me that a nation’s history is occasionally
strewn, as by black stones, with certain figures of darkness, figures doomed
to a special execration less for an excess of perfidy or betrayal than for the
faculty which distance in time seems to give them—the faculty to dissolve
until they actually unite their substance with the public agony or the
irreparable action which they have entirely assumed beyond what is usually
considered human capacity. As for these shadowy figures whose contours
and personal singularities time powerfully erodes faster than all others, the
universal violence of such repudiation tells us that it partakes—much more
than the colorless civic blame dispensed by history books—of the obsessive
character of remorse, and that it reopens the wound of a complicity
intimately experienced; this is because the force which drives these haunted
figures toward the margins of history, where the light falls more obliquely,
is that of an invalid beset by delirious dreams who feels not a freezing
moral obligation but the grip of a fever wasting his blood: the need to
deliver himself from evil. Such men have not been guilty, perhaps, of
anything but a particular docility to what an entire nation—exhausted after
the fact by having put in their hands, on the scene of the crime, the weapon
of the crime—refuses to acknowledge: that it has nonetheless for a moment
incarnated its will in them; the spontaneous withdrawal which isolates them
betrays not so much their personal infamy as the multiform source of the
energy which has momentarily transmuted them into projectiles. More
closely woven into the very substance of a whole people than if they were
its cast shadow, such men are truly its damned souls; the half-religious
terror which makes them loom larger than life derives from the revelation,
of which they are the vehicle, that at any moment a condenser can intervene
through which millions of scattered and unacknowledged desires are
monstrously embodied as will. The gaze which passes through these figures
is lost at a depth where one fears to read what can be seen; the fascination
they exert proceeds from our suspicion that the privileged communication

they have been granted has raised them, for the few seconds it was worth
the trouble of being, to a supreme instance of life: we bob like corks on a
seething ocean which at every moment exceeds us, but a moment of the
world in the full light of consciousness has culminated in them—the stormy
world of millions of scattered detonations has discharged them in a vast
flash of brilliance; their universe, converging from all directions upon them
around a passage in which we imagine that profound security is inextricably
mingled with anguish, has been for a second that of the bullet in the rifle
barrel.
The still-open porthole clanged loudly against the wall, as if the ship had
suddenly changed direction; as I turned around to secure it, I saw that the
sky had paled at the horizon. The wind had almost completely fallen, the
sea was calmer, here and there big black cormorants were rocking on the
waves quite close to the ship. Dense flocks of sea birds were flying
overhead, crying as they passed like showers of stones, and leaning out I
saw silhouetted on the horizon a tall black fang: we were in sight of
Vezzano. This was the limit Marino had assigned for the patrol runs: it was
time to rejoin Fabrizio on the bridge. At this hour of earliest morning—as in
a city on land—the labyrinth of corridors remained surprisingly empty; a
lifeless pallor, which seemed to seep from the metal walls, dimmed the faint
halo of the night lamps: I seemed to be floating like a shadow at the center
of the gray ship, of the gray day, of the gray sea, my heart subdued in this
grim expanse of first dawn.
Fabrizio was alone on the bridge. His small head with its boyish face
seemed to swim in the huge hood of his sea cloak; his features drawn by a
night’s watch made him look even younger. He turned toward me at a creak
of the stairs and watched me emerge from the hatchway without a word, his
forehead creased in an expression of ill-feigned surprise, and I realized that
he had been expecting me.
“There’s some hot coffee in the chest under there,” he said without
turning around when I had come up to him. “You’d better have some,” he

added when I didn’t move. “These early mornings off Syrtes are chilly ...
Did you have a good night?”
He was staring very meaningfully at the horizon ahead of the ship, his
voice eager to fill the silence. He was like a girl who both dreads and longs
for a declaration, and I suddenly felt more comfortable.
I sipped my coffee, in no hurry now, and stealthily glanced at him from
time to time. His eyes were fixed on the horizon, but his Adam’s apple kept
moving up and down in his throat, and the nervousness of his hands
betrayed him. “... Vezzano!” he said in a throaty exclamation, quickly
pointing to the island whose peak was emerging from a faint bank of mist
floating over the sea, a sharp serration against the clearing sky.
“Of ill repute ... !” I took my time and carefully swallowed another
mouthful of coffee. “... But they say you get a fine view from up there.” I
glanced at Fabrizio again out of the corner of my eye, and I think I saw him
blush. The ship was pitching in a faint oily swell; the sea birds’ cries as they
circled in dense flocks round Vezzano were furrowing the dawn, resuming
possession of the sea with the daylight.
“Maybe. Not this morning, in any case, with all this mess in the air.”
Fabrizio gestured with his chin at the fraying mist. “... Have you ever been
up there for a look?” he added in a tone of ill-feigned indifference.
“You’d have heard, surely ... I don’t happen to have a gunboat of my
own. But I was thinking maybe you ...”
“Never.”
“I thought you were so fond of excursions ...”
“Never seen Syrtes from higher than the bridge. Marino has no taste for
vantage points,” he added, shooting me a collusive glance for the first time,
but one I recognized: it was the look which prefaced our asides over the
fortress dinner table, when Marino began dozing off.
“Not everyone may feel as he does, back in Orsenna,” I said in a tone I
tried to make significant. No one at the Admiralty, I knew, was unaware of
the arrival of my secret instructions.

Once again Fabrizio shot me a quick glance. Silence fell again. He was
breathing faster: I could guess that he was weighing this serious bit of news.
“Going to have to bring her around,” Fabrizio murmured between his teeth,
using Marino’s peasant accent, as if he were eager to exorcize, to discharge
the ritual of its effectiveness.
The phrase lingered in the silence, meaningless as a puff of smoke;
Fabrizio’s hands were so utterly indifferent to it that they left the wheel and
carelessly lit a cigarette.
“It’s good to be at sea, Aldo, on a nice cool morning like this ...” He
stretched his arms voluptuously. “It’s always so stuffy in the Admiralty ...
You have the maps?” he added deliberately, indicating the roll I was holding
under my arm. I handed it to him without a word. “... The patrol run ...” he
announced in a pedagogic tone, running his finger along a dotted line. “It’s
quite difficult keeping inside it, Aldo, you can imagine,” he added,
sweeping the empty sea with an emphatic gesture, for Vezzano was already
quite far behind us. “Marino can feel it—he’s had enough experience to
have it in his blood, but I need to take my bearings.”
“And there aren’t many seamarks ...”
“That’s what I mean ... Actually, it’s all pretty much a fiction,” he broke
off with a knowing shrug, and the word sounded so odd in his mouth that
my extreme anxiety almost exploded in a burst of laughter. Silence fell
again. “All the same, I’m going to have to bring her around,” Fabrizio went
on with an impulse of authority, suddenly pretending to realize that Vezzano
was so far away.
“No hurry,” I said in a negligent tone, lighting a cigarette in my turn.
The ship was still making directly east; the sun, ahead of us, was rising
out of the sea in a cluster of brighter streaks.
“No, there’s no hurry ...” Fabrizio put his hands in the pockets of his sea
cloak and leaned against the partition, puffing feverishly on his cigarette.
“Absolutely none,” I concluded after a silence. And I leaned against the
same wall beside Fabrizio. Clumsily, feeling the seconds swallowed up and
time rushing down an irremediable slope, we both smiled blankly, blinking

in the sunlight rising out of the sea in front of us; the mist was dissolving,
and it promised to be a fine day. It seemed to me we had just pushed open
one of those doors you pass through in dreams. The stifling sense of a
happiness lost since childhood seized me; the horizon in front of us burst
into fragments of glory; as though caught in the current of a shoreless river,
it seemed to me that I was now utterly restored—a freedom, a miraculous
simplicity was washing the world; I saw the morning born for the first time.
“I was sure you were going to do it,” Fabrizio said, closing his hand on
my shoulder when—minutes rushing after minutes like the fathoms of a
sounding—there was no longer any doubt that the thing had now occurred
... “In God’s hands!” he added with a kind of enthusiasm. “I’d have hated to
miss this.”
The morning hours passed quickly. Toward ten, Beppo’s sleepy head
appeared in the hatchway. His astonished expression took in the empty
horizon, then turned back to us with a childish look of confusion and
worried curiosity, and I thought he was going to say something, but his face
suddenly assumed the nocturnal cornered look of some mole dazed by
daylight, and the news drained silently into the bowels of the ship. With an
absorbed expression Fabrizio plunged back into reading the maps. The
sleepy bridge grew warmer in the sunlight. A dozen slight heads were now
sticking out of the hatchway, eyes staring at their vision, nobody moving.
Fabrizio’s calculations coincided with mine: if the Redoubtable
maintained its present speed, we would be in sight of the Tängri by the end
of the afternoon. Fabrizio’s excitement was growing by the minute; orders
rained down. A lookout was sent up the foremast, and Fabrizio’s own
binoculars no longer left the horizon.
“Nothing fools you like an empty sea,” he answered knowingly when I
chaffed him. “And out here, it’s better to see before being seen ... We’ve got
to think of the consequences sometime.”
“We do?” I replied, deciding to provoke him with a hard look.
He laughed—a boy’s laugh, showing his big white teeth, the laugh of a
vigil of arms, and we went down together to get something to eat.

The afternoon passed in a kind of hysteria. Fabrizio’s abnormal
feverishness was that of a Robinson on his unmoored island, suddenly
leading a handful of Fridays. Marino, back at the Admiralty, retreated into
the mist. A little encouragement and Fabrizio would have run up the black
flag; his antics on deck, the constant neighs of his jubilant voice, were those
of a young colt frolicking in a field. The whole crew, hearing this voice,
functioned with a strange and almost alarming speed: from the bridge to the
masts, the vibration of strong, happy voices answered each other in chorus
and exploded in comical encouragements and shouts of good humor;
through the whole ship ran a crackling of anarchic energy which had in it
something of the rebellion of a convict ship or a boarding party, and this
excitement went to our heads like wine, sending our wake flying over the
waves, the ship shivering to its keel with a jubilation that had no content. A
cauldron suddenly seethed beneath me: there was no need to announce that
the lid had just been taken off.
But this feverish animation did not extend to me, or rather it hummed at a
distance from me, like a stormy rumor high above which I felt myself
floating, in a calm ecstasy. It suddenly seemed as if I had been given the
power to pass beyond, to slip into a world filled with intoxication and
trembling. This world was the same, and this endless stretch of empty water
desperately resembled itself everywhere one looked. But now a silent grace
gleamed upon it. The intimate feeling that had drawn taut the thread of my
life had been that of an ever deeper aberration; starting from the broad ways
of childhood, when all of life closed over me like a warm embrace, it
seemed to me that gradually I had lost contact, turned away with the
passing days toward ever more solitary roads where at times, disoriented, I
stood still a moment and caught no more than the brief and lonely echo of a
nighttime street emptying of all traffic. I had made my way into absence,
straying in an ever dimmer countryside, far from the essential Rumor whose
uninterrupted throb rushed toward a cataract behind the horizon. And now
the inexplicable feeling of the right road made the blank and watery desert
bloom around me, as though at the outskirts of a city still in darkness
behind the remote horizon, in every direction wandering lights crossed their

beams; the vacillating horizon grew brilliant with the winking of
reconnaissance signals; a royal road was opening onto the sea paved with
beams like a ritual carpet; and, as inaccessible to our inmost senses as the
other side of the moon to the eye, it seemed to me that a promise and a
revelation had been offered of another pole where roads ran together instead
of diverging, and of an effective glance of the mind for which the globe
itself serves as an eye. The fugitive beauty of Vanessa’s face appeared in the
warm mist that rose over the calm sea—blinding light blazed in the
rediscovered hearth of thousands of glances that were focused upon me—a
rendezvous had been given me in this venturesome desert by each of the
voices of elsewhere whose timbre had created the silence in my ears, and
whose murmur mingled in me now like that of a gathered crowd behind a
door.
The afternoon was already darkening; the faint white gauze that blurs the
sky on hot days in Syrtes was dissolving now, restoring to the air a
marvellous transparency. A more oblique light glazed the soft, slow
undulations of a silken sea; an enchanted calm seemed to linger on the
water like a scarf, paving our way across the waves. The ship moved into
the evening’s heart over a sea decked out as though for one of its great
festivities, tiny now and dissolved into the immense reverberation of space,
almost imperceptible in the unexpected signal, the indecipherable omen of
its smoke rising from the sea after so many years—a long flexible plume
that indolently dissolved its agitated spirals into the air.
“I’m going to shut down the engines,” Fabrizio said with some concern:
“that kind of smoke is a provocation. Besides, it’s better to keep our
distance till nightfall, if ...” His glance was clearly a question. The ghostly
solemnity of that late afternoon was affecting him, sobering him, and for the
first time I heard a kind of grave meditation in his voice.
“Yes,” I answered confidently, “I’ll take care of it.”
“Look!” he exclaimed, suddenly grabbing my arm, his voice little more
than a whisper.

A thread of smoke was rising ahead of us on the horizon, distinctly
visible against the sky already darkening in the east. A strange and
motionless column, it seemed pasted on the sky, its base like a string drawn
taut, thickening as it rose, and suddenly forming a flat and smoky corolla,
throbbing softly on the air and gradually erased by the wind. This viscous
smoke scarcely bespoke a ship; sometimes it resembled the thread rising
into the evening air from a dying fire, yet one could tell it was singularly
intense; a malefic impression emanated from its shape, like the incurled
umbel of certain poisonous mushrooms. And like them it seemed to have
grown, to have taken possession of the horizon with an amazing rapidity;
suddenly it had been there; its very immobility, deceptive against the
evening’s grisaille, must have long concealed it from our gaze. By carefully
studying the point on the horizon where the smoke originated, I thought I
could discern, above the bank of fog which was forming once again, a
double filament of shadow I recognized by the sudden leap of my heart.
“That’s the Tängri ... there!” I almost shouted at Fabrizio, with an emotion
so sudden that I dug my fingers into his shoulder.
He glanced feverishly at the map, then stared at the horizon in his turn
with an expression of incredulous curiosity.
“Yes,” he said after a moment’s silence, in a voice which slowly mastered
his stupefaction, as if he had not dared believe it. “That’s the Tängri. But
what’s the smoke?”
There was the same uneasiness in his voice that I felt in myself: a note of
alarm. Yes, for all it might signify of what was only natural, explicable in
the most ordinary terms, it was disorienting to see that unsuspected column
of smoke rising now above the long-extinct volcano. Its plume, wavering as
it faded in the freshening breeze, seemed to darken the sky more than the
night itself, to cast an evil spell on this unknown sea; more than another
eruption after so many others, it suggested rains of blood, statues that broke
out in a sweat, a black flag raised on that giant staff on the eve of a plague
or a deluge.

“It’s extinct,” Fabrizio murmured to himself, as though confronted by a
riddle that was beyond him. All his gaiety had abruptly collapsed. The
rising wind sent us a first faint squall; suddenly it was cold on the bridge. A
last flock of birds flying west passed over us, shrieking; the empty sky was
already darkening around the mysterious smoke.
“Let’s not go any farther,” Fabrizio said, grasping my wrist abruptly. “I
don’t like that volcano going to all this trouble for our visit ... Do you know
where we are?” he added in a frightened voice, holding out the map. The
finger that landed on it was already well past the red line: behind that
sinister vanguard, like a silent wave, the coasts of Farghestan rushed toward
us from all directions.
I met his eyes, and for an instant I felt my will hesitate. In Fabrizio’s
suddenly awed voice, the omens produced on the threshold of this wicked
night reverberated like a direr warning; the fallen fever of the afternoon left
me uncertain, my heart heavy. It seemed to me that a veil had been torn;
Fabrizio’s recoil left me face to face with the naked folly of our escapade.
“What will he say?”
“Marino, you mean?” I inquired mildly—too mildly, and all at once I felt
a cold rage rising within me: I realized with what persistent and ceaseless
cunning I had done nothing but stave off that name all night long.
“... What a bore, my boy,” I whistled between my teeth: “we always take
cover behind that name whenever we’re frightened.”
Now, I had denied him; now, and only now, everything had been said, the
way was free, the night lay open before us. Fabrizio understood everything,
and then a remarkable thing occurred: he let go of the helm a moment, and
all of a sudden, as if he had been alone, he crossed himself, the way a man
wards off a curse.
“Marino’s not afraid ...” he murmured in a faltering voice.
“Take her East! And increase speed!” I shouted into Fabrizio’s ear
against the rising wind. “The darkness will cover us—if we stoke up before
daylight, we’ll be out of sight ...” But it was as if my voice had been

swallowed in my throat or all his reflexes delayed; Fabrizio was like a man
walking in his sleep.
“You know what you’re doing, Aldo,” he whispered in a childish voice,
half scared, half tender. “But now it’s all changed,” he added, getting up
with a determined expression. “I have to give orders.”
In the gathering darkness the crew took their combat posts. The faces that
passed before me in the shifting gleam of a lantern gained a clumsy dignity
from the unwonted ceremony: Fabrizio called them up one by one and
assigned them their duties in a firm voice; performing such a ritual on the
Redoubtable dated back to the mists of time, and no one remembered how
to behave.
“Think it’s anything serious, Beppo?” a perplexed figure murmured
beneath me.
“Don’t worry about it,” cut in a teasing voice. “After the next watch,
you’ll be close enough to see for yourself.”
“Not a minute too soon. There’s a little too much doing over there. The
sea belongs to everyone, as they say at the Signory. The old Redoubtable’s
going to get a little air too ...”
There was a murmur of eager approval.
“No, no, not like that—you have to open the breech first, peasant!”
someone grumbled quite distinctly in the forecastle, amid smothered
laughter.
Silence fell again.
“You saw how they lit their pipe,” a remote voice concluded. “Going to
be a hot one.”
Fabrizio came back beside me on the bridge, whistling as if to raise his
spirits in the dark, but I divined in this young and easygoing creature a new
burst of confidence: he was in command of the Redoubtable and
confronting a possible danger, and the ardor and good humor of his men
had cheered him up. “I’ll answer for them,” he said, “we’ll open their eyes.
Lucky it’s going to be a dark night,” he continued, gradually reassuring

himself: “that limits the risks. And then—it’s our best chance—they must
have got out of the habit of being nosy.”
The smoke had long since melted into the dark sky. Huge clouds were
rising over the horizon in heavy spirals, dissolved at the sea’s edge in a
vestige of livid twilight.
“Now tell me, Aldo,” he continued hesitantly, “maybe it isn’t any of my
business, but what is it you want to see over there at such close range?”
I opened my mouth to answer, but my voice seemed to have been choked
in my throat, and I began smiling distractedly in the dark. Close to me as a
brother, there yet were no words to tell him what Marino or a woman in
love would have understood at a glance. What I wanted had no name in any
language. To be closer. Not to remain cut off. To consume myself in that
light. To touch ... “Nothing,” I told him. “Just a reconnaissance mission.”
The ship was churning ahead without a single light. The clouds, which
had mounted very high in the sky, were hiding the moon. Fabrizio was not
mistaken; luck was with us. My thoughts flew ahead of the ship boring
through that inky partition; it seemed to me I could feel the hidden peak
rising over us behind that suspect darkness, and with an impulse I couldn’t
control my hands kept making the gesture of a man who gropes toward a
wall in the dark.
“Two more hours and we’re there,” Fabrizio said in a sleepy voice ...
“Too bad we won’t have a view—there’s a full moon behind those clouds
...”
Behind his commanding-officer’s calm, I could tell he was as tense as I
was. Beneath us, drowned in the shadows, the vigilant crew kept a profound
silence, but their wide-open eyes were magnetizing the darkness; in this
secret advance upon the unknown, the whole ship was being charged with a
subtle electricity.
Fabrizio plunged back into his maps with a preoccupied expression: the
last part of our expedition was raising a difficult problem for him. An
uneven line of reefs, which the maps didn’t account for very specifically,
guarded the approaches to the Tängri, and memories at Orsenna had not

faded of losses suffered there by squadrons returning from the great reprisal
expedition. I myself went to double the watch on the foreposts, where a
man stood ready to take soundings. For several minutes I stayed there
leaning over the bow, lashed in the cold wind that smelled of snow and stars
and seemed to pour in icy layers from the inaccessible peak, trying to smell
in it the hints of the approaching shore, but the night seemed to have no end
to it: nothing but the inexhaustible hiss under the bow and that otherworldly
wind, that draught of bitter cold which carried the moan of ice floes on its
breath. The rhythm of this guesswork navigation was lulling me to sleep; I
rocked in those last minutes of calm and pure expectation, my empty mind
all at once strangely porous to a subtler concert and to indecipherable
coincidences. The earth’s familiar signs seemed to have withdrawn to a
great distance, yet important omens exchanged their symbols in the cloudy
air. All my life since leaving Orsenna seemed to me guided and
recomposed, during this flight into darkness, transformed into symbols
which spoke to me out of the depths of the night. I saw again the rooms of
the Aldobrandi palace, their arrogant patience, their moldy void suddenly
roused ... Behind me the torrent of smoke our stack belched out was
dispersed by the night like a blacker veil. I saw again the phantom gesture
our fortress had suddenly recreated upon the waters. I thought of this
mysteriously reanimated volcano ... My face rinsed by that cold bath, thrust
deep in a darkness that dissolved all contours, I pulled myself together,
summoning up all my blind being, and identified myself with my Hour,
abandoned myself to an ineffable immunity.
Towards one in the morning, there was a sudden calm: we were in the lee
of the volcano. A heavy, stagnant moisture enveloped us, the ship slid on
noiseless over an oily sea; in this oppressive silence that seemed to cast a
shadow at the very heart of the night, the enormous mass rushed toward us,
even more overwhelming than in broad daylight.
“Keep a close watch!” rang out Fabrizio’s tense voice in the excessively
calm darkness.

The ship reduced speed, the brighter wake under the bow subsided;
suddenly a slow, warm gust spread around us a scent at once pungent and
sweet, like the smell of an oasis diluted by the desert’s parching air. The
night was gradually brightening: overhead the masses of cloud seemed to be
rapidly disintegrating, and a few stars shone, infinitely remote and pure in
their intensely black lacerations, which the moon now fringed with a milky
halo.
“Aldo!” Fabrizio called in a low voice. I rejoined him on the bridge. “...
The storm is passing,” he whispered, pointing to the already clearer sky. “If
the moon comes out, in one minute it’s going to be clear as day. Did you
smell the orange trees?” he asked, shaking his head. “We’re almost in reach
of land ... You want to turn back?”
I cut him off with a shake of the head. At this moment, throat dry as
though in the presence of a coveted body gradually revealed in the
darkness, every nerve roused to famished expectation, I could not speak.
“All right!” Fabrizio sounded relieved, almost cheerful in spite of
himself. “It’s suicide, and I had to give you fair warning. May God help us
...”
He reduced speed still further, and calmly, meticulously, verified a few
calculations one last time. I glanced at him now and then: frowning with
attention and self-importance he stuck out his tongue like a schoolboy. An
extraordinary childhood seemed evident in all his features, erasing all signs
of exhaustion and sleeplessness, and an exalted sentiment of victory
suddenly seized me; the face I was carrying with me into my dream would
live as never before. “You want to turn back now, Fabrizio?” I asked,
staring ahead of the ship and gently laying my hand on his arm.
“I don’t know any more,” he said with a little giggle which betrayed all
his nervous agitation ... “You’re the devil!” he added, turning his eyes away,
and without looking up I could tell he was smiling. The lash of a squall
made the deck plates rattle, suddenly whipping the bridge and blinding us,
yet at the very heart of this brutal gust the darkness was thinning out as if,
very high up behind it, the reflector of some giant lamp had delicately

pulverized its shower of light. The rain stopped, the ship shuddered in the
calm, swathed in a light mist; in front of the bow, the clouds parted rapidly,
like a theater curtain.
“The volcano! The volcano!” thirty throats shouted in a single choking
cry, the shout that rises from a collision or an ambush.
In front of us, within arm’s reach it seemed, from the movement of recoil
which tipped back my head toward that terrifying peak, an apparition rose
out of the sea like a wall. The moon was shining now in all its luster. To the
right, the forest of lights at Rhages fringed the sleeping water with a
motionless shimmer. In front of us, like an illuminated ocean liner which
up-ends its stern before sinking, there swung above the sea at a fabulous
height a fragment of earth raised like a lid, a vertical suburb, riddled, tiered,
scattered to starry fixity with burning bushes and girandoles of light. Like
the torches of a great facade peacefully reflected on the gleaming pavement,
but on a level with the clouds, and seeming so close, so distinct in the rain-
washed air that we were certain we smelled the sopping gardens and the
varnished freshness of their dewy paths, the lights of the avenues, of the
villas, of the palaces, of the crossroads thinned out to the fires of outlying
suburbs clinging to their lava slopes, rose in the night interspersed by
landings, by cliffs, by balconies over the gently phosphorescent sea, drawn
out to a horizontal line of floating mist which yellowed and blurred the last
gleams and occasionally let one or another reappear higher still and almost
improbable, as an alpinist reappears in the field of the binoculars,
momentarily hidden by an outcrop of the glacier he is scaling. Like a
pedestal, the sparkling and truncated pyramid of an altar where the figure of
the god culminates in the penumbra, the tree of lights ended at that uneven
edge. And very high, very far above this black void, rising at a verticality
which overhung our heads so that we had to lean back to see, pasted against
the sky like an obscene and greedy leech, there emerged from a foam of
nothingness a kind of emblem of the end of time, a bluish horn of a milky
and faintly effulgent substance which seemed to float, motionless and
forever alien, final, like a strange concretion of the air itself. The silence

around this apparition which demanded a cry of recognition agonized the
ear, as if suddenly air had revealed itself to be opaque to the transmission of
sound, or again, facing this star-studded wall, it suggested the sickening and
soft fall of nightmares in which the world collapses and the shriek above us
of a mouth inexhaustibly open never reaches our ears.
“The Tängri!” Fabrizio murmured, pale as wax, digging his nails into my
wrist, as though facing one of those exceptional powers whose very name is
a prayer and which we may only recognize and call upon by name.
“Straight ahead! Closer!” I murmured in his ear in a voice which sounded
strangely guttural and harsh to me.
But it had not even occurred to Fabrizio to turn the helm. It was too late
now—later than anything. A spell already glued us to this magnetic
mountain. An extraordinary, illuminated expectation, the certainty that the
last veil would fall, paralyzed these desperate minutes. With every nerve
strained, the black arrow of the ship flew toward the illuminated giant.
“Full speed ahead!” Fabrizio shouted, beside himself.
The ship vibrated in each piece of its hull—the bow which rose above the
horizon with each minute already silhouetted black against the nearing
lights, the coast rushed toward us, motionlessly enlarging like a ship being
stoked. No, nothing could touch us now—luck was with us, the sea empty;
not one light moved in front of Rhages, which seemed asleep. The radiant
curtain shimmering on the shore protected us, dissolved our black shadow
into the night. A minute, another minute in which centuries pass, to see and
to touch one’s longing, welded to that final leap of speed, to dissolve in that
dazzling onset, to be consumed in that light rising out of the sea.
Suddenly, to our right, toward Rhages, the shore vibrated with the hurried
nictitation of heat flashes. A heavy, almost musical rustle lacerated the air
over the ship and, waking the mountain’s cavernous thunder, we heard the
echo of three cannon shots.
OceanofPDF.com

10
The Envoy
Standing near the bow to catch the first coolness, I watched the coast of
Syrtes grow larger in the dawn. Its yellow beaches still swept with lingering
mists and lying quite flat on the horizon, it looked to me sullen and paltry in
the faint light, more disinherited than usual and burdened now with my
sudden disillusionment. My heart was heavy; it seemed to me that the
Redoubtable was growing heavier and lagging over this flat sea, as if it had
taken tons of water into its hold. God be thanked, we were bringing the ship
back intact. With an instinctive swerve, Fabrizio had avoided the shots, and
a sudden cloud passing across the moon had enabled our escape. The
unexpected composure of our cannoneers, or perhaps their stupor, by
preventing them from replying, had avoided the worst. Yet the very
incoherence of this rapid engagement continued to intrigue me. It was
surprising—though Orsenna’s negligence in this respect may have exceeded
all bounds—that on the opposing shore they had kept watch so stubbornly
and so attentively. Surprising too that on such a dark night, Rhages had felt
no need to ascertain the identity of a suspect silhouette—as if they had
known what to expect from the first. No reconnaissance signal had
preceded the firing, and the more I thought about it the stranger it seemed
that at that minimal distance, the shots which had immediately surrounded
us should have proved so ineffectual. Fabrizio’s dexterity, on reflection,
could not blind me to the fact: this reprimand all too complacently
underlined a nuance of mockery and disdain, and that accommodating shot
which had ended by provoking our crew’s relieved laughter did not reassure
me. I couldn’t help relating the rumors current in Maremma about the
machinations at Rhages to this ineffectual cannonade. Ineffectual? I found
myself shaking my head; I needed to know just what it was they had
wanted, over there... .

I stared anxiously at the little group which our return, signaled from a
distance, had already gathered on the dock; more than anything at this
moment, I was afraid of being left alone with Marino. He wasn’t there, and
I felt suddenly relieved.
“Run her aground on a sandbar, boy?” Giovanni shouted to Fabrizio good
humoredly, taking his pipe out of his mouth while the hawsers were being
thrown.
The joke was a ritual. So early in the morning, his hunting rifle was
already hanging from his shoulder; the inexpressible monotony of
Admiralty life suddenly flowed back over me.
“We had some problems,” Fabrizio said, crestfallen and embarrassed in
front of his men. “I’ll explain later.”
The groups thinned out, casual and benumbed, the men strolling down
the dock and tossing pebbles into the water. Our little cluster was walking
in front, and in spite of myself I listened for voices from the groups
including our crew; reticent and abashed, in no hurry to tell the news, the
men kept to themselves: it was as if this return bewildered them. Giovanni
and Roberto said nothing, surprised that we did too, and so much silence
weighed heavily... .
“We went over there,” I said abruptly. “They fired at us.”
Giovanni and Roberto stopped short, mouths open, and stared at me with
sleepy eyes. “Over there? ...” Giovanni echoed finally in an almost natural
tone of voice, recalling his famous imperturbability of our nights on watch
—but Fabrizio came to my rescue.
“Aldo had his reasons,” he added dryly, cutting explanations short.
A wave of diplomatic discretion passed almost naively over those tanned
faces. The time we had spent at Syrtes suddenly revealed itself on these
faces: the Signory’s reasons of State still warmed them to a ready-made
veneration.
“Cursed dogs!” Giovanni grumbled between his teeth, pulling his pipe
out of his mouth. There was an accent of suitable condolence in his voice,

but I was surprised and heartened to see how much milder their
consternation was than I had expected.
“You went over there ... ?” Roberto repeated, incredulous. “Tell ... !” he
added, taking my arm with a conspiratorial expression as he eagerly pushed
open the common-room door.
Story and questions were endless. I felt remarkably comfortable. It was
immediately obvious that reasons were of no interest to Giovanni and
Roberto—they had no thought of asking for an accounting. With them I
advanced into a sort of fairy tale whose origins, in their haste to get to the
results, they immediately put in parentheses; and rather than rob the
adventure of its enchantment, it was as if Roberto and Giovanni wanted
instead to join in, to take part in the game, to be with us. Any allusion to
Marino was avoided as wet blanket; it was as if he had never set foot in the
Admiralty. Gently, joining hands, we removed the obstacles, the
embarrassing images, freeing the slope down which we wanted to slide. At
the descriptions of the Tängri, I saw their eyes light up with a new-minted
curiosity. Roberto sketched out a few critical considerations as to the
Farghestan artillery. Above all it was agreed, with many knowing nods, that
their firing at us without a challenge was “unprecedented.” Fabrizio realized
we could say whatever we liked, and put the last touches to the “victory” by
an unexpected inspiration: “Great idea you had, Roberto, getting the
fortress back in shape. As if you saw it coming.”
“I’ve always mistrusted anything from over there,” Roberto conceded
prophetically, and while pretending to suck on his pipe he blushed with
modest satisfaction. I realized I was going to leave the room whitewashed
—better still, glorified.
“Well, the wine is poured, it has to be drunk,” Giovanni said, raising his
glass with a sort of gaiety. “They got what they wanted. You can be sure
they won’t stop there!”
Roberto’s pipe enveloped him in a Jupiterian cloud; squinting, he peered
out the window at the horizon, suddenly an idol of penetrating sagacity; in
Marino’s absence the command of the Admiralty fell to him. “I wouldn’t be

too surprised if they repaid our little visit tonight,” he finally conceded in a
tone heavy with secret knowledge. “It’s clouding over—good weather for a
surprise ... Only between now and then, I’ll take a few precautions.”
“Have to,” Giovanni concluded in the approving silence. “The
Admiralty’s wide open ...” and we were all filled with the energetic
consciousness that we were not protected.
Thereupon he opened a little council of war to which I listened without
saying a word, benumbed as I was by a sensation of growing unreality at
seeing how matters followed their own course. Roberto proposed
emergency measures. Fabrizio leafed through the regulations. On its own,
having escaped my fingers, the spool I had loosed was unwinding... .
It was decided to leave the Redoubtable’s steam up; a night watch would
be set on top of the fortress. This afternoon Roberto, without making any
particular show of his actions, would inspect the old coastal battery—
dilapidated as it was—which commanded the channel mouth (the fortress
artillery had long been out of commission) and report on its equipment.
Finally, they would unmoor one of those pinnaces rotting in the mud flats
and use it to keep a night watch on the approaches to the channel. Though
there was no lack of enthusiasm the apprehension of Marino’s return, which
vaguely damaged their spirits, had discouraged more extreme measures: if
need be—this was everyone’s unavowed thought—the emergency watch
could easily be cancelled. Thus minute by minute a certain complicity was
established among the four of us.
“For the rest, the captain will decide,” Roberto concluded jesuitically.
“I’ll take the night patrol—instead of duck hunting ... !”
In incessant comings and goings between fortress, jetty and battery, the
day passed quickly enough. No one was bored at the Admiralty now. The
excitement had reached the men, and the snatches of their conversations we
could occasionally catch—for they fell silent more respectfully than before,
with a meaningful look, when officers passed by—suggested what
extravagant rumors had found credence, as if a sudden craving for the
unexpected and the unheard-of, long dormant in this monotonous life, had

exploded in these benumbed minds. Two or three times, in fact, questions
broke out as we passed, inquiries which Roberto eluded with half-closed,
mystery-fraught eyes; under this sky where suddenly, with a curiously
animal nose for it, they seemed to smell a storm rising, these reanimated
faces called for news, good or bad, as the earth calls for rain in its long
droughts.
Yet that night and the three next days passed quite calmly. The
excitement lessened. Marino was due back at the week’s end, and with each
new watch that turned out to be of no consequence, I saw Giovanni
returning more and more discouraged.
“I should have guessed,” he would say now, annoyed as a suitor who
finds his letters returned unopened. “They’re not only niggers but
numbskulls over there. You can do anything to people like that,” he added,
with a disgusted expression.
His imagination went no further than this compliment returned. Like
everyone at the Admiralty, Giovanni lived in the present, on the surface of
his skin. Orsenna’s ageless subsidence, patiently discouraging any sense of
responsibility and need for foresight, had modeled these old children into an
omnipotent and senile guardianship for which nothing could ever really
happen or be of any consequence. Any opportunities for diversion were
welcomed, but inevitably, one day or the next, these men would return to
their duck hunts.
During these agitated days, a very different concern had obsessed me.
Back in Marino’s office, which I occupied in his absence, no sooner had I
begun leafing through the routine stack of service papers, than suddenly, in
the lowering of the fever which had devoured the hours—as clear, as
perceptible as Marino’s face returning to this accusing room—the insanity
of my escapade rose up before me, so blinding that my eyes blurred and I
thought for a moment that my heart would stop beating. The muffled
silence of the room suddenly roared like the sea in my ears; after that night
so intensely experienced, my deed had been severed from me forever;
somewhere, very far away, with a faint and subtle rumble, a machine had

been started up which no one could ever stop: its distant hum penetrated the
close room, wakening that secluded silence like the murmur of bees.
“The wine is poured, it has to be drunk,” I repeated, nodding, terribly
sober now. My eyes fell on the pile of unopened mail which had been set on
the desk, and suddenly I realized I had to make a decision then and there.
To account to the Signory for so formal a violation of regulations would
be suicide; to pass over it in silence, even supposing the matter had no
consequences, would doubtless lead to an even more certain condemnation:
the entire Admiralty already knew. For a moment the situation seemed to
me so inescapable that I slumped over my desk, overcome by dizziness, and
with my head buried in my hands I prayed like a child for sleep, for the
oblivion which would transform this night into a bad dream, trying to
convince myself that somehow this nightmare would vanish away. All of a
sudden I saw a way out, and the vague hope, lacking absolution, of at least
a possible intelligence: I decided to request an audience from the Council of
Superintendence with regard to a serious matter upon which I desired to
furnish explanations in person.
That evening I went back to my room as soon as dinner was over: before
morning I had to prepare a text that was rather difficult to phrase properly. I
was playing a last card and couldn’t deceive myself I was playing it in the
dark. A misplaced word could ruin me, I was far from feeling comfortable,
and my work was not proceeding at all well. For a long time now, the
