Admiralty had fallen silent around me; the faint scratching of my pen was
all that stitched the slow hours together with its shipworm’s sound—and the
rustle of the pages I tore up one after the next. It might have been eleven
o’clock when my door thumped gently, and I scarcely had time to raise my
head before someone was standing in front of me.
“It is very late, Signor Observer—no doubt much too late to request an
audience,” pronounced a foreign and rather musical voice.
In the darkness outside the circle of my lamp, it was hard to make out his
features. Before me stood a vigorous yet delicate figure; in his impulse to
approach my table there appeared that elastic and silent lightness generated

by the custom of desert life. The extremely simple and almost sordid
clothes he was wearing were those of the boatmen who take Sunday
strollers across the lagoons; they added something ludicrous to the extreme
distinction of his voice.
“It is really very late,” he continued, consulting upside down the watch
on my desk, and from a quite casual movement he made, I realized he was
deliberately lingering there to keep his profile against the light. Suddenly I
remembered, and my heart began pounding; that dark skin, those fixed and
piercing eyes—it was the boat-keeper from Sagra.
“This will tell you in whose name I have come,” he said, reading my
discovery in my eyes and suddenly changing his tone; without any further
invitation from me, and with an unconstraint which was not in the least
impolite, he sat down, with a faint sigh of fatigue.
I unfolded the paper he held out to me, and suddenly my eyes grew fixed.
The right-hand corner bore a serpent coiled around a chimera, just as I had
so often seen it at the Diplomatic Academy at the bottom of dusty age-old
treaties; the seal of the Chancellery of Rhages starred the sheet. The text
certified the pacific nature of the bearer’s mission and requested specifically
that he be granted all the considerations and the official treatment reserved
for bearers of a flag of truce in wartime. The words were blurring before my
eyes as I pretended to reread the text: a sentiment of unfamiliar joy and of
marvellous election overcame me; it seemed to me that for the first time I
understood the meaning of the expression: to give a sign of life.
“I shall have to order your arrest,” I remarked in a deliberately uncertain
tone of voice, folding up the paper. “The recent titles attributed to you here
can hardly override your activities as a spy.”
Caught unprepared, I was clumsily trying to gain the advantage. “Don’t
try to deny it!” I stopped him with a gesture. “We’ve met before this
occasion. I believe I recall that you were not wearing a uniform, though you
were armed.”
“The livery of the Princess Aldobrandi,” he corrected, his voice still
courteous, with a slight nod of the head.

I frowned severely.
“... Let us leave that aside,” he added immediately, as though in excuse.
Obviously he wanted to avoid embarrassing me. “May we open a
parenthesis here?” he asked with a good-humored smile. And while I stared
at him uncomprehendingly, he stood up, calmly removed a pistol from his
pocket and set it beside me on the table. “I am your prisoner, if you like;
now perhaps you can be more comfortable. Let us leave that aside then, for
a while, and perhaps we can talk seriously.”
Suddenly I no longer felt in a very good mood. The stranger had a double
advantage over me: his careful insolence and my own inelegant reflexes. I
frowned as I turned the pistol over. “Well?” I asked, glancing at him
unpleasantly.
The stranger seemed to reflect a moment. “I might say, Signor Observer,”
he began, with a hesitation in his voice which oddly added to its charm,
“that my task is not so easy. My country and yours present proof that
between States, as between individuals, can be created quite singular false
situations. By the very fact of their ... longevity, they can last that much
longer.” He produced a discreet sigh of embarrassment. “...It happens, now
that we meet once again after a ... prolonged separation, that we no longer
know, even approximately, where we stand.”
“I am not a diplomat,” I remarked quite dryly. “The Signory has
doubtless drawn up its protocols. I am not in its confidence. I do not
question your mandate. But you are addressing the wrong man.”
I did not want to make things easier for him. I took a secret pleasure in
this uncertain tone of reticence, this groping approach of his. More perhaps
from it than from what he would have to tell me, I knew how to proceed.
“There’s no mistake,” he continued, lowering his eyes a moment. “You
are indeed our man,” he added, raising them suddenly, and it seemed to me
he was smiling at me the way Marino used to smile sometimes, with that
same remote, knowing smile.
“At the very least, that’s a singular way of speaking.” I felt less outraged
than I wanted to. He made a deprecating gesture with marked insincerity.

“I am doubtless not quite fluent in your language. I meant that whatever
judgment was made concerning this ‘false situation,’ there occurred a new
fact, a new situation as of last week. You are no stranger to the matter—less
a stranger than anyone.” He waited for an answer that did not come, and
then, after a moment’s silence, seemed to make up his mind. “I shall
therefore summarize the facts which motivate this conversation. Orsenna
and Farghestan are in a state of war ...” He seemed to weigh and to wield
the word with his expressive fingers, and once again shot me that veiled and
faintly amused glance. “... Such is indeed the case, is it not, Signor
Observer? From the state of war to the fact of war, in the case which
concerns us, there is, however, a considerable distance. The dispute is a
very old one. Time—as the saying goes—is a gentleman. The Sea of Syrtes
is broad. The two countries, as you know, have avoided any encounter for a
very long time. The war has dozed off; it is not even too much to say that it
seems to have fallen asleep.” Once again his glance remained without an
answer. He took his time with some satisfaction. “There is a phrase, I
believe, to designate a sound sleep—a phrase that refers to ‘sleeping on
both ears.’ In such a case, it is to be feared that the war is not going to sleep
much longer ...”
“You choose to say so ...”
“You choose to do much more. Last Thursday night, a suspicious ship
was observed cruising very close to our coasts. It came from Orsenna. It
was a warship. You were commanding it.”
“Your information is very specific and very swift,” I replied, annoyed.
“The night was extremely dark. To say the least, the matter does not seem to
have come as a surprise. Am I supposed to offer you personal
congratulations?” I added, in the most cutting tone I could manage.
He smiled again without impatience. “We are discussing a fact. I am glad
to see you are not contesting it. It is impossible not to regard it as extremely
serious. What might elsewhere pass for an error, a ... confusion of no
consequence, can here only assume the character of a calculated
provocation, and in our present situation its meaning is clear.”

“Our situation! It’s been so long ...” I interrupted him with an ironic tone.
“There is no statute of limitations in history, Signor Observer. Your ...
visit has awakened some very old memories. These memories are not
peaceful ones. They may once more become ... burning ones.”
He stared at me insistently. For the first time I distinguished an
unexpected note of exaltation in his voice.
“What are you getting at?” I asked him, my tone anything but assured.
“At the message it is my mission to deliver to you,” he resumed in a
neutral tone, as if he wanted to emphasize that he was here as no more than
a spokesman. “The government of Rhages considers that a period of de
facto peace, so continuously respected on either side, ultimately constitutes
a tacit promise of non-hostility. Nor has that government been responsible, I
must formally emphasize in its name, for the fact that this condition has not
been scrupulously observed. By Orsenna’s doing, this period has now been
brought to a close by a veritable act of war. Rhages now regards itself as
excused, de facto as already de jure, from its attitude of determined
abstention ...” He was silent for a moment, and then resumed, pronouncing
his words very distinctly: “... However, Rhages regards the matter as
contingent upon the wisdom, whose proofs it has so continuously given, of
allowing for a certain amount of reflection before launching uncontrollable
events. It prefers to declare that its intentions have remained unshakably
pacific. It even consents to admit—no material damage having in fact been
caused by this ... incursion—that the way remains open to a reasonable
settlement, if ...” The voice lingered complacently over the word. “... If
proofs can be duly given that no genuinely hostile purpose has inspired this
... indiscretion.”
“What form does that government envisage for such ‘proofs’?”
“The forbearance of the government of Rhages is extreme,” he murmured
with a barricaded smile (I was beginning to be a little intrigued by his
impersonal way of alluding to the authority he was representing). “Nothing
that would constitute for you an irritating or troublesome form, nothing that
would claim to be a satisfaction. Its uncertainty is great,” he commented

with a slightly forced warmth. “Either the fact is insignificant—or else, if it
signifies something, it annuls three centuries of security, if not of peace. It
is understandable, Signor Observer, that in the face of this somewhat
unsettling situation, we should ask once and for all, as I was saying but a
moment ago, where we stand.”
“In other words?”
“A disavowal,” he snapped in a very precise tone. “Express assurance
that this violation of our coastal waters has been involuntary, accidental,
and as such devoid of any signification. The promise that incidents so
prejudicial to a mutual tranquility will not be repeated. Naturally,” he
added, “the time necessary for you to refer the matter to the Signory will be
generously granted. I mean,” he continued rapidly, “thirty days, as of this
evening.”
There was a moment’s embarrassed silence. I realized that the official
communication had concluded.
“It may not be so easy for us to reach you with an answer as it has been
for you to put questions,” I said, to gain time. “Farghestan does not seem to
be readily accessible ...”
“Your imagination will certainly be of service to you here,” he replied
with an amused irony. “I myself have had to force your door in a somewhat
cavalier fashion. The choice is yours as to the form and the means of an
assurance which Rhages has no doubt of receiving.”
Silence fell again. The slightly oblique eyes with their heavy glance
waited greedily. It was as if he had relieved himself of an encumbering
mission which his detached and rapid tone had oddly tended to minimize.
Now he was growing animated before my eyes; his countenance, presented
to my curiosity, seemed to glisten with sudden vitality; the spokesman’s
role was over, and yet it seemed he had used this official mission as no
more than a singular kind of overture, a pretext for the tête-à-tête which
was beginning now.
Indeed he had nothing to fear: I would not let him leave now. I felt an
inexpressible relief, a marvellous suspension in the mere fact that he was

there: a silent apparition held for a moment within the luminous circle of
my lamp. Suddenly it seemed to me I had summoned him up then and there,
a silhouette issuing from another world, set down on the other side of my
desk in the intimacy of a nocturnal visit. Those eyes resting on me spoke
quite beyond what any words might say; I felt confirmed and recognized.
“And if not?” I said in a voice that sounded oddly calm and a little sleepy.
“If not?” “If the answer you are expecting does not reach you?” The
stranger’s gaze became fixed; one might have said that his eyes were veiled
with a faint cast. The figure, however, remained quite motionless. “The
instructions I have received include no information on that subject,” he
continued after a moment’s silence. He raised his eyes to mine and frowned
slightly. “I have acquitted myself of an official communication. A
conversation between us of a ... private nature would doubtless not be
without its uses. It could not, however, engage anything but my private
opinion. But I fear it is very late,” he hesitated, with an expression of polite
regret. I handed him a cigar case, and leaned back in my chair with studied
nonchalance. “Admiralty evenings are long,” I said, and caught myself
giving him an almost friendly look. “A ... tricentennial visit can hardly be
regarded as abusive.” I observed the seductiveness of his slightly cruel
smile. That hesitation in his voice I was imitating without realizing it gave
us a certain shared quality—suddenly put us quite at ease. All at once, in
the silences interrupting his incoherent conversation, had been established a
singular understanding of every nuance. “If not?” I repeated calmly, and
met his eyes. “People talk a great deal in Maremma, Signor Observer. You
might certainly be interested in the rumors which are rife in the city ...” The
voice drawled over the last phrase, and the smile once again emphasized its
deliberately alluring nature. I suddenly realized—but without anger, rather
with a feeling of conspiratorial curiosity—why Belsenza’s police had so
consistently drawn a blank. “The police are interested in them as well, I
should warn you. It would be a mistake to exaggerate the naiveté of the
police. Sooner or later they will lay hands on those who are spreading such
rumors, and I can promise you that the matter will then be closed.” “You are
mistaken about that, Signor Observer,” he remarked with an embarrassed

little cough. “I cannot believe you would reason like the simple police.”
“Excuse me. The simple police do not reason so badly,” I interrupted coldly,
“when they determine to track down a source which seems to me less and
less doubtful, and to place the responsibility on certain public poisoners. I
have no doubt that the sentiments your government exhibits are properly
appreciated by the Signory. But I shall permit myself to enlighten the
Signory somewhat, and it may discern that, between intentions and actions,
there is a considerable distance. If there had not been such stubborn
intention to infect public opinion, we should not have undertaken that
necessary precaution which seems to vex you so mightily.” The stranger
gazed out the window with a courteous and discouraged gesture. “I see that
it is very difficult for us to understand one another,” he remarked with a
resigned patience. “Indeed, I cannot feel quite comfortable dealing with an
agent provocateur.” On his part there was a moment of silence which was
not so much offended as distressed, like the silence of shocked propriety
which follows the excessively noisy smashing of an important piece of
china. “I am glad the word has been spoken,” he went on, with pitiless
insouciance. “Fearing to embitter matters, Rhages has perhaps evinced an
excessive zeal to avoid doing so.” Once again he made a gesture which
closed the subject, as though casually apologizing. The expression on his
face, which increasingly intrigued me, was that of a gambler who cautiously
turns over the cards of a deal one by one. “Let us leave this matter aside,”
he said in an irritated voice. “I am afraid we are slipping into a nasty
altercation.” He gazed at me again with an open and almost ingenuous
smile, the way one tries to wheedle a sulky child into good humor. “It
seems to me we are losing sight of a remarkable feature of the situation,” he
continued, looking back toward the window. “We have everything to gain,
if we want to understand each other, by not setting an undue importance
upon what I should call ready-made ill will. No, no, please, don’t speak!”
(There was a sudden haste in his voice, as if he had feared to see me
terminate the interview once again.) “I meant that if we were to resume the
routine language of police and chancelleries, we should not have words to
explain ourselves with regard to what I should call, if you like, the new

fact.” His eyes met mine again, and since I said nothing, his expression
suddenly brightened with a delicate and relaxed smile, a smile filled with
charm. Yet a wrinkle drew down one corner of his mouth, I now noticed,
making it stiff and austere as a scar, so that his smile was nuanced with a
touch of cruelty. “... Look here, Signor Observer,” he went on, “it is difficult
to talk, it is difficult to think in the context of an official language and an
inherited situation. The former speaks of ‘provocation’ and ‘espionage’ and
the latter calls itself war. You have reminded me just now, with a certain
humor, that there can be a considerable distance between sentiments and
actions. But I listen to you and I think in my turn that there is sometimes a
long way from words to ... feelings,” he concluded, looking into my eyes
with an amused expression. “Might I ask you to explain what you mean?”
“You have received me here this evening.” The stranger’s gaze slowly
circled the room, lingering over the shadows the lamplight vaguely stirred.
The calm around us had deepened in the sleeping Admiralty; it seemed to
me that the room had filled with that warm and lingering intimacy, riddled
with complicitous silences, which brings together under the lamp, for a last
cigar, two very intimate friends. In the distance, behind the fortress, roosters
crowed, deceived as they often were by the brilliant moonlight of Syrtes.
Suddenly it seemed to me that it was very late and that the sound of our
somnolent voices was sinking into an ageless obscurity, joining the murmur
of dreams that made the desert nights hum so faintly. “Don’t take it as any
special compliment,” I said, smiling in my turn despite myself. “We don’t
find anyone much to talk to, here in Syrtes.” I heard my sentence fall into
the silence with a sudden discomfort, struck by the ambiguity of that ‘find
anyone’ ... It was as if, in the stranger’s presence, words hesitated of their
own accord on the edge of a slippery incline, suddenly ready to say too
much. “What did you mean when you said ‘new fact’?” “Perhaps the
expression is excessive. It would be very misleading, in my opinion, Signor
Observer” (the voice once again obligingly emphasized the phrase) “if one
seeks to judge the changes which have occurred in the relations between
our two countries, to place oneself on the mere terrain of facts. By limiting
oneself to this terrain, it is not impossible that the government of Rhages

has had its attention drawn to certain protective measures which the
situation scarcely justifies ... I believe there has been a great deal of
construction at the Admiralty of late,” he commented with a smile. “And
yet, judging it from here, as I am now in a position to do, I am rather
inclined to suppose that I have seen certain ... defenses yielding, one by
one.” His glance slid between his eyelids like a knife blade. “Orsenna will
be grateful for your diagnosis,” I grinned, with an embarrassed look. “It will
nonetheless ask your pardon for finding itself in excellent health.” He
ignored my irony. “I have lived in your country, Signor Observer,” he said
in a serious, even a sad voice which no longer sought to be evasive. “And I
have loved it. And because I have loved it, I have hoped your people would
enjoy a happy old age—which is to say, a short imagination. It is not good
for imagination to ... affect a people, when that people is too old.” “You
have lived too much in Maremma,” I said, making sure I laughed again. “I
know our good city has been suffering from a slight bout of fever. No one
less than you, I believe, will be deceived by such rumors.” “Such rumors do
not risk remaining unmotivated for long. That is how I wanted to answer
your ‘if not?’. It happens that the fever is gaining ground,” he said,
weighing his words and slowly raising his eyes to meet mine. “A
disinterested observer may judge such an ... obsession to be merely curious,
but in actuality one never feels comfortable very long in being the object of
such a feeling of ... election.” “Do you intend to use unverifiable rumors as
the basis of your proceedings against what you are calling our intention?”
The stranger slowly shook his head. “I mentioned no proceedings of any
kind,” he said, choosing his words with great care. “I am trying to anticipate
developments. I am trying to foresee with you the possible course of events
between our two peoples, the working out of new relations which you will
doubtless agree in calling effective.” “You are mad!” I gasped, and felt my
cheeks turning red. “I am trying to make things easier for you,” he said,
lowering his gaze with confident nonchalance. “I feel great sympathy for
you. I know how embarrassing it can be in certain cases to make—how
shall I say ... ?” His glance met mine once more through the slit of those
oblique eyes. “... a declaration. I know how much the government of

Rhages is mistaken,” he added, eager to cut me off, “in its judgment of the
incident which is motivating our discussion ...” His sagacious and ironic
eyes were on me now—a fly that could not be driven away. “... I myself am
convinced that this incursion was not ... hostile.” “No, it was not,” I said in
a voice that sounded choked in spite of myself. He lowered his eyes and
seemed to meditate. The moonlight whitened the window and seemed to
make the lamplight paler. The night was opening like a glade, floating in the
incorporeal time that makes insomnia so endless; once again, somewhere in
that false dawn that was all too calm, roosters crowed. “Will you say as
much at Rhages?” asked the stranger in a neutral tone. “And if I did?” “If
you did?” He repeated the question mechanically. “... If you did ... well
then! there is no question, I think, that the situation would be closed. We
should merely suppose that Orsenna had temporarily suffered from a kind
of ... insomnia,” he continued in a voice whose excessive politeness now
had something insulting in it. “One cannot find a good reason, as a matter
of fact, for supposing that matters would fail to settle down once more. It is
not given to everyone to end tragically,” he added in a singsong tone that
disagreeably transformed his voice. “To end?” I asked in a stunned tone.
The word struck my opaque ear with a dull shock. “As you well know,” he
murmured, half rising from his chair and bringing his mouth closer to my
ear. “I have come to help you understand. You must not suppose you could
get off so cheaply ... I greatly esteem the sermons at Saint John
Damascene,” he said, fastening his gleaming eyes on mine while I
followed, almost hypnotized, the precise and delicate movements of his lips
—the way one reads those of a mute. “... And I think that here you fall a
little short of a serious argument.” “What are you getting at?” I snapped,
standing up in my turn. I could tell I was very pale. “I’m getting at where
you’re going,” the slightly singsong voice replied, unperturbed. “We’re in
no hurry. We’ve been waiting for you a long time—you had a rendezvous
with us since you’ve been here. But some day you’ll thank me for your
luck: you’ll have come with open eyes.” He bowed slightly and I realized
he was about to take his leave. “... Remember this, Signor Observer, it will
give you a new perspective on moonlight expeditions: for peoples, there is

only one kind of ... intimate relation.” “But where is the answer to be sent?”
I suddenly exclaimed, as though awakening, as he was already gliding out
of the room and through the door. The narrow eyes turned back to meet
mine out of the darkness. “You fail to do yourself justice. There will be
none,” he said calmly, and once again the door swung shut on darkness. For
a long while I remained sitting motionless at my desk. The silent, reptilian
way he had appeared out of the dark and returned to it, the fascination his
eyes and his voice had worked upon me, and this very late hour, might have
persuaded me that the whole episode had been an hallucination, if the
document starred with the scarlet seal of Rhages had not been lying on my
desk, like those malefic pacts one signs with one’s blood. A sinister note
echoed in the stranger’s last words; now that this presence, more
encompassing than any I had ever experienced, had withdrawn, it seemed to
me that the black chill of the small hours at Syrtes had slipped into the
unsealed room, and mechanically I went over to the half-open window.
There was nothing to be seen but the moon-whitened moor; the hoofbeats
of a horse trotting along the causeway were just audible. The desire to call
the stranger back rose in my throat so abruptly that I barely choked down a
cry; the hoofbeats were already fading in the darkness; the cutting edge of
that voice once again froze my ears: that figure so rapidly swallowed up by
the dark was one of those it is useless to pursue. I passed my hand over my
face: it was covered with a cold sweat; a dizzy spell stretched me out on the
bed, my head ringing. What remained of my thoughts followed the stranger
into that motionless dark; I realized that as soon as possible I must find
Vanessa. As I got out of the car in the chilly morning to hail one of the
palace boatmen always lounging along the wharf, I realized that Maremma
seemed to have wakened earlier than usual. I had had almost no sleep; the
tonic chill of this marine morning and my fast ride had momentarily shaken
my mind free of the memory of the interview of the night before. My
feverish need of Vanessa had become so blind and so exclusive that at the
very moment I was about to ask her to deny my basest suspicions I felt less
anxiety in the face of my uncertainty than joy in realizing that by adding to
so many suspect and shared secrets I would become more necessary to her.

It was market day in Maremma; many times already, leaving the palace at
dawn and still lulled in a half-sleep on the water cluttered with the debris of
vegetables, I had smelled the juicy, heady odor of the Syrtes melons, green
pyramids unloaded at a corner of the docks and still steaming with mist, and
had noticed the peasant clatter of bare feet on wet stones; but now, instead
of the hubbub of the market, the murmur was that of a crowd gathering
around a serious accident. It seemed to me that the Admiralty car parked on
the quay was provoking even more curiosity than usual; abandoning the few
open-air stalls, the crowd quickly formed at some distance; a mixture of
anxious curiosity and respect seemed to paralyze their faces, and from the
expressions of gravity which suddenly made them look older, I realized that
news of our nocturnal expedition must have leaked out. “Something new,
Beltran?” I asked the boatman, gesturing with my chin to the preoccupied
faces which now fringed the edge of the dock and followed us with insistent
stares. “It’s the disaster, Excellency,” he said, lowering his eyes and
speaking with that resigned inflection one hears in the poor people’s throats
in times of affliction, and he kissed the cross Syrtes fishermen wear on a
chain over their hearts. “...It was all forseen,” he added, shaking his head
with a senile gesture. “God wills it so. Since last week we’ve been praying
to Saint John Damascene night and day.” As I might have expected, the
Aldobrandi palace was taking more than its part in the effervescence that
prevailed in town. The corridors and the suites of rooms I crossed, already
filled with a turbulence of slammed doors, of hurrying footsteps and of
confabulations in corners, suggested the headquarters of a city under siege
and the confusion of a dying sovereign’s palace, its murmurs vacillating
between remedies of the quacks and the regency’s plots. I hurried through
the groups; once again, I filled my lungs with the familiar sensation that life
here was consumed faster than elsewhere. Yet Vanessa had not appeared;
her maid was dozing in front of her closed door.
“I’ve come early, Viola,” I said, and smiled as I rested my hand on her
shoulder. “Will the princess receive me at such an hour?”

“God be praised!” she said, grasping my hands with an exalted gesture.
“She’s been waiting for you these two hours.”
Vanessa had just finished dressing as I made my way into her room. I was
struck by how pale she was—an almost showy pallor which had nothing to
do with fatigue or sickness, though it was evident she had not slept for a
long while; rather such pallor descended upon her like the grace of a more
solemn moment; as if she had assumed it for the occasion. She was wearing
a black dress with long folds, of an austere simplicity; with her long hair
hanging down her neck and her shoulders so white above the dress, her
loveliness combined the fleeting beauty of an actress and the sovereign
beauty of catastrophe; she was like a queen at the foot of the scaffold.
“Behold the hero of the day,” she exclaimed, smiling with contained
excitement as she glided across the room to meet me. “You’ve been so
long!” she continued in a low whisper as she took my head in both hands
and raised it so that my eyes met hers—her eyes which answered me, which
answered everything ... “I’ve been waiting for you night and day.”
In an impulse of contradiction, I thrust her away, gently. Vanessa was not
unconscious of her weapons, and this abrupt embrace made me bristle. “...
Your friends in Rhages still have cannons, did you know that?” I said,
indicating the painting with my eyes and keeping my voice quite cool. “As
a matter of fact, if they had aimed a little better, it’s likely you’d have had to
wait for me a very, very long time.”
Vanessa remained silent.
“...I went over there, you’ll be pleased to hear,” I continued with marked
ill humor. “It seems to me I’ve provided your guests with an interesting
subject for conversation.”
“I’m not pleased, I’m happy,” she said, and seized my hands and began
kissing them passionately. “... Orsenna has remembered its arms. I’m so
proud of you,” she added with a vehemence I did not find altogether
convincing. There was a touch of rhetoric in it that wasn’t her style, or
perhaps I was merely responding to that shadow of embarrassment which is
always attached to manifestations of feminine patriotism.

“Who’s mentioned arms? It seems to me that people hereabouts are living
a little too much on your imagination, Vanessa,” I added coldly. “I must
warn you that the salons of the Aldobrandi palace have taken history a little
too much on credit. There wasn’t even a skirmish—I gave orders not to
reply.”
Which was overstating the case, but I was a man who suddenly sees his
horse take the bit in its teeth. Vanessa glanced at me twice, with an
expression of incredulous surprise—as if she refused to believe her eyes.
“Of course, Aldo—you were very discreet about the whole affair ...
You’re discretion itself,” she continued, as one might comfort a child.
“Everyone admires you here, I must say, for having shown so much
composure.”
“Everyone?” I echoed the word with astonishment, so little did this
appeal to received ideas sound like her. “... Everyone? But Vanessa, what
does that mean? There’s not one word spoken in Maremma that you haven’t
prompted.”
Vanessa stood up abruptly, and forthwith took the wind, as I used to call it
in our intimate moments when I teased her about that sudden impression
she gave of swelling with the breeze: she began walking up and down with
her elastic leonine stride, and all at once the room seemed smaller. Again I
received—even more strongly—the impression that she had not ceased to
be on stage for one minute since I had come in.
“You’re wrong, Aldo,” she said at last. “I might have yesterday, but not
today. Not any longer. All that’s out of our hands now,” she added, with a
kind of tranquility.
“It seems to me that not much of ‘all that’ has escaped you ... You wanted
me to go over there—you made that clear.”
Vanessa stopped at the window and glanced pensively toward the canal.
“Perhaps,” she said, shrugging with indifference. “It doesn’t matter much,
now.”
“Matter! ... The captain will be here in two days. I’m going to have to
answer for things,” I went on in a changed voice. “Do you suppose he’ll

forget about all that so easily?”
“You’re making yourself very important, Aldo,” she observed in a
faraway voice. “You’re not humble. Neither you nor I count for much in
this affair,” she added in a tone of literal observation.
“I went over there, Vanessa, and you wanted me to,” I said as I leaned
toward her, in a low and patient voice, the way you get the attention of
someone who has fallen asleep.
“No, Aldo. Someone went over there. Because there was no other way.
Because it was time. Because someone had to go ... Have you noticed,” she
said in a lower tone of voice, grasping my wrist, “when something’s going
to happen, how everything suddenly changes direction? ... Or meaning? ...
Did Marino ever tell you how he went down with his ship?”
She gave me a sidelong glance, and once again there was that tone of
intimacy and irony in her voice which she instinctively assumed whenever
she mentioned the captain. “It’s scarcely imaginable, Aldo—given his
passion for agriculture. But apparently you can’t judge people by the way
they look, and maybe it was in a previous life ... When he shows you that
hand with the two fingers missing, you can’t help thinking—you know?—
of someone who has received the stigmata!”
She broke into her silvery laugh.
“No one in Syrtes has a service record that compares to Marino’s.” I
answered dryly.
“Don’t be upset, Aldo ...” Again the delicate, rather fierce laughter
mocked me. “You know how much I care for him. We’re old friends. Well,
even when they were sinking ... Aldo! I don’t know if you can visualize
Marino as a man of the storms, arms folded on the bridge while the ship
was going under,” she mused, as though struck in spite of herself by the
comic impossibility of the scene.
“‘Women and children first’ ... Oh yes, I can see it perfectly,” and I
smiled in my turn, finding it less awkward to play the game. “He has a
natural dignity you aren’t doing justice.”

“There were no women or children, nothing but the crew: it was a
warship. The sea was rising, the men were clinging to the rigging, retreating
in front of the water inch by inch: you couldn’t have made them let go with
an axe, Marino told me. The water was rising very slowly, the ship was in
no hurry to sink—it had hit some sort of unmarked reef in a very calm sea.
Apparently they hadn’t heard a sound, and Marino said it wasn’t impressive
at all—actually it was rather a calm sort of disaster, as if they were scuttling
some rotten old hull to block up a harbor. All of a sudden there was this
huge ‘ploof’—Marino turned round and there was no one left on the
rigging, the crew was floundering in the waves or else going under; they
had all jumped into the sea at once,” she concluded, as though absorbed in
this vision, a greedy intensity in her voice.
“Rats also desert a sinking ship,” I said with a shrug. “It only proves that
men have no nose for catastrophes.”
“Are you so sure? ... Anyway, that’s not important, that’s not what
seemed so odd to me about the story. What struck me,” she added, letting
her gaze drift toward the window, “is that the signs must have changed. One
moment everyone is still holding on, and the next everyone jumps in, the
whole crew in the water ... Yes,” she continued, as if she were
contemplating some obvious fact, “there comes a moment when you jump
—it’s not fear and it’s not calculation, it’s not even the desire to survive; it’s
because a voice closer than any voice in the world speaks to you—because
it isn’t even the same as dying to go down with the ship, because anything is
better than being tied to a corpse, all of a sudden anything’s better than
being stuck to a doomed thing that stinks of death. The rising waters are
patient,” she said dreamily. “They can wait. Their prey will always make
the way shorter ...”
“So that’s what you’ve come here for,” I said, getting up with an abrupt
gesture. I couldn’t tell what I was thinking any more. It seemed to me I had
spoken the very words falling out of her mouth myself, yet they inspired me
with disgust and rage; Vanessa’s shamelessness lay upon me like a bold

hand; again she inspired in me that brutality which finally dissolved in
tenderness like a hailstorm.
“It seems to me you’ve come here too, Aldo. You’ve come even farther
than I.” She met my eyes with a smile of pride, and in spite of myself I
began yielding to the spell of that radiant smile.
“God knows what’s going to come of all this,” I said, staring at her
pensively. “I’m afraid we’ve both done something insane,” I added, taking
her hand in my turn, needing to know she wasn’t abandoning me.
Vanessa shrugged and seemed to dismiss an importunate thought. “Are
you trying to make me sorry?” She turned toward me and her eyes sparkled.
“All the same, Orsenna’s learned who we are,” she said between clenched
teeth. “My people have been a thorn in its side, and Orsenna’s been a
broken-down nag between our legs, driven to a last gallop. What do you
expect—that the rider should apologize,” she added with fierce irony, “for
having given the animal its head?”
“Not a flattering comparison,” I remarked coldly. “Besides, there’s a
proverb that says you shouldn’t waste time whipping a dead horse.
Orsenna’s sleeping peacefully. Why remember what the city’s chosen to
forget?”
“Go on, stick its nose in the feedbag,” she said with a smile of extreme
scorn. “Marino will help you.” She turned away, her face angelic with rage,
and her long warrior’s stride once again echoed up and down the room.
“Something insane?” she repeated, suddenly stopping as if she were
talking to herself. “Is a man insane who gropes for the wall in the midst of
his nightmare? Do you think people would talk so much—since it seems
they are talking—if their ears weren’t finally deafened by never hearing an
echo?”
“That’s where you may be wrong, and that’s just what I wanted to get at.
It seems that the wall isn’t altogether without an echo. I’ve already heard
one, if you want to know.”
Vanessa’s gaze became fixed, and her pupils contracted faintly.
Comforted by the intense curiosity which disarmed her gaze, I suddenly felt

more at ease.
“An echo?” she said, her voice incredulous.
“Do you still take your little excursions out to sea?” I asked her in an
indifferent tone of voice.
“What do you mean?”
“Nothing special. I’d have liked to know, for instance, if your boat is still
ready to sail.”
There was a moment’s silence.
“How do you know?” Vanessa finally said, her voice stupefied.
“Perhaps you’d like to know where it’s gone? It happens I have some
light to shed on the matter.”
Vanessa stared at me with an uncertain and embarrassed expression.
“Gone?” she repeated, incredulous. “You don’t mean ...” She started
suddenly, as though all at once struck by an idea.
“Over there. Yes!” I snapped at her, and watched her face carefully, but
the sudden start I expected did not occur: Vanessa’s eyes narrowed again,
with a dangerous expression of amused and conniving cunning, then
brightened with a sudden glow. “Vanessa!” I exclaimed, and put my arms
around her and shook her until her hands flapped. “Vanessa, don’t you
understand what it means? Don’t you see whom you’ve been protecting—
covering?”
“Did you come here to demand an accounting?” she said, staring at me
with disdainful composure ... “I didn’t know anything of the kind, of
course,” she went on with a shrug—“my word will have to be enough for
you.”
“You didn’t know. Of course you didn’t know. But perhaps you suspected
...”
Vanessa burst into an insulting laugh. “‘You suspected’ ...” she continued,
imitating my voice. “‘You suspected’ ... My word, Aldo, this is an
interrogation. You can’t imagine how much you interest me in the role of
Grand Inquisitor ...”

“You will answer,” I said, standing up in an impulse of icy rage as I
grabbed her wrist. “I swear to you, Vanessa, I don’t feel like joking. Did you
suspect?”
Vanessa raised her head. “What if I did?” she said in a low, clear voice ...
“Yes, since you want to know.”
I suddenly dropped the tense hand, and I felt I was sitting down heavily:
my head was spinning. What I felt was not disgust, nor anger, but instead a
scared and troubled amazement, as in the presence of someone who
suddenly begins walking on water.
“You’ll just have to get used to it, Aldo,” Vanessa said very distinctly
behind me. “Things don’t fall ready made into our hands.”
“You could do a thing like that?” I answered, my voice incredulous in its
turn.
I suddenly turned around, surprised by her silence. Vanessa wasn’t even
listening to me; over my head, she was staring at the painting on the wall.
“You’ve looked at it often, haven’t you?” I went on, venomously. I stood
up and took a step toward her, but suddenly stopped. Vanessa wasn’t
looking at me, and I was overcome in spite of myself by the magic of that
portrait. Even now it was imposing silence... .
“I wonder what he’s thinking about,” Vanessa said in a tone of the
deepest concentration. “I often wonder. You guessed right, Aldo,” she said,
taking another step, as though fascinated. “I even get up at night sometimes
to look at him. I wonder if you and I have ever been so close,” she went on,
and her tone made my throat tighten. “You know, those summer nights that
are hotter than the days, when it seems as if Syrtes were macerating like a
body in its own sweat ... I get up barefoot on the cool tiles, in that white
nightgown you like”—she turned toward me with a gleam of provocation in
her eyes—“I’ve cheated on you often, Aldo. It was an assignation. At that
hour, Maremma seems dead; not a sleeping city but a city whose heart has
stopped beating, a city that’s been sacked—and if you look out over the bay,
the lagoon is like a crust of salt, and you think you’re looking at one of the

seas on the moon. As if the planet had cooled while you were asleep, as if
you got up during a night on the other side of time.”
“You think you’re looking at what will be happening some day,” she
went on with an inspired exaltation, “when there’ll be no more Maremma,
no more Orsenna, not even ruins, nothing but the lagoon and the sand and
the desert wind under the stars. You think you passed through the centuries
all alone, and that you’re breathing deeper, more solemnly, because so
many millions of rotten lungs have disappeared. Were there never any
nights, Aldo, when you dreamed the earth was suddenly turning just for
you? Turning faster and faster, so that you were leaving behind all the
animals with weaker lungs? Those are the ones that don’t like the future;
but the animal who feels he has a heart for that impossible speed—what’s
crime and perdition to him? Only what keeps him from leaping ahead,
nothing else. To think men live together because they live side by side ...
There are cities that are damned for some people by the mere fact that they
seem created to close off the distances that are the only reason for living.
Comfortable cities—you can see the world in them as you can nowhere
else: like a squirrel on its treadmill. But the only ones I like are the ones
where you feel the desert wind blowing through the streets; and there are
days, Aldo,” Vanessa said, turning toward me and staring at me with her
hard eyes, “when I’ve had a serious quarrel with Orsenna: all you can smell
there is the swamp, and sometimes I’ve thought it was keeping the earth
from turning ... There’s something scary about staring at a portrait by night,
by candlelight. It’s as if a legible face, a face from the depths of the
shadows that have dissolved it, is eager to reach the surface, to form again
in contact with this little fluttering life that separates the light from the
darkness all over again—as if it were desperately calling, as if it were trying
to be recognized one last time. If you’ve ever even seen such a vision, Aldo,
you’ve seen how the darkness is populated—you’ve seen the night take
form. The man who called to me was of my blood, my race, and I felt that
beyond shame, beyond the dishonor assigned for order’s sake, like
decorations in wartime—I felt his way of smiling summoning me to a secret
over which the city’s stupid conscience had no judgment and no rights... .

“I’d like to make you understand something, Aldo. There’s a story my
father used to tell me when I was a little girl, a story which had a
tremendous effect on me; he had heard it from my great-uncle Giacomo,
Giacomo the Profaner, the one who led the San Domenico uprising, during
the Merchants rebellion ... Well, there’s no help for it, alas!” Vanessa broke
off, shooting me a smile of half-amused insolence, “the genealogy of the
Aldobrandis is the inadmissible Gotha of perfidious factions, as our history
books say. When Giacomo and his armed bands seized the building of the
Consulta, as you may remember, where they could hold out for only a few
hours, Giacomo managed to lay hands on the police archives and
discovered the list of spies the Signory had planted in the people’s party.
They were hunted down right away to be shot then and there; you
remember, during that affair, no quarter was given on either side. Do you
know where those men were found? You’ll never guess ... On the
barricades, where they were firing bravely against the Signory’s troops;
some had already been killed, others had to be dragged off the parapets to
be shot against the cobblestones. ‘A dreadful mistake!’ is reputed to have
been my great-uncle’s lament after the fact, hiding his face in his hands (I
can’t help it, he wasn’t as sensitive as you), ‘does a winemaker burn his
barrels because they’ve already been used?’ I hope you find it in your heart
to forgive him, Aldo,” Vanessa went on, giving me that demonic sidelong
glance of hers, “he was a cynic of the kind you see only too often—well, I
mean ... such remarks don’t reveal an enthusiast of inviolable personality;
all he saw was strength wasted, and perhaps from his point of view he
wasn’t entirely wrong. But from a more ... contemplative perspective, if you
like, and setting aside, of course, the extremely blameworthy meaning
attached to such actions by our sentiment of virtue (again Vanessa darted
me an extremely enigmatic glance), one might consider so singular a man
rather differently. It’s jumping to conclusions, Aldo, to speak as one does in
such cases, of people who have ‘treason in their blood.’”
Suddenly Vanessa’s voice became more serious.

“Perhaps it’s merely a question of riper and wiser connoisseurs of action,
of people who like to consider things all the way around, even if it’s
dangerous, of minds bold enough to have understood, faster than the rest,
that beyond the blind and stupid excitement struggling in the darkness of
our petty wills, there’s room, if you’re not afraid of being alone, for an
almost divine delight: to pass over to the other side as well, to experience at
the same time the effort and the resistance ... The ones whom Orsenna, in
the naïveté of its (not always so naive) heart inconsiderately calls turncoats
and traitors, I’ve sometimes called in mine poets of the event. I’d like you to
understand these things, Aldo, if you want us to continue to understand
each other, and I’d like you to realize how far, but no farther, your little
delicacies come into consideration. And I also wanted to tell you, if you
went on imposing them—since I’m just back from Orsenna,” she added in a
serious tone of voice, “that not everything there is going quite the way you
suppose, and that from now on people there might be taking things ...
impatiently.”
“I know your father’s back in favor,” I said as circumspectly as I could
manage. “I should tell you I don’t regard the fact as exactly reassuring
news.”
Vanessa seemed to ignore my insinuation. “You’ll find considerable
changes in Orsenna,” she went on, squinting slightly. “And perhaps not the
ones you’re thinking of ... The city seemed more ... alert than I’d have
expected,” she added after a pause. She seemed to be searching for the
words with which to say something difficult.
“Really?”
“It’s not all that obvious—in fact, you need sharp eyes to see what I
mean,” Vanessa went on. “There are signs that can be read only by eyes that
have been waiting for them a long time—watching and waiting.”
“In the memory of man, no one’s read the signs of Orsenna,” I said
ironically. “There are no omens among our people, as you know very well.
There are only anniversaries.”

“I’d be surprised, though, if you didn’t read them the way I do,” Vanessa
answered, pensive now. “Not that there’s anything so very specific ...” She
resumed walking up and down, stopping sometimes as if she were trying to
seize a fleeting impression. “... People can’t be judged by what they do,
that’s what it is. You think they’re elsewhere, that they’re always thinking
about something else ... The faces you meet in the streets—and you
remember, Aldo, there were lives so intense and so full, as present as if you
were walking through a garden—sometimes they made me think of the
carefully preserved facades of evacuated houses. You walk in Orsenna
today as though in an apartment you’re about to move out of ... Not that I
had anything against that,” she added with a smile. “There were days when
it seemed the streets had come apart—that there was a little fresh air in
them now.”
“I see that you went out a good deal in Orsenna,” I interrupted
humorously.
“It seems as if people are instinctively making room for something that
hasn’t happened yet,” she continued as if she hadn’t heard me. “Frankly,
Orsenna’s salons aren’t much improved for that: I’ve never heard such
boring conversations! You know what they’re usually about. Well, the lack
of enthusiasm for discussing the November ball at the Consulta or the next
promotion of the Cross of Saint Jude really stupefied me.”
“Which will be all to Maremma’s advantage. You pointed out, I suppose,
how busy tongues are here.”
Vanessa gave me a little ironic pout. “You’re in a bad mood, Aldo. I’m
sure that soon enough Orsenna will have nothing to envy us with regard to
subjects of conversation. You wouldn’t believe how fast fresh news travels
...”
“You don’t mean they know ...” I said, standing up. I felt I had suddenly
gone pale. “I’ve barely had time to send in my report.”
“You’re a child, Aldo. It was known in Maremma that same morning, and
I heard it just one day later. It seemed to me there was something in the air,
and I arranged matters to be informed. What do you expect, Aldo—I like

knowing things,” she went on, staring at me intently. “And I had no reason
once I was back there to make a secret out of a piece of news that would get
out sooner or later. You know how flattered women are to seem
‘informed,’” she continued, smiling with sinister gaiety: “it’s a kind of
innocent obsession.”
I wiped my hand across my forehead, but wasn’t even sweating. It
seemed to me that I had been caught, naked and frozen in the beam of a
powerful floodlight. I gave no thought to the consequences; all I felt was
the raw horror of an almost physical contact: those thousands of eyes
trained on me in Orsenna now knew. “It’s the end!” I murmured stupidly,
almost whispering, and I felt that this was a wish rather than an observation;
at that instant of brutal collapse, I passionately longed for the earth to open
under me. At that moment, and at that moment alone, I understood
everything; by the light which suddenly starred all those faraway pupils, I
finally saw what I had done.
“You don’t understand, Aldo,” Vanessa went on eagerly. “I took the
offensive. I presented the affair in the most favorable light for you. Of
course I gave matters a little push. A little twist. Everyone in Orsenna now
believes you were treacherously attacked at sea.”
I stared dully at her, still uncertain of her betrayal. “I’ve received a
formal notification from Orsenna—very serious,” I began in a low voice.
“You knew that, didn’t you—or you suspected it ... You’re so well
informed. You want Orsenna not to withdraw, that’s what it is, Vanessa,” I
said, standing up, beside myself at her silence, and I spoke very close to her
face between my clenched teeth. “That’s why you wakened public opinion
in advance, that’s why you closed every door behind me. Don’t lie!” I
screamed at her, “you did it, you wanted it this way, but I didn’t, I swear
before God, Vanessa, you know I didn’t, and you know what this means ...”
“War?” she said after a pause, in a neutral tone of voice, and slowly
raised her unseeing eyes to mine. “It’s never been anything else, as far as I
know. Why are you afraid of the word? Suppose you leave God out of it—

what a coward you are, Aldo,” she went on, with a smile of extreme
disdain.
“You wanted this! I didn’t ...”
I made a clumsy gesture, as if to ward off a curse, and suddenly in spite
of myself, my tears began to flow, thick and silent. I wept without shame,
turning my naked face toward Vanessa; standing bolt upright in a dark
corner of the room, she watched those tears flow in silence.
“You ... me ...” she said at last, shrugging awkwardly, “Can’t you say
anything else but those two words?” She came to me and gently laid her
hand on my shoulder, lowering her eyes. “I love Orsenna more than you do,
Aldo, it’s in my blood, do you understand? And I’m more submissive than
you are, I’m prompter to obey all the city’s desires. If you were a woman,
you wouldn’t be so proud,” she added with a persuasive softness in her
voice, as if someone else suddenly—a spirit of the shadows—had spoken
through her: “You’d understand better. A woman who’s borne a child
knows: knows that you can want ... you don’t know who or what, you really
don’t know—something through yourself, and that it’s frightening, and
profoundly restful ... if you only knew, to feel what will come passing
through your body. Listen!” she said suddenly, raising her hand in a gesture
of fascinated attention.
A sound was now filling the room, a sound both muffled and distinct,
that seemed to well up from everywhere at once, like the murmur of the sea
far away on calm nights: Maremma was speaking behind the door, and in
the sleep of that cottony morning the murmur of the feverish palace made a
sickly roar that drowned out the silence, like a distinct whirlwind or a cloud
of locusts, as if the mandibles of millions of insects were interminably
gnawing something... .
“Did you hear?” Vanessa said, brushing my hands with hers. “That’s how
they spend their lives now ... They’re absolving me: they have nothing to do
with me—they’ve no more need of me, they’ve never needed me.
Something’s come, it’s here now—what do I have to do with that? When
the wind blows pollen over a flower, there’s something in the ripening fruit,

something that mocks the wind. There’s a kind of certainty that there never
was such a thing as wind in all the world, since the fruit is there ... They’ve
never needed me, and I’ve never needed you, Aldo, and it’s good that way,”
she went on with a kind of profound assurance. “Once a thing is really
brought into the world, it’s not like a thing that ‘happens’; all of a sudden
there’s no other eye but its own to see with, and there’s no longer any
question that it might not exist ... All’s well!”
OceanofPDF.com

11
Final Inspection
“Old Carlo’s dead,” Fabrizio hurriedly informed me as I entered my office
in the Admiralty. “They’re burying him this afternoon at three. In the
military cemetery: Giovanni thought that would be all right with you. You
know—it’s the custom here,” he added in a graver voice. “Besides, Marino
was very fond of him ...”
Fabrizio’s last sentence fell into a deeper silence than this expected news
might have created. I had returned from the palace in a calmer frame of
mind, as if, once again, I had somehow been assuaged by Vanessa’s
composure, her incomprehensible certainty; the news darkened that bright
morning for me. More than once it had occurred to me, during these last
anxious days, to pay another visit to Ortello: as if the old man’s mere
presence might have calmed my agitation and my uncertainty, or as if
something might have passed, from me to him, without words and without
effort, of all that constituted my anxiety. Now he was dead; his last words
came back to me, poignant as a hand one has not taken; all of a sudden it
occurred to me that perhaps—undoubtedly—he hadn’t known before he
died. “It’s now, and it’s come too soon,” he had told me; despite myself, in
the light of what had ensued, old Carlo’s words assumed a prophetic
resonance, as if at the last moment the news had failed, perfidiously, to
reach the only one who would have understood it; as if, less fortunate than
the aged Simeon, his eyes hadn’t seen the only sign they still kept open for.
Suddenly I had a vision of the sand spread over the wretched anonymity of
the graves, and with a pang of pity, I felt that the man we would bury,
whose bristling beard was still sprouting in his coffin, was now deader than
any of those who had rotted here for ages.
It was the custom in Syrtes to bury the masters of the great estates in the
military cemetery—the Admiralty garrison had seemed much more devoted

to them than to wartime service. And there was a kind of justice in it: more
than one had served under fire, not long since, against the last desert
bandits, before Orsenna’s exhausted peace had fallen on these lands for
good. A powerful race of soldier-farmers had long controlled this remote
South of ours, talking loud and clear with its subalterns, more military than
the pale bookkeepers who had succeeded each other until Marino, and
resembling, in these faraway outposts, the last green suckers that still
emerge from the ground at a great distance from the exhausted trunk. This
race was dead in its turn, just as noble families had long since died out in
Orsenna; we knew that today we were burying the last of them, and our
little troupe walked more silently on the familiar road than usual. Out of the
milky sky, a calm, gray afternoon was falling over Syrtes, untroubled by the
flaccid sound of the wavelets; for days on end, sometimes, the cold current
that swept the coasts condensed into soft, deceiving mists which forever
promised rain and made the beach into a chilly desert, and in its sickly
breath our muscles went slack and our minds darkened.
“Old Carlo chose his moment,” Giovanni murmured distractedly, pulling
his cloak closer, “this is a real November day.” He darted a bored glance at
the empty coast. “... Syrtes is hardly an earthly paradise, this time of year.”
The four of us were walking along the gray road, our minds blank. The
equally blank sky was making all this land into a silent limbo; the cemetery
ahead of us looked like a grayer, gloomier puddle of boredom, of black
absence, of lugubrious incuriosity.
“He was someone, old Carlo,” Fabrizio announced, his voice heavy with
feeling, and I realized, smiling in spite of myself, that he was thinking of
the sumptuous hunt dinners Ortello had offered us last autumn.
“Yes,” Roberto agreed, nodding. “Marino will be sorry to have missed
this. He said he’d be here soon, though,” he went on in a different tone. “I
wonder ...”
We all knew that he was wondering. On my return from Maremma, I had
found the whole Admiralty crestfallen. The patrols had been cancelled, and
the night watches as well—everything in the fortress, as though by magic,

was the way it had been, hastily shrouded for another winter, each man
sealed in his shell—there had never really been anything to do: the captain
was coming back.
“Shouldn’t we ask the family to dinner?” There was a certain hesitation
in Roberto’s voice: “Ortello’s a long way off. That’s what the captain would
have done.” And in the silence that followed, we all felt how bereft our
little group had remained.
We waited a few minutes, bare-headed, at the cemetery gate. Soon there
appeared, at the turn of the road, one of those long, high-axled carts used
for work in deep sand. The coffin was set on it, open according to the Syrtes
custom, and when it was laid on the ground, I saw that it was filled to the
brim with late, sweet-smelling clusters of the wisteria which twined all over
the verandas of the South; the woodsman’s great papery corpse emerged
through a foam of fragile blossoms. His family and servants followed the
mortuary cart on horseback; one of those white-robed itinerant monks who
occasionally serve in the forsaken chapels of Syrtes was riding behind the
oldest son, and I suddenly realized I was watching a very ancient spectacle:
this long procession indifferently advancing over the flat earth, the slow
gestures and the tanned faces that the desert had turned ageless and
expressionless—all made me think of some cortege of barbaric nomads
bearing their leader’s body to some remote freshwater pasturage. One after
the other we brushed the old man’s forehead with the fingertips of our right
hand in farewell. When it was my turn, the older son, a giant with stubborn
curls, made me a clumsy sign, and I realized he wanted to say something.
“My father will lie in Orsenna’s ground. It is a great honor for us.” He
twisted his belt buckle between his fingers with an embarrassed look. After
a moment I realized what this apparently obscure sentence meant: the earth
of each military cemetery of Orsenna had long ago been mixed with a little
clay brought from the City. Suddenly his hand lay on mine in a gesture of
timid fervor: “I wanted to tell you ... There aren’t many of us here in the
South. It will be as God wills. But everyone here is loyal to you—count on
us, when the time comes.”

The coffin was lowered into its hole in the sand. A light desert wind was
already smoothing the friable crest—sand tumbled into the grave in endless
rivulets without a sound. There was something futile in the measured
gesture of the hands that successively scattered handfuls of sand over the
coffin; this earth so many times mingled with the wind was quintessential
dust, and I felt that the old man would have approved of his jeopardized
lodging. This soil which shifted like the dunes beneath its folds of sand did
not hold its prey forever. For me there was an infinitely disturbing symbol
in this patient, muffled life that clutched the earth so desperately and was
repossessed at its very end—so detached, so light—by a mysterious breath,
a symbol which allied itself with this nomad cortege, with this soil
imperceptibly restored to movement. There was nothing here which spoke
of a last resting place, but on the contrary the cheerful assurance that all
things are eternally returned to the cycle and destined elsewhere than our
own choice; I remembered the old man’s vague smile, which scarcely
encouraged sympathy, and I felt somehow understood and forgiven; it was
good to be here this afternoon, here in this cemetery as though on a first
winter morning, under the dry wind that chases the leaves down the roads.
The priest finished the last Latin prayers, and an awkward and
embarrassed silence fell around the grave. The horses neighed behind the
cemetery wall; on the road we could still hear the creaking of the now
lightened cart; trivial noises, muffled by the warm gray mist, suddenly
made this tiny bit of earth into an extraordinarily vacant place. Behind me I
heard the gate open, and turned around nervously. Marino was walking into
the cemetery.
I had expected, I had feared this return as the moment of my greatest
ordeal, and yet, hearing that slow, heavy trend coming up behind me on the
sandy path, what I felt was anything but fear: it was a profound relief, as
when you bathe in a spring—an inexplicable alleviation.
I stole a few glances at him while, in his slow, peasant’s voice, he
addressed a few words of condolence to the dead man’s family. Again the
faint wind from the sea stirred the grey locks above his extraordinarily

heavy face. In the long stiff folds of his yellowed greatcoat, he seemed to be
part of the earth, a sandy lump. Never so much as after this long absence
had I felt that this bit of ground fulfilled itself in him with a kind of groping
genius; that he belonged to it no longer even as a serf to his glebe but, more
purely and more intimately, as an element of the landscape. He was more
alive in the middle of this grim cemetery than any of the young men who
happened to be gathered there—alive with a kind of hibernal and vegetative
immortality, as if he had drawn into himself the last essence of this
exhausted soil, equally inured to the seasons and the weather, to drought
and hail, indomitable as those sandy-stemmed lilies which clutched for
sustenance at the crumbling ground. More than the stele of Orsenna
encrusted in the wall, it was he who was the symbol of this existence slowly
enmeshed with things, who was ultimately assumed in the uninterrupted
passing of its generations by the indistinct earth, like the varnish that
evaporation leaves on the desert stones. As if in him one had reached the
low-water mark of consciousness, the desert spaces of a life without
memory and without wrinkles seemed to appear on that countenance of
such naive emptiness, of such nocturnal incuriosity. And yet that face had
changed. I looked at it, almost alien to what was to follow, with a kind of
detached impartiality; and suddenly I realized—as if it had concerned me,
like a woman to whom her mirror reveals the first overwhelming sign—
how much and how suddenly he had aged. I knew that Marino was no
longer a young man, but it was not the calm approach of old age which was
divulged, in the presence of this earthen face, this heavily motionless mask,
by the warning that rose from my own flesh. Rather he seemed to be one of
those legendary kings sleeping for centuries in some cavern who waken
from a magic slumber only to fall into dust and to perish in an instant—as if
time, through him, had changed rhythm and speed, had suddenly come
loose before my eyes. This scarred face absorbed my gaze not like the
remote envelope of mists in which our way will some day be lost, but like
the crevice left in the middle of the road by an earthquake.
As the tiny group filed out of the cemetery, I saw the captain lingering
among the graves as if he were waiting for me; he joined me in front of the

gate; we were alone—in the empty enclosure behind us, the indifferent
wind was already beginning to stir the sand.
“Let’s walk back along the beach, Aldo, all right?” he said, sliding his
arm into mine with a familiar gesture. “My legs don’t feel so strong, you
know?” He gave me a wink that did not take me in: “It must be from riding
that damn horse so long; a sailor on horseback is always bad news.”
We walked a minute in silence. This solitude seemed to absorb all sound,
as the sands absorbed the rain; already the cortege had melted away into the
skimpy grass. Soon the desolate arc of the beach stretched before us, almost
level with the waves. Flocks of sea birds landed here and flew up,
undulating over the damp ramparts of sand like a light mist; the benumbed
earth had never stirred here, except for this faint palpitation. Marino knew
how much I enjoyed roaming these washed-out, deserted beaches, but this
afternoon their very impoverishment did not distract me. I was attentive to
one thing only: the weight of an arm which now pressed more heavily on
my own. My mouth felt dry and my throat painfully choked. Marino was
suffering—with that stupefying suffering of dumb beasts that seems to have
reached us from another world. An impression of anguish came to me from
that arm, sometimes relaxed and then suddenly stiffened by an
awkwardness, which lay against my own with an oppressive animality.
“How was your little tour, Aldo—everything go all right?” he asked, his
voice almost timid.
“A little longer than expected, I’m afraid ... I have something to tell you
that you’re not going to like,” I added in a hard tone of voice. “The run
wasn’t kept to—we went as far as the opposite shore.”
Marino turned toward me suddenly. At that moment I realized that he
knew, and yet in spite of himself his eyes fastened on mine, with a kind of
dry relief. “Over there, yes, I know,” he said with an effort, his voice
labored. “They fired at you.”
“Do you want an accounting?” I asked, nervously compressing my lips,
and I could feel the nape of my neck prickling, as if someone had asked me
to stand at attention. I realized with despair how badly the conversation had

begun, and how much it was my fault that it had. Marino felt this too, and
dismissed formalities with a shrug of his shoulder against mine.
“Some people might find it useful. What does it matter?” he went on with
a strange expression that erased me from his gaze. “I’ve always known
you’d go over there ... It’s really too bad,” he resumed after a pause, his
voice absolutely expressionless. I was struck once again by his suddenly
senile behavior: as if the mouth, in this straightforward old man, no longer
answered for the words spoken.
“Why did you let me take that patrol?”
Marino seemed to ponder for a moment, with great effort. “I asked you to
go,” he said in a voice that was almost apologetic. “Don’t you remember?”
“If you knew I’d go over there, then you knew before I did. When we
began hearing those rumors, you’re the one who advised ... or permitted me
—yes; that’s what I thought, I’m sure of it—to write to Orsenna.”
Once again Marino seemed to search his memories with tremendous
effort. “Yes, perhaps,” he said at last, his voice thoughtful. “I’m very much
to blame in all this. I was hoping ...” He made a dismissive gesture with his
hand, a gesture of childish discouragement. “... I was thinking they’d calm
you down in the right way. I was hoping for help. I didn’t think the trouble
had gone so far.”
“What do you mean?” I asked abruptly, and almost stopped where I
stood, struck by the tone of muffled grief in his last words.
“They’re getting rid of me,” he said, turning his head away. “By the day
after tomorrow, I’ll have left the Admiralty for the last time.”
At first the words reverberated in my head, meaningless as pebbles
shaken in an empty box. Then a hollow formed in my stomach, and I felt
myself invaded by that sickening sensation common in dreams, when you
feel the railing over an abyss give way under your fingers, inch by inch.
“They can’t do that,” I said, and felt my face go white.
“Let’s sit here a minute, all right? The wind’s dying down,” the captain
said. He seemed to be cheering up a little.

The afternoon was drawing in, but it was still fine out here on the warm
sand. Once we sat down, the landscape around us vanished as if we had
ducked into a trench. Above us, flocks of sea birds, flying up with the tide,
kept passing over with a single deafening shriek. On that causeway
hemmed in by the waves, it was really impossible to be more isolated, and
for the first time I realized how alien this eccentric promenade was to
Marino’s habits. More and more it seemed to me that there was an
unaccustomed clumsiness in his too carefully monitored gestures—
something imperceptibly borrowed. As if the captain were playing a part.
The visor of his service cap pulled down over his forehead, he was staring
out to sea; mechanically, one hand poured a handful of sand through the
fingers of the other.
“You knew what the navigation rules in Syrtes were, didn’t you?” he
finally asked, coughing to clear his throat. “This is just a formality I have to
clear up,” he added hurriedly, “but things need to be settled: I have to send
in a report myself.”
“I’m ready to clear you in writing from any responsibility,” I said as
deferentially as I could. “What I did was done quite knowingly—quite
intentionally.”
Marino turned his face toward me as if he had been moved by clockwork.
“Intentionally?” he repeated pensively, and I noticed he was having trouble
breathing. “... You don’t know what you’re saying,” he added, and he shook
his head with a bitter expression.
“You told Fabrizio that once, and you believed it,” I said gently, for the
pained sadness in his voice just then filled me with pity. “Fabrizio was a
child. But to me—you don’t believe what you’re saying.”
The old man raised his transparent eyes to meet mine. “I’m very fond of
you, Aldo,” he said with a kind of embarrassment, “don’t you realize that I
care so much about you because I know you better than you think? At your
age, you don’t like finding excuses for yourself, because you’re never sure
of being as compromised as you’d like by what you do. Try to speak
without pride, in a moment when you’re in great danger of being judged.”

“Who’ll be the judge?” I said with an unconvincing shrug, for Marino’s
tone had suddenly become remarkably firm. “I must account for my actions
to other people,” I added, turning my head away. “I hate having to do so for
the first time just when we’re leaving each other.”
Marino went pale, and his gaze met mine with a gleam of arrogant
severity. “I’m not talking about the Signory. The Signory has its business,
and you know what it is, I suppose, better than I do ... Whatever I have to
tell you about that will keep. I’m talking about Orsenna.”
“About—or for?”
The old man seemed to meditate so intensely for a moment that his hand
trailed at his side like an abandoned oar, mechanically tracing a little furrow
in the sand. “Blood isn’t everything, Aldo,” he said in a slow, serious voice.
“Yours is hot, and we all know the quality of your birth. I’ve grown old
here,” he went on, a remote, almost misty expression in his eyes. “This is
my territory: I can move around in it with my eyes closed and name each
lump of earth. That’s why I have something to tell you today. This earth
isn’t just another card in a gambler’s hand.”
“I wasn’t alone on the Redoubtable,” I said after a moment’s silence. “At
the point we had reached here, you know as well as I that something would
have happened in any case ...You’re blaming me for an inevitability,” I
added with a touch of grandiloquence, and instantly felt that I was blushing
despite myself.
“There are inevitabilities which deserve hanging,” the old man broke in
with singular intensity, “when there’s still time for that. I don’t mean you,
Aldo,” he added in an awkward voice, “you know that.” He calmed me with
an extenuating gesture. “You couldn’t live here?” he asked, staring at me
with an expression of curiosity both intense and timid. It was as if clumsily,
desperately, he had determined to knock at a closed door, trying to adjust
his myopic eye to a chink that looked through to the other side.
“No,” I said, “I couldn’t. Maremma couldn’t either, and neither could old
Carlo.”

I saw the old man’s eyes darken. “Old Carlo ... Yes,” he said, suddenly
pensive; “it was ever since then that I’ve been scared. Something broke that
day—like a landslide. But why?” He looked at me blankly, with the docile,
lost expression of a faithful dog watching a gesture of its master’s that it
doesn’t understand.
“Hard to say ...” I looked away and also began starting out to sea, more
embarrassed than I could tell him by this confidence and this humility. “...
Is it possible you lived here years and years knowing there was ... that over
there—as if it was nothing in particular?”
“I have no taste for things that are distant—things that are dubious,”
Marino said more firmly. “The thread was broken: so much the better, if it
was. That was all long before my time, and it could last till long after.
That’s how it was. There was Orsenna, and then the Admiralty, and then the
sea ...” the old man said as though to himself, squinting in the salt wind.
“And after that ... nothing?”
“And after that nothing,” he said, turning toward me and looking straight
into my eyes. “Why go on worrying about what no longer asks anything of
you?”
“The Admiralty, and then the sea, and then nothing ...” I repeated and
glanced at him in perplexity. “Yesterday, and then today, and then tonight ...
and then nothing?”
“You find that absurd because you’re young,” Marino replied with a
strange intensity in his voice. “I’m old now, and the city’s very old too.
There comes a time when happiness—when tranquility—means having
used up many things around you, worn them out by having rubbed up
against them so long, too long—by thinking about them too much. That’s
what they call the egoism of the old,” he added with a kind of anxious
smile: “they’ve only become thicker because so many things around them
have become thinner. They don’t wear out,” the captain nodded stubbornly:
“it’s things around them that they wear out.”
“Orsenna couldn’t live forever with its head in the sand,” I snapped.
“You’re the only one who’s been able to live here without choking to

death,” I went on in a kind of rage. “Even Fabrizio left whenever he could.
He didn’t know why, but he left. Even old Carlo would have gone, you
know. It wasn’t ... possible any more.”
“Yes it was, Aldo,” the voice answered in a tone of wise tranquility, “it
was possible. You can’t understand because you’re not from around here.
But for those of us who’ve taken the blood in our veins from Orsenna—to
what is elsewhere, to what will come later—it’s a great feat just to be. Here.
Now. Orsenna’s where things have ended,” Marino continued, nodding with
a gesture of insistent and heavy certainty. “Orsenna had stopped having
anything to think about. It endured, eyes open.”
“Hardly,” I answered bitterly. “You’re giving it too much credit. The
dead, too, if you don’t touch them, keep their eyes open. Orsenna was
sleeping with its eyes open.”
“But forever,” the old man said, in a tone of invocation or prayer, his
gaze sliding out to sea. “You don’t know what a deliverance that is: a state
beyond which there’s ... nothing.”
His hand made a gesture toward the beach. The sea was rising, already
sending flat ripples of foam close to us as it rustled over the sand.
“A land where it’s good to lie down and go to sleep,” he added, lost in
that heavy and almost organic reverie which seemed to signify the extreme
limit of his attention. And then he said, in a kind of daze: “... When they lay
me in it, I think I’ll pull it over my face with both hands, and it won’t weigh
on me at all, light as it is with all the weight I’ve taken out of it.”
I nodded toward the cemetery. On the low horizon, it was no more than a
black line above the sands. “There’s Orsenna!” I said, taking his arm.
“Wherever it’s sown its graveyard dirt. Is that what you’re protecting?”
“It’s endured,” the old man repeated, a religious tremor in his voice. He
turned his eyes to me—the murky eyes of a blind man. “... Here, when a
body falls into the grave, there are a hundred million bones that shudder all
the way down to the bottom, the way a mother feels her dead child weigh
on top of her in the earth. There’s no other eternal life.”

“Yes there is,” I said, going pale, “there’s another life. But there’s a curse
on the last born of a city that’s too old.”
“It isn’t old,” the old man interrupted in a dry voice. “It’s ageless—like
me.”
He murmured between his teeth, as though to himself, the city’s motto.
And for a second I was dazed, my eyes blinked; for a second it seemed that
what he was saying was true, and that the heavy silhouette, in all its
formidable immobility, was going numb—petrifying before my eyes.
“I don’t think we have much more to say to each other,” I observed,
standing up and nervously dusting myself off.
We resumed walking in silence. The sun was already very low; over the
flat land, the red horizon was blurred with fog; this heralded a spell of those
