dry, bright days, clear as a pane of glass and filled with the hot breath of the
desert. Along the narrow strip of dry sand the rising tide left at the foot of
the dunes, we walked on without saying a word, hurrying now to be done
with it.
“Let’s go to the fortress,” Marino said suddenly. “I have some things to
show you; you’ll be in command here after I leave, until my successor
arrives. They’re sending us reinforcements,” he added in a perfectly neutral
tone of voice. “Two gunboats—they’ll be here in eight days, I’m told, and
the artillery will be put in working order. Which will require some work
here—provisions to get in, and temporary lodgings for the men ...”
“Reinforcements ...” I said, glancing at him with disbelief. “Do they
anticipate ... ?”
“I don’t know,” he interrupted more gravely. “No one’s told me anything.
Something’s happened at Orsenna ... It was as if I were talking to people I
didn’t know—strangers ...”
“What do you mean?” But I stopped: the distress in that pathetic voice
warned me that Marino was asking me for help.
“Something’s changed at Orsenna,” the old man went on. He shook his
shoulders slowly with a wretched, chilly gesture.

“At the top?”
“No, not at the top, Aldo, that I know of ...” He lowered his head and let
his chin rest on his chest. “... It’s the heart: the heart’s giving out, the way it
does before a storm, when a nasty wind comes up. You don’t know the
desert just before a sandstorm ... Your eyes sting, your blood blinds you,
you can’t see clearly any more. Your nerves knot up, your throat goes dry,
you stare at the horizon and long for the storm to break ...” Mechanically
the old man squinted into the wind, as though to examine the foggy horizon.
“... It’s a bad time,” he went on. “Our crews, when we used to hire them
from the Syrtes farms, would call it sand lather.” Marino sighed and was
silent for a moment. “... But you—maybe you’ll know,” he said at last with
a touch of deference, even timidity in his voice. “They’ll listen to you at the
Signory. I’ve brought the mail—there’s a summons for you.”
“Concerning ... ?”
“Concerning what you know perfectly well. The Council of
Superintendance wants to have you sent back.” The name fell from his lips
with the nuance almost ritually attached, at Orsenna, to the evocation of a
dreaded and unknown power.
“Then it’s serious?” I said in a troubled tone, my eyes questioning him.
“Yes,” Marino said, pausing and slowly raising his eyes to meet mine as
if he were recognizing my features one by one in the light of a lantern.
“And considering who you are, I’m surprised they’re sending for you.
Usually the Council deliberates only on documents. Everything will be
decided on that day.”
In Marino’s eyes I saw a darker gleam—a world of troubled feelings:
panic before the unknown power, and at the same time a kind of anguished
veneration in the presence of someone about to see it face to face; as if,
through me, he had almost touched, with blind adoration, the City’s
supreme power, its blackest heart.
“... Don’t you have something more to tell them?” he asked, in a voice
that faltered in spite of himself. “Not everything’s been said ... I beg you ...”
he ended, lowering his eyes.

“What’s there to say?” I shrugged my shoulders involuntarily. “... There’s
a time to interfere with things and a time to let things alone. What’s come
has made use of me, and now it’s leaving me: everything will work out
now, without me.”
We started walking again. The captain had recovered his silence, as if he
henceforth regarded everything as said.
At this late hour of the day, in winter, it was already dark in the fortress
corridors. Marino, still without a word, lit the lantern hanging in the guard
room, and at the glow which barely penetrated the yellowish mist, I seemed
to read in his face and in the feverish gesture of his hand striking a light the
signs of an unwonted nervousness. As always in winter, despite Fabrizio’s
repairs, the walls were streaming with a cold sweat, and once or twice I
distinctly saw Marino’s shoulders shiver under his heavy greatcoat.
“Let’s come back tomorrow,” I said. “There’s no hurry. It’s freezing
tonight.”
“No,” the captain muttered between his teeth without even turning his
head. “This will be over soon enough.”
The glow from the lantern scarcely penetrated the milky darkness, but all
at once the outline of the high vaults rose over us through the dark, in the
hollow vibration of voices echoing like broken glass. “Not that this is a very
hospitable sort of place tonight ...” he added in a voice which rang out
complacently, as if he were showing these great halls to some tourist. He
seemed restored to an expansive and almost alarming good humor. “... But
it’s my last round. And then,” he added, glancing at me obliquely as he
swung the lantern, “it seems to me you used to like it.” He suddenly halted,
and his lantern, raised now, faintly illuminated a cartouche carved in the
wall.
“... ‘In sanguine vivo’ ...” he made out, as if he were deciphering the
syllables one by one. The rest was lost in a vague and prolonged murmur.
This time there was something so distinctly abnormal in his behavior that I
felt almost annoyed.
“Well?” I asked, staring at him with a barely polite impatience.

“The meaning’s not clear, Aldo,” he said, touching my arm, his voice
guttural: “Haven’t you ever noticed? The meaning could be either that the
City survives in its people, or else that it requires the sacrifice of blood ...”
“This is hardly the time for an exegesis, is it?” I broke in, increasingly
annoyed. With every minute I felt less comfortable. There was something
fixed and lugubrious in Marino’s eyes—was it a reflection of this ghostly
lighting?—which belied his nonsensical conversation. The lantern, set
down on the floor between us, scarcely drew our faces out of the aureole of
mist; our long shadows arched up high into the vaults—cold drops ran from
their stones, one by one, between my coat collar and my neck.
“As you like,” the old man said, suddenly compliant. He picked up the
lantern again and went on walking, his long strides irregular—on humid
days, the captain favored an old wound; once again our shadows swayed
above us. Marino opened the doors one by one without a word, thrusting
keys into rusty locks with a great clatter of cold metal: a dense smell of
mildew and rusty iron met our noses, like a gust from those sealed
fortifications opened after centuries; a cold unleavened stench that turned
the stomach, flavored by centuries of poisonous rot. I followed Marino from
battery to battery without a word, our heavy boots pressing a pestilential
spongy litter. The lantern’s wick sputtered and smoked in the nauseating air,
and vague shadows swarmed over the filthy vaults. There was a kind of
funereal omen, as if a giant had been disturbed in his heavy sleep and
suddenly gave off this intimate coffin-stench, so sharp in our nostrils we
could scarcely breathe.
“The smell of Orsenna,” I murmured, my voice hostile.
Marino swung his lantern without a word, and suddenly there appeared
on his lips—or reappeared—a strange smile, the one he had shown in the
map room. “We still have to inspect the platform battery,” he said in a
sleepy voice. “Those are the pieces they want to replace.”
It was very difficult, in that labyrinth of ramps and stairs, ever to know
just which floor you were on, once you had made your way into the interior
of the place; but suddenly, to my astonishment, our coats flapped in the sea

wind: what I took on my left for doorways, deceived by their shadowy
depressions, was a row of abandoned gunports. Marino set his lantern down
on a lump of darkness which barred our way; the wind suddenly shook the
flame, and a ray of light slid down a metal barrel: even before recognizing
the cannon and the platform, I realized where, after so many twists and
turns, the captain had brought me.
“Tonight’s calm, but the wind will come up tomorrow,” Marino said
mechanically, in his most incontrovertible tone, leaning out and sniffing the
air even so; but the place and the moment paralyzed my impulse to smile.
The night had fallen completely now, and it was very dark, but beneath
us, through the bluish mist, rose a pungent, moist breath and a faint murmur
of water, like the rustling of poplar leaves. Leaning out of the port, I could
see to our right the motionless light on the jetty: occasionally a gleam,
reflected off the coal heap, pierced the vacant night. It was as if the
darkness would never end: everything rested in the black intimacy of a bell
of shadows; the sleeping lights navigated the mist with a star’s calm and
fixity. Nothing had happened: the Admiralty was regaining the
inexpressible tranquility of things at anchor.
“Remember the night when I came to find you in the map room,” Marino
asked in a low but distinct voice.
“As I do the day you brought me here ...” I turned toward Marino. In the
half-darkness it was hard to make him out. “There’s something I’ve always
wondered: what was it that struck you so that night?”
“Your look—the way you looked at things,” Marino said in a very
explicit tone. “It wakened too many things. I didn’t like that look of yours
.... All the same I was very fond of you, Aldo,” he suddenly said, with an
unaccustomed seriousness, as if he were offering testimony.
I glanced away, strangely moved, and stared out to sea. “You’re right,” I
said, “there wasn’t room for both of us here.”
“No,” he said in an awkward voice. “There wasn’t room.”
There were a few seconds of silence. Suddenly I felt a kind of prickling
in the nape of my neck which spread to my shoulders, as if the barrel of a

weapon had been trained there, at the same time that a brutal and imminent
sensation of danger cramped my chest. Instinctively I threw myself on the
floor, grabbing the low parapet at the very edge of the abyss. At the same
moment something stumbled against my leg with a heavy breath, then
swayed above me, scraping along the rim. Huddled against the stone, my
head pulled between my shoulders, my heart stopped beating for a moment
of supernatural silence, then, with a flabby noise, a body heavily slapped
the calm waters.
I remained motionless for a few moments. Leaning over the gulf, the
absolute silence that had closed over itself like a trapdoor and the torpor
which benumbed my brain made me raise my hand toward my head with a
mechanical gesture, as if a hood had been dropped over it. Then I slowly
stood up again, and with a gesture of absurd incredulity, I gently raised the
lantern above my head. The yellow light slid over the slimy stones, and
brutally silhouetted the empty gunport against the night, its emptiness so
intriguing that I groped with my free hand, in a blind gesture, along the
stone rim, as though in front of a frame behind which the wall had revealed
a secret panel. There was no one there.
The searching went on late into the night. The pinnaces from the wharf
were unmoored, and all the available vessels at the Admiralty, even the
lifeboats of the Redoubtable which the emergency crew, alerted by calls
from shore, had lowered into the sea of their own accord. Standing bolt
upright in the prow of a boat, with his torch flashing in the heavy air, a man
occasionally rose up out of the mist like a ghost gliding over the calm oily
waters; for a long time the guttural calls, in which anguish gradually gave
way to a still-incredulous resignation, passed each other in the calm night.
The body did not reappear; according to Giovanni and, as the search turned
out to be increasingly futile, to almost all the others, the captain’s heavy
clothes and boots must have dragged him under immediately after he had
lost consciousness—down to the muddy bottom of the lagoon, from which
no body, in living memory, had ever risen again—and no one seemed to
doubt the explanation of the accident I had immediately given: in other

words, that the captain, trying to step around the cannon’s chassis, had
slipped on the wet stones. A deeper meaning was attached, for me, to this
disappearance without a trace; to me it seemed that the captain, who had
never quite lived at the Admiralty but more properly had haunted it in the
fashion of a torpid genie of earth, had passed into the heart of this black
night and of this sleeping lagoon in a manner too suspicious not to have
become one of those signs to which life at the Admiralty had given me a
clue—as if the very spirit of these heavy waters and these rotting stones, a
spirit in which time itself had seemed to benumb its rhythm, had regained at
the appointed hour and at the chosen place the refuge of the black depths in
order to seal over itself their sanction and their sleep.
OceanofPDF.com

12
The Secret Towers of the City
I reached Orsenna at the end of a sullen afternoon. Jolting over the sodden
roads had put me in a wretched humor; the solitude and dilapidation of the
waste stretches the car had passed through, by daylight this time, for hours
on end, filled me with grim forebodings: at the very moment when Orsenna
may have been approaching its final hour, in the rainy grisaille of its
deserted roads, in the aspect of the tumbledown sheepfolds huddled in
hollows of the terrain, I seemed to read how impoverished and weak the
City was—as if I could feel passing over it, with the wind which swept
these shivering plains, Destruction’s very wing. Even my gaze seemed to
have changed: what I now saw in these miserable landscapes was no longer
the city’s ubiquitous talons sunk deep in the soft earth, and everywhere so
patient an effacement of the accidental and the illusory that the earth’s worn
surface seemed to reveal a semblance of eternity. Today this same earth
timidly shrank beneath a sky laden with dull clouds, as if suddenly it had
taken all the room there was, and the prostrate life of these solitudes, buried
in too much memory, finally turned its blind gaze up toward those touseled
shapes, those omens which coursed over it with the wind. Sometimes little
groups would be standing near the shabby post offices where we would stop
to take on mail; sunk in the inert rumination of plains shepherds, perhaps
they had spent the night there, swathed in the heavy blankets that served
them as cloaks, motionless as statues under the streaming rain. They would
not speak, would not even look up—a tiny runnel of water overflowed their
hatbrims and trickled down their noses as over the marble lip of a fountain;
only when the car slowly moved on would they turn their blank eyes in our
direction. From the mud that covered them, one assumed some of them had
covered great distances to reach the road, and their mute vigil, while the

men were busy around the car, made me uncomfortable; it seemed as if the
whole country was mysteriously on the lookout behind those fixed pupils.
“What are they doing here?” I asked one of the postmasters—a man
almost as muddy as the shepherds—while the mail sacks were being
loaded.
He shrugged with an exasperated expression: “Oh, rumors! rumors! ...
It’s all nonsense!” he added, raising his voice, his hands on his hips, staring
angrily at the group. “Sometimes it’s hard to tell what’s going on in those
pitiful minds,” he whispered in my ear, his tone suddenly confidential:
“they live in such isolation out there ... The way you see them now, these
fellows are waiting for the end of the world, if you please, or might as well
be ... Oh, work doesn’t hang heavy on their hands, you can say that for
them. They’ve all seen signs in the moon ... Isn’t that right, Fausto?”
He slapped one of the shepherds on the shoulder and winked at me. The
shepherd shook his head gravely.
“Yes, signs ...” he said, his voice like a rusty key turning in the lock.
“Evil omens ... Death—” he went on, shaking his head, with a childish
singsong voice that was louder now—“death in the fire that will come over
the water. Orsenna is doomed in seven times seven days.”
“All right, now clear out of here, you worthless beggars!” the postmaster
shouted, beside himself. He began throwing stones at them. Dragging their
feet, as if it were merely raining a little harder now, the group moved a few
yards off, and once again froze in its tableau of stupefied expectation.
“We can’t drive them away from the road!” The official wiped his
forehead, his face crimson. “Old crows!” he screamed at them, furious.
“‘Death in the fire that will come over the water.’ They make my flesh
creep, with all that,” he continued, suddenly ill at ease. “I know nothing
happens here, no one ever comes ... But I myself—there are moments when
I begin staring at the bend in the road, in spite of myself.”
The car started up. Behind me, I saw the postmaster throwing more
stones at the shepherds, without conviction—out of habit, it seemed. The

blankets scarcely moved, and I realized that the scene was not a new one.
This man, too, had found his drug.
I reached Orsenna late in the evening. The avenues under their vaults of
trees seemed abandoned and chilly; I remember thinking that the city was
shutting itself up earlier than usual. In the lower town, the mist from the
swamps was already filling the streets: the familiar rotting smell passed
over my face like the touch of a blind hand and gave me a pang—I had
returned. No sooner had the car stopped in front of the house than my father
and Orlando appeared behind the grille, while several shutters gaped in the
houses nearby. From those piercing looks and the feverishness of my
father’s hands as they fumbled with the lock, I realized how apprehensively
I had been awaited: in living memory my father had never come in person
to open his door to a visitor.
“At last you’re here!” he said, seizing my hands with an emotion he
could not master as he led me toward the house with great strides.
Instinctively, Orlando fell back behind us, intimidated, as if making way for
the principal actor; his gaze felt heavy on the nape of my neck, full of
embarrassment, respect, and gravity.
Approaching Orsenna, I had grown increasingly nervous about this
meeting with my father; knowing his hot temper and his attachment to the
city’s policy of official inertia, I had feared that the old man, who could no
longer be unaware of my lapses, would burst into furious reproaches;
already I was gritting my teeth against the rather theatrical pathos his
remonstrances always betrayed; in his relations with me (and nothing had
done more to dispel any sort of familiarity on my side) my father had
always preferred to play to the gallery, and I was only too aware of the
temptation afforded by the role of a father welcoming the prodigal son. I
waited, nerves on edge, for a storm that never broke. After I had been
served a summary meal, the three of us sat down near the fire; there then
occurred a rather heavy silence—my father lit a cigar, an unwonted sign of
a scarcely contained excitement, and I noticed that the almost disconcerting
vivacity of his blue eyes made him look much younger. He seemed to be

forcing himself to avoid any sudden gestures, and I realized that I had been
mistaken about his impatience; he was glad to see me: in his occasional
possessive glances, there was an indulgent satisfaction, as if a precious
piece from his collections had just been returned to its cabinet.
“It appears there’s a great deal of talk about you these days, Aldo,” he
said at last, his eyes narrowing as if he were repressing an almost childlike
pleasure with enormous effort. “You’ve set many tongues wagging here—
the ones that like to wag, wouldn’t you say, Orlando?” he inquired, taking
his cigar out of his mouth. His eyes were dancing. Orlando solemnly
acquiesced. Coming from my father—who did his thinking, one might say,
in public, and whose fine bass voice seemed to have been created only to
give the conventional note its organ tones—such a welcome offered food
for thought. I mused on what Vanessa had said about the wind that was
blowing over the city.
“What do people here really think about this business?” I asked in a tone
less uncertain than might have been expected, and then, determined to play
the game, I produced a sigh intended to illustrate my sleepless nights. My
father loved nothing so much as to explain to people the situations they
would by nature understand much better than he. “... At the Admiralty, no
one can make it out.”
The old man coughed to clear his throat and assumed his augural
attitude, which is to say that his modestly downcast gaze now fastened itself
on the ceiling with an expression of deep thought and diplomatic finesse.
“The Admiralty is an executive organ,” he began with a touch of indulgence
which served to put matters in their place, “whose opinion no one would
dream of asking. Moreover, since my extremely modest functions have
terminated, officially speaking, I am no longer privy to the Council’s
secrets.” (The tone and the familiar reference implied—quite mistakenly—
that nothing of the kind was the case.) “I can only give you the free and
independent reaction—absolutely free, if you understand what I mean,
which commits no one—” (and in his voice appeared the energetic tremolo

of a Cincinnatus returned to his plow) “of a considerably experienced mind
which has weathered many a storm.”
Not yet certain of his new audience, he turned toward Orlando, from
whose resigned expression I realized that for the last week he must have
had to play, however reluctantly, the part of audience to this well-rehearsed
eloquence.
“... Your friend Orlando, who will one day be a guiding light of our
Signory but who is meanwhile still content to draw upon an old man’s
experience, knows what I think about this matter: there is a middle way to
take between the reefs among which I cannot watch the Signory navigating
without anxiety. Yes, Aldo,” he admitted in an impulse of concern, “today is
not the first time I have permitted myself to deplore that traditions—quite
respectable ones, of course—have a little too frequently authorized the
Signory to mistake inertia for prudence. A new day is dawning for
Orsenna,” he went on in a firm tone which read the future like an open
book, “and the Signory owes it to itself to confront every occasion without
unnecessary excitement, but with the necessary initiative—nuanced with a
certain reserve, I fully concur ... Our blood must be young, albeit
experienced. Let us not deceive ourselves: the situation is serious, though
hardly critical. And I fear that a staff trained in the routines of another age
may no longer be equal to the task which the present moment quite
incontrovertibly imposes: the task of re-con-sid-er-ing the situation in the
light of new developments. Moreover, as I have told your friend Orlando
more than once, it was childish to bury our heads in the sand and suppose
that such developments would allow themselves to be postponed
indefinitely. We have not been timely in understanding,” he continued with
a sarcastic glance, “the lesson of iam proximus ardet Ucalegon. For my
part, I had always supposed it would come to this. What was required was a
decision: now it is upon us, and what do we see?” He paused for effect. “...
A stone has fallen in our garden, and now all our frogs are croaking as
though in a swamp. Where is the ‘know in order to foresee, and foresee in

order to provide’ which is the rule of all good diplomacy? Has our inertia
not bordered upon frivolity?”
Anticipating that this flood might continue for some time longer, I
offered my exhaustion as an excuse and stood up without excessive
politeness. Orlando hurriedly imitated me. The old man, after a moment’s
hesitation, tugged my arm with a timid gesture. Orlando realized that his
presence was inopportune and preceded me down the corridor.
“... A summons has come for you here. The Council has set your hearing
for the day after tomorrow,” my father murmured rapidly. He coughed, and
his embarrassed eyes avoided mine. Again his voice became hasty and
stammering: “... I wanted to tell you, Aldo, since you will doubtless have
occasion to see my old friend Danielo tomorrow ... a friend of thirty years
... But we haven’t seen much of each other lately ... I wanted to tell you that
I gladly authorize you ... ummm ... observing the necessary discretion, of
course—whatever strikes you as suitable—to inform him of our
conversation this evening. And to inform him, I mean ... to remind him that
every man of feeling closes ranks with the Signory ... What I mean is ... that
I am at the City’s service in these grave circumstances, though they are
scarcely critical, are they? Of great concern, though scarcely critical,
remember that. The situation requires courage, a cool head, equilibrium ...
and experience. And boldness!” he added after a pause.
I rejoined Orlando down the corridor.
“He’s beginning to fail quite noticeably,” he whispered to me in a neutral
tone, “but you can see that the weathervane still turns with the wind.”
“Is this what we’ve come to ... ?” I asked, taking his arm with an habitual
gesture which gave me some comfort, for I found this sudden and senile
collapse dreadfully embarrassing.
“Yes,” Orlando said. “‘A new day is dawning for Orsenna.’ By which
your father means a second career for himself, but I suspect that there’s
something wrong in all this ...”
“You mean something’s actually going to be done?” I felt my heart
beginning to beat faster. Orlando stopped and gave me a thoughtful stare.

Darkness had fallen now, an indolent breeze rustled the trees in the garden
we were walking through, and their branches rained heavy drops upon us.
Orlando’s courteous and friendly voice retained a cool accent, and I felt he
was hesitant to speak.
“... Whether or not your behavior in this ... episode was prompted by
higher authorities—which I have no means of knowing,” he began in a
careful tone, “the skirmish itself is a trifle which, considered objectively,
cannot take us far. Moreover I have no specific notion of the Signory’s
intentions, though God knows no one here has been reticent about
announcing what they ought to be. But the atmosphere is sinister ... The odd
thing is—and it’s quite disturbing,” he continued, lowering his eyes and
playing with his watch-chain, “that so few people here, on hearing the
news, can consider the episode objectively.”
“Orsenna is very bored, I know,” I said, shrugging without conviction.
“Yes, strange to say, the news has been regarded as good news,” Orlando
observed thoughtfully. “You know,” he went on, forcing himself to smile,
“down there in your Admiralty you’ve become quite a fashionable figure ...
Your father had every reason to ask you for a recommendation to the
Signory.”
“It seems to me,” I said as ironically as I could manage, “that in the old
days we didn’t pay so much attention to what was said in the streets. I think
I remember the old theories. Something about airtight compartments ... The
subtlest consciousness removing to the heights—wasn’t that it?”
“It’s just what’s said in the streets that worries me,” Orlando replied in a
preoccupied tone. “Usually, what comes through are rumors about what’s
going on at the Signory, and I’m in a better position than most to set them
straight. Granted, our State secrets had assumed a rather anodyne character
—you remember how we used to laugh at them at the Academy ... Well,
that’s all changed now. For some time there’s been a kind of isolation, a
withdrawal ... You must have noticed how deeply hurt your father is at not
being able to approach old Danielo any more ...”
“I’ll be seeing him the day after tomorrow.”

Orlando met my eyes. “God knows I don’t think we should give this
business any more importance than it deserves ... Yet I feel like doing just
that. And more than one man around here would envy you, too.”
“You’ve regressed to idol worship, Orlando?”
“Not exactly,” he answered, frowning. “Jokes are all very well, but they
don’t mean the same thing any more. There are days when a man jokes in
the consciousness of his power, and days when a man makes jokes the way
he whistles in the dark. I used the word importance: maybe we’re learning
all over again what power really is ...” Orlando broke off and rested one
hand on my shoulder. I realized we were about to part company. “Look
around you, now that you’re going to be in the city a few days. Nothing’s
changed, and yet it’s as if the lighting weren’t the same ... There’s a new
glow on certain peaks, the way there is on the tips of lightning rods when a
storm is on its way: as if the whole earth were concentrating all its energies
to release the lightning. Men and things are still the same, yet everything
has changed. Look carefully.”
I spent almost the whole of the next two days in the city. News of my
return had spread quickly; my friends were eager to see me, I was even—to
my surprise—included in circles traditionally closed to my family, but it
seemed that in Orsenna certain social taboos were losing their rigor.
Everyone’s curiosity was focused on my remote expedition; I said little,
taking refuge behind the report I would first have to make to the Signory.
Usually, when I appeared in the salons, a sudden silence occurred, and from
the look of excitement I could detect on people’s faces it seemed to me that
this ominous impulse was welcomed as a fresh wind, and that I left my
hosts inexplicably reassured by my visit; sometimes, when I did speak, I
surprised expressions on their faces I had never seen before: their eyes
appeared strained by the effort of an unfamiliar accommodation trained on a
point so remote from their normal field of vision that, as in cases of extreme
fatigue, they assumed a disarmed and unwonted expression of absence.
Especially the women abandoned themselves to such lapses without
reserve; noticing the sparkle in their eyes magnetized by my tale, and the

resentment I could read in the men’s, I realized that women possess a
greater stock of feeling and effervescence to which the usual course of life
gives no outlet and which is released only by the profound revolutions
which change our hearts, the ones which in order to occur require long
immersion in the blind warmth of a confinement: thus the aura which halos
great historical parturitions can be discerned first of all in women’s
prophetic eyes. I realized now why Vanessa had been given to me as a
guide, and why, once I was in her sway, the daylight part of my mind had
been so worthless: she belonged to the sex which presses so hard at the
gates of anguish, to the mysteriously docile sex that already consents to
whatever looms beyond catastrophe and darkness.
I was astonished to realize, in the course of overheard conversations, how
little critical reflection was being attached to what was known—quite
inaccurately and quite imperfectly (the version Vanessa’s people circulated
had done its work)—as the Syrtes Incident, and how seldom, to my relief
even more than to my surprise, people were concerned to assign
responsibilities with any sense of impartiality. Meticulous sifting of past
priorities and merits had hitherto constituted the common basis of all our
political meditations: each man, daunted by the almost materially perceived
weight of centuries devoted to the accumulation of an incomparable mass of
wealth and experience, regarded and conducted himself more or less
instinctively as an heir. Familiarity—experienced more vividly than
elsewhere—and a virtual connivance with a long long line of ancestors,
blinding us to all spontaneous variation, stultified any reasoning which was
not engrossed by consideration of that immutable and fertile past whose
lengthening procession seemed in and of itself to grant each man his true
weight: all parties in Orsenna, without exception, were parties of historical
rights. Having been away so long, I was all the more struck by what seemed
insidiously different about the present perspective. The moment was
committed to an open credit rather than to the scrupulous settling of
accounts. New and sometimes disconcerting faces of bold speakers were to
be encountered in the city’s most exclusive circles, to which a worldly
passport no longer seemed required, and it was almost alarming to see what

credence they met with upon divulging and unhesitatingly discussing the—
crudely inaccurate—resolutions they attributed to the Signory, provided
they were of a nature to inflame the imagination.
A need for the unheard-of seemed to have seized on people’s minds, a
craving which produced, in this ancient and skeptical capital, something
like a sharper resonance in the emotive tide which had already submerged
Maremma; it was as if each man delighted, upon reaching the air of the
summit, to feel his imagination freer and more alert than he had supposed
possible, and the last thing one would have thought to query, apropos of the
fantastic rumors that ran through the town almost by the hour, was their
origin: the instantaneous speed of their transmission by hundreds of mouths
gave them, in and of itself, a kind of consistency no one dreamed of
questioning; as if they set, from moment to moment, like the ice of a pond
on which one can walk, and it was apparent that they testified to an
unexpected change of temperature. Orsenna’s intoxicated spirit demanded,
like the air one breathes, its habitual dose of daily change: absence of
change would have left it in a necessitous condition which ran no risk of
exacerbation, for there was no lack of those eager to furnish the drug. They
were especially to be found—which could hardly surprise me—in the
entourage of old Aldobrandi, whose worldly situation had now reached its
zenith. No one any longer recalled his exile and his troublesome history of
conspiracies: in this society so busily reconstituting itself, its moorings cut
as on a liner hoisting anchor, an expectation and a measureless credit were
extended only to those expected to enliven the crossing, and the old pirate’s
murky past suddenly gave him more prestige than any of the established
notabilities possessed, at the very moment when each man foresaw that he
must finally plunge into this dubious element. I had glimpsed him
momentarily in Orlando’s mother’s salon where I had paid a brief visit, and
his aspect had struck me as that of a man who bears not the stupid varnish
of success, but the feverish awareness that his hour—on the dial where it is
marked in advance—has suddenly come round.

He seemed extraordinarily rejuvenated; his hand jerked back and forth
over his short black beard; his glittering wolf’s eyes had, in discussion, a
fencer’s vivacity and economy of movement. He spoke in curt phrases that
he let fly at random, like a man accustomed to letting others pick up after
him; wherever he was, people kept hurrying in and out, and for these
messengers he would scribble a few words on a scrap of paper without
interrupting the train of his remarks. Surrounded by a kind of obsequious
little court, a prestigous silhouette was constituted as though by magic out
of each of his summoning gestures, as if his reach had suddenly been
extended, and one might have supposed that the city had closed ranks
around him, as if, beyond the walls, he had remained immediately and
directly in contact with each of its remotest capillaries. His gestures and his
remarks were singular in that they appeared to refer to an order of
consideration and scorn, of hopes and fears, quite alien to those Orsenna
once commonly acknowledged; his gaze and the inflexion of his voice
made everything new: thus, to the eye of a barbarian in the armies of the
Byzantine Empire, a new and utterly unsuspected landscape must have
filtered out of the aging and immutable earth: cities to be razed, fields
turned back to pasture, and the grounds on which his tribe would pitch
camp. A new social hierarchy came to life under Aldobrandi’s gaze; he was
simultaneously a mystagogue, a general in the field, and an outside-broker.
Here was the fauna which, household by household, now colonized the
haughtiest quarters in the city.
At Orsenna, the closer one happened to be to the apparent center of
power, the less one was concerned, as at Maremma, with Farghestan. The
point passionately argued was whether the Signory would undertake a
military expedition or if traditional policies would prevail and the incident
become a means of resuming contact and resolving an old dispute: in that
Middle Empire which had come to be the city’s image of itself behind the
isolation of its deserts, no one seemed capable of realizing that the
adversary might judge and determine autonomously, independently of the
intentions formulated by the city—which for so long had had none
whatever. Thus once they had escaped from the atmosphere of panic so

prevalent in Maremma, men’s minds seemed by contrast to function here in
an unreal and almost delirious security—the very familiarity of the inactive
and decrepit city maintained the universal authority of the sign over any
reality of the thing signified. The arguments I overheard around me seemed
to draw their apparently convincing force from a special kind of algebra
whose code I had lost. Behind words quite familiar to my ear, I kept
pursuing an unknown quantity whose notion was imposed upon me, in spite
of myself, by common consent, so enormous for example was the gap
between the “Syrtes fleet,” whose ready authority swelled these over-
assured mouths with utterly fictitious power, and the mud-silted shallops I
knew were rotting in our harbor—between the carelessly dropped epithet
“savages,” who were to be forced to see reason, and the disconcerting,
ironic, and excessively self-assured person who had paid me a midnight
visit ... In order to pass off, the fever of agitation which had seized upon the
city found no external point of leverage—atrophied imaginations conceived
no such thing—and what was childish in this salon excitement proceeded
from the fact that Orsenna seemed to be scaring itself, conceiving no other
means of exorcizing its boredom. The likelihood of an expedition or of a
war was vented all the more complacently in that it involved, for almost
everyone in the city, no more than an abstract and colorless representation,
even a vaguely fantastic one: the image of the city’s power, for so long
vigorously wielded, parting the milky fogs which had not ceased to thicken
on its frontiers, now found no eyes to visualize it and make it vivid; on the
contrary, repercussions of the affair on an internal level were everywhere
calculated and exaggerated in the most impassioned manner: the possibility
of a serious external crisis was actually conceived almost exclusively as the
promise of a change of personnel. Thus a failing centenarian forgets his
dispute with the very rhythm of the planet, suddenly concentrating an
absurd attention on the prospect of a new liver cure—and thus a crumbling
empire, already invaded and largely overrun, reacts (States always believe
they die on their feet) to its fundamental impotence by a petulant ministerial
crisis. In short, I rediscovered in Orsenna a nation which nothing had
disposed to think tragically. Confronted with a problem so remote from its

habitual angle of vision, one in which the unknown quantity prevailed over
the facts, Orsenna was reacting with the stubborn myopia of extreme
decrepitude: just as an old man increasingly succeeds in parenthesizing
such imminent and as considerable preoccupations as Death or Eternity, and
makes it a point of honor still to behave as a “natural person,” so the city,
not suspecting it had parenthesized itself, and long since, never suspected
what ill wind had risen from beyond the deserts, and why its own fingers
trembled as it picked up these all-too-familiar cards, always the same ones
which it had shuffled to the point of demoralization, in the stupid certainty
that anything which would ever concern it was conveniently represented in
them and could easily be read there. Just as continuous performance of a
game, by familiarizing the mind with the rules, unconsciously persuades it
that they will never again be questioned, merely because it has sacrificed
too much to them, and that they really exist, merely because they have been
able to mislead it, the way a tree or a stone exists, the factions and
combinations at Orsenna could change, but the idea of changing the rules
that presided over them was no longer conceivable: that would have
required some realization that it was merely rules which were in question.
Leaving the salons and their boastful arguments so tangled in worldly
illusion, I wandered through the streets, taking deep breaths of fresh air: I
realized that any consequential idea had lost all meaning, that everyday life
was stammering a language here which no lexicon could account for. In this
overbuilt city, outdoor life, perhaps as a reflection of the ancient military
disciplines which had ennobled it, had always preserved a character marked
by austerity and coldness: the generally sombre colors and simplicity of
people’s clothes, the haughty reserve of the women, the repugnance to
engage in sidewalk conversation or to mingle with any street crowd had
long since cast Orsenna, in the eyes of the exuberant Southern populations
astonished by such distant dignity, as the Signory’s “cold heart”: more than
in any other capital, here one perceived virtually at a glance the inveterate
proximity of a great power, of which each man—so much more a citizen
than an inhabitant—insisted upon respect for its fraction lodged in himself.
Yet now, to my surprise, the streets of Orsenna had grown animated. They

seemed to attract more people than usual; strangers would question each
other, and once a voice sounded a little louder than was customary, an
orientation seemed to occur in the indifferent chaos of comings and goings:
the dark figures clustered, attentive, as if from this mouth they hoped to
catch a voice from a greater distance, an oracular murmur, or perhaps an
access to something in themselves which they could not express and which
might have somehow released them. The group immediately scattered, and
men’s faces as they took their various ways assumed a closed and
disappointed expression. To my gaze as I passed through the darkening
streets, the black dots thronging there no longer evoked an incoherent
swarm of insects, but rather a field of iron filings combined and recombined
by the passage of an invisible magnet; at the fateful hour which was
approaching, it sometimes seemed that the lines of force which Orsenna’s
history had inscribed in the city’s soil were being charged once again with
an active electricity, recovering the power of organizing these long-dormant
shadows, and newly conscious of a resonance proceeding from a greater
distance than the zone of received ideas. Thus the upper city, originally
Orsenna’s nucleus, clustered on a hilltop around Saint Jude’s Cathedral and
the palace of the Council of Superintendence, now saw its winding streets
humming once more with the after-work crowds which had long since
abandoned it for the more spacious and active districts—in the marshes—to
which most commercial establishments had moved; mysteriously
ennervated once again after centuries of lethargy, it seemed as if, at the
day’s close, the city’s reactivated heart was beginning to beat again. From
these dark alleys to the blind and echoing facades, people from outlying
suburbs who came here to argue until late in the evening made an exchange
for news and an arena for street orators; war cries and patriotic slogans rang
out from the very porches where guild banners had once been raised to
signal uprisings, and mouths gaping as they shouted their catchwords
suddenly seemed full of shadows, as if through them all the darkness that
lay buried in the city’s graves were being disgorged. As places of ill repute
often huddle in the lee of sanctuaries, so old Aldobrandi had his lair here—
a town house for which he had left the dim palace of the Borgo—and a

particular quality in the crowd’s ferment always betrayed his approach
through the alleyways: remarks became more brutal, expletives more
peremptory, and watchwords rang out that were taken up by the corner
speakers, nor were street fights infrequent; it was also said that at nightfall
certain more sinister arguments were ventured, and that money and wine
were liberally distributed; but for me it was a more troubling sign that the
police stubbornly avoided making their rounds in the vicinity of the Council
palace: here was already being created—as happens whenever authority
flags—by a complex interplay of calculation, inertia, and fear, a kind of
concession, a free zone where people flocked by instinct and overflowed
the bounds that ordinarily contained them: as in a worn canvas that, once it
is stretched, reveals by patches of brighter threads the places where it will
come apart, there appeared in Orsenna, without anyone’s daring to admit it,
certain patches of insurrection. By day, voices were raised against the
Signory’s avowed weakness in the face of provocations; by night, the
assertion was proved by breaking into empty shops and looting the goods
on display: neither proof, thus administered, altogether failed of its
effectiveness.
Yet no one appeared to be troubled by such conduct, and even among the
officials responsible for the city’s security, it was remarkable that these
symptoms were received so blandly and that the agitators could count, even
here, on at least a semblance of complicity. A kind of acceleration swept the
city; a secret craving, an admiration which was not always acknowledged,
now attached itself to everything that seemed to move forward, to
everything that seemed to advance more rapidly ... In the most exclusive
salons, where Aldobrandi now had his acknowledged confederates, a new
prejudice prevailed, by which moreover he covered his actions with a
consummate cynicism: any disapproval expressed against the behavior of
his men was taken as a sign of the worst possible taste, of a spirit
measurably behind the times, a condemnation without appeal now that
fashionable opinion had decreed that “times had changed.” Why they had
changed was what no one could quite explain, and perhaps the phrase had
to be interpreted less as the specific acknowledgment of an alteration in the

order of things than as the activation of that infinitely subtle touch which
links us to the wind’s direction, to the imperceptibly increased weight of the
air, and in the absence of any material evidence unmistakably warns us of a
“change in the climate.” And it was not only this more sinister color—
darkening each man’s mental landscape now as if he were reading the
future through a smoked glass—which seemed new: it was as if the very
rhythm of time had changed in Orsenna. Between the high walls, sometimes
sealed in their medieval nakedness, sometimes draped in the frivolous garb
which centuries of opulence had thrown over the facades like the
embellishment of a night of riot, when the sickly gusts of wind which rise in
Orsenna each evening had swept the last dead leaves from the pavement
and the last residents of the lower city were hurrying home through a pause
punctuated by the occasional slamming of heavy doors, I was struck by an
altogether new impression as I followed these avenues that seemed more
readily accessible to the tread of new crowds and to the sun of a new day—
an impression that time itself was flowing, flowing like blood, flowing in a
torrent through the streets. And one might have said that each man drank
his hope and his strength here, as in the first gust of wind from the high
seas: beyond differences of class and circumstance, this kind of
spontaneous fraternity in the streets resembled that of people embarked on
the same vessel, linked by the solidarity of a crew’s reflexes when their ship
is getting under way and when the words “death” or “disease” yield in the
imagination to the words “typhoon” or “shipwreck.” A great shared
privilege relaxed the impulses of jealousy and envy, leveled ranks, and
stirred together a mass more easily united—the privilege of a whole people,
fastened to the soil and now informed through its inner ear, a people which
new times were pushing on stage and which, abandoning its alleys and
cellars, was instinctively making its way pell-mell toward the only light
worth being blinded by: toward broad daylight.
These were the reflections which preoccupied me, while in the dimming
twilight of the brief winter evening I made my way through the little streets
of the upper city toward the Council’s old palace. A sudden desire to walk
there had made me send back the ritual closed car which the Council,

reluctant to expose to scrutiny the rare figures admitted through its doors,
discreetly sent to whomever it had decided to summon. The air was cold
now; a dry north wind had swept away the marsh mists, and sometimes the
winding labyrinth of alleys revealed a narrow prospect which flashed
downward toward the city lying bluish and flattened below, the first stars of
its lamplight fringing the black smears of the nearby forests, while
sustained bugle calls rang out limpid and clarified by distance as they rose
from the barracks through the transparent air: so it must have been for the
magistrates in the old days, perched up here in their eyrie, hidden on the
horizon of each gaze where the city had pressed its heavy weight upon the
earth, still profiled on the margin of each thought ... From the scattered and
remote cottages, already lost on the forest’s verge, columns of smoke rose
one by one, and as these drifted together they swathed in soft mist the city’s
core bristling with steeples and towers. To the North, the already indistinct
horizon was melting into deep forests among which the frontier wound its
way; in the gaps of the narrow streets which plunged southward, brighter
traces paled still farther off: the bald steppes which began beyond
Mercanza, the damp, still-gleaming cobbles of the old trade routes deeply
scored the steep prospect of the sleeping hillsides—markets, fortresses,
warehouses, battlefields were organized by the scars of that dead moon
among the tranquil evidence of the stars. If you knew how to read it, you
might have said that Orsenna was holding an open palm to the sky. Walking
at this hour through the sharply ridged, windy little streets into the grim
squares paved with hard stones and between the facades that glistened like
tiled pits, hemmed in by all these pinnacles of severe blocks which
constituted the upper city, still so reminiscent of the cloister and the
fortress, I felt a kind of austere power and melancholy rigor. From this
lookout with its hard, almost vitrified sky, with its dry and sober lines, as
though from the bridge of man-of-war, one dominated the soft shadows
encircling the wrinkled lands below: here was the habitation, amid an air
without taste or scent steeping the high heaps of bare stones, of a spirit of
altitude and desiccation, unwinking and tearless eyelids long folded over
secret and precise instruments of measurement, pupils concentrated on

deciphering the spectacle of lines and dots, the gaunt diagram which
Orsenna’s earth became under one’s plunging gaze.
Though I vainly reminded myself that I had been summoned merely to
furnish certain details to supplement my report, and that there could be no
question for anyone in my subordinate position of having anything to do
with secrets of State, I was filled, as I approached the palace, by an intense
curiosity, a virtual fever. The day before, I had had a more precise
conversation than usual with Orlando, who had been pretty well informed
of the struggles for influence and the shifts in equilibrium occurring in the
upper echelons of power. If I allowed for a certain aura of mystery, the
romanticism with which Orlando always tinged political matters—as people
who abide only on power’s fringes instinctively seek to exaggerate its luster
so that some reflection of it will cling to themselves—there nonetheless
emerged quite clearly from his remarks the fact that, in these latter days,
though almost no one in the city had been concerned about it, a new figure
had assumed a leading position in the Council: the figure, in fact, of old
Danielo, who had been so intimate a friend of my father’s. According to the
accounts Orlando had given me, the interest which led the ruling faction to
bring Danielo to prominence became quite distinct if one put side by side,
like pieces of a puzzle, certain “corrected” drawings by lot which had
occurred in recent months with a view to renewing the Council, and all of
which had tended to reinforce Danielo’s position there without anyone’s
attention ever being roused by a spectacular change. I had long known
through my father about this “proportional” practice, subtler than the art of
any chef, by which a winning party—and for this the Signory’s means were
virtually infinite—incorporated by infinitesimal fractions into the body
politic certain elements which might at first seem quite unassimilable; nor
did I disagree with Orlando that the operation had all the more significance
for having been even more clandestinely undertaken. According to him, the
provocative agitation which focused on Aldobrandi had served quite
deliberately to mask this sapping enterprise and had flexed the expected
resistances elsewhere than where they might have been more pertinently
applied; he alluded to this operation as already largely accomplished and,

taking into account only the clues at his disposal, “absolutely successful”:
according to Orlando, old Danielo had already and quite automatically
gained the decisive seven votes which in the Council of Superintendence
constitutes the “emergency majority” and deprives the minority’s
reservations of any practical effect. The figure of old Danielo had therefore
preoccupied me since the night before; now I was inclined to see his hand
in the instruction which I had received at the Admiralty and which so
remarkably countered all the Signory’s neutral routines; I decided to take
my father’s senile gabble less lightly and to regard as quite natural the
flattering notion that I would be seeing Danielo in person. I summoned up
all I had been able to learn about him from my conversations with my
father, annoyed now at having granted them so unconcerned a hearing; what
little I recalled assumed a vivid aspect for all its discontinuity, but they were
in fact only picturesque details of no definite drift, the sort of thing that
recurs to the mind through the mists of childhood. The most remarkable
feature of old Danielo’s career was that, oriented since his youth toward
purely disinterested and speculative investigation (he was the author of a
History of the Origins which Orsenna considered authoritative with regard
to the period of the city’s foundation), at the age of sixty he had begun
taking part in the city’s political intrigues, at a time when most statesmen
seek a justification for their past actions in a biography of Agathocles or
Mark Antony; indeed the prejudice of temporization and inertia which
worked against a scholar had long prevailed, to the point of somewhat
limiting, even in Orsenna, this second career, despite proofs of a
stubbornness and an incisive will which he had lost no time in evidencing.
His touchy character gained him few friends; aside from the hours when
service to the State required his presence in the city, he appeared to live
virtually alone on his Bordighera estates, buried in his library. From certain
anecdotes which my father had regaled me with or which had swept
through the city, there appeared, in the intensity of misanthropy and scorn
which tinged the figure, something violent and almost mad; yet, in the
voices which agreed, after the excessively knowing salon giggles, in
acknowledging him as a “character,” there invariably appeared something

intimidated and circumspect, as if the shadow of a bird of prey with
powerful talons had suddenly soared over the sheeplike gathering.
Moreover, it was singular that in Orsenna—where the very least that was
required of those aspiring to power was that by family alliances, by more or
less secret weaknesses, and by the commitments made to the clans, such
men should grant pledges and concession on all sides—an individual had
been permitted to reach the highest echelons of power without offering
more in the way of hostages. Without a wife, a mistress, or friends, without
known vices and without a murky past, he seemed to have nothing about
him of that seamy rind over which the politician’s rather uncertain thumb
likes to pass, in order to reassure itself and to affirm its common touch; this
naked, smooth but long-protected and carefully shielded strength suggested
instead, Orlando remarked, a sword in its scabbard. Yet Danielo was old—
he bore upon him the city’s curse; he had grown old in Orsenna; I imagined
his wavering figure, his dry and liver-spotted hands, his tentative footsteps
beneath the Council’s long black gown; Orsenna had worn out others, and I
knew what could subsist of a man once he had left, in official channels, in
order to become this august and emaciated ghost, every unacceptable
impulse of independence, will, and hope.
There was no trace, as I approached the Council’s palace, of the agitated
comings and goings which designate the nerve centers of a city at moments
of crisis. At this hour when it had already been abandoned by all the minor
functionaries, the palace seemed almost empty, and the few figures I passed
scurried down the corridors with the intimidating familiarity of a
freemasonry which recognizes its members and regards itself as altogether
at home; these silhouettes, some of whom I could identify as members of
illustrious families and who addressed each other by their first names with
brief, routine salutes that were incomprehensible to me, made me anything
but comfortable; I realized I was entering a closed world: the very air I
breathed in these empty, chilly halls—darkened by the lozenged and opaque
latticework of their windows and so quickly surrendered to gloom that my
footsteps unintentionally grew faint as they ventured into these somnolent
spaces—seemed vaguely impregnated with a more volatile essence, the

kind of which it is expressively said that they exist in the state of scents,
traces which escape attention once they have roused it, and in whose subtle
distillation time—a time which instead of devouring itself seemed here to
decant and thicken itself like the lees of old wine, with that almost spiritual
succulence by which certain noble vintages make the years themselves
explode on the tongue—counted for almost everything. It seemed rather
than that it was confined by them that this air had preserved the old walls,
like those putrescent swamps which give the pilings driven into them the
eternity of stone, so that out of this ancient and immaterial element the gold
leaf of the faded ceilings, the heavy leather peeling from the walls, the
dense substance of the chiseled tables, the barbarous thrones of raw oak
continued to nourish and imperceptibly make lustrous their worn patina, a
life which abandoned the comings and goings of the pale shadows still
faintly throbbing with the slowed pulse of the winter season: as in certain
monuments more deeply perforated than a coral reef and more organically
encrusted by the centuries, on which the population instinctively feels that
the very survival of certain very old cities concretely depends, here one
touched Orsenna’s depths and, almost materially, the uninterrupted series of
its strata—a sustaining polypary thickened here and grew ever higher,
raised by the very enormity of its mass to the point where it virtually
floated.
The usher who had taken charge of me upon my appearance at the palace
doors (one never walked unaccompanied through the Council’s corridors)
showed me into a dim and rather low-ceilinged hall on an upper floor. A
long, heavy table filled one corner; strangely massive and set off with
carved plaques, it oddly evoked, in this crumbling palace, the barbarous
ages of Orsenna—iron crowns set with rough stones, all the splendid and
savage ceremonial of the Lombard ages. The walls, as everywhere in the
palace, were covered from floor to ceiling with dark leather; the windows,
with their narrow leading and translucent panes, admitted no more than a
diffused and exhausted glow, as if the palace had sunk to the bottom of a
sealed courtyard; one of these windows, however, was open, revealing a
narrow rectangle where the clear sky was already darkening to night, and

the eye—attracted as though it were a prisoner’s—plunged down the city’s
slope toward the horizon of distant woods; gradually the gleam of a lamp
set on a bracket near the huge table extinguished the vestiges of daylight
and gave the room a look of anonymous intimacy, the sort of calm vigilance
one might expect in a chapel, inspiring in me the secure and infinitely
comforting sense that I was expected. I heard muffled footsteps
approaching, severe yet somehow expressing an indefinable feeling of
relief, like the steps of a priest crossing a church after performing his
functions; a hand was laid on my shoulder, or rather brushed over it, but
with a nuance—as subtly expressed as that of any pianist at a keyboard—of
reserved yet benevolent ease, and even before turning around I realized in
what manner the face behind me was smiling.
“So it’s you ...,” breathed a voice whose charm consisted of an
expressible composure, as if the syllables reached the ear distinct and new,
having been steeped, one by one, in a transparent liquid.
Old Danielo’s hand slid off my shoulder as he walked very deliberately
around my chair and stared into my eyes a moment. A sudden acuity
seemed to pass into the kindly smile; as I stood up, the hand returned to my
shoulder with a gentleness and a remission in the gesture permitted only to
someone inveterately obeyed.
He seemed in no hurry to sit down and, planted quite motionlessly in
front of me against the now very dim windows, appeared to enjoy—like a
man who neglects none of his advantages—the supremacy of his strange,
tall silhouette which towered over me. His features were swallowed up by
the almost opaque shadows, yet there was a tension in the motionless figure
observing me, supple and almost elegant beneath the long Council gown:
there was something oppressive about it. I felt, at the touch of theater in this
encounter, that in these premises, where traditions of the secret police
mingled with the highest executive decision (in Orsenna, the Council’s
interrogations made more than one face turn pale at their mere recollection),
the game was played closer to the man, without any of the usual forbidden

moves, in a tête-à-tête which had, here, assumed only too often a dreadfully
concrete meaning.
“I used to be very fond of your father, Aldo. And for a long time, now,
I’ve been eager to know you ...”
The lamplight fell across his face as he sat down now, articulating the
famous and imperious nose of the Danieli, so insolently recognizable that I
felt a shock—as if I had identified a king walking in the street by the profile
engraved on the coins of the realm. A kind of mist floated around his gray
eyes, veiled yet watchful in their heavy repose, at once those of a hunter of
beasts and a waking dreamer. It was the face of a man whose blood was
heavy, full of brutal passions and earthly appetites. And yet an inner ardor
seemed to temper these overwhelming lineaments of a whole race: as if this
refinement—the more supernatural for being so visibly unwelcome—this
clumsy and almost uncouth gentleness had been imprinted, after years of
fierce fighting, by the cloister’s long-sealed door upon the face of a
converted knight. “... I regret that it should be under such painful
circumstances.” The gray eyes met mine as his head rose in a brusque
movement, and I felt that I was stiffening in my chair, but what followed
disconcerted me even more. “... We have not been able to recover, I am told,
Captain Marino’s body. How greatly all of us have felt his loss. He was a
distinguished officer and a loyal servant of the State.” The voice seemed to
adjust itself to a narrower compass, the way you might slide your fingernail
into a crack. “... I know you had become close friends ...”
“The captain was a man without guile, and never attempted to blame
others ... It’s true that I was very fond of him, and I was grateful to him for
making my work at the Admiralty so much easier.”
“I know the captain would have chosen to rest in Orsenna’s ... earth,” the
voice went on with sudden gravity. “More than anyone else, he was entitled
to do so. I therefore request that upon your return to the Admiralty, you
continue the search for his body.” His fingers drummed on the table,
irresolute and bored, and for a moment I thought the audience was going to
stop short. A weary expression appeared in the gray eyes. I suddenly felt

extremely uncomfortable. “Do you sleep without dreaming, Signor
Observer?”
The question was asked in a tone of neutral politeness. For a second I
remained stupidly silent, then I felt that my blood was draining from my
face, and my fingers clutched the arm of my chair. “I thought ...” I began in
a broken voice, and I felt my mouth going dry, “...as God is my witness, I
thought ...” I half stood up from my seat, suddenly overcome by panic. “...
The instructions I received seemed ... well, led me to believe ... I thought I
was being told—without anyone’s saying as much, of course ... that I
should go across and have a look,” I stammered out, my throat convulsed.
The gray eyes did not blink, but something like a smile passed over the
half-illuminated countenance. “Relax, please sit down ... Your blood is so
hot, perhaps that’s only natural in so young a man. There! There!” he added
with an almost kindly irony, leaning toward me. “I haven’t said I was
sleeping well.”
An enormous weight suddenly slid off my chest, and I realized that it had
been many days since I had really breathed. The man before me had the
power to loosen and to bind. A mad craving passed through me: to kiss that
long, dry hand which hung before me in the shadows at the armchair’s
edge.
“How many men are at present on duty at the Admiralty?” old Danielo
suddenly asked in a short sharp tone of voice, raising his head. He was
holding a pencil now, and tapped the table with the point.
“Two hundred, minus the groups needed to restore the coast batteries.”
“Marino must have informed you of the imminent arrival of two
gunboats ... They’re being put into service now—you’ll be receiving them
with skeleton crews, which you will complete down there.”
“But ...”
“I know,” the black silhouette interrupted, the voice calm and rather low
now, suddenly without energy. “These are not, apparently, your duties. But
circumstances require ... No successor to Captain Marino has been

appointed, for the time being. Moreover, you have the help of experienced
officers on the spot.”
Something in the complacent voice signified that such a nomination
would not occur for a long time. I bowed with a gesture of uneasy
deference. “I’ll do the best I can, if I have deserved the Signory’s
confidence.”
“You do not have ‘our’ confidence,” the voice resumed, and this time a
note of murderous irony sounded in it. “You do not deserve it, and have
never had it. You have our ... authorization. Which is all that can be
expected of a State cast into difficult circumstances, and subject to ...
chance.” There was a tiny spasm of fatigue, and suddenly he seemed
terribly old. “I’m going to trust you with a State secret, one it is not wise to
share with too many of the staff,” he went on, raising his head again and
smiling into the gloom, “a secret of weakness. Initially, you see, when an
unforeseen incident occurs which takes a bad turn, we always maintain in
command the man on whose account it all began ... Does that seem odd to
you?” he inquired, trying to meet my eyes.
“You may have reasons I know nothing about,” I remarked, embarrassed
and circumspect.
“Several, as I see it,” he said, his voice clear and slow. “The intellectual
sloth endemic to good governments, first of all. And the instinct to cover
one’s tracks in the eyes of public opinion, which is always ready to assume,
when one tightens the reins too soon, that ‘if things had only been left
alone,’ but to which, if worse comes to worst, one will be in a position to
sacrifice an appropriately black scapegoat. No, I don’t mean you ...” he
smiled, seeing my uncomfortable grimace. He seemed to reflect for a few
seconds, and on his face appeared that vague and almost absentminded
expression which contrasted so vividly with that determined countenance
and those powerful jaws. “Perhaps too there is a darker reason—one more
difficult to explain. Once a man has been deeply involved in certain acts
which exceed his grasp, there is a sense that some part of him has remained
unacknowledged, unrecognized, since such acts have been generated by just

that aspect ... And perhaps there’s a kind of sacrilege in putting asunder
what the event had united. Don’t you think, Signor Observer, that there are
men who belong to certain acts—acts particularly difficult of access,
incomprehensible acts, precisely because the ladder has been removed—the
ladder that leads from them to him? ...”
“If I do belong to this act, I can’t be the only one who does,” I said, my
voice a whisper. “A clear order from the Signory would have kept it from
happening. I don’t believe I ever had occasion to receive any such order.”
In an abrupt movement that he suddenly seemed unable to control, old
Danielo stood up and began walking across the room. He walked extremely
slowly, extremely silently. When he turned back toward me, a faint rustle of
silk ran through his black gown, and the flame of the lamp wavered. He
looked like a man who gets up in the middle of the night and walks through
his bedroom under the burden of an intolerable thought. It was as if he had
forgotten I was there.
“No, you’re not mistaken,” he said at last, his voice very low, “I shall not
be so vain as to deny it. The cause was put in your hands, you had been
given permission. I didn’t know you would go across ... But I knew it was
possible. I knew I was leaving a door open.”
“Why did you permit such a thing?” I asked softly, leaning my head
toward him.
He glanced at me suspiciously, his eyes filled with disdain—the disdain
of a man of power caught off guard; I was interrogating him, and for a
second I felt he was hesitating, in offended majesty, to answer me, but he let
his head drop almost imperceptibly. “There are certain questions one does
not ask. But I have summoned you here without witnesses ...” He smiled
again, a smile which seemed elsewhere, like a man who pretends to pursue
a polite argument but who conceals a weapon in his sleeve. The memory of
the Council’s prisons and of the discreet executions performed there crossed
my mind, but now I was confronting something quite different from fear—
an intense, almost painful curiosity drowned out all apprehension. “To
whom would the explanation be made but to the one man capable of

understanding it?” he said suddenly, with a smile of extreme intimacy.
“What I’m going to tell you now ... no one here would understand. And no
one will hear it from you,” he added in a swift, harsh tone of voice. “I am
the master here, remember that, Aldo, and anything you repeat will cost you
dear. I have decided to speak to you as man to man, tonight, because you
are close to me, because I have followed you, at a distance, from hour to
hour, because I was the force which sustained you and impelled you—
because I was with you on the boat ...” He continued walking up and down,
taking slow, silent steps. “... I have loved power,” he continued rather
loudly, which made me listen all the more closely, since the tone accorded
so poorly to the resonance and the dimensions of the room—as in a room
where someone is talking in his sleep. “I shall not reject my pleasure ... It
has entertained me for years. Power is a great thing, Aldo; since you, too,
can lay claim here to a certain importance, do not believe those who will try
to disabuse you of its charms. There is a certain kind of philosopher who
grows like moss on ruins ... the kind who celebrates the nourishments of air
and anathematizes what grows in the fertile earth; such men will put you on
guard against the vanity of experience and warn you against everything
which isn’t born in desiccation; but believe me, Aldo, it’s worth the trouble
to put down roots—it’s worth the trouble to rule over even a ruined State.
One advances between two rows of bowing men, and if you’re an amateur
of human affairs, it’s worth the trouble to observe a bowing man—it’s a
way of gaining time—and only in that position do they give off a certain
scent, one which is theirs alone, just as the quickest way to become intimate
with certain perfumes is to break a branch or two. That’s how I knew your
father, not so long ago, Aldo—I knew nothing of him—he had merely been
my friend for twenty years; he had to come and ask me for a position ...
There’s a kind of amusement in that, a real entertainment, and then
something else concerned me too ... For thirty years I’ve been a man of
books, and—well! let’s say I understood the march of history through the
small end of the telescope: relationships, necessity, the mechanism of
affairs, everything except one thing which is the great childish secret, which
you have to put your finger in the pie to learn: the ease—the disconcerting

ease with which things are done. So for me there was also that almost
inexhaustible amusement: to realize that the machine works, that a thousand
gears will mesh and function when you press the button. At the beginning,
this was almost beyond belief: to find yourself in front of buttons you can
still push—it makes you a little dizzy; and then comes another pleasure, the
pleasure of reaching one and the same goal by several circuits. You never
get tired—it’s a long time before you get tired of seeing that those gears
mesh: the perfume of the pulverized human substance—I promise you
that’s a stench you don’t get out of your nostrils, it’s quite a different matter
from understanding how the mill works. Finally, I’ve taken a certain
pleasure in this machinery which actually functioned—it was more than the
screech of axles and the grating of wheels. I’ve had my good times, I don’t
regret it. Only something else has come ...” He stopped a moment and the
wrinkles of his forehead seemed to pursue an irritating thought. “...It isn’t
coming quickly, Aldo. It makes itself known from very far off, but only at
intervals—for after all, it was a full life one was living—by a sort of
blinking, only a little brighter, like the first heat lightning at the end of a
summer day. A thing which takes its time ... A thing which is in no hurry,
which grows of its own accord, which can wait, which knows it will profit
from ... everything. A preoccupation which is anything but ... yes, an
obsession which leaves you long respites, more respites than other kinds,
but stubbornly refuses to mix with the rest, which disdains and withdraws,
which actually eclipses itself rather than compromise, but which makes you
realize that only one hour counts for it, and except for that one hour nothing
else counts: the hour when it leaps upon you, the hour when it seizes you
completely. The woman who will ruin a man’s life often announces her
presence by such casual eclipses: a little tap at the windowpane from time
to time, almost imperceptible yet distinct, with that percussive accent that
makes everything give a little start, and never mingles with any other
sound: she has passed by again, deep inside yourself you know it, that’s all;
perhaps you’ll have to wait, wait a long time still, but a nerve has been
touched deep inside you, a nerve which keeps listening for that one sound,
nothing else can reach it. As for me, it was Farghestan whose tap on the

windowpane I was waiting for ... In the lulls of the murmur woven around
me by the world and its affairs, I suddenly became aware of a curious
silence, almost a rude silence—one of those gaps in a conversation which
are so upsetting, and if you yield to the void they cause, they lead without
your even realizing what’s happening to two wide eyes—two eyes which
stare at you without a word being spoken, two eyes which have managed to
create a silence around them. I was concerned with that kind of silence. The
thing which was advancing so cautiously behind it was making me a sign,
sometimes seemed to keep its distance but never took its eyes off me; I had
an assignation with it—an intimidating tête-à-tête ... And a strange sense of
power, a real exaltation dawned at my approach: between every act—
between the one I alone was beginning to glimpse and the one no one
remembered any longer, was the act I could perform. That act baptized the
world. Instead of its being a conclusion, everything started from it anew. It
was terrible, it was rash: the wisdom of men, the security of the city argued
against it ... The world, Aldo, expects certain beings and certain moments to
restore its youth to it; something uncertain rattles the door which needs no
more than permission to open, a permission in which the whole soul is
steeped. Could I have thought for a second of the security of an old and
rotting city? That city lies stiff in its grave and ripe on its inert stones—and
what can still delight an inert stone except to become, once more, the bed of
a raging torrent?”
Old Danielo leaned forward with a weary gesture and rested his forehead
in his hands. There was a moment’s silence. Suddenly it seemed to me that
this silence had grown deeper; the distant murmur of the newly deserted
palace had long since ceased; the ticking of a clock, only recently
perceptible, scratched at this smooth silence like some insect’s mandibles. I
stared at the square of sky outlined by the window, quite black now: a few
stars glittered there, sifting into the room as though to the bottom of a well.
Suddenly it seemed as if nothing had ever been so much at rest as on this
evening: the faint, even glow of the warm room cast a magical silence over
the sleeping city.

“... Why do I suddenly need to tell you such things? And you of all
people? ...” Danielo continued in a pensive tone of voice. “A time comes
when the idea that the meaning of one act—the most singular act in your
whole life—might vanish with you forever becomes intolerable. I think the
time has come for me to bear witness—to testify,” he said after a moment’s
pause, with a strange smile.
I said nothing. There was nothing to say—the old man expected me to
say nothing; for the last few minutes I felt that my presence had become
vague to him, and that he was speaking in his own presence with a singular
unconcern for my attitude and my gestures, the way one speaks at a
deathbed.
“... The City ...” he continued while a kind of cold glow passed over his
face, like the distant reflection of some great fire ... “It seems to me I can
speak of the City ... For them it was the legacy one hands on intact, the bit
of earth one cares for and says farewell to; for me it was the pyre prepared
for my torch—a thing that awaited its meaning and its consummation from
me. I had a tryst with it, it seems to me, at closer range ....
“I’ve followed you, Aldo—from a distance. I know what you wanted ...
and I know that only going all the way was enough. There was that act
before me—not even an act, hardly a permission, an acquiescence—and
