through it everything possible collapsing, an avalanche, everything which
makes the world emptier, if I don’t commit that act. Emptier forever if I
don’t commit it ... And behind, nothing: the mummified repose of that
vague phantom; the void intensified on earth by that obscene yawn and
those ears accustomed to nothing but the tiny intimate creaks of the coffin.
It’s a terrible thing for a man to be a channel, a sheath for nothingness—to
make his will into a stone tossed into the current. I had time to become
serious, I had stopped wanting to win—now it was time to hasten the
advent ... The world, Aldo, blossoms through those of us who surrender to
temptation. The world is justified only at the eternal cost of its security. All
I wanted was to tell you how things have happened,” he continued, his
voice faintly more relaxed than during the rest of his discourse, which had

never departed from the tone of polite conversation, “and that’s why we
happen to meet here tonight.”
“And now?” I asked uncertainly, more to break the heavy silence,
embarrassed as I was at having to answer this ready and mannerly
confession, as if the whole time the old man had taken me for someone else.
“Now?” Danielo echoed, raising his eyebrows with a touch of
astonishment. “... When you set out on that ... crossing of yours, you didn’t
wonder—did you, Aldo?—what was behind you. No one here wonders.
There’s something more urgent ...”
“More urgent?”
Danielo’s eyes closed and his face assumed an intense, almost agonized
expression. “There are things more urgent than saving a life, aren’t there,
Aldo? If in fact Orsenna is still alive ... There’s salvation. Things don’t
always end at the threshold you expect—the only one you can see ...”
The old man’s eyes lingered a moment on the crimson seal of the pass
lying on the table in front of him. There was neither hate nor fear in his
gaze, rather a contemplative and purged glow. Suddenly a strange
comparison occurred to me: I thought of that “society” which, for all
Maremma, now commanded Rhages; Orlando’s remarks on the
“proportioning” by which the new spirit had infiltrated Orsenna came back
to me, and suddenly it was as if, between these shadowy forces, the envoy’s
face made an unexpected hyphen. It was as if old Danielo, behind his veiled
pupils, had pulled the strings and kept for himself the secret meaning of that
dialogue which had so baffled me, and which I had not been able to sustain
that night.
“Is that the Pact you meant?” I snapped at him, suddenly struck by a
recollection. “... The pact that would bind the city? ... Am I to understand
that you’ve determined its fate and chosen the worst?”
The old man shrugged. “Chosen ... Determined ... Could I do any such
thing? What it has now, the city gave itself. Only the city could have
empowered such a pact. It had to believe in it—and that depended on no
one in the world.”

“What it has ... ?”
“A destiny,” Danielo said, turning his head away, as a physician utters the
fatal diagnosis. “Haven’t you noticed the signs? Haven’t you seen,” he
continued with dreamy irony, “how everything here has been miraculously
renewed?”
“It’s impossible,” I argued, and I could hear the desperation in my voice.
“There’s no destiny that forbids you to survive ...”
“You’re wrong, Aldo, it’s not a matter of subsisting,” the old man said
coldly. “I’m not a politician. There’s a time for such men—a time to
maneuver between the reefs, and a time to clutch the thread that leads to the
heart of the labyrinth. You held that thread, and it led you where you ...
where you went.”
“I was obeying orders,” I said harshly, “or believed I was. I wasn’t
responsible for the City. You were.”
Danielo shrugged again, with weariness and vexation. “Do you really
think so?” For a moment he seemed to ponder deeply, and again the
wrinkles on his forehead pursued some obsessive thought. “... When you
rule, nothing is worse than losing hold. Once the Thing came to me, it was a
strange discovery to realize that this was the only way to hold onto
Orsenna. Everything that focused on Syrtes again, everything that led to the
renewal of your ... episode made the old gears turn with almost
phantasmagorical ease, everything that failed to concern it met with a wall
of inertia and unconcern. The Thing took advantage of every instance—the
gestures to accelerate it and the gestures to slow it down—like a man
sliding down the slope of a roof. Once the question was raised—how can I
put it to you?—everything was mobilized of its own accord. In the
Council’s deliberations, suddenly, without any reason, at the turn of a
phrase, in the absurdity of a play on words, by a preposterous angle the
Thing came to rest on those dead mouths, like a fly you keep brushing away
—and those extinguished faces suddenly burst into flame, like a stick of
charcoal! When you rule, you must always go as fast as you can, and the
fastest—unbelievably—was always that nonexistent Thing which uttered its

mute cry—more energetic than any noise, because it was like a pure voice
—which was carving out its place in advance, which distorted everything,
that sleeping Thing the City was pregnant with, and which made a terrible
hollow in its womb ... We were all carrying it ... Yes,” Danielo went on, and
once again he seemed to be staring into the void, “everyone was an
accomplice in that business—everyone helped. Even when people thought
they were doing the opposite ...”
“Old Aldobrandi, among others, I should suppose, hardly thought so.”
Danielo shrugged again. “There are days when Aldobrandi and his clique
remind me of spontaneous generation. If he didn’t exist, Orsenna would
invent him ... You too,” he went on, turning his unseeing eyes to me, “if you
hadn’t been there, the city would have invented you ...”
“Maybe,” I said after a moment’s pensive silence. “But here! Didn’t
anyone really weigh the possibilities—didn’t anyone calculate before
taking this ... this risk?”
“No one ... nothing, Aldo. Oh, we seemed to. Or else we calculated with
false information, with false numbers. Which deceived no one, but which
saved face. Because real calculations would have kept us from taking the
risk, and it was the risk that led us on. Not even the risk ...” he added in
almost a whisper. “Maybe there are moments when you rush into the future
as into a fire—helter skelter. Moments when it intoxicates you like a drug,
when a debilitated body no longer resists ...”
“I know,” I said with an effort. “I saw Maremma catch the disease.
Maybe I caught it myself ... Luckily there’s still time. You know it’s in your
power to put everything back to sleep.”
The old man slowly straightened and met my eyes with an icy, almost
inhuman determination. “You’re wrong there, Aldo. It is too late.”
“Too late?” I had stood up in spite of myself, and I felt my face go pale.
“Too late, Aldo. I’m no longer the one who decides. Orsenna has come
on stage now. Orsenna will not draw back.”
“So you want ...”

“Everything that will come, yes, and may God help us, for there will be
need of that—great need.”
“You’re mad!”
Danielo slowly raised his eyes to mine, without surprise and without
resentment—eyes which seemed to have been steeped by a single flicker of
the lids in some deep and icy solution, eyes suddenly extraordinarily
distant. “You seem strangely mistaken, Aldo, as to what my presence means
here,” he resumed in a cold, calm voice. “It’s no accident that I’m where I
am. Do you think Orsenna is still in a position to play for small stakes?”
“I think I see quite clearly what you’re up to, and for those stakes, in any
country, there’s a name ...”
“Say it then,” the old man sighed, with the same singular calm. “Are you
afraid to?” He pushed away several papers on the table in front of him.
“Make no mistake, Aldo. What I’m telling you, no one will ever hear. It’s
terrible, at certain moments, to feel you’re alone—and to whom would I
speak, if not to someone closer to me than all the others—to someone who’s
been over there. Everything will be submitted in due form, everything will
be put in order—irrefutably—and the noble graybeards that seem to be
painted on the Senate paneling will acquiesce, one after the next, once I
read my report, as if in all their lives there had never been a question of
doing anything else: does one evade the voice of the fatherland? The voice
of the fatherland! ... It never speaks so loud as when it’s a matter of
destroying yourself for no good reason—and it’s not hard to suggest the
kind of language it uses: making the dead speak with discernment and
relevance is the ruler’s art, and it’s Orsenna’s favorite sin to listen to it; you
know how incongruous it always seems to Orsenna to be spoken to in the
present indicative. The honor of Orsenna ... The infidel’s insolent defiance
... A cause which was settled by God centuries ago and which it was none
of our doing to awaken ... The growing insecurity of the Syrtes outposts ...
Our forces multiplied by the calm assurance of their rights ... (ad libitum) ...
The danger of the remote Southern provinces seceding, a menace which,
even if other reasons were not more urgent, must of itself incite us to firm

measures ...” Once again he produced that strange throaty laugh, and once
again choked it off short. “... No, don’t think I’m as cynical as all that.
Everything said will be at least half true. As always. A war is never entirely
lost in advance, and it’s certainly more serious if a State yields when it’s put
itself in the wrong. The reasons Chancelleries offer are always more or less
... reasonable: one asks little more of them than to be decorative—to plug
the holes. False reasons, if you like, but false here, most of all, because they
are instead ...”
“Instead of those that aren’t acceptable?”
“Instead of those that aren’t accepted. There’s no known language, Aldo,
in which a tottering state can confess its innermost difficulties, like a sick
man to his doctor. No language—and that’s too bad. The leaders of old
States are congenitally regarded as fools and hypocrites. As if everyone
weren’t sealing him up in his hypocrisy, that old man in whom every organ
is collapsing and who still, everyone insists, must be in good shape! As if
everyone weren’t in league—and his own family more than all the rest—to
keep him from speaking out about the little discomforts that will soon be
the death of him. Yet that’s what he wants to do—and that’s what he needs
to do, more often than not. And they’re not imaginary diseases. People talk
around him, they talk to him as if there were nothing the matter: legacies,
dividends, marriages, lawsuits ... As if his business could continue as usual,
could hope to catch up with him where he’s going! Sometimes—more and
more frequently—a lull occurs in all the fuss, and a noise can be heard
which he alone pays any attention to: the noise of shores being lapped by
the waves which rapidly subside behind a ship putting out to sea ...I’m
telling you these things because I’m old, and I’m telling you with good
reason because I haven’t grown old alone.”
“The thought of the end?” I ventured, with an involuntary shrug.
“Nonsense!”
“It’s not a thought, Aldo. You’ve understood a great deal, but you’re too
young to understand this. It’s not even an idée fixe. It’s a last will.”

“You enjoy saying such things ...” I was moved more than I cared to
admit by his tone of certainty. “... No one at Orsenna has any great craving
to commit suicide, I can assure you. No one I know. You’re exaggerating.”
“You don’t really believe what you’re saying, Aldo. ‘Suicide’ is an easy
word to say. A State doesn’t die, it’s merely a form which disintegrates. The
fasces are untied. And a moment comes when what’s been fastened together
longs to come apart. And when the time comes, I call that a good thing.
There’s a name for it—it’s called dying the right death.”
“Orsenna’s disintegrating? Who could make it do that?”
“Solitude,” Danielo answered thoughtfully. “Solitude and boredom. It’s
what happens to something that’s felt itself gathered together too long, too
... exclusively. The vacuum that occurs at its frontiers—a kind of numbness
which is generated on its torpid surface as if it had lost the sense of touch—
lost contact. Orsenna has made deserts around itself. The world is a mirror
in which it looks for its image and no longer recognizes it. For years now,
Aldo, I’ve been living with my ears glued to its heart: that heart no longer
expects anything but the dead marsh and the black wave that will submerge
it. It’s been too long since Orsenna has taken chances. Around a living body
there’s the skin that permits contact, the pores that can breathe ... But when
a State has known too many centuries, the skin becomes a wall, a great
wall: then the time has come ... Then it’s time for the trumpets to sound, for
the walls to fall, for the centuries to boil away and the horsemen to enter
through a breach in the wall—the splendid horsemen who smell of wild
grass and cool night, with their alien eyes and their cloaks fluttering in the
wind ...”
“I’m sure you’re right,” I said nervously. “And next thing you know,
heads blossom on the ends of pikes. But you need a little distance from such
things—to gain some perspective ...”
Danielo kept silence a moment and stared at me haughtily. “My blood
belongs to the City,” he observed with cold decision; yet his voice was
trembling. “I’ve done nothing but serve it—inadmissibly. Do you suppose I
could survive it, if things were to reach that point?”

“That point! ... But what forces you to ... that point?” I was shouting, and
I could hear the desperate rage in my voice. “A gesture—one gesture, that’s
all, one that doesn’t even cost your pride anything, and the world goes back
to sleep. A gesture that you can make. That you can also make, for here
everything obeys you.”
The old man seemed to hesitate a second, opened a drawer and held out a
roll of paper.
“Read this,” he said sharply.
The scroll was a police report from Engaddi, a wretched village in the
interior of Syrtes which served as a supply stop for the caravan drivers of
the extreme South. The report was brief and specific: it indicated that a
caravan recently arrived at Engaddi had made contact at the Sarepta water
hole with a dismounted raiding party of Ghazanid nomads, who only rarely
ventured into these regions (through which winds a quite theoretical
frontier) before the return of good weather. Armed Farghian detachments
had just driven the cattle from their remote winter pastures, requisitioning
horses for their cavalry remounts, and information furnished by
eyewitnesses suggested that a “numerous” though unspecified Farghian
army was following them, a few stages away, skirting the Syrtes Sea toward
the Eastern frontier. Questioned as to when the Ghazanids might reoccupy
their pastures, the leader of the advance detachment had replied with a
smile that the cattle and the transport horses would stay there only briefly,
and that “everyone knew that good winter pastures were to be found on the
other side of the frontier.” As this news spread, panic had seized the
population of Engaddi; in order to calm the disturbances, the police had
been obliged to evacuate the women and children to Maremma and to
distribute weapons to the able-bodied men. The police chief demanded
immediate orders “in the face of this new situation.”
“So they’re coming!” I said, and all my rage suddenly collapsed, giving
way to a feeling of certainty and of marvellous peace: it was as if the torpor
of the sands had suddenly been pierced by the sound of a thousand springs

—as if, under the shock of the mysterious army’s millions of marching
steps, the desert was blooming all around me ... to infinity.
“Yes,” old Danielo said, and it seemed to me that his face was suddenly
filled with light. He stood up and walked over to the window. The same
patch of black sky was framed by the dark rectangle, the same stars
glistened there. The suspense of this calm night was so deep and so intimate
that it seemed you could hear someone walking ....
It was very late when I left the Council’s palace. Old Danielo had sent for
the liaison officer attached to the Superintendence services, and we had
discussed at length the military measures that would have to be taken at the
Admiralty. It was decided to make patrols a daily matter, and henceforth to
ignore the “line” which limited their offshore range; in a situation which
seemed to be growing more intense by the hour, it was obviously ludicrous
to observe a discretion which could cost the Admiralty a nasty surprise. The
regions around Vezzano were to be watched particularly closely. A state of
siege would be immediately declared in Maremma where, in the frenzy
possessing the city, arrival of refugees from Engaddi risked creating
dangerous outbursts. All the coastal batteries still in service at the
Admiralty would have their forces immediately filled to capacity. The ships
available for combat—including the reinforcements, their number rose to
four—were to be ready to embark in two hours. Once the ordinance
proclaiming a state of siege was written and sealed, Danielo dismissed the
officer and kept me with him a moment longer.
“So then, Aldo, we’re to part. Tomorrow, at the first possible moment,
you’ll return to Syrtes. God knows how and when we’ll ever meet again.”
“God knows,” I repeated, shaking his dry hand. It was trembling faintly
—as if the night’s chill had suddenly entered the room through the open
window. “Nothing has been said yet,” I added in a voice without conviction,
“the army hasn’t crossed our borders—perhaps they’ll stop there ...”
“No, Aldo.” The old man shook his head slowly. “No. No more than the
stars will stop in their courses. No more than two bodies beginning to make
the gestures of love ... Neither fear, nor rage, nor convenience, nor flight

will save Orsenna from the promised thing it has become for the open eyes
which are upon it ... Nor does Orsenna want to be saved. My memory will
be accursed, perhaps, if there is a memory for me ...” Danielo shrugged his
shoulders. “... When a ship is rotting on the stocks, the man who sends it
back into the waves ... can be described as unconcerned with its loss, but
not with its destination ... Have no regrets,” he said, shaking my hand again
with a sudden emotion, “I myself have none. It’s not a matter of being
judged. And it wasn’t a matter of a good policy, or a bad one, in fact. It was
a matter of answering a question—an intimidating question—a question
which no one still in this world could ever leave unanswered, with his last
breath.”
“What question’s that?”
“Who’s alive?” the old man said with a smile, “qui vive?”—and his
motionless eyes plunged into mine.
The night was bright and clear when I left the deserted palace. A cold
mineral glow scoured the stone cornices, casting inky shadows from the
elaborate wrought-iron trellises of the old wells still used in the upper city’s
little squares. Through the night’s stillness, beyond the naked walls, faint
sounds rose at intervals from the lower city: running water, the rumble of a
distant cart—distinct yet intriguing, like the sighs and movements of a
restless sleeper, or the occasional cracking of desert rocks contracting in the
night’s cold; but in these dry upper districts, the harsh patches of bluish,
milky light clung unwaveringly to the stones like a layer of paint. I walked
on with a pounding heart, my throat dry, and so perfect around me was the
silence of the stones, so compact the ringing frost of that blue night, so
puzzling my footsteps which seemed to float just above the cobblestones,
that I felt I was walking through the bizarre decoration and the bewildering
lighting of an empty theater—but a harsh echo dogged my way for a long
while and ricocheted against the house fronts, a footstep finally fulfilled the
expectations of that empty night, and from now on I knew what the stage
was set for.

Dust cover blurb
With an oeuvre that includes four idiosyncratic, elegantly written novels,
Julien Gracq has established himself as one of France’s eminent postwar
fiction writers. A mysterious and retiring figure, he characteristically
refused the Goncourt, France’s most distinguished literary prize, when it
was awarded to him for this novel, published in 1951.
Set in the mythical Mediterranean nation of Orsenna, The Opposing
Shore concerns Aldo, a young aristocrat sent to observe the activities of a
naval base on the waters separating his native land from Farghestan, the
power with which Orsenna has been in a state of dormant war for three
centuries. The battle has become a complex, tacit game in which no actions
are taken and no peace declared. Aldo comes to understand that everything
depends upon a boundary, unseen but certain, separating the two sides, and
he becomes himself obsessed with this demarcation. Each chapter is a
further initiation into the possibility of transgression, symbolized by
Vanessa, a woman whose complex ties to both sides of the war pull Aldo
deeper into the story’s web.
Richard Howard, acclaimed poet, critic, and translator of more than 150
works including those of Barthes and Baudelaire, calls The Opposing Shore
“a charged, oneiric exploration of the meaning of self-revelation by self-
destruction; with the discovery that only what has been alive can truly die,
and with efforts to overcome the inertia of death-in-life by an ultimate
resolution to ‘cross the line.’”
With its emphasis on transgressions and boundaries, The Opposing Shore
became for many in France a powerful metaphor of that nation’s fall in
1939. “In the strongest transformation,” writes Howard, Gracq’s tale
“becomes a meditation on the French fascination with defeat, and a deeper
reading of history, an exploration of what it means to try to waken the will
through transgression, the consequences of surrendering old loyalties in

order to arrive at new energies. In theme, in style, in atmosphere, this is a
novel of decadence transfigured, the venture (or cast) made beyond what is
eternally rotten to what is (momentarily) mortal.”
Long famous in France as Les Rivages des Syrtes, this novel is
considered by many to be Gracq’s masterpiece. As the latest work in the
Twentieth-Century Continental Fiction series it is now available for the first
time to English-speaking readers.
Twentieth-Century Continental Fiction
JULIEN GRACQ (the pseudonym of Louis Poirier) was born in 1910 in
Saint-le-Vieil. Three of his other novels have appeared in English as A Dark
Stranger, The Castle of Argol, and Balcony in the Forest (also translated by
Richard Howard).
